%% ============================================================
%%  I. THE OBJECT AND METHOD OF PSYCHOLOGY (pp. 1--35)
%% ============================================================
% --- p. 1 ---
\chapter[The Object and Method of Psychology]{The Object and Method of Psychology\opage{1}}
\label{ch:object-method}

\noindent\textbf{1.} Psychology is the science of the soul --- that is
the shortest description we can give of the object of the inquiries we
are here to undertake. But it is a purely provisional description, which
yields no clear and distinct concept. By it we merely set up psychology
as the science of that which thinks, feels and wills, in contrast to
physics as the science of that which moves in space. Within these two
domains lies everything that can be an object of human inquiry.
Psychology is as little obliged to begin with an explanation of what the
soul is as physics is obliged to begin with an explanation of what
matter is. Nor does this general statement of the object of psychology
contain any presupposition as to whether or not the soul exists as an
independent being, separate from matter. Our endeavour is to keep
psychology a pure science of experience, and to distinguish sharply
between what is given and the hypotheses we use to order it and make it
accessible to understanding.

But as soon as we assume that psychical phenomena have certain
characteristic marks by which they can be distinguished from material
ones, we presuppose a piece of knowledge which first appeared at a
certain stage of intellectual development, and which even now cannot be
said to have fully prevailed. It will throw light on the object of
psychology to bring out some features characterizing the way in which
the idea of the psychical has developed in the human race, and still
develops in each individual.

% --- p. 2 ---
\opage{2}\textbf{2.} It is with the mind's eye as with the bodily eye: from the
first it is turned outward. The eye perceives external objects, their
colors and forms, and only by artificial detours does it see itself and
what is within it. And as regards external objects themselves, the eye
is naturally adjusted to the far point, the greatest distance of vision;
while we feel a certain strain in accommodating the eye to nearer
objects, it is with a feeling of relief and rest that we turn our gaze
from the nearer to the more distant. In the same way we are taken up
with external objects long before we think of the very sensing and
imagining through which alone external objects exist for us. Our
immediate, natural life we lead in sense-perception and fantasy, not in
subjective reflection. This is connected with the fact that man is a
practician before he is a theorist. His weal and woe depend on his being
able to forget himself in his surroundings. Observations of the doings
of men and animals, of the appearance of plants and fruits, of the
course of the heavenly bodies, etc., are more important in the struggle
for existence than observations of oneself. Only at a higher stage of
culture can the command be uttered: Know thyself! --- and with it the
way be opened for direct psychological inquiry.

\textbf{3.} Since language has developed under the influence of
attention directed to the external world, we find that the expressions
for psychical phenomena are originally taken from the material world.
The inner world of the spirit is designated by symbols taken from the
outer world of space. This observation had already been made by
\emph{Locke} and \emph{Leibnitz}, and recent linguistics has confirmed
it. ``All roots, i.e.\ all the material elements of language, are the
audible signs of sense-impressions, and of such only; and since all
words, even the most abstract and the most exalted, are derived from
roots, comparative philology fully confirms the conclusions which Locke
% footnote *) on p. 2
reached.''\footnote{\emph{Max Müller}: Vorlesungen über die Wissenschaft
der Sprache. II. p.~320. --- Locke gives as examples, among others,
\textit{to imagine, comprehend, conceive, spirit}. Müller adds, among
others, \textit{animus} from \textit{anima}, breath; cf.\ Sanskrit \textit{an},
to blow, Greek \textit{aënai}, and Sanskrit \textit{anila}, Greek
\textit{anemos}, wind.} Interesting examples are found in the words that
designate spirit and soul in the various
% --- p. 3 ---
\opage{3}languages, in the names of the mental activities, and in the
meaning of the prepositions. ``It may be taken as granted that all
prepositions proper originally designate relations in space exclusively
--- not only because every tracing of their particular meanings back to
the source leads there, but because all particular ideas are arranged
for the imagination into connection in the schema of space, and because
these spatial relations were the only ones that could be pointed out and
that presented themselves in such a way that an understanding could form
about words attached and applied to them. The task of language, i.e.\
the need and endeavour of speaking men, was therefore to make
intuitable the non-spatial relation in which ideas were seen, through
% footnote *) on p. 3
analogy with and subsumption under spatial relations.''\footnote{J. N.
\emph{Madvig}: Sprogvidenskabelige Strøbemærkninger. 1871.
(Universitetsprogram). p.~9. --- \emph{Leibnitz} had already stressed
this in particular. Nouveaux Essais III, 1, 5. (Opera ed.\ Erdmann,
p.~298).}

\textbf{4.} If, then, the first circle of ideas into which man lives his
way draws its elements from external nature, how does it come about that
we learn at all to distinguish between our own ego and the things
outside us?

The beginning of conscious life must be placed before birth. The tender
organism is, it is true, still separated from the great surrounding
world; some of the most important sense-impressions (sight, hearing and
smell) first become possible at birth; and the sensations that can be
had (taste, touch and muscular sensations) no doubt stand out only
faintly and dimly from the general feeling of vegetative well-being and
ill-being. Yet the experiences that can be had before birth are perhaps
already enough to form the basis for the consciousness of a surrounding
world. The feeling of well-being or ill-being, together with the
muscular sensations, or the sensations of energy expended, can already
here come into a certain contrast with the sensations of resistance,
touch and taste. It goes without saying that this first germ of
world-consciousness is dim and dreamlike, and that from our waking,
fully conscious standpoint we easily come to read too much into it. But
these first stirrings must nevertheless be taken into account,
especially since they give a hint of how
% --- p. 4 ---
\opage{4}difficult it is to assume any definite point as the transition
from unconscious to conscious existence. It is the merit of
Cabanis to have shown the importance of this first period of the
life of the soul.

The revolution that takes place at birth thus consists not in an
ensoulment, but in a great change in the conditions of life, both inner
and outer. The general sensation is altered by the fact that nourishment
and breath no longer flow in directly from the mother's organism, with
which the fetus was united in one living unity, but must be taken in
from outside and assimilated by special organs (the alimentary canal and
the lungs). Besides greater energy and independence in the internal
functions, this also brings stronger fluctuations in the general
sensation, since the inflow, which before was continuous, now becomes
periodic and interrupted, so that the contrast between sensations of
pleasure and of displeasure becomes more intense. At the same time the
impressions of the larger surrounding world now storm in upon the
delicate organism, which must be especially susceptible to impressions
of cold. The cry of pain with which the newborn child begins its life
finds its most probable explanation partly in the want of breath brought
on by the separation from the mother's organism, partly in the
sensations of cold.\footnote{\emph{Adolf Kussmaul}: Untersuchungen über
das Seelenleben des neugeborenen Menschen. p.~27 f.}

Although the general sensation still continues for the time being to
play the largest part, such a multiplicity of impressions gradually
streams into the infant consciousness that a contrast can arise between
a subjective pole and an objective pole. The feeling of pleasure and
displeasure, the general sensation, and the sensation of unimpeded
movement do not lead consciousness beyond itself. But now, first of
all, the sensations of sight gain a dominant influence. Light acts early
on the newborn child, although in this as in several other respects
individual differences at once make themselves felt. The child seems to
feel pleasure in the impression of light and tries (already on the
second day after birth) to turn toward it so as to keep hold of it. The
ability to fixate definite objects develops from the third week on; it
is of course near and conspicuous objects that are chiefly held fast.
Before that time
% --- p. 5 ---
\opage{5}sight is a mere staring without definite direction or
expression. Bright, strongly colored and moving objects especially
attract attention. Memory now also plays a prominent part: instead of
continuing to cry as long as it feels hunger, the child is already
quieted by noticing the preparations for satisfying its need (in the
third week), and recognizes (from the third month) its mother as the
source of this satisfaction --- in which the sensations of sound also
play their part, the head being turned toward the quarter from which the
% footnote *) on p. 5
sound comes.\footnote{\emph{Kussmaul}. p.~26. 39. --- \emph{Vierordt}:
Die Physiologie des Kinderalters. p.~154, 159.} --- But neither in the
sensations of light, of sound, nor of touch lies the full explanation of
the consciousness of a surrounding world distinct from the individual
itself. They show themselves, indeed, to be independent of the
individual's feeling of pleasure and displeasure and of its active
movement, but they do not come into outright conflict with these. That
is first done by the sensation of resistance and limit. Such sensations
of arrested and hindered movement are, as remarked, possible already in
the fetal state, but now become more manifold and more vigorous. The
great vegetative energy finds an outlet in movements of the limbs, and
the child is thereby led to experiment with the things around it. The
active experiment is for the adult, too, the surest means of finding his
bearings. The child does not wait for the surrounding world to come to
it; by its involuntary movements it reaches into the world of itself
from the first, and thereby gains the best knowledge of where the
boundary lies between the world and itself. At the points where movement
meets resistance, especially when this resistance causes pain, the
non-ego begins. --- When the ideas of memory become frequent and
connected, we get a third factor of importance, namely the contrast
between the clearer and more vigorous impressions, which arise
immediately, unexpectedly and often without connection, and the fainter
images which are at the disposal of consciousness in all circumstances
--- that is, the contrast between observations and recollections.

The question now is how much is counted as belonging to the ego. If the
sensations of resistance and the feeling of pain are what most strongly
determine the distinction between ego and non-ego, then
% --- p. 6 ---
\opage{6}the boundaries of the ego will for the time being coincide with
the boundaries of one's own organism. Everything that takes part in the
movement and meets resistance we count as ourselves. The limitation can
only be learned by way of experience. The child discovers its own body
successively. First the hands become a familiar member of its own
organism; they are examined especially with the help of lips and tongue,
the child sometimes putting its fingers in its mouth and sucking on them
even on the first day. Later the child learns to fixate them with its
eyes; a firm association of ideas then soon forms between the muscular
sensation that accompanies the movement of the hands and the sight of
this movement. Later the feet are discovered; this can only happen when
the child can sit up and see them, or, lying on its back, can stick its
legs in the air to look at them and grasp at them with its hands. The
great interest with which the child regards its limbs and their
movements may perhaps be derived from the strangeness of there being
something here that can be seen and touched, and that offers resistance
--- and yet takes part in the active movement. It is an object that
nevertheless belongs to the subject. The child here has the same
experience as the dog that runs round after its own tail. When a child,
even toward the end of its second year, offers its own foot a rusk, it
no doubt still regards the foot as an independent being. The mutual
touchings of the limbs, and their resistance to one another's movements,
gradually clarify the idea of one's own body as both akin to and in a
peculiar way different from other objects. This idea becomes clearest
when the child causes itself pain by treating parts of its organism as a
mere object.

One step more is possible here; but it is taken only at a later age, and
not by all, or at all times. One's own body, which till now was
distinguished from the non-ego, nevertheless has essential marks in
common with it: it can be perceived by the senses and can offer
resistance. It thereby comes into contrast with the feeling of pleasure
and displeasure and with the inner stream of recollections and ideas.
What we feel pleasure and displeasure at we can perhaps observe with the
help of the senses, but not the feeling itself. What we remember and
imagine can be an object of external observation, but not the
recollection and the idea themselves.
% --- p. 7 ---
\opage{7}This contrast is so decisive that the idea of the body passes
over to the objective pole, to the non-ego, and what we then retain is
the idea of the ego as the subject of thought, feeling and will. The
contrast between the inner and the outer is now sharpened, or rather, we
keep the expression ``inner'' as a figurative designation for the
psychical domain in contrast to the bodily as the ``outer.'' Inner
experience, then, concerns thoughts, ideas and feelings as psychical
states; outer experience concerns what can be intuited and can offer
resistance to movement in space. Outer experience must necessarily be a
part of inner experience, be enclosed by it. When at the primitive stage
the ego is made one with the body, this really means: with the bodily
sensations, which in reality, too, make up an essential part of the
individual's history. Jeppe knew himself by his sore back and his hollow
tooth, because these marked outstanding points in the connection of
% footnote *) on p. 7
recollections which made up his ego.\footnote{How much influence such
bodily sensations have on self-consciousness is clearly seen in cases
where one seems a stranger to oneself because of changes in the general
sensations. The physiologist \emph{Harless}, on waking from an
anaesthesia with sulphuric ether, saw two persons about him, but missed
with unease a third, who had been present before, without its occurring
to him that this third was himself. Another time, on waking from the
anaesthesia, he could not distinguish his personality from another
person on whom his eye fell; only when a little later he looked in a
mirror did he come fully to himself. (\emph{Lotze's} Medicinische
Psychologie, p.~502). In certain morbid states the individual seems to
itself to be another; everything that moves in it is as if it took place
in another being (Revue philosophique I, p.~289 ff.).}
% TR: Jeppe = Holberg's Jeppe paa Bjerget.

\textbf{5.} Folk psychology teaches that the idea of the psychical has
passed through stages in the history of the race similar to those in the
single individual.

The current mythological theory ascribes to primitive man a tendency to
apprehend and explain all natural phenomena as the intervention of
personal beings. Everything acquires life and personality for him,
because he puts his own personal life into everything. To explain means,
after all, to refer the unknown to the known, and from where should man
at
% --- p. 8 ---
\opage{8}first take the elements for the explanation of the phenomena of
nature, if not from himself? --- So much, too, is clear: the
mythological view of nature differs from the scientific by its
personifying tendency. But might not this tendency itself need an
explanation, and might one not ask whether there are necessary
intermediate links through which the idea of personal powers is
transferred to natural phenomena? The theory in question seems,
besides, to ascribe to primitive man a creative fantasy that is possible
only at a higher stage of development. Finally, if the theory were
correct, one would have expected language to designate bodily things by
originally spiritual expressions, whereas in reality it designates
spiritual things by originally bodily expressions.

Recent inquiries into the mental life of savage peoples have therefore
led to a modification, if not an outright rejection, of the theory of an
original personifying faculty. \emph{Tylor}, \emph{Lubbock} and
\emph{Spencer} have shown that dream-images play an exceedingly large
part in the formation of the primitive view of the world. In dreams man
sees himself and others, and since he originally has no reason to
suppose any difference between dream-images and observations, he
regards the former as just as real as the observations of waking life.
Just as a child sends greetings from those it has dreamt of, primitive
man takes what he lives through in dreams for real experiences. He has
thus been in distant regions, though it turns out that his body has not
stirred from the spot, and others have visited him, though it is certain
that they were far away or dead. Besides dream-images, mirror-images of
the individual himself and of others also lend support to another form
of existence than the one given with the body's presence at a definite
place. A savage who was allowed to look in a mirror cried out: ``I am
looking into the world of spirits!'' One of Darwin's children, at nine
months old, turned toward the mirror when it was called by name. These
experiences lead to the assumption of a double existence: as soul, man
is a free, ethereal being; as body, bound to definite and limited places
in space. This doubleness now forms a fixed point of support. The
striking phenomena --- change, coming into being and passing away, life
and death --- are now naturally explained
% --- p. 9 ---
\opage{9}by assuming a similar doubleness everywhere. The souls of the
departed in particular furnish an important element of explanation; it
is with them that fantasy is most occupied, and it is therefore inclined
to find their intervention everywhere. It is from the world of dreams,
then, that the whole of nature is peopled.

\emph{Spencer}, however, no doubt goes too far when he thinks he can do
entirely without the presupposition of a personifying tendency. It is
one thing that merely appealing to such a tendency is not enough, another
whether it can be dispensed with altogether. Apart from the fact that
without it one can hardly give a not overly artificial explanation of
the worship of plants and
% footnote *) on p. 9
animals,\footnote{See Tylor's criticism of Spencer's theory in
\textit{Mind}, April 1877.} it is clear that man must after all recognize
himself in the dream, and must put a life of feeling and will
corresponding to his own into the images of other men that the dream
shows him. If one denies the personifying tendency entirely, one cannot
explain how man can assume the existence of personal beings outside
himself at all. The dream theory marks an advance in so far as it makes
it easy to understand that man puts his psychical life into his double;
here he immediately recognizes himself under other conditions. But what
happens here differs only in degree from what always happens when he
acknowledges a psychical life in other beings that make movements
resembling those he himself makes in certain moods. His instinct of
self-preservation leads him early to interpret the movements of other
beings, to read certain looks and features as signs of certain feelings.
It is a natural inference by analogy through which even primitive man
arrives at the assumption of psychical life outside himself. How far
the analogy is extended depends on the standpoint; we shall see that it
is not at all easy to fix measure and limit here. At the mythological
standpoint man is very lavish in bestowing a psychical life of the same
kind as his own on the things around him. The boundary between ego and
non-ego is here, too, indefinite, just as for the child. Only advancing
experience can draw sharper boundary lines.

% --- p. 10 ---
\opage{10}At this stage the soul is thought of as an ethereal being in
contrast to the body as a coarser and heavier being. This doubleness has
still many changes to undergo before it becomes the contrast between an
immaterial and a material being. Only slowly and successively are the
physical characteristics in the concept of the soul worn away.
In the case of Greek intellectual development, such a wearing away took
place in the time between Homer and Plato. For \emph{Homer} the soul is
only a fainter counter-image, a reflex of the body; in his childlike way
of apprehending things, man's real self coincides with the body, which
is why (in the 3rd and 4th verses of the first book of the Iliad in the
Greek text) he makes the souls of the heroes go to the underworld, while
they \emph{themselves} become the prey of dogs and birds! --- \emph{Plato}, on
the other hand, makes Socrates say to his friends, on the occasion of
Crito's question how he wishes to be buried: ``I cannot convince Crito
that I am the Socrates who is now talking with you and has ordered the
whole discourse; he thinks I am the one he will soon see as a corpse,
and so he asks how he is to bury me.'' (Phaedo. Heise's transl.
p.~119). --- Here a purely spiritual idea of the soul is expressed, its
essence being placed in the activity of thought. But this pure idea,
which the ancient philosophers did not, moreover, carry through, was
obscured again in the Middle Ages, whose faith had a decidedly
materialistic stamp and, for example, imagined the souls burning in
purgatory. As Plato stood to Homer, so in modern times does
\emph{Descartes}, who placed the essence of the soul in consciousness,
stand to the medieval view. --- Were we to explain this transition from
Homer's to Plato's standpoint by way of psychological analysis, we
should come back to a process like the one we have already described
above within the individual consciousness (pp.~3--7).

\textbf{6.} That we know psychical life directly only in ourselves, and
discover it outside us by way of analogy, we can finally convince
ourselves by taking up a physiological standpoint. As long as the
organic processes and movements were still explained by a special vital
force or by the unconscious activity of the soul, one could of course
extend psychical life to all organic phenomena. But even then the
% --- p. 11 ---
\opage{11}question became one of the relation between the ``soul'' in the
wider sense, as vital force, and the soul in the narrower sense, as
consciousness. \emph{Descartes} was the first to assert sharply the
purely psychological criterion of psychical life, in contrast to the
older, Aristotelian view, which also called the principle of nutritive
life soul. He preferred to avoid the word ``soul'' (\textit{anima}) because
of its ambiguity, and put the word ``consciousness'' (\textit{mens}) in its
place when he wished to designate the object of psychology. The land of
souls was thereby considerably reduced. Descartes himself found occasion
to assume a consciousness only in man; the animals he regarded as mere
machines. This was a paradox; but it points to a reform in the view of
nature. Instead of appealing to mysteriously acting forces, a purely
mechanical explanation of nature can now be introduced, once the
``soul'' has been separated out from the world of bodies. The phenomena
of organic life, too, now come under the general mechanical principles.
Where physiology has arrived at an explanation of an organic phenomenon,
it is by applying this principle.
% footnote *) on p. 11
Physiology is organic physics.\footnote{\emph{Panum}: Indledning til
Forelæsninger over Menneskets Physiologi. p.~13.} But if the soul can
thus no longer be used to explain the bodily phenomena, if appealing to
the soul as a cause is the same as giving up all explanation, how can
one at this standpoint be led to assume a psychical life at all? Here we
seem to have the complete opposite of the mythological standpoint: while
that found the soul everywhere, at the physical-physiological standpoint
the soul seems to vanish from existence altogether. The physiologist
sees only movements of material parts, and it rests on an inference when
at certain points he interprets these movements as the expression of an
activity of consciousness.

The difficulties with which such an interpretation may be attended are
seen from the following examples. --- A frog whose head has been cut off
does not thereby lose the ability to perform purposive movements. Some
physiologists therefore think that one must ascribe a ``spinal soul'' to
the headless frog; others deny that this is warranted. Precisely the
purposiveness
% --- p. 12 ---
\opage{12}could, after all, point to a fixed mechanical connection; it is
in itself just as characteristic of conscious beings that they make
mistakes as that they behave rationally or purposively. --- In other
cases physiological inquiry reaches a result different from what seems
self-evident to the popular view. The death struggle is as a rule held to
be very painful; but that rests on a false interpretation. ``As a rule
the pains diminish when death does not come suddenly but approaches
slowly, and they cease altogether when the death struggle begins. This
struggle, or this period of transition, may last a longer or shorter
time, often even many long hours; and in most cases consciousness is lost
only toward its end. Even after the circulation of the blood has
stopped, the activity of the brain has ceased and life is therefore
over, so that it may safely be assumed that the soul has left the body
and the spiritual and bodily connection has ceased --- even after that
one can sometimes see convulsive movements of inspiration, with
twitchings in all the inspiratory muscles about the nose and mouth,
alternating with a faint, drawn-out, rattling sound of expiration (the
so-called death rattle, which comes from mucus in the windpipe). These
twitchings, which often give the face of the dying a suffering or even a
frightful expression, are nevertheless not real manifestations of life,
but a kind of galvanic reflex movements, which soon pass into a complete
slackening of the muscles. Since they seem to be the last efforts of the
body to hold on to life, it is no wonder that, with them in view, the
transition to death has been regarded as a hard struggle and as a bodily
% footnote *) on p. 12
suffering --- which, as was said, it is not.''\footnote{E. Hornemann: Om
Menneskets Tilstand kort før Døden. Kbhvn 1874. p.~18.} --- In contrast
to this, there are cases in which immediate apprehension would
confidently assert that there is no suffering consciousness present,
while a skilled examination nevertheless arrives at a quite different
result. Some South American Indians poison their weapons with a poison
called curare, which rapidly brings about a progressive paralysis of all
the vital functions. Death comes without struggle or rattle, so that the
onlooker must believe that it is the mildest manner of death
% --- p. 13 ---
\opage{13}possible. But \emph{Claude Bernard} has proved that curare
attacks only the motor nerves, not the sensory nerves, so that
consciousness and will are not abolished, and he thinks that this death,
which seems so mild, must be precisely the most dreadful one can
conceive. ``Can one conceive a more fearful suffering than when the
conscious spirit is thus witness to the successive sinking of all its
organs, and in a manner finds itself shut up alive in a corpse?'' ---
``The faculty of sensation,'' Bernard remarks, ``can no more than any
other of the faculties attached to the nervous system manifest itself by
itself. These faculties necessarily need the contractile or muscular
system to announce their presence or manifest themselves to the outer
world. Consequently we can judge of the sensations of men and animals
only by their movements. Yet we should have been in the most complete
error if, in the animals poisoned by curare, we had inferred from the
absence of movement the absence of the faculty of sensation
(sensibility). This example proves that \emph{only in our consciousness
do we have an absolute criterion}, and that, when we set about
interpreting external phenomena, we are everywhere surrounded by causes
% footnote *) on p. 13
of error and illusion.''\footnote{\emph{Claude Bernard}: La science
experimentale, p.~299 f.}

If, then, we wish to come to know conscious life, we must first and
foremost study it where it is \emph{immediately} accessible to us,
namely in our own consciousness. It is from this immediate experience
alone that the physiologist, too, draws when he seeks to determine the
significance of the various organs of the brain for psychical life. It
is the fixed starting point for all our knowledge of the spiritual
world.

\textbf{7.} But how much, now, lies in this starting point? The object
of psychology is not something that can be presented to fantasy or to
sense-observation. It is not something we can find directly outside
ourselves, but something which, where we find occasion for it, we
transfer consciously or unconsciously from ourselves to others. We have
therefore no right to take as our basis more than what the inner
observation of our own consciousness teaches us. But here, too, is
something whose reality no one can
% --- p. 14 ---
\opage{14}dispute. No one can deny that there are sense-sensations and
ideas, feelings and resolutions; and when we say that psychology is the
science of the soul, we provisionally understand by soul no more than
the sum of all these inner experiences. In this sense the existence of
the soul is no problem, but on the contrary a necessary presupposition.
There is, however, another sense in which a question may arise as to the
position of science on the existence of the soul. Not content with the
simple starting point in psychological experience, it has been thought
necessary for psychology to take as its basis the idea of the soul as an
absolutely independent individual being, subsisting by itself. It is an
idea that historically points back to the mythological doubleness, as
this was purified and given form partly by ethical and partly by
theoretical motives. On the one hand, what was at work here was the
feeling of the high worth of psychical life, the feeling that everything
that interests us dwells in this inner world of thoughts and feelings,
and that the outer world of matter itself has value for us only as an
object of thought and feeling. This inner world was therefore raised
high above the material world and sharply separated from it. On the
other hand, the spiritualistic view also has its basis in an analysis of
the characteristic marks of psychical states. It is peculiar to
consciousness that it gathers what is scattered in time and space. In
memory the differences of time are abolished, in comparing and combining
thought the distances of space. Here is a thoroughgoing unity, an inner
connection, to which we have no counterpart. Is that not warrant enough
for ascribing to the soul subsistence in and for itself, and conceiving
it as an immaterial substance?

However great the warrant may be, we cannot in any case acknowledge it
right at the beginning of psychology. Here above all it is a matter of
building on nothing but immediate observation. And that assumption does
not even give us any help toward a better understanding of psychological
experiences. One infers a soul-substance from the character of certain
experiences, but knows only as much about it as lies in them --- and in
so far as one wants to know more, the warrant of the inference in any
case ceases. Experience certainly teaches us that the peculiarity of
consciousness, in
% --- p. 15 ---
\opage{15}contrast to material phenomena, is the inner unity in all the
multiplicity of the content of consciousness, a unity such as is not
found in the world of space; but it does not teach us that this unity is
absolute, unconditioned and independent. In so far as the concept of
substance is taken in the strict sense, namely of that which subsists in
and for itself, independent of and not resting on anything else, we have
no right from experience to apply it to the soul. The most gifted
defender of spiritualistic psychology at the present day, \emph{Hermann
Lotze}, has also clearly seen this. When he ascribes substantiality to
the soul, he means merely to designate it as an independent element in
the world, a center of acting and suffering, and not to assert anything
about its absolute essence. He therefore also declares that from the
essence of the soul as substance in this sense no further conclusions
can be drawn --- as the earlier spiritualistic psychology thought --- in
respect of the philosophy of religion; no way is thereby opened to us to
knowledge of the fates of the soul, its prospects for the future, or its
origin. He even agrees with \emph{Spinoza} that, if we wish to think the
concept of substance in the strictest sense, there can be only one
single substance, in whose essence everything else must be moments of
content, whose reality rests on their significance within the nature of
the primal substance. So even for metaphysical speculation the idea of
the soul as substance is useless and unfruitful.

But even with the restriction Lotze has given the concept of substance
in its application to the soul, misgivings remain for empirical
psychology. What Lotze has chiefly in mind when he determines the soul
as substance in the sense of an independent starting point and center is
its relation to bodiliness. On his view, then, it is supposed to be
clear from the very chief characteristics of the psychical nature that
this is in itself something different from bodiliness, and that a
relation of interaction must be assumed between them, however one may
more precisely conceive this. Here again more has been put in than
experience for the present warrants. From immediate experience of our
inner states we can infer nothing whatever about their relation to other
sides of existence. Psychological experience acquaints us only with the
inner psychical phenomena themselves,
% --- p. 16 ---
\opage{16}not with the way in which they are connected with other
phenomena. That is a question by itself, which psychology cannot settle
one-sidedly and directly. Other circles of experience besides the
psychological must be drawn in to settle this problem, and it is of
great importance that, for each single circle of experience, no
unwarranted ideas be put in that might anticipate the decision in one
direction or the other. We cannot decide from the outset whether two
different principles or substances underlie the psychical and the
bodily. We see two domains of experience before us, each with its
peculiarities, and study these, in order then, always under the guidance
of experience, to attempt to determine their mutual relation.

This is also in the well-understood interest of metaphysical speculation
itself. The human spirit will never let itself be kept from brooding
over the ultimate principles of the existence of which it is a member.
It will always seek to form certain highest, concluding ideas for its
view of the world. But what it must learn, and what it already could and
should have learned, is that speculation must not meddle in the daily
housekeeping of empirical knowledge, must not anticipate the settlement
of purely empirical problems. The point is not that speculation should
wait until experience is exhausted; for that will never happen. But the
truly superior metaphysician is he who lets his ideas move in the
direction in which the leading features of empirical knowledge already
point. He thus only utters the thoughts that more or less unconsciously
underlie empirical inquiry itself. He thereby does not come into
conflict with it, although he goes beyond it. He seeks an ultimate,
concluding hypothesis, but the foundation is common to him and the
empiricist.

Psychology as we conceive it is a ``psychology without a soul'' in so
far as it asserts nothing about the absolute essence of psychical life,
or about whether there is any such absolute essence at all. Just as
little as physics pronounces on the transcendent questions (those going
beyond empirical knowledge) in the domain of outer nature, just so little
does psychology do so in the domain of inner nature. This is not to say
that psychology cannot make an essential contribution to the
% --- p. 17 ---
\opage{17}general view of the world. On the contrary, nothing can be of
greater significance for this than knowledge of the psychical phenomena,
their mutual relations and the laws of their development. And precisely
a view of these phenomena carried through empirically will be able to
clarify the points of view and correct many prejudices.

When we assert the empirical character of psychology against
metaphysical speculation, it is materialism just as much as spiritualism
that we thereby exclude from psychology. In what precedes we have spoken
especially of spiritualistic psychology, because it is of the greatest
interest and has the most acute defenders. But it will easily be seen
that materialism commits the same encroachment as spiritualism. It, too,
infers a substance supposed to lie behind the phenomena of
consciousness, but it finds this substance in matter, not in a spiritual
principle. Spiritualism holds to the difference of psychical life from
material phenomena, and infers from it a special soul-substance which in
itself has nothing to do with matter. Materialism, on the other hand,
infers from the connection of psychical life with the material that the
``soul'' must be a material being. ``Let it suffice us,'' says
\emph{Holbach} (Système de la nature I. p.~118), ``to know that the soul
is moved and modified by the material causes that act upon it. From this
we have the right to conclude that it must be material.''
\emph{Broussais} defined the soul as ``un cerveau agissant et rien de
plus'' (an acting brain, nothing more). Both in what it asserts and in
what it denies, materialism, like spiritualism, goes beyond the
standpoint of empirical psychology. It is another matter that psychology
through its own inquiries can bring us to a point where we can pass
judgment on these hypotheses.

Psychology is not in itself a part of philosophy, if by philosophy one
means a striving for a general view of the world; like the empirical
science of outer nature, it is a preparation for philosophy, a part of
the foundation on which philosophy is to build, one of the witnesses
philosophy must examine. Nor, if by philosophy one means a critical
theory of the nature and limits of knowledge, is psychology philosophy:
while psychology has a purely natural-historical character, considering
the psychical phenomena in their development
% --- p. 18 ---
\opage{18}and mutual relations, the theory of knowledge seeks by critical
analysis to set up the general principles of knowledge. The theory of
knowledge, too, therefore presupposes psychology.

On the other hand, philosophical thought itself becomes one of the
objects of psychology. Philosophy, as a form of mental activity, falls
within the domain of psychological observation. And philosophical
inquiry has in many ways played into the hands of psychological inquiry;
consciously or unconsciously to itself, philosophical speculation always
operates with psychological elements, and many profound psychological
observations and ideas have been laid down in the philosophical systems.

It is the merit of \emph{the English school} to have asserted the
independence of psychology from metaphysical speculation.
\emph{Descartes} had, it is true, taken the decisive step toward freeing
the concept of the soul from the mythological ambiguities, by stressing
consciousness as the characteristic mark of psychical phenomena. Yet he
did not hold fast to the empirical standpoint, but laid the very
foundation of metaphysical-spiritualistic psychology by setting up the
soul as ``thinking substance'' (\textit{substantia cogitans}) instead of
keeping to the phenomena of consciousness as the sure empirical
foundation. Kant's epoch-making philosophical reform became important for
psychology through his criticism of metaphysical (``rational'')
psychology, a criticism whose significance has been shaken only
temporarily by romantic attempts at restoration.

\textbf{8.} We have attempted to set up psychological inquiry as an
endeavour distinct from the view of outer nature and from metaphysical
speculation. Immediate self-observation and self-consciousness is, for
the physiologist and the metaphysician alike, the source from which they
draw, but which they often overlook, because their essential interest is
not this immediate observation itself, but what they think they can
infer from it. Granted, then, that it is necessary to draw from this
source, the question becomes whether independence as regards the
\emph{principle} also brings with it independence as regards the
\emph{method}. This leads us to a closer examination of the nature and
% footnote *) on p. 18 (continues on p. 19)
limits of subjective observation.\footnote{On the method of psychology
one may compare \emph{Stuart Mill}: System of Logic. Book VI. Chap.~4.
--- \emph{Bain}, Logic. II, p.~275--292. --- Ad.\ \emph{Horwicz}:
Methodologie der Seelenlehre (Zeitschrift für Philosophie und philos.
Kritik. 60ter Bd. 1872.) --- Delboeuf: La psychologie comme science
naturelle. Paris 1876. --- Wilh.\ Wundt: Über psychologische Methoden.
(Philosophische Studien, herausgeg.\ von Wundt. I, 1. Leipzig 1881.)}
% TR: "Bain" is letterspaced in the print (checked at the image); the
% transcription does not mark it.

% --- p. 19 ---
\opage{19}\textbf{a.} The first difficulty that presents itself here
springs from the fact that psychical states are not resting and fixed
objects like those that are the object of physical observation. As space
is the peculiar form of material phenomena, so time is the form of
psychical phenomena. What goes on within us, in our thought and feeling,
is unstable and changing. When the botanist has laid a plant before him,
or the chemist has got a substance into his retort, they can calmly
observe how the objects look under certain quite definite conditions.
But a state of consciousness cannot be isolated in this way; it has no
boundaries in space; every moment brings, or can bring, new elements in.
In the moment I wish to observe a state of consciousness, therefore, it
is already gone, or has undergone fusion with other elements. Now it is
true that several different series of ideas can move through our
consciousness in one and the same moment; our ego is not a unity in the
sense that different, even conflicting currents are excluded. One might
then imagine that, while the main current consisted, for example, in the
contemplation of a work of art and admiration of it, an undercurrent ran
alongside it which in turn stood in a contemplative relation to the
main current. In that way one would be able at once to admire and to
study the psychology of admiration. Everyone no doubt knows from his own
experience states of this kind, in which an inner spectator keeps
talking the whole time, although one seems to be quite taken up with
something altogether other than oneself. Such states can hardly be
avoided entirely in men whose reflection has once been awakened; and a
double current of this kind can be of importance especially in ethical
respects, where it is a matter of judging, and gradually driving out, a
reprehensible current in the life of the soul. Criticism then has its
say as an undercurrent, as an opposing motive that seeks to overcome
what for the time being prevails. But although such double currents can
also be made fruitful
% --- p. 20 ---
\opage{20}for psychological observation, psychology must nevertheless, in
its own interest, warn against them. The capital of energy at the
disposal of psychical life is necessarily divided in them, and each
single current becomes weaker. If a mood is to be lived through fully
and wholly, the undercurrent must be driven out, and one must not listen
to one's inner stirrings, so as not to withdraw from them any of the
energy at hand. Besides, the observing undercurrent is not indifferent,
but always alters the main current more or less. Conscious attention
necessarily affects the immediate state toward which it is directed, and
can thereby in part destroy its own object. It puts art in the place of
nature.

But what cannot happen in the very moment of experience can happen
later. During the experience one should only haul in the net with
everything that is in it, or, like the botanist, gather the plants that
come one's way. What has been fully and clearly lived through will remain
in memory and can be examined through it. It is the rhythmic alternation
between self-forgetfulness and self-consciousness that makes
psychological self-examination possible, and psychological talent rests
on the ease and elasticity with which one can pass from the one of these
states to the other, so that one keeps what was immediately lived through
clear and pure in the moments of memory and reflection, but, conversely,
does not let reflection disturb the immediate stirrings. And yet the two
states will not remain wholly untouched by each other. Unconsciously ---
and therefore without harm --- practice in memory and reflection will
work to make a stronger light, or a more vigorous accent, fall in the
immediate experience itself on the elements that are of special
psychological interest. It is the general law of practice that is at
work here. In the same way the botanist, without thinking of it, passes
by many plants that are of no interest for his plan of study.
Involuntarily the observations pile up, and one fine day one finds
oneself in possession of a rich psychological material.

\emph{Ludvig Feilberg's} book \textit{Om størst Udbytte af Sjæleevner}
[On the Greatest Yield from the Powers of the Soul] provides a fine and
illuminating example of the nature of psychological observation. The book
warns against ``Beflitning'' [over-diligence], i.e.\ against the
inclination not to let the events of the life of the soul develop freely
and vegetatively, but to intervene, hastening
% --- p. 21 ---
\opage{21}and regulating. ``Such treatment is against the nature of
thought, just as it is against the nature of the kitten to be mauled by
the children. It must stand unheeded like the wild plant; then it
develops normally and healthily. A sudden notion breaking in is a teacher
to whom one must listen attentively and calmly, without noise of any
kind\ldots. Certain elves slip away when one looks for them.''
(p.~70~f.). But on the other hand the book contains precisely a
multitude of psychical observations, of moods and states which --- as
the fruit of long spiritual botanizing --- have fixed themselves in the
author's memory and, without harm to their freshness and purity, are
made the basis of reflections and inferences.

\textbf{b.} Even if one now succeeds in overcoming the difficulty just
mentioned, a new one arises, namely that because of the individual
differences between observers one has no guarantee that they really see
the same thing. For here they do not have the object outside and between
them; each has it within himself!

This holds more or less, however, of physical observation too.
Observation is a subjective activity; what each observes exists for him
just as he observes it, and only by comparison can he infer that others
observe the same (cf., e.g., the perception of color). He who wants to
show others something must lead them to see it themselves, must awaken
their own activity of observation. What individual differences always
make themselves felt here is seen, for example, from the fact that
between two astronomical observers' determinations of the time of a
star's movement a difference always appears, varying with the observing
individuals and resting on the different speed with which an impression
is apprehended and noted. In so far as more perfect recording apparatus
does not remedy this, observations are begun by determining ``the
personal equation'' relative to other observers. It has then turned out
that this mutual individual difference is not constant, but subject to
fluctuations from day to day as well as in the course of
% footnote *) on p. 21
years.\footnote{\emph{Wilh.\ Wundt}: Grundzüge der physiologischen
Psychologie. 2te Auflage. Leipzig 1880. p.~269~f.} Such mutual
% --- p. 22 ---
\opage{22}control is --- though of course in a far more imperfect form ---
the only means of raising psychological observations above the merely
individual, or rather of distinguishing between what is individual and
what is of more general validity. The distinction between the typical
and the non-typical must begin already within the individual: when the
individual wants to know his own inner nature, there are many
observations he must disregard, because they stand isolated and owe
their origin only to single passing situations. Everyone makes such a
sifting involuntarily, and just as involuntarily daily intercourse with
other individuals leads to distinguishing between the common, universal
and the merely individual in personal observations. Psychological
inquiry in both respects only continues what was begun without conscious
intent. But in its critical testing it must here seek supports that lie
outside the purely subjective method, whose limitation here already
comes plainly to light. Already in the thorough control of individual
experiences the merely subjective starting point has been left. But
before we go more closely into the objective psychological method, we
must dwell on the way in which subjective observations are worked up.

\textbf{c.} The scattered observations form a chaos which is to be
ordered into a world. The first working-up is a classification, by which
definite groups or kinds of psychical phenomena are formed. According to
their most prominent characteristic marks, the single states are
referred to different classes. But such \emph{classification} is not as
easy to carry out as was for a time believed. Just as classification in
the zoological and botanical domain has led to the idea of eternal and
unchangeable species, so that it now costs a hard struggle to show that
these species are the fruits of a natural history of development, so
psychological inquiry, too, for a long time thought it had reached its
goal when it referred the various inner phenomena to various
``faculties'' of the soul --- a procedure that came into a strange
conflict with the strictly spiritualistic view of the unity of the soul.
At the same time these ``faculties'' were regarded as the causes of the
phenomena in question, and the need for causal
% --- p. 23 ---
\opage{23}explanation was thereby satisfied in a way that was certainly
very easy, but purely illusory. What was chiefly overlooked was that in
the classification only one predominant characteristic mark was taken
into account --- that it was thus not the real concrete \emph{states}
themselves that were classified, but the \emph{elements} of which on
closer examination they turn out to consist. There is --- as will later
be shown in detail --- hardly any single state of consciousness that is
\emph{merely} idea, \emph{merely} feeling or \emph{merely} will. The
psychological rubrics can therefore be very useful for a provisional
orientation; but for them to have scientific significance, they must be
built on a thoroughgoing \emph{analysis}, which seeks out the single
elements and the laws of their combination and interaction. It is not
enough to appeal directly to what one feels in oneself, or believes one
feels; the psychological facts are not so simple. Just as little as
immediate consciousness can teach us anything about whether it is the
sun or the earth that moves, just so little does it contain in itself
any psychological theory whatever. Our thoughts and feelings are highly
composite products of the mind, fruits of a long, quiet and mostly
unnoticed growth. The mere observation and description of them therefore
has value only as a foundation.

Psychical states follow one another and call one another forth. Can
laws and rules now be set up for this interrelation, and can it be shown
which elements in the various states of consciousness lead from the one
to the other? These are the questions with which psychological analysis
is occupied. It therefore goes in two directions that stand in inner
connection with each other: it seeks out the common features, what runs
through the special cases, and by this route sets up general empirical
laws (e.g.\ for the association of ideas, for the relation between idea
and feeling, etc.) --- and it seeks within each single state to discover
the single elements of which it is composed. A thought, a feeling, a
resolution is not an absolute unity; on closer examination it shows
itself to be the fruit of a long course of development, in the course of
which it has taken in nourishment from many sides. The feeling of love,
conscience and --- to take a purely intellectual example --- the idea of
an external object stand as something whole and complete,
% --- p. 24 ---
\opage{24}and yet turn out to have their history and to rest on an
interrelation between simpler psychical elements, which testing and
appraising reflection brings to light. Analysis here goes from the
composite to the simple, whereas there it went from the single cases to
the general rule. One might call the one form \emph{generalizing}, the
other \emph{elementary} analysis. But the laws of sequence and the
nature of composition will turn out to belong closely together; for the
most general laws must be those that hold for the most elementary
activities, for the psychical functions that recur in all psychical
phenomena.

\textbf{d.} It can never be known with complete certainty whether the
explanation is exhaustive, and whether one really stands before elements
that cannot be further reduced. This in itself is something that holds
not only for psychology but for all our knowledge; the last thing we
reach at any point is only something last for us. But we cannot even
know whether advancing inquiry will not lead further, so that the limits
of human knowledge have not yet been reached. Where the single inquirer
and the single age stop, later times with fuller experience and clearer
principles can reach further. A remarkable example of a decisive change
in the fundamental psychological view is the predominant importance that
since Rousseau's time has more and more been ascribed to feeling in
relation to the other sides of psychical life, whereas for a long time
the elements of feeling were referred partly to the psychology of ideas,
partly to that of the will. Apart from clarity in observation and
analysis, the possibility cannot be excluded that human psychical life
undergoes slow changes.

In the physical domain, however, it can in many single cases be proved
whether in one's explanation of a phenomenon one has really taken
account of all the co-operating elements. For there a quantitative
determination of the elements is possible, and it thus becomes a simple
arithmetical check whether one has got all the elements in. When the
chemist has resolved a compound substance into its constituents, he
knows that their weights together must equal the weight of the compound.
In the transition from one physical action of force to another there is
always one definite measure of the latter corresponding to one definite
measure of
% --- p. 25 ---
\opage{25}the former. But such a quantitative estimate seems to be
excluded in the psychological domain. An exact check on the sum is
therefore impossible, and further correction always possible. The only
way in which a confirmation of the result can be obtained is to test the
empirical laws found in as many typical and varied cases as possible.

This impossibility of an exact rational foundation for psychology is
closely connected with the nature of the phenomena of consciousness. They
do not arrange themselves, as external phenomena do, in the schema of
space. One feeling does not lie to the right or left of another, one
thought not above or below another. Different currents can, as already
touched on, move simultaneously in our consciousness, but here we have no
form of intuition that, like space in the domain of physical experience,
can furnish the basis for a mathematical construction. We therefore get
nothing here, either, corresponding to the mechanical laws of motion,
which contain the most general principles of all exact explanation of
nature as far as physical experience is concerned.

There is one property, however, by which psychical phenomena turn a side
toward mathematics, namely their differences of strength. Psychical
states can be present in infinitely various degrees, from the faintest
glimmer to the most violent tension. Here it might seem that measurement
was not excluded, and it is indeed at this point that attempts have been
made to introduce mathematics into psychology. The difficulty in these
attempts always lies in finding a fixed starting point. \emph{Herbart}
wished to give the mechanics of the intensive states of the soul. He
starts from the assumption that ideas (which for him are one with the
simplest psychical elements) inhibit one another in a ratio determined
by the degree of their opposition. But what, now, is the \emph{absolute}
intensity of an idea? Since this question cannot be answered, Herbart
sets up as a hypothetical presupposition that the sum of the degrees in
which the ideas present inhibit one another is at every moment as small
as possible --- which is the case when it is not all the ideas that
inhibit one another mutually, but when all direct their inhibiting
influence against a single one, namely the one least opposed to the
others. This presupposition is, as
% --- p. 26 ---
\opage{26}\emph{Wundt} has shown (Physiol.\ Psychol.\ II. p.~315),
improbable, since there is no reason why the tension between two ideas
should cease because a third is added. \emph{Fechner} has chosen a surer
starting point. Although psychical magnitudes --- so his reasoning runs
--- cannot be measured directly, they can still be measured by means of
physical magnitudes with which they stand in lawful connection. It would
be simplest to take as basis the physiological process in the nervous
system (the psychophysical process), since it stands in the most
immediate relation to consciousness; but we do not know its nature, nor
can we measure its strength directly. We must therefore take the
strength of the external stimulus as basis. The strength of light and
sound, weight, etc., can easily be measured, and it then remains only to
discover the ratio in which the psychical sensations grow or diminish
when the strength of the external stimuli is changed. But in order now
to get a real measure for psychical states, we must have a unit, a
smallest unit, to take as basis. This psychical unit Fechner finds in the
least noticeable degree of strength of a sensation, that point, then, in
the process of development of a sensation where it just rises above the
threshold of consciousness. The presupposition Fechner starts from, then,
is that these \textit{minima perceptibilia} are equal.

The question now is whether this presupposition is warranted. In the
first place it holds only within one and the same modality or circle of
quality of sensations. The least noticeable sensation of sight need not
have the same strength as the least noticeable sensation of sound. This
Fechner himself indicates (Elemente der Psychophysik, I. p.~63). In any
case, then, we miss a universal psychical measure; each class of
psychical elements must have its own special scale. In the second place,
equal noticeability is not the same as equal strength. What can just be
noticed may at different times and in different states have different
psychical strength. Every change in sensation alters the state of the
organism in greater or less degree, so that every new change in the
strength of the impression meets new conditions, and the just noticeable
sensations must therefore at the various stages --- of fatigue or of
adaptation --- be of different strength.
% --- p. 27 ---
\opage{27}Finally, it is a question whether we do not immediately feel
the difference between a weight of 29 lod resting on our skin being
increased to 30 lod, and a weight of 29 pounds being increased to 30
pounds, although each of the two increases (according to
\emph{Weber's} experiments) is just noticeable. We probably have such an
immediate sensation of difference when we make the two experiments so
close after each other that the memory of the first has not weakened when
we pass on to the second. If Fechner's presupposition seems to hold in
some cases, it is only an illusion that is soon corrected, just as the
poor boy soon discovers that it is a greater advance in value when 10
kroner rise to 100 kroner than when 1 øre rises to 10
% footnote *) on p. 27
øre.\footnote{These or similar objections have been made chiefly by
\emph{Hering} (Ueber Fechner's psychophysisches Gesetz. ---
Sitzungsberichte der Akademie der Wissenschaften in Wien. 1875),
\emph{Delboeuf} (Revue philosophique 1877 and in separate writings) and
\emph{Funke} (Der Tastsinn und die Gemeingefühle. --- Hermann's Handbuch
der Physiologie. 3ter Bd. 2ter Th. Leipzig 1880).} Against such
objections it is hardly of use to point out that it is not the sensation
itself but our \emph{estimate} of the sensation that is accessible to
measurement, while direct examination can say nothing at all about the
sensation independently of the comparison we make
% footnote **) on p. 27
when we estimate it.\footnote{\emph{Wundt}: Physiol.\ Psychol.\ I.
p.~322, 332. --- \emph{Sigm.\ Exner}: Physiologie der Groshirnrinde,
p.~215~ff. (Hermann's Handbuch der Physiologie. II, 2).} For in the first
place the difficulty pointed out returns in the case of the estimate:
how do we know that what in the single cases we \emph{estimate} as just
noticeable always has the same strength? And in the second place the
sensation --- as will later be shown in a more special connection --- is
from its first emergence so interwoven in the connection of
consciousness, which is always changing and is thus different for each
new sensation, that the distinction between the sensation and its
estimate is in fact impossible; the sensation does not exist at all
except as a link in a chain, and always has other links as its relief.
What already in the physical domain sets limits to the accuracy of
measurement,
% --- p. 28 ---
\opage{28}namely the close connection between the various phenomena,
which in practice makes it impossible to isolate a single one entirely,
holds in far higher degree in the psychical domain, where the phenomena
are even more intimately interwoven. If a measurement is to be reached
here, it can surely happen only by adopting an even more abstract point
% footnote *) on p. 28
of view than Fechner adopted.\footnote{\emph{Delboeuf} has attempted a
recasting of Fechner's theory in this direction. Fechner's error, he
says, is that he takes no account of the change the organ undergoes as a
consequence of the stimulation itself. But it might be that his theory
had a quite abstract validity, namely if one starts from organs of
perfect elasticity, able at every moment to function in a quite adequate
way --- that is, by making the conditions far simpler than they are in
reality. (Revue philos.\ Mars 1877).} What is significant in Fechner's
investigations is that he rightly saw that one has a prospect of reaching
a measurement of psychical phenomena only by using physiological
experiment. The investigations of physiological time show how much of
significance for psychology can be reached by this route, even if it
should not prove possible to overcome the difficulty of determining a
psychical unit.

\textbf{e.} The strictly psychological standpoint keeps to the phenomena
of conscious life. We have stressed this so strongly in order to avoid
ambiguities and confusions. Directly, we know only as much of psychical
life as we know of the phenomena of consciousness. But we have already
seen that consciousness is not a closed world: new phenomena constantly
emerge which at that strictly psychological standpoint we cannot derive
from anything earlier. Each new sense-sensation seems to arise out of
nothing. We can perhaps follow its transformations and effects within
consciousness, but can say nothing about how it first gets there.

Besides, we seek means of remedying the imperfection of the strictly
psychological standpoint for other reasons as well. Comparison of our own
observations with those of others is, as already stressed, necessary in
order to avoid individual one-sidedness, and further also in order
% --- p. 29 ---
\opage{29}to make sure whether all the elements that co-operate in the
given psychological phenomenon have really been brought to light.
Finally, it is not enough for concrete psychological knowledge to know
the general laws of the connection of the phenomena of consciousness; in
practice there appears such a multiplicity, such a welter of
possibilities, that it becomes impossible to infer from the general
psychological law which direction conscious life will take. For each
single individual and in each single situation the result rests on the
original temperament, on the circumstances of life and on special
experiences. Whether an idea (b) will, by virtue of ``the law of
similarity,'' call forth kindred ideas (b$_{2}$ b$_{3}$ b$_{4}$), or
whether, by virtue of another law of the association of ideas, it will
call forth ideas it has previously been together with (a c d\ldots) ---
that can only be decided and understood by means of an all-round
examination of the single case.

The strictly psychological standpoint must therefore be supplemented by
physiological and historical (sociological) inquiry, or, as we may also
say (using a term introduced by H.~\emph{Spencer}), subjective psychology
must be supplemented by objective psychology. In accordance with what has
been developed earlier, it must be held fast that objective psychology
always rests in the last instance on an inference by analogy; only
subjective psychology stands face to face with the phenomena themselves.
What we as objective psychologists think we discover of psychical life
outside our own consciousness we reproduce in ourselves by virtue of a
sympathy closely bound up with the analogy. But these analogies can
furnish indispensable correctives to our immediate observation.

The contrast between subjective and objective psychology does not
coincide with the contrast between \emph{abstract} and \emph{concrete}
psychology. The immediate subjective observations of inner states are
concrete; it is only analysis that leads to abstract laws and rules. And
on the other hand objective study (e.g.\ by physiological experiment)
can lead to abstract psychological results, the conditions being made as
simple as they never are in reality. In physical nature, too, the
phenomena are so intricate that there is a
% --- p. 30 ---
\opage{30}great distance between the general law and each single concrete
case.

Objective psychology consists of \emph{physiological} and
\emph{sociological} psychology. The former rests on the intimate
connection of psychical life with organic life as a whole. Every piece of
information \emph{physiology} can give about the functions of organic
life can from one side or another benefit psychological knowledge. What
must especially be stressed here is the way in which conscious psychical
life fades off into unconscious organic life. Physiology examines
precisely the unconscious functions that precede psychical activities and
form their constant basis. At the border points between the conscious
and the unconscious, where the light of subjective observation falls only
faintly, physiology by its physical method is able to establish definite
relations. But throughout there is an intimate interrelation between the
conscious and the unconscious: it is not only the infant that awakes to
consciousness out of the night of unconsciousness; sleep is every day a
relative renewal of this night; in instinct, impulse and habit we have
forms in which the unconscious takes the conscious into its service, and
in which conscious life in turn, by producing new habits and impulses,
acts on the unconscious. The physiological study of these elementary
psychical phenomena throws light on the more highly developed psychical
life as well. The debate here comes to turn on the degree to which, and
the modifications with which, what nerve physiology and the physiology
of the senses reveal about elementary psychical life can be transferred
to the higher --- whereby it must never be forgotten that the
physiological experimenter and observer himself, in his interpretation of
elementary psychical manifestations, infers back from the fully developed
consciousness to the elementary one. What interests the physiologist
proper in the investigations of nerve physiology and sense physiology is
not the states of consciousness as such, but the physical processes to
which they are attached. For the physiologist psychological experiences
are only criteria from which he infers physiological facts. He starts
from the presupposition that to every psychological experience there
corresponds a physiological process, which it is his task
% --- p. 31 ---
\opage{31}to demonstrate and explain according to the general principles
of natural science. For the present it is the more elementary phenomena
of consciousness that prove most accessible to this explanation; but a
principle is thereby given which physiological psychology with full right
takes as its basis in the investigation of the higher psychical phenomena
as well. Just as a physiology of the senses has developed hand in hand
with general nerve physiology, so a physiology of thought and feeling
will develop.

When we consider that psychical life, as we know it, develops only under
definite physical and chemical conditions and through a series of stages,
of which the lower and the higher mutually illuminate one another, it is
clear that --- despite the independence we claim for psychology at the
outset --- we must nevertheless regard it as part of general biology.
Biology must set up a concept of life that fits all its stages, from the
organic process of nutrition in its simplest forms to the most ideal
process of feeling or thought. It seems that the biology of our time is
approaching such a comprehensive biological view when it regards life in
all its forms as an adaptation of the inner to the outer. Conscious life
marks the highest point of the development of life, shows us the highest
forms in which beings fight the great struggle with the conditions of the
world and in this struggle unfold their nature. A purely subjective
treatment of psychology would overlook the great truth that everything
that moves in the soul is conditioned by its position in the great
connection of the world.

Besides nerve physiology and sense physiology, the study of mental
diseases is also an important auxiliary source of psychological insight,
both by what it shows about the connection between psychical and bodily
disturbances and by its investigations of the forms and course of
development of morbid psychical life. In the latter respect it forms a
transition from physiological psychology to \emph{sociological
psychology}, which considers psychical life as it reveals itself in
movement and action, in word and image. The material of sociological or
comparative psychology is animal life, child life, the savage tribes of
men, the whole
% --- p. 32 ---
\opage{32}of human history, works of poetry and biographies.
Sociological psychology can therefore be divided into a great number of
branches of study (child psychology, animal psychology, folk psychology,
the psychology of language, the psychology of literature, etc.), all of
which lead into the great historical connection within which the
individual consciousness develops, just as physiological psychology led
over into the physical connection by virtue of which psychical life
takes part in the life of the universe. The way in which thoughts and
feelings take shape in the individual at a given time is conditioned not
only by his inherited original organization but also by the atmosphere
of cultural history in which he develops. The physical and the historical
connection stand in inner relation. The study of the influence of
heredity forms an intermediate link between physiological and
sociological psychology, especially when we widen our view to the great
horizon that the hypothesis of evolution opens for us. Organic nature,
indeed the whole connection of nature and its laws, thereby acquires a
historical character, just as, conversely, the organic forces and laws
are seen in their influence on the historical development of human
nature and of the conditions of human life.

\textbf{f.} One needs only a quick glance at all these various sources to
know that psychology cannot be a sharply bounded science. It can be
pursued in many ways and along many roads; here we have wished only to
impress the relation in principle between the various roads. There is,
then, not one psychology but \emph{many psychologies}. But in accordance
with the position in principle that subjective psychology occupies, it
will --- despite the growing importance of objective psychological
study --- always be a natural and warranted endeavour to take it as the
basis and to gather the contributions of the other sources of knowledge
about it as \emph{center}. This is in fact the road psychology has taken
since \emph{Aristotle} founded empirical psychology. It is only
temporarily that psychology, led by a misunderstood spiritualistic
interest, has attempted to separate its cause from physiology and the
other objective sciences, so that it could look like a new discovery when
the connection was asserted again. Subjective psychology has had to wait
for objective psychology; once it had become properly clear about its
% --- p. 33 ---
\opage{33}principle, it soon reached a certain completeness in its broad
features, before physiological and sociological study had developed far
enough to play seriously into psychology's hands. In this respect a
turning point may be said to have come in our time, which has broken
down so many barriers and united so much that did not seem to belong
together.

He who studies psychology from a philosophical interest will have a
special reason to hold fast everywhere to the relation in principle
between the various psychological sources of knowledge, so that the
contribution psychology has to make to the general view of the world may
not be distorted. Here, as everywhere, the task is to spread out a
content of experience for consideration in such a way that the given and
the hypothetical are sharply distinguished, while on the other hand the
general and pervasive features, the common laws, stand out clearly and
distinctly; for it is on them, and not on the gaps that always remain in
human knowledge, that further philosophical inquiry must rely.

\textbf{9.} Psychology thus stands at a point where natural science and
the sciences of the spirit intersect, where the one passes over into the
other. In its principle it has the center about which the currents from
both sides circle, since all knowledge --- as knowledge on the basis of
human nature and organization --- also becomes, directly or indirectly,
knowledge of man.

Psychology forms the foundation on which the ideal sciences of the
spirit, logic and ethics, build. What is true and good can be determined
only from the human standpoint and cannot be understood without
knowledge of real human nature. Logic and ethics set up models for human
striving in thought and action. But if these models are to hold in
reality, they must also have their root in it, and must therefore be
formed on the basis of a knowledge of the deepest and most pervasive
elements and forces of human nature. \emph{Logic}, as the theory of
method, studies the special methods of inquiry and seeks to trace them
back to a fundamental method that follows immediately from the nature of
human consciousness;
% --- p. 34 ---
\opage{34}as the theory of knowledge, it seeks to set up the general
principles of human knowledge and the limits connected with them; but
this endeavour cannot succeed without psychological insight into the
development of the life of ideas, although it does not coincide with it.
\emph{Ethics} seeks to find the practical principle that makes possible a
harmonious development of the powers of personal life, in the individual
as in the race; but in this endeavour it must always start from the
really given human nature and its possibilities as determined by the
psychological laws. It is among these possibilities that ethics makes its
choice, after having appraised them.

On the other hand it leads to a distortion of psychology when logical and
ethical ideals are confused with psychological reality. Psychology has to
do only with what is, not with what \emph{ought to be}. Of course the
state of consciousness in which it dawns on us that there is something
we ought to do itself falls under psychology; it is examined here like
any other. But psychology makes no appraisal; it asks only about the
factual connection and about the way in which, and the laws by which, it
has developed, and leaves it to ethics to pass its judgment on the
various states. Psychology regards psychical phenomena as natural
phenomena and examines them all with the same calm and impartiality. This
independence of psychology from ethics \emph{Spinoza} already asserted
energetically (see my work \textit{Spinozas Liv og Lære}, p.~120~ff.),
but it is far from sufficiently acknowledged even now. There is still a
constant inclination to regard certain forms of psychical life as raised
above explanation and analysis because of their exalted and valuable
character. But precisely the psychological phenomena most valuable in
ethical and aesthetic respects are not single and uncompounded, since they
stand as the culmination of a long and rich process of development. From
their very value, then, what follows is precisely the opposite of an
exemption from the general psychological conditions. It is altogether a
false presupposition that the determination of value and causal
