explanation necessarily exclude or work against each other. Theoretical
inquiry can
% --- p. 35 ---
\opage{35}certainly expose illusions; it calls in any case for a renewed
testing of the correctness of the appraisal; but in itself it can very
well be united with the determination of value. It is only mythological
superstition or blasé satiety that thinks a phenomenon loses its value
because it is understood. Yet it must be granted that the harmony between
appraisal and causal explanation is still only coming into being; the
life of feeling has not yet adapted itself in this way, and knowledge on
its side has not yet come so far that H.~C.~\emph{Ørsted's} words fully
hold: ``The full truth always brings its comfort with it.''


%% ============================================================
%%  II. SOUL AND BODY (pp. 36--84)
%% ============================================================
% --- p. 36 ---
\chapter[Soul and Body]{Soul and Body\opage{36}}

\noindent\textbf{1.} The foregoing inquiry has established that the
knowledge of the psychical and the knowledge of the bodily spring from
two different sources. The question that now arises concerns the
relation between these two different domains of experience. This
question, as it is here put, does not lead us into metaphysical
inquiries. We take the word soul merely in the sense of consciousness,
the expression for the unity of all our inner experiences (sensations,
thoughts, feelings and resolutions), and ask what indication experience
itself gives us concerning the connection of these experiences with the
material phenomena that present themselves to sense-intuition.
\emph{Our} standpoint is thus for the present purely \emph{empirical} or
\emph{phenomenological}, not metaphysical or ontological. On the view
given in the foregoing, the work of metaphysics begins only when the
domain of experience has been traversed and the tendencies and
possibilities appearing there have been discussed.

What here, as everywhere, marks the difference between the popular and
the scientific view is that the former is a mixture of experience and
metaphysics. The popular view often seizes with instinctive sureness
upon certain salient experiences; but it interprets and paraphrases
them under the unconscious influence of metaphysical ideas, partly
handed down, partly growing wild. The scientific view, on the other
hand, seeks first and foremost to become conscious of the source of its
assumptions, and to distinguish sharply between experience and
interpretation. Difficulties and problems thereby arise for it which the
popular view does not feel.


% --- p. 37 ---
\opage{37}As long as neither the phenomena of consciousness nor the
material phenomena are sharply apprehended, each with their own
peculiar character and inner coherence, the problem of the relation
between soul and body does not really exist. When by soul one
understands, in all indefiniteness, a moving principle, an inner force
in things, one has no reason to see any difficulty; for such principles
and forces may be posited with equal right in every possible domain.
Only when the concept of soul is definitely restricted to the life of
consciousness and its facts, and when on the other hand the material
phenomena close themselves off as a world that has its own principles
and laws --- only then does the difficulty of connecting these two
domains arise. The problem thus does not arise from a dogmatic and
absolute separation of two substances or beings; whether the difference
is a difference of essence or not, we do not as yet know; we know only
that here there is a difference, and we ask what lies in and follows
from this phenomenal difference given in experience. To answer this
question we bring together the most decisive features that characterize
the two domains of experience.

\textbf{2.} The first chief characteristic mark of \emph{material}
phenomena is that they appear in the form of space, that all of them
can directly or indirectly be traced back to a motion in space. By this
they are distinguished from the states of consciousness, which can only
symbolically be represented as something spatial. But in this
characteristic mark there lies in itself as yet nothing that sharply
delimits and closes off the material as a world by itself. For those
spatial motions themselves might, after all, be conceived as brought
about by something that was not spatial. The material world would then
lie open to intervention from without.

Natural-scientific research cuts off such a possibility more and more.
It does not regard it as an explanation of a material phenomenon that
one appeals to a non-material principle. It finds a thoroughgoing
explanation only when the given experiences have, by continued
analysis, been traced back to the laws of mechanical motion. What cannot
yet be reduced in this way remains, so far, obscure and not understood.


% --- p. 38 ---
\opage{38}All research rests on the principle of causality. The
difference lies in, and the disputes turn on, where one seeks the
causes, which causes one recognizes as real. Now natural science carries
out the general principle of causality in such a way that for every
material change it seeks a material cause. Its principle is therefore
that nothing arises of itself, and nothing can absolutely perish. This
principle, which was already pronounced as an axiom by the founders of
modern natural science, has in the last century received a wider,
empirical grounding.

Since Lavoisier's reform of chemistry it has been shown that the sum of
the particles of matter always remains the same, however much the
compounds vary. The elements neither lose nor gain by entering into
chemical compounds: as soon as the compounds are dissolved, one gets
exactly the same number of elements (atoms) back again. When, therefore,
a new substance apparently arises, there takes place only a conversion
into a new form of what was present beforehand, in the same quantity,
under another form. Oxygen and hydrogen combine under certain
circumstances into water, and when the water is decomposed again, one
gets the same number of particles of oxygen and hydrogen back.

In chemical combination and decomposition other forces come into play
besides chemical attraction. In chemical combination heat arises. But
just as much heat as arises in combination, just so much disappears
again in decomposition. Whence, now, does this heat come, and whither
does it go? It arises as the equivalent of the elastic force that held
the parts apart before the combination, and it again gets its
equivalent in the tension with which the parts are held separated from
one another after the decomposition.

For it is not only with respect to the \emph{fundamental parts of
matter}, it is also with respect to the \emph{fundamental forces of
matter}, that the material principle of causality holds. The force with
which a stone falls to the earth depends on the height from which it
falls; but the height depends in turn on the force with which it was
lifted up into the air. When the stone is stopped by the earth, force
seems to be lost; for the stone is not able to move the earth; but here
too the disappearance of the force will only
% --- p. 39 ---
\opage{39}mean its conversion into another form --- into heat. The same
holds where the motion does not stop entirely, but is checked by
frictional resistance. The force that a body loses through frictional
resistance is not lost, but becomes heat. The wheel of the water-mill
produces heat by striking against the water. Conversely, heat can
produce mechanical motion, as when the steam, expanded by the heat,
drives the piston which in turn sets the wheels of the locomotive in
motion. And repeated, ever-confirmed experiments have shown that the
quantity of force or
% footnote *) on p. 39
energy\footnote{Since the expression force is ambiguous, one as a rule
uses the word energy, by which nothing else is meant than the capacity
to perform work or to overcome a resistance.} that disappears under the
one form gets its definite equivalent under the other form, so that the
same quantity of the same form of energy can be restored again.

However much the various forms of energy may each vary, their sum thus
always remains the same. But by energy we must then understand not only
the actual performance of work (living force, actual energy), but also
the possible performance of work (tensional force, potential energy),
the stored-up work that under certain conditions can be released and
applied. When Sisyphus has got his stone rolled up the mountain, he has
really accomplished something; his labor is not wasted; for the stone
represents a greater energy when it lies up on the mountain than when it
lies at its foot. In both cases it is at rest; but the potential energy
is greater in the first case than in the second, which shows itself as
soon as it is set in motion. Sisyphus's misfortune consists merely in
this, that he cannot apply the greater potential energy to anything
useful to himself or others; he must always begin over again. --- It is
thus the sum of actual and potential energy that --- in so far as we
regard the universe as a closed whole --- always continues to be the
same.

But it must be firmly held (though even physicists do not always hold
it firmly) that the law of the \emph{conservation of energy} does not
hold for limited beings or relative wholes. Every single system can, as
experience amply shows, lose energy that it never gets back. When
Sisyphus was condemned for all
% --- p. 40 ---
\opage{40}eternity to roll the stone up the mountain, only to see it roll
down again every time, he can to that extent console himself: there
will come an end to his sufferings; he and the stone and the mountain
will all be worn out in the end. They gradually give up their energy to
other systems. As soon as there is anything outside a being or a
system, this will receive influences from it that raise or lower the sum
of its energy. Neither our earth, nor our solar system, nor our stellar
system have we any right to regard as a finished, closed system. They
have limits, with the limits the possibility of influence from without,
and with that in turn the possibility of loss or increase of energy.
Only for the \emph{whole} universe --- if we were able, as in reality we
are not, to think it as an absolute whole --- would the law of the
conservation of energy hold completely. This law thus has the same
validity as so many other laws and principles (e.g.\ the law of inertia)
which cannot appear purely in our experience, but which are nonetheless
proved by showing their validity the more, the more we are able to
simplify and isolate the phenomena.

\textbf{3.} Yet there is one class of beings which not only the popular
view, but for long periods science too, has been inclined to regard as
exceptions to this general law of the world. The organisms, with their
peculiar development of form and their capacity for self-preservation
over against the surrounding world, seem to be small worlds that can
draw their life out of inner sources in themselves. For a long time the
explanation of the phenomena of organic life by means of the general
forces of nature was regarded as materialism. But since, on the other
hand, it was clear that the conscious soul is not directly the cause of
the organic processes, which so often take a direction wholly opposed to
the conscious will of the individual, a so-called vital force was
inserted between the conscious soul and the body to explain those
phenomena. Here, then, something new was supposed to have come in which
was found in no sense in the rest of nature. This view, so-called
vitalism, could of course not overlook that living beings stand in
manifold and constant interaction with the surrounding world, at every
moment receive and give off influences, and that their process of
development and of dissolution is conditioned throughout by this
relation. The
% --- p. 41 ---
\opage{41}``vital force'' had thus to run through a similar course. But
what dazzled vitalism was the peculiar way in which the organism answers
all influences from without. That a ball rolls when we push it seems to
us quite natural; but that the plant turns toward the light, that food
is converted into flesh and blood, that the fingers bend together at a
gentle touch of the inner palm of the hand --- that seems to us very
wonderful, since the answer here does not seem to stand in proportion to
the address. Here vitalism commits the error of making the organism an
absolute unity, whereas in reality it is an extraordinarily composite
whole. An influence received is propagated from part to part within this
whole, and thereby gradually changes its appearance entirely. Partly it
is converted into other forms of energy, partly into potential energy;
partly, finally, it serves to release potential energy or tensional
force in the organism itself. As research seeks step by step to follow
these conversions, it begins to understand that something quite
different can come out than went in. It understands that the organism,
on account of the store of potential energy that is accumulated in its
tissues and circulates through it in its fluids --- which are as it
were a surrounding world within the organism (a \emph{milieu
intérieur}, to use Claude Bernard's apt expression) --- must stand more
independently over against the surrounding world than the inorganic
forms of existence. The organic answer to the address from the
surrounding world (irritability) must become the richer and more
peculiar, the more bound forces there are to release. The doctrine of
the ``vital force'' is really only a mythological expression for the
wonder that the peculiarity of the organic phenomena called forth.
Modern physiology has advanced beyond this standpoint by the analysis of
the individual links in the life-process. The idea of the one,
indivisible vital force has thereby given place to the picture of an
extraordinarily intricate interaction, in which the individual
manifestations of force can be traced back to the general forces of
nature, and the individual particles of matter to the common elements.
That is the principle with which physiology now works, and to which we
must therefore keep here as well, even if no one dares to assert that
it is, or perhaps ever can be, carried through everywhere. The main
thing is that all the advances of physiology point toward it. However
many riddles still
% --- p. 42 ---
\opage{42}remain, especially in the morphological domain, no explanation
is any longer accepted that does not rest on the stated principle. The
burden of proof lies in any case on those who appeal to the intervention
of immaterial causes.\footnote{Cf.\ \emph{Panum}: Indledning til
Fysiologien [Introduction to Physiology]. København 1865. --- \emph{Charles
Robin}: Anatomie et physiologie cellulaires. Paris 1873. Introduction.
--- \emph{Claude Bernard}: Leçons sur les phénomènes de la vie. Paris
1878. (Note here especially the following definite statement: »Quel que
soit le sujet qu'il étudie, le physiologiste ne trouve jamais devant lui
que des agents mécaniques, physiques ou chimiques«, p.~52). ---
\emph{Exner}: Physiologie der Grosshirnrinde. 1879. (Hermann, s Handbuch
der Physiologie. II, 2). p.~189--191.}

Organic life is thus subject to the law of the conservation of energy.
For everything that arises and disappears in the organism, physical and
chemical equivalents must be sought, either in the organism or outside
it. Organic life is thereby drawn into the great circulation of nature.
It is especially in the green plant cells that the transformation of
inorganic substances into complicated organic substances takes
place.\footnote{``Only the green parts of plants are able to produce a
new organic substance from carbonic acid and water, by means of other
substances already found in their cells\ldots\ all animal substances are
descended from those which the green plants have formed. The substances
that compose our blood, our muscles and nerves, our brain, are only
slightly modified plant substances, which are produced in the
chlorophyll-bearing cells\ldots. But the means that enables the green
plant to convert inorganic substances into organic ones is light; only
under its influence are the chlorophyll-bearing cells able to produce
carbon-containing compounds from the carbonic acid of the air,
compounds which, after having undergone new changes, are just as fit to
form new parts of plants as to nourish our body.'' J. \emph{Sachs}
(Tidsskrift for popul. Fremstillinger af Naturvidenskaben [Journal for
Popular Expositions of Natural Science], 5te Række, 3die Bind, p.~111).
--- According to Pringsheim's theory (see the journal cited, 1879,
p.~316 f.) chlorophyll is not directly active in the process of
assimilation, but serves only as a protection against the light and as
a regulator of respiration. --- Sachs expresses himself too absolutely
when he denies to the animal organism the capacity to form organic
compounds from inorganic elements. This synthetic process is not
entirely lacking in the animal organism, although it finds especially
favorable conditions in the plant organism. Cf.\ \emph{Pflüger}: Ueber
die physiol. Verbrennung. (Archiv für Physiologie. XI. p.~345).} The
organic
% --- p. 43 ---
\opage{43}matter thus accumulated is consumed by the functions of plant
and animal. Metabolism rests on the conservation of energy, and on
metabolism in turn rests the activity of organic beings. The form under
which, and the manner in which, the accumulated tensional force is
applied depend on the structure of the organism. Here the relations of
form become of great importance. Every organic cell contains a capital
of energy; but it depends on the structure of the organs what
application this capital receives.

The law of the conservation of energy thus closes in on us ever more
nearly. Vegetative life, the functions of nutrition, one would perhaps
even hand over to it with a light heart. But neither can the nervous and
muscular system be exempt from coming under it. The energy that every
nervous and muscular activity consumes has been stored up during the
activity of nutrition. The nervous and muscular systems are themselves
only peculiarly shaped (differentiated) apparatuses for the exercise of
functions that also go on, in extraordinarily simple form, in the
homogeneous, structureless protoplasm. Already here an impression at
one place on the surface of the organism can propagate itself through
the mass and release movement from quite other places or from the whole
mass. It is the progressive division of labor that makes different
systems necessary in the higher organisms. But this richer elaboration
(differentiation) cannot emancipate from the general elementary
laws.\footnote{``The nerves are to be regarded as places in the tissues
which are more easily traversed by the train of entering influences,
without our needing at once to seek in them more mysterious forces than
in other parts of the body.'' \emph{Lotze}: Allgemeine Physiologie des
körperlichen Lebens. Leipzig 1850. p.~386. --- A proof of this has been
sought in the fact that the effect of narcotic substances on nerve
tissue differs only in degree and speed from their effect on other
organic tissues. Cf.\ \emph{Laycock}: Further Researches into the
Functions of the Brain. (The British and Foreign Medico-Chirurgical
\emph{Review}. July 1855). p.~185. \emph{Claude Bernard}: Leçons sur les
phénomènes de la vie. p.~289. \emph{Herbert Spencer}: Principles of
psychology. I, p.~631 ff.} These laws still hold, only under
extraordinarily composite and often inscrutable conditions. Both nerves
and muscles behave differently in chemical and physical respects during
and after their function than before it. The blood that flows through
active muscles contains
% --- p. 44 ---
\opage{44}several per cent less oxygen, and on the other hand more
carbonic acid, than the blood in resting muscles. The nerve tissue, both
the fibers and the central organs, cannot function without an ample
supply of blood containing the material that is necessary for the
metabolism heightened by the function. The brain is affected by every
change in the circulation of the blood; both anemia and hyperemia bring
disorder into its activity. Brain-work draws on the organic capital just
as the activity of every other organ does.

What it really is that goes on in the nervous system during its
activity has not yet been cleared up. Only so much is clear, that it
cannot be a substance that is propagated from one end to the other (as
was earlier assumed of the so-called ``animal spirits''). The nerve
process probably consists in a change advancing through the nerve
fiber, a release of tensional force, which is called forth by an
impression from without (the irritant) and is continued successively
from part to part, so that the one nerve element serves as irritant in
relation to the next. The tensional forces thus successively released
seem to be of a chemical nature; yet a purely chemical theory of the
nerves founders on the difficulty of explaining why not the whole of
the tensional force, but only a part of it standing in a definite
proportion to the irritation, is released.\footnote{L. \emph{Hermann}:
Allgemeine Nervenphysiologie. 1879 (Hermann's Handbuch der Physiologie
II, 1). p.~186--193.} But everything indicates that we nowhere here
come upon any exception to the great law by which physical nature now
appears to us with a magnificent unity and coherence --- a unity which
had indeed often been divined and pronounced in the form of principle
and postulate, but which only the research of our own time has
supported by the control of experiment.

\textbf{4.} \textbf{a.} The plant is wholly absorbed in the life of
nutrition. It takes up and excretes matter, grows and propagates itself.
What it needs in order to lead this life it finds in its immediate
vicinity, and it can live only when it finds it there. Air, water,
light, etc., must bathe the surface of the plant if it is to be able to
subsist. The plant is like a fetus that still rests in the womb of
nature and has not emerged from it into independent, individual life.
The fetus, after all, receives its
% --- p. 45 ---
\opage{45}nourishment immediately from the mother organism. The properly
animal life is conditioned by the fact that everything is not thus laid
ready. The animal must seek, work and fight to get its needs satisfied;
it must therefore act as a whole over against the surrounding world,
must be able to gather its energy and direct it upon definite points.
It must also be able to take account of relations and facts that do not
immediately touch it at the moment. These demands are satisfied by the
\emph{nervous system}: through it the various parts and regions of the
organism are brought into close mutual connection, so that the organism
becomes a whole in a more intimate sense than the plant could be said
to be; and through it there arises the possibility that the conditions
in the surrounding world can determine the movements of the organism
not only directly but also indirectly.

It is true that nerves have not yet been demonstrated in the lowest
animal organisms, and on the other hand many plants perform actions
similar to those which in higher animals are performed by means of the
nervous system; but on the whole plant and animal may nevertheless be
characterized as two types of life, of which the one stands merely in
immediate interaction with its surroundings, the other in mediate and
indirect interaction as well. The higher we go within animal life, the
greater the part played by the nervous system, because the interaction
with the surrounding world spreads in ever wider and wider circles, and
thus becomes less and less immediate and momentary.

\textbf{b.} The simplest form of nervous activity is the so-called
\emph{reflex movement}, in which an influence is conducted along an
ingoing (centripetal) nerve fiber to an inner center (a ganglion), and
from there in turn releases an impulse which, through an outgoing
(centrifugal) nerve fiber, sets a muscle or another organ (a gland, for
example) in motion. We have here the simple schema that seems to repeat
itself at all stages of the development of the nervous system, only in
extraordinarily many coordinate and subordinate layers. For as a rule
the ganglion sends out not only outgoing fibers to the organs that are
to be roused to movement; there also go out from it ingoing or upgoing
fibers to higher centers, which thus receive impulses from several
sides, impulses that can partly reinforce and partly inhibit one
another. The ganglion itself exerts a checking influence on
% --- p. 46 ---
\opage{46}the impulse, since experiments show that the nerve process
goes on more slowly during its course through brain and spinal cord than
through the peripheral \emph{nerves}. This checking seems to make it
possible for the impulse, before it is propagated further, to be altered
by the influence of other impulses. It is this central working-up of the
peripheral impressions that brings it about that the movement they
release is not determined by merely local and momentary effects, but to
a certain degree by influences from the whole organism. The nervous
central organs are thus organs of modification and combination.

A very simple example of this relation is found in the
% footnote *) on p. 46
suckers\footnote{I cite it here after \emph{Carpenter}: Mental
physiology. p.~50.} of the cuttlefish. Each sucker on this animal's arm
has its own ganglion, and can therefore be brought to contract and
fasten itself by suction when an object is brought into contact with it
alone. This can also happen when the arm is severed from the rest of
the animal. Here we have a nervous function in its simplest form:
propagation of the impression to a simple central organ, and release in
this organ of an impulse to contraction. But now the ganglia of all the
suckers are connected both with one another and with the animal's
highest centers (the pharyngeal ring), so that the animal, when it
clasps an object with its whole arm, can set all the suckers in activity
at once. The single elementary nervous function then appears as a link
in a whole system of functions.

In higher animals too this relation between subordinate and
superordinate centers can be demonstrated, although the more intimate
connection and mutual dependence of the organs here makes it the more
difficult to see through the relations, the higher one goes up in the
series of development. In cold-blooded animals (such as the frog, which
for just that reason must especially serve in physiological experiments)
the independence of the subordinate centers is greater than in
warm-blooded ones, and within the series of the latter this independence
is greater in birds and rabbits than in the ape, and especially than in
man. The complete removal of both cerebral hemispheres can be survived
only by animals whose cerebrum has not reached a higher degree of
development. Higher mammals, on the other hand,
% --- p. 47 ---
\opage{47}quickly perish when they are deprived of the whole mass of the
hemispheres.

\textbf{c.} The vegetative \emph{organs} are connected by ingoing and
outgoing fibers with the spinal cord and the brain, and are regulated
from these central organs. Yet some of them seem to have special nerve
centers, to a certain degree independent, in or near them. An excised
frog's heart continues to beat for several hours, and thereby proves its
relative independence of higher centers. Experiments (on dogs and
rabbits) have shown that when the heart was freed, by severing the
\textit{nervus vagus}, from its connection with the medulla oblongata,
the pulse beat faster. In violent fright the rabbit's heart stops, only
to beat more rapidly than before; but after the \textit{nervus vagus}
had been severed no influence on the heartbeat was to be traced. With
the intestines the case is similar. The peristaltic movements can
continue after the connection with higher
% footnote *) on p. 47
centers has been cut.\footnote{M. \emph{Foster}: Text-book of
Physiology. London 1877. p.~81. --- \emph{Eckhard}: Physiol.\ des
Rückenmarks (Hermann II, 2). p.~71. \emph{Exner}: Physiol.\ der
Grosshirnrinde (Hermann II, 2). p.~289.}

The \emph{spinal cord} is an important seat of reflex movements. In the
headless frog one can, by sufficiently strong stimulation of one or
another part of the skin, release reflex movements in all directions.
The remarkable thing about these movements is their coordination and
purposiveness. In so far as it has been possible, by severing the spinal
cord and applying an irritation below the cut, to call forth reflex
movements in mammals, these proved indeed to be coordinated to a certain
degree, but little purposive. In the higher animals the spinal cord
seems more and more to be absorbed in the activity of forming the
connecting link between the brain and the
% footnote **) on p. 47
extremities of the organism.\footnote{M. \emph{Foster}, p.~420.}

In the \emph{medulla oblongata} a number of centers important for the
maintenance of life are localized, which can function independently of
the higher parts of the brain, and which set very intricate mechanisms
in motion: thus the respiratory center, the center for the regulatory
cardiac nervous system, and those for the secretion of saliva, the act
of swallowing and the secretion of urine. They seem to be set in
% --- p. 48 ---
\opage{48}activity partly reflexly, partly automatically (i.e.\ by the
constitution of the blood directly affecting the central nerve cells).

A frog that has been deprived of the cerebrum, but still has the
\emph{mid-brain} (the cerebral ganglia lying in front of the medulla
oblongata), proves still to command the motor apparatus that is
necessary for the performance of independent movements; but it moves,
as it seems, only when a definite sense-impression rouses it. The
capacity to take the initiative is lacking. It has this advantage over
the mere spinal frog, that it can be determined by sense-impressions and
is therefore not wholly passive. Whereas the spinal frog is of course
unaffected by light, and sinks to the bottom when it is thrown into the
water, the mid-brain frog avoids a very dark shadow, and when it is
thrown into the water, the impression of the movement of the particles
of water makes it swim. But it always needs an impulse from without in
order to move. --- Similar features are found in birds and mammals when
these survive the removal of the cerebral hemispheres. There is a lack
of initiative and of the capacity to help themselves in cases that are
at all difficult, whereas single elementary sense-impressions can call
forth movements that are in part very composite.

\textbf{d.} The part that can be reserved for the most important portion
of the brain,
% footnote *) on p. 48
\emph{the cerebrum},\footnote{The cerebellum is not discussed here,
since the results of the investigations concerning it are very unclear.
Its significance in the various vertebrates is probably different, and
moreover no sharp boundary can be drawn between it and the adjoining
parts of the brain. \emph{Eckhard}, p.~102.} can be no other than that
of working up and combining the elementary impressions received in the
medulla oblongata and the cerebral ganglia (perhaps also in the
cerebellum), and of employing the motor apparatus lying ready in these
lower parts of the brain in accordance with the result of this
working-up. The cerebrum stands as the keystone of the ingenious
structure of the nervous system. The nearer we come to it, the more
intricate the relations become, the more numerous the nerve cells and
the connecting fibers. Here paths are laid ready which make the most
composite interaction between the various impulses
% --- p. 49 ---
\opage{49}possible. When we remember that every impression acts by
releasing tensional force in the organic cells, and that the result of
this release in the single cell can be combined in the cerebrum with the
results from millions of other cells,\footnote{\emph{Meynert} and
\emph{Bain}, independently of each other, estimated the number of nerve
cells in the cortex of the human cerebrum at a thousand millions.
(\emph{Meynert}: Zur Mechanik des Gehirnbaues. Wien 1874. p.~7). ---
``A portion of the gray substance on the \emph{surface} of a cerebral
convolution, only as large as a very small pin's head, contains parts
of several thousand nerve fibers.'' \emph{Beale}, quoted by
\emph{Maudsley}: Physiologie de l'esprit. \emph{Trad}. de l'angl.
p.~110.} the mind reels at the combinations that here become possible.

The question whether the cerebrum functions as a unity, or whether its
various functions are each bound to a region of their own, has received
changing answers and is still an object of debate among physiologists.
\emph{Gall}, the founder of phrenology, taught a very far-reaching
localization of all the higher and lower faculties of the soul, but
brought the idea of localization into discredit by his uncritical
procedure and his fantastic cranioscopy. The reaction against his
doctrine is represented by Flourens, who concluded from his experiments
that any parts whatever of the cerebral hemispheres could be injured or
removed without the functions of the brain suffering thereby.

This theory prevailed for nearly half a century, during which it was
contradicted only by Broca's demonstration (1861) that the most
important central organs for language and speech are found in the third
frontal convolution of the left hemisphere. A new period in the
physiology of the brain begins with the investigations of Fritsch and
Hitzig (1870). These researchers believed they could show that
irritation of definite points on the surface of the cerebrum calls forth
definite movements of definite parts of the body. Later \emph{Hermann
Munk} in particular has sought to demonstrate definite organs in the
cerebrum for the apprehension and recognition of the elementary
sense-influences (a visual sphere, an auditory sphere, etc.). There
would thus be reason once more to assume a localization, a division of
labor within the cerebrum --- yet with the great difference that it is
only the elementary psychical activities
% --- p. 50 ---
\opage{50}that are localized, not thinking itself, or
% footnote *) on p. 50
``intelligence.''\footnote{»Die Intelligenz hat überall in der
Groshirnrinde ihren Sitz und nirgend im besonderen; denn sie ist der
Inbegriff und die Resultirende aller aus den Sinneswahrnehmungen
stammenden Vorstellungen«. H. \emph{Munk}: Ueber die Functionen der
Grosshirnrinde. Berlin 1881. p.~73.} But even with this restriction the
new theory of localization does not stand unchallenged. In the first
place it has been shown that the local motor centers do not act in the
first 8--10 days of the newborn dog's life (not before the animal has
begun to see). There might thus be reason to assume that they are first
formed under the influence of experience and practice.\footnote{Soltmann's
experiments. See \emph{Vierordt}: Die Physiologie des Kindesalters.
p.~134 ff.} But in the second place \emph{Goltz} has come forward with
an objection to the new theory of localization. Goltz takes up an
intermediate position between Flourens's doctrine and the new theory.
He does not deny the possibility of a localization of the various
functions of the cerebrum, and he disputes the correctness of Flourens's
doctrine that any part of the cerebrum was able to act vicariously for
any other. On the removal of large parts of both hemispheres a lasting
weakening of the intelligence sets in. But the weakenings of definite
sensory and motor functions that follow the removal of certain areas of
the cerebral surface Goltz explains as phenomena of inhibition brought
about by the operation. If the injury is not too extensive, the animal
finds its bearings again --- which the theory of localization explains
only in an unnatural way, by assuming that new special centers develop
in regions of the brain which had hitherto not possessed any such at
all. The whole of what Munk calls the visual sphere can, according to
Goltz, be removed without the animal becoming
% footnote ***) on p. 50
blind.\footnote{\emph{Goltz} in Pflüger's Archiv für Physiologie. 20de
og 26de Bd.}

Whereas, then, there is dispute as to how far there is a localization of
the individual sensory functions in the cerebrum, both of the contending
parties agree that the higher psychical manifestations of life
(understanding, feeling and will) are not bound to definite regions of
the cerebrum. In holding that ``the most important functions of the
cerebrum, the actions from which we infer intelligence, feeling, passion
and instinct, cannot depend on
% --- p. 51 ---
\opage{51}limited sections of the cerebrum,''\footnote{\emph{Goltz} in
Pflügers Archiv. XXVI. p.~35.} both Goltz and Munk agree with Flourens.
The cerebrum thus stands before us as an extraordinarily composite organ
which --- however it may be with the distribution of labor in its
interior --- takes up the more elementary influences from the lower
central organs, works them up and combines them, and which thereby makes
possible a more many-sided and more concentrated reaction upon the
surrounding world.

\textbf{e.} A couple of examples will show us the physiological relation
of the cerebrum to the other nervous organs. --- The study of the
disturbances of speech seems to have led to the result that in the
regions of the brain lying beneath the surface of the cerebrum are found
only the arrangements for the mechanical execution and connection of the
movements of sound, whereas the properly linguistic formation of
syllables and words goes on in the surface of the cerebrum. The
primitive sounds of the infant have perhaps even the conditions for the
mechanism that orders them in the medulla oblongata alone, whereas the
developed sounds of the national language, which are joined into
syllables and words and are bound up with the development of the
intelligence, presuppose the intervention of higher
% footnote **) on p. 51
centers.\footnote{Ad. \emph{Kussmaul}: Die Störungen der Sprache.
Leipzig 1877. p.~71, 80.} --- The movements that are initiated from the
mid-brain, the medulla oblongata and the spinal cord have the character
of simple reflex movement. Actions, on the other hand, that are guided
by the will, since they presuppose more or less distinct ideas of
movement, come about only with the
% footnote ***) on p. 51
cooperation of the cerebrum.\footnote{\emph{Munk} p.~51 f. Cf.\
\emph{Goltz} in Pflügers Archiv. XXVI. p.~36.} --- While elementary
sense-impressions, as stated above, also affect an animal that has been
deprived of the cerebrum, the proper apprehension and understanding of
the impression takes place only when the cerebrum is intact. After
extensive injury to both hemispheres of the cerebrum a dog no longer
understands the meaning of what it sees and hears. It does not care if
it is threatened with the whip, pays no heed to its food when this is
not set in the usual place, is not frightened by noise, does not obey
when it is called, does not mind tobacco smoke, and devours a dog's
carcass without any sign of disgust.
% --- p. 52 ---
\opage{52}On the other hand, such a dog goes around obstacles in its way
and shuns glaring light. \emph{Goltz} and \emph{Munk} describe the
condition of such an animal in much the same way, but differ in the
explanation. According to Munk this ``psychical blindness,'' as he calls
it, is bound up with an injury to a certain limited region of the
surface of the brain; according to Goltz every extensive injury to the
cerebrum brings such a condition with it. Munk explains the condition
psychologically by the loss of the ideas of memory by means of which the
new impressions can be recognized and understood, while Goltz explains
it by a general intellectual dullness, perhaps especially a want of
attention. On both explanations the significance of the cerebrum in
relation to the subordinate centers comes
% footnote *) on p. 52
clearly to light.\footnote{\emph{Goltz} in Pflügers Archiv. XXVI.
p.~42 ff. --- \emph{Munk} p.~29.}

But the cerebrum stands not only in a positive but also in a negative
relation to the subordinate nervous organs, since it can inhibit their
activity. The vegetative functions therefore go on more briskly during
sleep, when the cerebrum has laid down its scepter. Even in lower
animals, where its position is not so dominant as in the higher ones,
even there this inhibiting influence is noticeable. When the headless
frog has recovered from the operation, its mobility becomes even greater
than before. The subordinate centers yield more easily to impressions
than the higher ones. This is in itself a direct consequence of the fact
that in the higher centers the impressions have a long process to go
through, have, so to speak, to be confronted with so many other parties,
that the single impression does not so easily and so completely get its
will as in the less composite organs. Thus every new impression modifies
--- either reinforcing or inhibiting --- the movement that preceding
impressions have called forth.

The increased briskness with which subordinate nervous processes go on
after the removal of higher centers is explained by some from the fact
that the quantity of nervous activity which has been roused in the lower
center through the ingoing nerve now spreads over a smaller area, and
must therefore produce quicker and more powerful effects. But all the
phenomena cannot be explained in this way. Strong
% --- p. 53 ---
\opage{53}brain-activity, as in sudden and powerful sense-impressions,
emotion, or the labor of thought, seems directly to affect the
subordinate centers, so that the direct impressions upon these do not
have the effect that they
% footnote *) on p. 53
would otherwise have.\footnote{\emph{Eckhard}: Physiol.\ des
Rückenmarks. (Hermann. II, 2). p.~37.}

Through such inhibitory activity, what goes on in the higher centers
acquires significance for the lower. It is well known, to take a simple
example, how a sudden sense-impression can prevent sneezing. Violent
emotion or pain inhibits the movement of the heart (by an influence from
the brain through the medulla oblongata and the \textit{nervus vagus}),
and thereby causes fainting. Strong fear can prevent the secretion of
saliva, which lies at the bottom of that ``judgment of God'' by which
the accused was held guilty when he had rice in his mouth without its
being moistened. A sudden and gripping impression can stop the pains of
labor, and an influence on the \textit{nervus splanchnicus} the
peristaltic movements of the intestinal canal. It is not only single
sense-impressions that release inhibitory effects in this way; more
intricate functions of the brain also exert this influence, and we shall
see in a later section that an important part of the dominion of the
will is connected with it. The only thing still worth noting here is
that the phenomena of inhibition appear the more strongly, the more
vigorous the organism is, whereas they are weakened by fatigue. The
condition of the central organ is of decisive influence here: if it is
exhausted, badly nourished, affected by cold or by strychnine and
certain other poisons, then the reflex movement gains in rapidity,
strength and extent. In ``nervously weak'' persons, whose morbid
condition is bound up with disturbances of nutrition in muscles and
nerves, there is a strong tendency to reflex movements and
spasms.\footnote{\emph{Wundt}: Physiol.\ Psychologie. I. p.~260--263.
--- \emph{Ranke}: Physiologie des Menschen. 3 Aufl. Leipzig 1875.
p.~871 f.} --- In so far as one wishes to speak of reflex movements (in
the sense of immediate motor discharges) within the brain itself, the
brain is indeed a whole little world, which in its myriads of cells and
fibers has means enough for inner reinforcement or inhibition, for inner
debate and struggle for dominion among all the impulses that can arise
in it.


% --- p. 54 ---
\opage{54}\textbf{5.} From the purely \emph{physical} point of view,
which physiology too acknowledges as its own, everything that goes on in
the nervous system, even in its highest centers, is a conversion of
force, in which an impression, from the organism's surrounding world or
from its own interior, releases the tensional force stored up in the
nerve tissue. The \emph{physiological} expression for this is that the
impression (the irritant) calls forth a reaction, which consists either
in a muscular movement, or in a glandular secretion, or else in a more
comprehensive process within the centers of the nervous system itself.
But now it turns out that in the case of some of the phenomena that fall
under these points of view a third point of view must also be applied,
namely a \emph{psychological} one, since certain states of consciousness
are bound up with the physical-physiological processes. The question
then becomes one of the relation between these different points of
view. Is the one subordinate to the others, so that, just as the
physiological point of view falls within the physical when this is
taken in the widest sense, the psychological point of view in turn falls
within the physiological as a special form of it? Is what shows itself
to us from the one point of view perhaps the cause or the effect of what
shows itself to us from the others?

We still lack something here for the discussion of this question. For
while we have given an outline of what physiology can teach us that is
of significance for our problem, we have not yet given a fuller
characterization of the phenomena of consciousness. The psychological
point of view has thus not yet been distinctly indicated. Now it is
really the task of all the following inquiries to give a
characterization of the psychical life, and to that extent it will be
impossible to present one already here. But since I have nevertheless
chosen to treat the general problem of the relation between the
psychical and the bodily ahead of the more special psychological
inquiries, which in many ways presuppose a definite view of that
problem, there is nothing for it but to give here a provisional
characterization of the psychological phenomena, reserving for the
following sections the task of furnishing fuller proofs of its
validity. ---

It is with consciousness in general as it is with special forms or
elements of consciousness (e.g.\ colors and tones) in particular: a
description or a
% --- p. 55 ---
\opage{55}definition of them is impossible, since they are fundamental
facts that cannot be traced back to anything still simpler and clearer.
Now this does not exclude that one can nevertheless bring out the most
important characteristic marks. For one can direct one's attention to
limiting cases, where consciousness is about to glide over into
unconscious states; and one can attend to the transitions from weaker
and dimmer to stronger and clearer consciousness, and examine what it is
that conditions the higher degrees of consciousness.

Peculiar to all life is a constant unrest, a struggle with changes, a
want of equilibrium. With complete equilibrium death sets in. This
general mark of life we find in the life of consciousness too. When we
become conscious of something, a movement, a change must have taken
place. Something must in one way or another have made itself felt which
was not there before. A completely uniform existence abolishes
consciousness. Without a resistance to our accustomed and uniform
movement the spark of consciousness is not struck.

\emph{Thomas Hobbes}, the founder of English psychology, already taught:
it comes to the same thing to feel one and the same thing constantly
and not to feel at all. An experimental proof of the correctness of this
proposition is found in the fact that the sustained direction of
attention upon one single sensation or idea produces a sleep-like state
(hypnotism). As the condition for the onset of such artificial sleep the
discoverer of hypnotism, James \emph{Braid}, therefore gives
``monoideism,'' i.e.\ absorption in a single idea. (Cf.\ \emph{Preyer}:
Die Entdeckung des Hypnotismus. Berlin 1881. p.~41 f.\ 81). --- The
history of mysticism also teaches, in many examples, how one approaches
the limit of consciousness the more change, movement and alternating
ideas are kept away.

Strong impressions are needed to wake one from sleep or from
absent-mindedness. When consciousness is awake, it is sharpened and
heightened by contrasts and changes. We feel the cold more strongly when
we come from a warm room; light appears with a special brilliance when
we come from deep darkness. Of stillness and rest we become properly
conscious only when we come from the noisy town or from hard work.


% --- p. 56 ---
\opage{56}But change and contrast are nevertheless not enough in
themselves. They call forth a sudden shock, a surprise; but if their
effect were not preserved, it would be only like a flash of light that
vanished at once. One could imagine a living being so organized that
from time to time wholly isolated sensations arose in it. Such separate
flashes would not correspond to what we notice as sensations in our
consciousness; in us the single elements of consciousness are not
isolated, but stand in firmer or looser connection from first to last.
Such a connection is necessary if the single impressions themselves are
each to be able to make themselves felt. For a change to be able to have
its effect, it is necessarily presupposed that the preceding state has
not entirely vanished, for in that case the background, against which
the new was to stand out, would also be lacking.

For consciousness there is therefore required, secondly, the
preservation of the impressions and \emph{interaction} between the
elements that make up the content of consciousness. --- This is
confirmed by the fact that incoherence and want of inner mutual
interaction between the elements of consciousness is a sign of the
beginning dissolution of the life of consciousness. In advancing mental
illness ``fixed ideas'' form, which make the free, natural movement and
debate among ideas impossible. Later on not even such fixed ideas can be
held fast and carried through. No comparisons and combinations can be
made. Finally there sets in a complete absence of images and thoughts.
The sense-impressions are no longer worked up, memory is almost
extinguished, and language for the most part
% footnote *) on p. 56
lost.\footnote{Cf.\ \emph{Griesinger}: Die Pathologie und Therapie der
psychischen Krankheiten. Stuttgart. 1861. p.~323--351.}

This second mark can of course be present in countless degrees. Seen
from an ideal standpoint we are really never fully conscious. We always
have a multitude of elements in our consciousness which are not brought
into distinct mutual connection, are not measured against one another,
and are therefore not organized as their nature would require. But there
nevertheless always asserts itself an involuntary tendency to compare
and order all the content of consciousness according to definite
principles. For every
% --- p. 57 ---
\opage{57}newly emerging element of consciousness calls forth --- and
this is the third characteristic mark of the life of consciousness ---
according to certain laws, remnants in memory of elements given earlier.
To become conscious of something is to \emph{recognize} it, to refer it
to a group of similar elements, to distinguish it from elements of a
different kind. It is never the single elements (sensations, feelings,
ideas, etc.) that determine the state of consciousness; what matters is
the relation between the new impressions and those preserved in memory.
The single impression is known, exists in consciousness at all, only
through its relation to the other elements of consciousness. In
unconscious beings the one moment falls outside the other; the past is
gone and exists only in its effects. The conscious being, on the other
hand, can reproduce earlier states and thereby alter the present state.
In the moment of recognition the difference of time is abolished.

There are thus three chief properties that characterize consciousness:
\emph{change or contrast} as the condition for the arising of the single
elements of consciousness (sensations, feelings, etc.), ---
\emph{preservation and reproduction} of elements that have arisen
earlier, --- and \emph{connection} between these and the new elements.

When we think back on our earlier states of consciousness, they stand
before us as a series of sensations, ideas and feelings, as a stream
whose higher or lower waves succeed one another. It can often look to
memory as if this series were composed of independent, separate links
that were connected with one another only in an external way. Some
psychologists (especially \emph{Hume}) have therefore depicted
consciousness as a mere succession of ideas without inner bond and
coherence, or more definitely as ``the series of our possible and
actual sensations'' (\emph{Stuart Mill}). What speaks decisively against
the correctness of this description is the impossibility of the single
elements occurring in \emph{absolute} separation within what we know as
consciousness. Each single element belongs to consciousness only through
its connection with other elements. The emphasis must thus be laid on
the connection, the coherence, and not on the links in their
singleness. The peculiarity of the phenomena of consciousness, in
% --- p. 58 ---
\opage{58}contrast to what is the object of the outer apprehension of
nature, the material phenomena, is precisely the inner connection
between the single elements by which they stand as states of one and the
same subject --- a connection that has its typical expression in
\emph{memory}, which may therefore be called the fundamental phenomenon
in the spiritual domain. What has fallen out of memory can still exert
a great influence on our life of consciousness indirectly, through its
unconscious after-effects; but it is no longer itself a part of that
life. In the physical world one can speak of memory only figuratively.
Wherever a development takes place, what comes later is determined by
what went before; and by virtue of the law of the conservation of
energy nothing happens, be it ever so insignificant, that does not have
its effect on what happens afterward. But only on the presupposition of
a consciousness is what is past itself lived over again, itself made a
link in a later spiritual connection, so that the differences of time
are abolished. --- Of the various stages and forms in which this
fundamental psychological phenomenon appears, a following section will
treat.

What must still be added here is that this inwardness and unity can
appear in many degrees, and that in our psychological experience we
never meet with it in the highest conceivable degree. Beneath the
clarity and coherence of our consciousness there is always a more or
less obscure chaos, whether it appears as a barrier to clearly conscious
spiritual activity or as a nourishing, refreshing source for it. The
primordial elements of our consciousness must always arise in it as
something given to it as material for its activity. There is thus a
passive and an active side in the nature of consciousness, the first
corresponding most nearly to the multiplicity of the elements, the
second to the unity and coherence in all the content of consciousness.
The energy of consciousness shows itself in the way in which the single
elements are connected and brought into mutual interaction. Kant has
therefore rightly characterized consciousness as a synthesis. But the
synthesis presupposes, at whatever stage in the development of the life
of consciousness we look for it, a given multiplicity. It unites the
single sensations into observations and ideas, the ideas into concepts,
and so on. Such work goes on right from the threshold of consciousness:
it lies already in the fact that the more
% --- p. 59 ---
\opage{59}consciousness approaches the point where only one single
element holds sway, the more it approaches unconsciousness. To that
extent consciousness, as soon as it awakens to clarity, finds its own
work already in full swing. The synthesis is, to use the words of the
thinker whose genius in research has especially thrown light on these
obscure paths, ``the mere effect of a blind, though indispensable,
psychical activity, without which we should have no knowledge at all,
but of which we are only seldom
% footnote *) on p. 59
conscious''\footnote{\emph{Kant}: Kritik der reinen Vernunft. 1st ed.
p.~78. (Kehrbach's edition p.~95). --- Kant calls this faculty
``imagination'' [\textit{Einbildungskraft}].}

It is above all this fundamental property of the life of consciousness
that makes its first arising so great a problem. For even if one were to
presuppose elementary sensations bound up with forms of development
standing lower down, what is quite decisive is, after all, precisely the
connection, the unity of these sensations. The first sensation cannot,
after all, attach itself to any other spiritual element; but how, then,
can there be synthesis and consciousness here? --- Such questions arise
everywhere when we go back to the beginning. Possibly, however, the
difficulty here is connected with the fact that we speak quite
unwarrantably of single sensations. As will be discussed in a later
section, there are circumstances which might indicate that what we
regard as psychologically single is not so in itself.

The history of psychology shows that the various schools have laid
different weight on the two sides of the nature of consciousness.
\emph{The German school} (Leibnitz, Kant, Hegel) lays predominant
emphasis on the synthesis, the activity, the unity. \emph{The English
school} (and in Germany Herbart) has especially brought forward the
passive or mechanical side, the multiplicity of the elements and their
mutual interaction. Each school has its strength in the treatment of
different problems. The English school has more eye for the elementary,
real side of the life of consciousness, for the way in which the
spiritual edifice is raised by the joining together of the fundamental
elements; the German school, on the other hand, more for the coherence
and unity that is the characteristic mark of consciousness from first
to last. The \emph{more recent} English school seems to meet the German
in the
% --- p. 60 ---
\opage{60}recognition that the single sensation or idea exists only by
being a link in a connection of consciousness, for which reason
consciousness can never be conceived as a mere sum or a mere
% footnote *) on p. 60
product.\footnote{See on this my book \textit{Den engelske Filosofi i
vor Tid} [English Philosophy in Our Time]. København. 1874.}

German psychology has often shown a tendency to approach metaphysics;
English psychology, on the other hand, has approached mechanical natural
science, and has carried analogies from it over into the conception of
the psychical phenomena.

In the characterization of consciousness now given we have kept chiefly
to its morphological side. Only in the more special psychological
discussion can we go more closely into the more real side. In the
present connection we must content ourselves with pointing out that the
unity in the psychical life has its expression not only in memory and
synthesis, but also in a dominant fundamental feeling, characterized by
the contrast between pleasure and displeasure, and in an impulse to
movement and activity proceeding from this fundamental feeling. At the
simplest stage of consciousness that we can conceive, the psychical life
consists in an oscillation between pleasure and displeasure, such that
each of these states, while it holds sway, all but displaces the other,
for which reason memory plays a very subordinate part. At this stage
the life of consciousness plainly shows its original practical
character, which at higher stages is more hidden and indirect. Like all
life, the life of consciousness too is intelligible only through the
living being's relation to, and position in, the world. Life is
everywhere a struggling harmony between the inner and the outer. The
rhythm of pleasure and displeasure must therefore be connected with a
rhythm in the organism's conditions of life. This practical character
is never entirely lost. Even those of our thoughts and feelings that do
not concern our own personal existence nevertheless spring from our
relation to other beings and our position in the universe. The thinking
and feeling ego is always determined by its relation to the non-ego,
whether this non-ego is a world of sense or a world of ideas.


% --- p. 61 ---
\opage{61}\textbf{6.} With these last remarks we have already overstepped
the strictly psychological characterization and hinted at a
parallelization of the life of consciousness and the nervous system.
This hint we shall now carry further. Here one parallel will present
itself after another, so that the craving which on the naive standpoint
so often makes itself felt, to get an intuitable picture of the soul, is
properly speaking satisfied by nature itself in the form and mode of
functioning of the nervous system. The next task then becomes that of
interpreting the picture, of finding out the relation between the
symbol and the thing itself.

\textbf{a.} The great significance of the nervous system was, as we saw
in an earlier connection, to serve as a connecting central organ for the
various parts of the organism, to guide their activities in mutual
harmony, and to make possible a unified action over against the
surrounding world. But consciousness solves precisely the same task in
its own way. In it what is scattered in time and space is united; the
surge of the conditions of life expresses itself as the rhythm of
pleasure and displeasure; and in memory and the activity of thought the
most intimate concentration that the whole circle of our experiences
can show is revealed.

\textbf{b.} That one becomes conscious of something presupposes a
change, a transition, a contrast. The content and the energy of
consciousness must be brought out of their equilibrium; attention must
be aroused. An arousal, an influence (an irritant) is likewise the
condition for the functioning of the nervous system. The impression acts
by releasing bound force, by abolishing the equilibrium in nerve fibers
and nerve centers.

\textbf{c.} But the impression does not act merely on a single center;
owing to the manifoldly branching connection between the various nerve
centers, it releases a series of processes which mutually promote or
inhibit one another, so that the total effect depends on the result of
this physiological debate. Psychologically seen, what corresponds to
this is that the single sensation calls forth the ideas with which it
has most kinship. The single sensation therefore has not a single but a
very composite effect. The psychological relation between sensation and
idea has its physiological parallel in the relation between the arrival
of an impression at the nervous central organs and their mutual
interaction. Not only from the
% --- p. 62 ---
\opage{62}purely physical, but also from the physiological and the
psychological point of view, the effect of the spark on the gunpowder is
the most apt image of what goes on.

\textbf{d.} The formation and arising of sensations and ideas take a
certain time. None of our movements take place so quickly as the
unconscious ones. The greater the deliberation, the slower the action.
The more intricate the spiritual operations that are carried out, the
longer the time required. The nerve process, too, takes a certain time,
which physiology has begun to measure. What interests us in this
connection is that the movement in the nerve fibers goes on more quickly
than the movement through the nerve centers (the gray matter), and
especially that the central nervous functions to which the activity of
consciousness seems to be bound (the psychophysical functions) take
longer than the merely physiological ones.\footnote{See \emph{Wundt}:
Physiologische Psychologie. II. p.~227. Cf.\ \emph{Spencer}: Principles
of Psychology. I. p.~107.} It agrees with this that actions which at
first are performed with consciousness come, through frequent repetition
and practice, to be performed unconsciously --- and more quickly. The
child who is learning to read looks at each letter carefully before it
recognizes it, and applies special attention and effort to pronounce it
correctly. But gradually it learns to read aloud without thinking of
the form of the letters and the character of the sound. It is the same
with dressing and undressing, walking, dancing, swimming, and many of our
daily occupations. The shorter the time between the impression and the
movement released by it (the time of reaction, the physiological time),
the more unconsciously the action takes place.\footnote{Sometimes, to
be sure, it is precisely a straining of the attention, a concentration
of the will, that can lead to greater speed in thinking and acting. But
the presupposition of this is previous practice, or else an originally
adapted organization which needs only an impulse to be set in
function.}

\textbf{e.} To the physiological hierarchy of superordinate and
subordinate nerve centers, and the relative independence of the latter,
there corresponds the fact that activities can go on in our organism
which under normal conditions are not bound up with consciousness, but
become so when they depart from the normal conditions. The functions of
nutrition, for example, as a rule go on without our sensing
% --- p. 63 ---
\opage{63}anything of them. Only when they are especially favored or
hindered do sensations arise in us, bound up with pleasure or
displeasure. When food is lacking, the flow of blood to the stomach,
which now no longer has anything to work on, also ceases, and in the
opinion of some the feeling of hunger arises on account of the
deficient nourishment of the nerves thereby brought about in turn. The
\textit{nervus vagus} forms an intermediate link between the organs of
feeling and the brain; in a high degree of hunger the sensory nerves of
the small and large intestine also seem to play a
% footnote *) on p. 63
part.\footnote{\emph{Ranke}: Physiologie des Menschen. p.~219 f. --- It
has, however, been found that the sensation of hunger persists after the
\textit{nervus vagus} has been severed. --- According to E. H.
\emph{Weber} the contraction of the muscles of the empty stomach also
contributes to the sensation of hunger.} The sensation of hunger is
precisely a good example of a transition from unconsciousness to
consciousness, since it runs through a whole scale of degrees, from the
first indefinite feeling of discomfort to the most violent torment. The
opposite feeling does not stand out with so sharp a character.

From another side the psychological parallel to the relation between
higher and lower nerve centers appears in the phenomena of movement. We
have just seen how new reflex movements can arise through the constant
and repeated functioning of the higher centers. But the higher centers
also, as was brought out earlier, act to inhibit the involuntary
movements that can be released by lower centers. Just as the training of
new reflex movements corresponds to the positive work of the will, so
the inhibition of original and involuntary movements corresponds to the
negative work of the will. Both our education and our self-education
consist partly in accustoming ourselves to something, partly in
breaking ourselves of something. This will be treated more fully in the
section on the psychology of the will; here it was only to be hinted
that even the struggle between ``the spirit and the flesh'' has its
physiological counterpart.

\textbf{f.} Finally there appears a parallel between the various sides
of the life of consciousness and the various organs of the nervous
system. In knowledge and feeling the life of consciousness turns, as it
were, an open side out toward the non-ego; in this openly exposed region
it behaves predominantly as receiving and appropriating.
% --- p. 64 ---
\opage{64}In the will (at its various stages as impulse, desire and
resolution), on the other hand, we have the response of the life of
consciousness, the psychical reaction. The nervous system shows
precisely the same two-sidedness in the contrast between the sensory and
the motor organs. It would be to renew the misunderstandings of
phrenology if one were to imagine knowledge, feeling and will each
localized in its own place in the brain; none of them is,
psychologically seen, a single and simple process that could be
conceived as going on in a definite organ; they interpenetrate one
another, as will be shown later, to such a degree that it is only in
abstract usage that we can speak of them as different processes. We
therefore keep here merely to the general schema of the nervous system:
an ingoing movement, a central working-up of it, an outgoing movement.
The same schema fits the life of consciousness.

\textbf{7.} These parallels must have a real significance; there must be
an inner connection between the life of consciousness and the brain.

The matter could be settled most simply of all if we could feel
immediately that the life of consciousness was bound to the brain, so
that simultaneously with a certain state of consciousness we had a
sensation of a certain state in the brain. In strenuous spiritual
activity we do, to be sure, believe that we sense something in the
brain; but it is not then the function of the brain itself that we
sense. ``When,'' says
\emph{Griesinger},\footnote{Die Pathologie und Therapie der psychischen
Krankheiten. 2 Aufl. p.~26.} ``in strong alterations of the life of
ideas one sometimes seems to sense something going on in the head (as
though something opened or closed, or the like), there can be no
question of changes in the processes of the brain being sensed; they
seem rather to be processes connected with the membranes of the brain
and their supply of blood.''

It turns out, too, that it took a long time before men became fully
convinced that consciousness was bound to the brain. In antiquity the
seat of the soul was thought to be in the blood, in the diaphragm, or in
the heart. Among the older Greeks only \emph{Alcmaeon} and \emph{Plato}
taught that we think with the head. It was first the great Alexandrian
anatomist \emph{Herophilus}
% --- p. 65 ---
\opage{65}(c.~300 B.C.) who placed the soul in the brain, relying on
definite facts, namely the observation that the nerves, especially the
sensory nerves, gathered in the brain as their final
% footnote *) on p. 65
center.\footnote{\emph{Exner}: Physiologie der Grosshirnrinde.
(Hermann's Handbuch. II, 2). p.~193.} Yet this anatomical proof has not
been sufficient to establish the conviction of the real connection
between consciousness and brain. What has been of decisive importance
here, on the other hand, is a series of comparative observations and
experiments.

In the lowest animals no nervous system has yet been found. In mollusks
and arthropods there is found only slight centralization of the nervous
system; the central nervous system consists at most of a ring of nerve
ganglia. The lowest vertebrate, Amphioxus, has only a spinal cord, no
brain, and in the lower classes of vertebrates the brain is developed in
a far smaller degree than the spinal cord.

The greater the weight of the brain in relation to the rest of the body,
and the more prominent the cerebrum in relation to the other organs of
the brain, the more highly developed the life of consciousness proves to
be. The higher centers, again, occupy a far larger place in the human
cerebrum than in that of the animals, in which the immediate centers for
sense-sensations and muscular movements seem to preponderate. The
greater or lesser richness of the brain in convolutions also proves to
be connected with the higher or lower stage of development of the life
of consciousness. The brain of the more intelligent breeds of dogs has
more convolutions than that of the less intelligent; man in this
respect far surpasses the apes, which otherwise stand so near him in
structure; eminent men have very large cerebral hemispheres, rich in
convolutions.

The constitution of the brain of the fetus and of the newborn child also
agrees with this. At the earlier stages of fetal development the
cerebrum in man, as in all vertebrates, lies in front of the other parts
of the brain without covering them. In the course of growth it comes, in
man (and in part in the apes), to cover the mid-brain and at last the
cerebellum. The cerebrum of newborn children is little developed as
regards both structure and
% --- p. 66 ---
\opage{66}capacity to function, whereas subordinate cerebral apparatuses
can be taken into use at once.

Finally it has been shown by experiment that sensations arise only when
the impression on the outer surface of the organism is propagated to the
brain, and that voluntary movement is possible only when the motor
centers in the brain are uninjured. If the cerebrum is removed, the life
of consciousness in those animals that can survive this operation is
weakened; deliberation and initiative fall away. Conversely, a dull and
undeveloped life of consciousness (as in idiots) is bound up with
deficient development and nourishment of the brain; and the progressive
dissolution of the life of consciousness in mental illness is
accompanied by a progressive dissolution of the brain, first and
foremost of the cerebrum. If the vessels that supply the brain with
arterial blood are tied off, an unconscious state sets in which leads to
death.\footnote{\emph{Exner}. p.~193--206. --- \emph{Wundt}: Physiol.\
Psychol. I, p.~150. 204 f. --- \emph{Griesinger}. p.~418--444. ---
\emph{Tardieu}: Etude médicolégale sur la folie. 2 éd. p.~85--89. 119.
--- \emph{Carpenter}: Mental physiology. Chap.~2.}

\textbf{8.} Whither, now, are we led by this formal agreement and real
connection between the life of consciousness and the life of the brain?

Only that hypothesis can be justified which lets all the factors
brought out in the foregoing come into their own. Now, from the nature
of the case, four possibilities can be conceived here. a) Either
consciousness and brain, soul and body, act on each other as two
different beings or substances; b) or else the soul is a form or a
product of the body; c) or conversely the body is a \emph{form} or a
\emph{product} of the soul; d) or finally soul and body, consciousness
and brain, develop side by side with each other without our being able
to derive the one from the other. These various possibilities we now
test, relying on what has been developed and set forth in the
foregoing.

\textbf{a.} The common view is that the soul acts on the body and the
body on the soul. One perhaps even believes that one can sense this
immediately, although the very fact that not everyone agrees that there
exists an independent soul, separate from the body, and that it was in
any
% --- p. 67 ---
\opage{67}case only by many detours that knowledge was reached of which
part of the body the soul is especially bound to, seems to tell against
it. --- ``But are there not indisputable facts from which that
assumption can be inferred? An impression on a sense-organ is propagated
to the brain and there passes over into sensation; conversely, our will
is able to set our body in motion!'' But the question concerns precisely
the relation between what goes on in the brain and the states of
consciousness; and if what has been adduced were facts, we should have
no reason to ask; we should then know enough for the present. When the
state of the brain to which the sensation or the resolution is bound
does not itself become an object of consciousness, it is impossible to
discover whether a relation of cause and of interaction really exists
between brain and consciousness.

This view rests, moreover, on the presupposition that a causal relation
can take place between the spiritual and the material. But such a
presupposition is in the highest degree improbable. For it would conflict
with the \emph{law of the conservation of energy}. At the point where the
material nerve process was converted into psychical activity, a certain
sum of physical energy would disappear without being replaced by any
corresponding sum of physical energy. It has been answered to this that
it is, to be sure, incomprehensible how a material activity can pass
over into spiritual activity, but that every transition, every causal
relation and every conversion of force is at bottom incomprehensible to
us --- and that the law of the conservation of energy, moreover,
requires only that a certain corresponding sum of energy come into
action in place of the one that has disappeared, no matter whether this
equivalent is of a physical or a psychical nature. But this would be a
bold and unwarranted extension of the law of the conservation of energy,
which in the form in which it stands is a purely physical law. Such an
extension would presuppose that it was possible to obtain a \emph{common
measure} for the spiritual and the material. Now what common denominator
is there for a thought and a material motion, what common form contains
them both? Until such a common form has been demonstrated, all talk of
interaction between the spiritual and the bodily is, scientifically
seen, meaningless. So long as we move within the material, we move
safely; and so long as we move within the spiritual, we move
% --- p. 68 ---
\opage{68}safely; but as soon as we are required to imagine a
transition from physical laws to psychological laws or conversely, we
stand before the incomprehensible. In the concept of cause, as in all
the metaphysical concepts that condition our view of reality, there is
contained an epistemological problem; as everywhere when we approach the
boundary-posts of our knowledge, difficulties crop up; but it becomes a
difficulty raised to the second power when the concept of cause is to
be used to connect two terms that have no common measure. It is not too
strong a word that \emph{Griesinger} has used about this: that even if
an angel came from heaven and explained it to us, we should not be able
to understand it.

Now in reality the usual view also involuntarily brings about a
homogeneity, namely by now conceiving spirit as something material, when
it lets it be affected by material motion, now making matter into
something spiritual, when it lets it be accessible to spiritual
influence (as when in Plato reason ``persuades'' matter).

It will easily be seen that it is of no use to say that the soul cannot,
indeed, increase the \emph{sum} of physical energy in the world, but
that it can alter the direction in which energy is applied. A physical
motion does not alter its direction except under the influence of a
physical force of a certain strength. This way out, too, therefore
necessarily makes the soul into a physical energy.

The application of the concept of cause presupposes not only a common
measure, but also a \emph{difference of time}. The question that has
been raised, whether cause and effect are simultaneous or not, rests in
part on a misunderstanding, and in any case does not concern us here.
But no one can deny that we should never have formed the concept of
cause if no changes took place in the phenomena. If everything were
uniform and unchangeable, we should have nothing whose cause we could
