ask about. The causal relation presupposes that an event has taken
place. If the relation between soul and body, or consciousness and
brain, is a causal relation, there must therefore be a difference of
time between the process in the brain and the act of consciousness. But
this presupposition conflicts with the pointers that physiology gives.
As we have seen,
% --- p. 69 ---
\opage{69}modern physiology aims at conceiving all processes in the
organism as physical or chemical. It does not pretend to have explained
the origin of organic life; it asserts only that every time it has
arrived at an understanding within the domain of organic life, this has
happened by tracing the organic phenomena back to physical and chemical
laws. If, then, there is a transition from physiological function to
psychological activity, from body to spirit, physiology as such cannot
in any case discover it. And yet such an assumption presupposes that at
certain points the physiological process is broken off by a
psychological one (when the impression becomes a sensation in the soul),
only to begin again under altered conditions (when the soul has
recovered from the material influence and answers it with an act of
will). This assumption thus forces such a break upon physiology. But
physiology will hardly ever agree to concede this break. Apart from the
physical difficulty of the violation of the law of the conservation of
energy --- which physical difficulty is at the same time a physiological
one --- the nerve process must, from a physiological standpoint, be
conceived as a coherent circuit. A very great deal here is still
unexplained. The relation between nerve fiber and nerve cell is very
% footnote *) on p. 69
obscure;\footnote{Cf.\ \emph{Ditlefsen}: Menneskets Histologie [Human
Histology]. København 1879. p.~582: ``The more recent discoveries
concerning the structure of the nerve fibers have not taken us further
toward knowledge of the relation between them and the cells; for the
present, therefore, we must presumably hold fast to the view that the
nerve cells are centers for the nerve fibers, and continue to direct
histological investigations toward furnishing a satisfactory
morphological understanding of this physiological fact.'' --- See also
\emph{Wundt}: Physiologische Psychologie. I, p.~35.} the physical
properties of the ganglion cells themselves, and with them the physical
origin of the simplest reflex movement, are not yet understood; it is
not even quite certain that it is the ganglion cells that form the
intermediate link between the ingoing and the outgoing nerve fiber. Nor
has it been possible to demonstrate the anatomical connection between
the centers of the centripetal and the centrifugal nerves in the spinal
cord.\footnote{C. \emph{Lange}: Rygmarvens Patologi [Pathology of the
Spinal Cord]. p.~24. --- \emph{Eckhard}: Physiologie des Rückenmarks und
des Gehirns. (Hermann. II, 2). p.~7--19. 61 ff.} But in spite
% --- p. 70 ---
\opage{70}of all this physiology cannot let its boundary be staked out
for it from without. Its fundamental thought is the thoroughgoing
coherence in organic life; the most intricate processes it seeks to
explain by tracing them back to the simpler; it learns from the lower
phenomena how the higher are to be understood, since the principles of
structure and activity are the same throughout. Thus it is, for
example, the doctrine of reflex movements that for physiology throws
light on the way in which the highest functions of the brain are to be
explained,\footnote{After Marshall \emph{Hall} had set up the theory of
reflex movement in 1833, \emph{Laycock} showed in 1840 that its
principles must also find application in the physiology of the brain.
(On the reflex action of the brain. --- The British and Foreign Medical
Review 1845). Apparently independently of him, \emph{Griesinger}
expressed the same thought in his treatise »Ueber psychische
Reflexactionen« (Archiv für physiologische Heilkunde. 1843).} without
its therefore being right, without further ado, to say that all activity
of the brain is reflex activity. In so far as one can speak of available
and settled results in the physiology of the brain, it presents to us
the picture of the brain as a republic of nerve centers, each with its
function and in mutual interaction; but there is no trace of the
physiological \emph{process} anywhere being able to cease in order to
pass over into a \emph{process} of a wholly different kind.

The usual doctrine of interaction (the doctrine of \textit{influxus
physicus}, as it was called in older times) appears in a stricter, more
metaphysical form and in a vaguer and more indefinite one. In its
metaphysical form it appeared in Descartes, who conceived soul and body
as two wholly heterogeneous and yet mutually influencing substances.
Here, then, that hasty fixing of the soul as an independent substance
which was mentioned earlier has been carried out, whereby the standpoint
of the psychology of experience has been exchanged for metaphysics. Yet
precisely the sharp and clear form in which Descartes presented the
current doctrine has contributed extraordinarily to laying bare its weak
sides. It falls as soon as one tries to present it in its full
consequence. Descartes therefore has the merit of having set up the
problem of soul and body. For to the current view in its vaguer form
there exists
% --- p. 71 ---
\opage{71}no difficulty in this relation. With justified
thoughtlessness practical usage disregards the theoretical difficulties.
Just as little as the current usage of language respects Copernicus's
\emph{doubt} whether the sun really moves around the earth, just so
little does it take account of the fact that physiology and psychology
shrink from letting brain and consciousness influence one another. In
addition, usage has been formed under the influence of a metaphysics
partly spiritualistic, partly materialistic.

\textbf{b.} An end is made of this inconsistency and vagueness when one
simply strikes out one of the terms that are to be connected. Now since
the intuition of the outer, material world plays an overwhelming part in
the common circle of ideas, while inner self-consciousness is not easily
worked out to the same degree of clarity and distinctness, it lies
perhaps nearest to hand to make the material one with the real, and to
conceive the spiritual as a form or an effect of it. Historically,
materialism is no doubt also older than the current doctrine of
interaction. Homer and the oldest Greek philosophers (before Socrates and
Plato) are materialists; even among the Christian Fathers before
Augustine materialistic ideas prevailed. In modern times materialism has
its firm standing ground in the law of the conservation of energy and in
the doctrine of physiological coherence. It is fully justified against
every idealistic line of thought that leads to setting external limits
to the series of physical and physiological causes. As a
natural-scientific method materialism is unassailable. It is another
matter when the method is rewritten into a system. It is fully entitled
to regard every change in and function of the organism, and especially
of the brain, as material; but as a metaphysical theory it goes further
and maintains that the phenomena of consciousness are only changes in or
functions of the brain, and in this consists its encroachment.

\emph{Carl Vogt} in his day aroused great offense by declaring (in his
``Physiologische Briefe'') that ``in the same way as contraction is the
function of the muscle, and as the kidneys secrete urine, in the same
way the brain produces thoughts, movements and feelings.'' He could
thereby seem to give a choice between two ideas: either to conceive
thought
% --- p. 72 ---
\opage{72}as a substance or as a motion. The first way of thinking,
which lies nearest to immediate apprehension, and to which ancient
materialism therefore especially kept, has, however, for any closer
reflection something so baroque about it that it needs no further
discussion. Nor, indeed, was it probably Vogt's meaning; the chief
weight must be laid on the \emph{activity of secretion}, not on the
product. But in principle nothing is thereby altered. Even among
sober-minded physiologists, some of them philosophically trained, one
does sometimes meet the proposition that the activity of consciousness
is a function of the brain. It seems, however, as if precisely the
strictly physiological use of the expression function must conflict
with such a proposition. That, for example, contraction is the function
of the muscle means only that it is a certain form and state of the
muscle in motion. As \emph{Goethe} has said: »Function ist Dasein in
Thätigkeit gedacht«. The muscle in function is just as material as the
muscle at rest, and what does not have the properties of matter cannot
be the form of activity of anything that is material. The concept of
function
% footnote *) on p. 72
(in the physiological sense)\footnote{In the mathematical sense, on the
other hand, it can with full right be said that consciousness is a
function of the brain, since experience shows us a certain
proportionality between the degrees of development of consciousness and
of the brain. The activity of consciousness would then, strictly, have
to be said to be a mathematical function of the physiological function
of the brain.} points, just as well as the concept of substance or
product, to something that appears to us as an object of intuition in
the form of space. But thought and feeling cannot be pictured as
objects or motions in space. We come to know them, not by outer
intuition, but through self-sensation and self-consciousness --- a
source to which the physiologist too, without always being clearly
conscious of it, has recourse when he wishes to investigate the
relation of the life of consciousness to organic life. It is by many
detours that it is discovered that certain definite phenomena of
consciousness are bound to the function of certain definite parts of the
brain. There is no doubt that even the very highest activities of
consciousness have their corresponding functions of the brain --- just
as even the most beautiful melodies are not too exalted to be expressed
in notes. But the activity of consciousness and the function of the
brain we always come
% --- p. 73 ---
\opage{73}to know by using different sources of experience. The
encroachment of materialism consists in simply drawing a line through
this essential difference. By simply ascribing to the brain a capacity
for consciousness, or perhaps even making the brain the subject of the
% footnote *) on p. 73
manifestations of consciousness,\footnote{Ch. \emph{Robin}, for
example, defines »sensibilité« thus (Anat.\ et physiol.\ cellul.
p.~540): »Ce mode de la névrilité est caractérisé par ce fait, que
\emph{les éléments} nerveux qui en jouissent, après avoir reçu une
impression du dehors, la transmettent de ce point à un autre où ils
[sic] la \emph{perçoivent}.« A similar mode of expression occurs in
\emph{Broussais}. But what idea can really be connected with nerve
elements observing or perceiving something?} it goes back at bottom to
a mythological, fantastic standpoint.

We have regard here especially to \emph{empirical} or
\emph{phenomenological} materialism, i.e.\ the view that regards it as a
direct result of the science of experience that the phenomena of
consciousness are a form or an effect of the material phenomena, so
that all reality can be traced back to motion in space. We thereby move
not only in the domain in which we ourselves would most like to move,
but also in that in which materialism has always believed itself to
move. It has not noticed that even if it were right in all its
assertions, there would still be something that it always overlooked,
and by which a new problem, and for it a terrible one, arose: namely the
circumstance that motion in space is known to us only as an object of
our consciousness. \emph{Epistemologically}, then, concepts such as
consciousness, idea and intuition lie deeper than concepts such as
matter and motion. An absolute and resolute materialism was therefore
possible only in antiquity, before the awakening of more penetrating
philosophical reflection. \emph{Democritus} is the only consistent
materialist. None of the modern materialistic writers can speak with a
calm and sureness like that with which \emph{Lucretius} in his majestic
verses sets forth the doctrine of Democritus. The moderns for the most
part concede that even if we can trace everything back to matter, we
still do not know what matter is in itself. So La \emph{Mettrie},
\emph{Holbach}, \emph{Cabanis}, not to speak of the
% --- p. 74 ---
\opage{74}wildly fluttering inconsistencies in the writers of the most
recent time (\emph{Büchner}, \emph{Moleschott}).\footnote{Cf.\ my work
\textit{Filosofien i Tyskland efter Hegel} [Philosophy in Germany after
Hegel]. Kbhvn. 1872. p.~94--117.}

What we have here urged against materialism is not, however, the
\emph{metaphysical} and \emph{epistemological} inconsistency that it
commits in demanding that consciousness should acknowledge as the
absolutely original and alone real something that is given only as an
object for consciousness, and that can be imagined and known only by
consciousness's own activity. Our task here, on the contrary, is always
merely that of finding which view the given itself drives us toward;
and the result of our critique of materialism is that it already sins
against the very concepts of experience.

\textbf{c.} When, as the third possible hypothesis, we discuss
spiritualism, we must constantly hold fast the difference between a
\emph{phenomenological} and a \emph{metaphysical} mode of consideration.
A very great many obscurities regarding our problem stem from this
difference being overlooked. Like materialism, spiritualism has almost
always mixed together metaphysical and \emph{empirical} results.
Spiritualism is the view according to which the spiritual is the only
reality; everything material, all motion in space, is only an outer
and, in the last instance, illusory form of a spiritual life. It relies
on the impossibility of explaining the spiritual from the material, and
on the fact, always partly overlooked and partly underrated by
materialism, that our concept of matter is a spiritual product, and that
we do not know what matter is apart from our concept of it. The
spiritual is thus a \textit{prius}, a presupposition on which all
thinking rests; a reasonable hypothesis results only when we trace the
less known back to the better known. The spiritual is really the only
thing that is fully intelligible to us, where we have not merely a
knowledge of external circumstances and relations (\textit{cognitio
circa rem}), but also a knowledge of the thing itself (\textit{cognitio
rei}).

Even if spiritualism is right in all this, it nevertheless does not
belong here. For granted that everything is at bottom spiritual, and
that nothing exists but thoughts and
% --- p. 75 ---
\opage{75}ideas: there still always remains a difference between
\emph{ideas of material motion} and \emph{ideas of phenomena of
consciousness}, and the problem then arises again of how these
different ideas, which have arisen in experience, are to be combined.
In other words, the empirical problem is independent of the
metaphysical one. We do not discuss here whether spirit or matter is
what lies at the ground, the truly real; we investigate how the
spiritual and the material phenomena are connected in the experience
that every metaphysics, consciously or unconsciously, presupposes.

The foremost representative of spiritualism in the philosophy of our
time, \emph{Hermann Lotze}, has with great clarity impressed upon us the
difference between the phenomenological (or, as he called it in his
earlier writings, the occasionalistic) inquiry and the metaphysical
one.\footnote{Cf.\ \textit{Filosofien i Tyskland efter Hegel}.
p.~293--295; 299--302.} But he has not held fast to this difference,
but on the contrary has pushed it aside in an arbitrary way.

Lotze is one of the writers who in recent times have most energetically
maintained the mechanical conception of organic phenomena, and have
shown the concept of mechanism to be the one called to govern our whole
conception of nature. All the more remarkable is it that he refuses to
concede the full consequence of the law of the conservation of energy.
``The physicists,'' he says in one of his earlier writings (Allg.\
Physiol.\ p.~461), ``seem convinced that every motion of masses in space
and time can be annihilated only by another equally great and opposite
one, and from this the task presented itself of following every motion,
once it has arisen, through all its changes of form, right up to its
final passage from the organism to the surrounding world, or to its
cancellation by opposite motions. I am not fully convinced of the
unconditional correctness of this principle; on the other hand, from my
view of the philosophy of nature I cannot meet with any conclusive
counter-argument the possibility that spatial motions may be absorbed by
passing over into intensive states.'' At the point where this transition
from the spatial to the non-spatial world takes place we must,
according to a later statement of his (Mikrokosmus. I. 2 Aufl.\
p.~326), seek the seat of the soul. In his most recent work (Drei
Bücher
% --- p. 76 ---
\opage{76}der Metaphysik), the last that it was granted to this
brilliant researcher to give us, he expresses himself more precisely on
his position regarding the law of the conservation of energy. He lays
the weight on the point that this law in itself states only an
equivalence between the manifestations of force, promises us only a
certain replacement for the energy that ceases, but says nothing about
what kind this energy is. He therefore does not think it impossible to
apply it to a case where a spiritual energy takes the place of a
physical one, or conversely. Phenomenologically, then, he takes his
stand on the standpoint of the natural doctrine of interaction, and to
that extent his theory falls before the objections directed against
that doctrine (see above, p.~67--68). But he does not remain standing
on the phenomenological standpoint. The distinction between spiritual
and material phenomena is for him only the point of departure; the
spiritual is --- as he endeavors to show by metaphysical inquiries ---
the only reality, the material phenomena only the manifestation of the
interaction of spiritual elements; and a transition from the spiritual
that underlies the material phenomena to that which appears in the
phenomena of consciousness entails no theoretical difficulties: the
heterogeneity, the want of a common measure, has fallen away through
metaphysical reflection.

Lotze really always evades the question whether a transition actually
takes place. He builds his metaphysics on the assumption of such a
transition, as though experience immediately offered one. The form in
which spiritualism appears in him is thus bound up with --- not to say
encumbered with --- an assumption which a consideration based on the
analogy of our experience must reject. The idealistic view of life is in
principle independent of the form that Lotze has given it. It can very
well be combined with other phenomenological assumptions than those its
eminent philosophical champion has taken as his basis.

\textbf{d.} There then remains only the fourth possibility. If a
transition from the one domain to the other conflicts with the law of
the conservation of physical energy, and if the two domains nevertheless
stand in our experience as different, they must unfold, each according
to its own laws, simultaneously with each other, so that to every
phenomenon within the world of consciousness
% --- p. 77 ---
\opage{77}there is a corresponding one within the world of matter, and
conversely (so far as there is reason to assume a life of consciousness
connected with the material phenomena). The parallels we have drawn
earlier point directly to such a relation; it would be a strange
coincidence if the marks should thus repeat themselves were there no
inner connection lying at the ground. Like the \emph{parallelism}, the
\emph{proportionality} between the activity of consciousness and the
activity of the brain also points to an underlying \emph{identity}. The
difference that nevertheless remains in spite of the agreements compels
us to assume that one and the same principle has found its expression in
a twofold form. We have no right to assume soul and body to be two
different substances in mutual interaction. But we are led to conceive
the material interaction between the elements of which the brain and
the nervous system consist as an outer form of the inner, ideal unity in
consciousness. What we become conscious of in our inner experience as
thought, feeling and resolution thus has, within the material world,
its representation in certain material processes in the brain, which as
such come under the law of the conservation of energy, whereas this law
can find no application to the relation itself between the processes of
the brain and the processes of consciousness. It is as if one and the
same content were expressed in two languages.

Only experience can decide whether the two forms really extend equally
far. We have already touched on the difficulty of fixing the limits of
the life of consciousness downward; the following section will give us
occasion to take up this question again. On the other hand there are
still those who think that the noblest spiritual manifestations of life
are not bound to material processes. That sense-perception and
so-called physical pleasure and pain are bound to certain nerve
processes no one will deny; it is only in the case of the higher
phenomena of consciousness that it is considered necessary to assume a
wholly new principle. But already the general characterization of the
life of consciousness given above leads us to see the impossibility of
drawing any boundary line between a lower and a higher within
consciousness. The same type prevails from the simplest to the highest
forms. However high the ideal world of thoughts and feelings
% --- p. 78 ---
\opage{78}seems raised above the series of single, momentary sensations,
it is nevertheless the same principle that prevails in both; it is only
the degree of development that is different, not the plan of
construction or the building material. The more special psychological
inquiries that follow will show this more closely.

Just as little as a sharp boundary can be drawn between a lower
(``sensuous'') and a higher (``spiritual'') content, each with its own
conditions of existence, just so little is it justified to regard the
material or content of consciousness as bound to physical processes,
while the forming and elaborating activity of the spirit was to have no
physical parallel. Already in the simplest observation, right at the
threshold of consciousness, we find the result of an activity of the
spirit, a connection of elements into a unity, a synthesis. An absolutely
single state would not be conscious. At no point, therefore, can matter
and form be separated. The physiological coherence and interaction
between the extraordinarily numerous centers in the brain furnishes,
moreover, a sufficient basis for the assumption that not only the
spiritual elements but also their connections have their physical
expression.

Both within the spiritual and within the material world we hold fast to
the law of continuity. The parallel theory, or \emph{identity theory},
sees the two worlds as two expressions, given in experience, of one and
the same being. They are two languages in which the same thought is
expressed, but whose roots we are unable to trace back to any common
root. Moreover --- so long as we keep strictly to experience --- the one
domain stands before us as a fragment, while the other extends into the
infinite and rounds itself off into a closed whole. The law of the
conservation of energy makes the material world a totality which we can
never, to be sure, measure out, but within which we can in principle
follow the fate of the single forms and elements. The spiritual world
has no corresponding law to show. The spiritual elements arise and
disappear in experience without any equivalent being demonstrable that
is consumed in the first case and takes their place in the second. The
mere circumstance that spiritual states cannot be measured as physical
energies and chemical substances can is enough to destroy the hope of
finding a spiritual parallel
% --- p. 79 ---
\opage{79}to the law of the conservation of force. But in addition to
this, the very fundamental concept of spiritual existence brings
difficulties with it here. Material existences can pass over into one
another, in such a way that all the energy that is lost with the one is
preserved in the others. The law of the conservation of energy shows us
the unity and eternity of nature beneath the arising and perishing of
all material beings. But spiritual existence has, as we have seen,
memory, synthesis, for its fundamental form; --- and synthesis
presupposes \emph{individuality}. The material world shows us no real
individualities; these are first known to the psychological point of
view, from which inner centers of memory, of acting and of suffering are
discovered. Now if we were to imagine that the single spiritual elements
(sensations, thoughts, feelings, etc.) were converted into other
combinations, like the chemical atoms, we should be conceding that they
could have an existence outside an individual consciousness, which
according to the foregoing is meaningless. Sensations, thoughts and
feelings are spiritual activities, which cannot subsist when the
individual connection in which they occur has ceased. They correspond to
the organic functions, not to the chemical elements. When the organism
is dissolved into its elements, the organic function is impossible. The
spiritual individuality has its physical expression in the sum of
energy that the organism commands in its germ and in the course of its
development, and in the organic (especially neurophysiological) form
under which this energy finds application. Even the most spiritualistic
view must concede that here something is given which can indeed be
altered, but not wholly created, by the later development.

The theory to which we have here been led is no complete solution of the
problem of the relation between soul and body. It is only an
\emph{empirical formula}, a designation of how the relation provisionally
stands when, following the pointers of experience, we attend at once to
the close connection between the spiritual and the material, and to the
impossibility of tracing the one back to the other, and of assuming a
transition from the one to the other. Of the inner relation itself
between spirit and matter we learn nothing; we know only that one being
works in both. But which is this being? Why has it a twofold form of
manifestation, why is one not enough? These are
% --- p. 80 ---
\opage{80}questions that lie outside the domain of our knowledge. Spirit
and matter stand before us as an irreducible duality, like subject and
object. But the spiritual does not on that account become a superfluous
addition. On the contrary, from a psychological standpoint --- which is,
after all, the one from which we finally judge existence --- we can find
meaning and value in the world only in this, that the same thing which
works, spreads and forms itself in the outer world of matter opens
itself in its inner being to itself as thinking, feeling and willing.
All determination of value is bound to the spiritual, and has the
material for its object only indirectly. Even when we admire the play of
masses striking one another in space, what we really admire is the
reflected image of a connection of thought that the lawfulness of
physics shows us. When we ask about the inner connection between the
physical and the spiritual world, we stand at the limit of our
knowledge, and no view whatever has yet answered the question of the
necessity of spiritual life in existence except by a teleological
postulate, whose justification it is not the business of psychology to
discuss. Only so much is certain, that if we are right in assuming that
duality in the world of phenomena, it must also have its ground. It is
not the only place in our experience where we are compelled to assume a
connection that actually subsists without being able to demonstrate its
necessity.

The empirical formula with which we here conclude does not exclude a
further-reaching metaphysical hypothesis. The fundamental thought of
idealism, of the spiritual as the true essence of existence, can thus
very well be reconciled with the empirical assumption of the parallel
theory. For this theory, as an empirical formula, says nothing whatever
about the two forms of existence being absolute or possessing validity
when we disregard the human point of view. It was a hasty metaphysics
when \emph{Spinoza} taught that spirit and matter were two equally
eternal and infinite attributes of the absolute substance. We do not
know the absolute substance, and so cannot know whether spirit and
matter are equally essential to it. On the contrary, epistemology leads
(as was indicated under c) to regarding the phenomena of consciousness
as the most fundamental facts in our experience, since the subjective
point of view always lies deeper than the objective.


% --- p. 81 ---
\opage{81}It can therefore easily lead to misunderstandings when the
identity or parallel hypothesis is designated as neo-Spinozism. It is,
to be sure, bound up with Spinoza's name; it is he who has the honor of
having first set up this theory and thereby led beyond the contending
materialistic and spiritualistic theories. He was guided in this by two
different motives. On the one hand he wished to remove all imperfect
ideas from the idea of the infinite being; there was to be nothing
outside it, nothing that it did not pervade. But besides this
religious-philosophical or metaphysical motive, the conviction of the
unbroken coherence of the physical series of causes also made itself
felt. If the spiritual activities cannot intervene in this series of
causes, there is nothing for it but to assume that the spiritual and the
material activity do not succeed each other, but go on simultaneously
(\textit{simul naturā}. Eth.\ III, 2. Schol.), especially since they
cannot be traced back to any common measure. Spinoza anticipated in a
brilliant way the triumphal progress of the mechanical conception of
nature; he saw that principles had been set up in Galileo and Descartes
under which the whole of material nature must be brought. It is
therefore no wonder that the attention of many researchers, now that the
law of the conservation of energy passes one empirical test after
another, has also turned to this profound thinker. But from the nature
of the case it is the experiential rather than the speculative side of
his doctrine that is here the most important
% footnote *) on p. 81
for us.\footnote{Cf.\ moreover my book \textit{Spinozas Liv og Lære}
[Spinoza's Life and Teaching]. Kbhvn. 1877.}

Spinoza's theory has really never disappeared from philosophy since it
was once set up. Even before the renaissance that Spinozism celebrated
at the end of the last century, \emph{Kant} had come by his own path to
the identity hypothesis. In certain ways Kant and Spinoza are
antipodes; what was for Spinoza the clearest of all, the absolute being
or the substance in all things, is according to Kant precisely what our
knowledge can never reach, ``the thing in itself.'' This contradiction,
however, points to a unity lying deeper. One need only put X in Spinoza
wherever he speaks of substance, and one has Kant's doctrine. Kant
teaches, quite like
% --- p. 82 ---
\opage{82}Spinoza, that we have no right to regard spirit and matter as
two different substances. The duality is only empirical or phenomenal
and does not exclude a unity in essence.\footnote{Kant hardly knew
Spinoza's theory at first hand. At least he does not mention it at all.
He reproaches the earlier theories with having regarded matter as a
``thing in itself'' instead of regarding it as phenomenon. This
criticism does not strike Spinoza, for whom matter was not substance
(fundamental being) but attribute (fundamental form). --- For the rest,
Kant himself declares, in spite of the subjectivistic turn he gives the
matter, that ``in all problems that can occur in the domain of
experience we treat those phenomena [soul and body] as objects in and
for themselves, without troubling ourselves about the first ground of
their possibility'' (Kritik der reinen Vernunft, Kehrbach's edition.
p.~330). This is precisely the point of view I have held fast with
regard to the relation between psychology and physiology on the one
side, and theory of knowledge and metaphysics on the other. See above,
74 ff.} Kant therefore excludes, just as Spinoza does, both
spiritualism and materialism. ``The ego, represented through the inner
sense in time, and objects in space, outside me, are indeed
specifically quite different phenomena, but they are not thereby
thought of as different things. The transcendental object which
underlies the outer phenomena, and likewise that which underlies the
inner intuition, is neither matter nor a thinking being in itself, but
a ground, unknown to us, of the phenomena that give us the empirical
concept of the first as well as of the second kind.'' ``Thus the very
thing that in one respect is called bodily would in another respect be
at the same time a thinking being, whose thoughts we cannot indeed
intuit in the phenomenon, but whose thoughts' expression we can
intuit. The expression that only souls (as particular kinds of
substances) think would thereby fall away; it would instead be said, as
in everyday speech, that men think, i.e.\ that the very thing which as
outer phenomenon is extended is in its inner being (in itself) a
subject, which is not composite, but is simple and
thinks.''\footnote{Kritik der reinen Vernunft. Kehrbach's edition p.~320
and 306. --- It is in the first edition that Kant speaks most fully and
most clearly about this question, in the section ``on the Paralogisms
of Pure Reason'' (the critique of rational psychology).}

For Kant the epistemological point of view stood in the foreground;
therefore he does not lay the weight on the principle of physical
% --- p. 83 ---
\opage{83}continuity, although he acknowledges it. In his successors,
too, we meet the identity hypothesis. But in them the
empirical-phenomenological way of looking at things was more and more
displaced by the speculative; the task then was no longer to formulate
the ultimate principles of empirical knowledge, but to deduce
everything from an absolute principle. In \emph{Schelling} and Hegel
the identity hypothesis passes over into a spiritualism that breaks off
the connection with the science of experience. Only in researchers who
worked in Kant's spirit and with sober regard for the given, such as
\emph{Fries}, and among us \emph{Treschow} and \emph{Sibbern}, do we
find the hypothesis clearly set forth. It obtained a firmer foundation
when the law of the conservation of energy received one empirical
confirmation after another. So far as I know, it is \emph{Fechner} who
in our time first saw the consequences of that law
% footnote *) on p. 83
for this problem.\footnote{In his psychophysical law for the relation
between physical impression and sense-sensation, according to which an
increase in the strength of the impression acts in proportion to the
impression already present (dγ = dβ/β), Fechner also saw a law for the
relation between soul and body, since he thought that a simple
proportionality probably obtains between nerve process and physical
impression. In the course of the discussion of Fechner's psychophysical
law it seems, however, to have become clear that \emph{in so far as}
Fechner's law has validity (see on this below, V A, 4), it holds for
the relation of the impression to the nerve process (Fechner's
``psychophysical motion''), whereas the relation itself between nerve
process and consciousness (body and soul) must be assumed to be one of
direct proportionality. As \emph{Hering}, Fechner's most important
critic, has remarked (Ueber Fechners psychophysisches Gesetz. ---
Wiener Sitzungsberichte. 1875. --- §~4--5), this also agrees best of
all with Fechner's own fundamental view. --- Cf.\ further on this
question: J. \emph{Ward}: An Attempt to interpret Fechner's Law.
(Mind. 1876). G. E. \emph{Müller}: Zur Grundlegung der Psychophysik.
Berlin 1878. p.~224--374.} Numerous psychologists and physiologists
have now come over to the identity hypothesis, by different paths and
with different degrees of clarity. It surely also gives the most
natural settlement of the boundary dispute between physiology and
% footnote **) on p. 83
psychology.\footnote{Dr.\ \emph{Kromann} has, in his work \textit{Den
exakte Videnskabs Lære om Sjælens Existents} [The Doctrine of Exact
Science concerning the Existence of the Soul] (Kbhvn 1877), given a
critique of the identity hypothesis (or, as he calls it, ``duplicism'').
But his objections do not hit the mark, chiefly for two reasons. In the
first place he confuses the empirical and the metaphysical point of
view, and in the second place he constantly imputes to the adherents of
the identity hypothesis the view that the material is what is really
real and the spiritual a powerless appendage to it. (See my review of
this work in \textit{Nordisk Tidsskrift} 1878).} Their object is one and
the same,
% --- p. 84 ---
\opage{84}only they approach it from different sides. They therefore
play into each other's hands and can make use of each other's results.
The contradictions between them must rest on misunderstandings and
errors. Every phenomenon of consciousness gives occasion both to a
psychological and to a physiological inquiry. The psychological and the
physiological point of view belong together just as closely, and are at
the same time just as different, as the convex and the concave side of
an arc of a circle. The identity hypothesis thus becomes fruitful for
special psychological research, since it enforces the necessity of
illuminating and correcting the psychological results by the facts of
physiology.


%% ============================================================
%%  III. THE CONSCIOUS AND THE UNCONSCIOUS (pp. 85--102)
%% ============================================================
% --- p. 85 ---
\chapter[The Conscious and the Unconscious]{The Conscious and the Unconscious\opage{85}}
\label{ch:conscious-unconscious}

\noindent\textbf{1.} In the characterization of the life of consciousness
given earlier, weight was laid on two main features: the occurrence of a
change, whereby new elements of consciousness arose, and the connection
among all the elements of consciousness. If this characterization is
correct, consciousness can cease for two reasons: either because the
individual elements do not possess strength enough to assert themselves,
or because the connection among them ceases.

So long as we hold firmly to the fact that we know the soul only through
the manifestations of consciousness, the domain of psychical life is of
but small extent. It is not all nerve processes to which we have reason
to think of consciousness as attached, and even the nerve processes with
which this is the case can also take place without consciousness when
their intensity is not great enough. A physical impression can thus act
upon the nervous system without any sense-sensation arising; the
sensation arises only at a certain definite strength of the impression.
The nerve process, on the other hand, must already be going on at lower
degrees of strength, so that when the sensation crosses the threshold of
consciousness, the nerve process has already reached a certain strength.
Let $x$, for example, denote the degree of strength of a nerve process
which is just strong enough that a just noticeable sensation, which we
will call $y$, corresponds to it. We then face the peculiar relation
that while the degrees of strength on the physical side decrease
continuously downward from $x$, the psychical side goes blank, stops
suddenly at $y$. This is how the relation presents itself, whatever
fundamental assumption regarding the relation
% --- p. 86 ---
\opage{86}between the psychical and the bodily we may start from. It is
the same with composition as with degree of strength; for there is only a
difference of degree between the structure and mode of operation of the
lower and of the higher nerve organs. Is it now reasonable that at a
certain step of the scale something should arise which did not exist at
all on the lower steps? When the series is continuous within the one
sphere, should it not also be so within the other? We have, after all,
no right anywhere in nature to assume leaps or gaps; at least the
progress of knowledge consists above all in the filling up and bridging
of intervals and chasms.

The question we here face is whether the unconscious can become anything
other than a purely negative concept. In everyday speech (and more than
is proper in scientific usage as well) we use expressions such as
unconscious sensations, unconscious ideas, unconscious feelings. But since
sensations, ideas and feelings are states of consciousness, the
expression is really meaningless. If by an unconscious idea is meant an
idea that I have, then the predicate ``unconscious'' says only that I do
not think of or attend to the fact that I have it. This use of the word
unconsciousness is connected with a double use of the word
consciousness. For this word is used not only to designate the inner
appearance of our sensations, ideas and feelings, but also
self-consciousness, the attention expressly directed toward our
sensations, ideas or feelings. We naturally \emph{have} many sensations
and ideas of which we are not \emph{conscious} that we \emph{have} them;
many feelings and impulses \emph{stir} in us without our clearly
\emph{apprehending their nature and direction}. In this sense one can,
e.g., speak of unconscious love; he who has this feeling does not know
what it is that stirs within him; others perhaps see it, or he discovers
it himself by degrees; but he has the feeling, his life of consciousness
is determined in a peculiar way.

When, on the other hand, we here wish to investigate the relation between
the conscious and the unconscious, we mean by unconsciousness a state
which lies below the threshold of our consciousness altogether (not
merely below that of our self-consciousness). We wish to investigate
whether the unconscious
% --- p. 87 ---
\opage{87}might not prove to be akin to the conscious, so that the
difference, on the psychical as well as on the physical side, is merely
a difference of degree. For the time being, however, we hold fast to its
being a purely negative concept. It is not our intention to follow
\emph{Eduard} v.~\emph{Hartmann} along the mystical paths he believes he
has cleared in his \textit{Philosophie der Unbewussten}. Hartmann not only
makes the unconscious without further ado into a positive concept, but
uses it as a principle of explanation wherever he thinks the physical
and psychological causal series gives out. --- Psychology proceeds safely
only when it keeps to the clear and certain phenomena and laws of
consciousness. But from this standpoint it now discovers precisely the
unconscious, and sees to its astonishment that psychological laws hold
sway beyond the domain of the life of consciousness as well. In what
follows we bring forward some examples to illustrate
% footnote *) on p. 87
this.\footnote{In \emph{Carpenter}: Mental physiology. p.~515 ff.\ a
long series of examples is to be found. Some of those adduced in what
follows are taken from his collection.}

\textbf{2.} Repeatedly, memory has been pointed to as the typical
phenomenon of consciousness. But memory presupposes that the elements of
consciousness change. What, then, becomes of the vanished ideas until
they are called up again? In everyday speech memory figures as a
storehouse or treasury where the ideas are kept until they are to be
used again. It is only in a figurative way that we can thus ascribe a
real existence to the ideas that have vanished from consciousness. But
the remarkable thing is that it looks as if they nevertheless play a part
in the actual activity of consciousness. When we want to call something
up in our memory and cannot hit upon it, it is a well-known remedy to
leave off searching for it and think of quite other things; the idea
sought may then suddenly emerge. Here we give up the conscious search and
let an altogether unconscious process take its place. A similar case is
when we have collected material for a piece of work, e.g.\ for the
treatment of a scientific question. We are then often so overwhelmed by
the particulars that we are unable to order and combine the material.
Here too, then,
% --- p. 88 ---
\opage{88}occupation with quite other things can be a good remedy. In the
midst of the new pursuit the right way of treating the question may then
suddenly, as if of itself, present itself to consciousness. The
unconscious working has accomplished what the conscious, direct and
strained labor perhaps would never have managed. Naturally one never
comes to such results in one's sleep; the strenuous labor is the
presupposition, the unconscious working puts the crown on the work.

Some psychologists explain such phenomena simply by the greater freshness
of brain and mind when one has ``slept on'' the matter. But this
explanation can fit only cases where one straightforwardly takes the
matter up anew after the interruption, not those where the interrupted
subject suddenly presents itself in full clarity to thought in the midst
of occupation with other things. Here the subject itself has undergone
further treatment since last time, but this treatment has taken place
below the threshold of consciousness; it has taken place in us, not by
us. And yet this unconscious labor bears the stamp of the same principles
and laws that govern conscious labor.

\textbf{3.} What thus holds for many of our thoughts holds --- as the
physiology of the senses has shown --- also for our apparently simple and
immediate sensations and perceptions. We form a coherent visual image,
although there is a point on the retina (the blind spot) which receives
no impression. Many of our sensations of color, which we take to be
immediate, are determined by the effect of contrast with other
\emph{colors}. The apprehension of space, which unfolds before us at a
single stroke, has arisen through the combination of various impressions.
Our two eyes do not have the same field of vision, and yet we believe we
immediately survey the binocular field of vision with both. The direction
of the line of sight we judge (according to Helmholtz) by the effort of
will with which we try to change the position of the eyes. But all these
combinations are not conscious to us, and when it has been said that our
sense-perceptions are the results of ``unconscious inferences,'' it must
be well noted that this expression is admissible only because we have no
other designations for these processes, known to us only from their
results, than such as are taken from the analogy with
% --- p. 89 ---
\opage{89}the higher life of consciousness.\footnote{\emph{Helmholtz}:
Physiologische Optik. p.~811. --- Later Helmholtz has avoided using this
expression on account of the abuse Schopenhauer and Hartmann have made of
it. He maintains, however, that we have here to do with an elementary
process that lies at the basis of all thinking properly so called.
»Die Thatsachen in der Wahrnehmung«. Berlin 1879. p.~27 f.} It is a kind
of inverted anthropomorphism that we here employ.

\textbf{4.} Not only can conscious results be formed from unconscious
preliminary work, but in the midst of conscious work unconscious
intermediate links may be found. When the idea $a$ is associated with the
idea $b$, and $b$ in turn with $c$, $a$ will at last be able to call up
$c$ directly without taking the way round by $b$. A proposition we have
learned to see by way of proof goes on living in our consciousness after
we have forgotten the proof. All education rests on intermediate links
being able to sink below the threshold in this way. The authority of the
educator is indispensable at first, but by degrees steps back. Often the
intermediate links are so numerous or hidden that they are not found, or
are found only with great difficulty. What leads us from the one thought
to the other we do not always become conscious of. Many psychological
paradoxes and sudden inspirations can find their explanation in this
unconscious motivation of conscious ideas.

\textbf{5.} Everything we call instinct, tact, self-acquired or inherited
disposition, works in this way. Habits and tendencies which we have
ourselves acquired or fallen into, or which we have received as an
inheritance from earlier generations, live on long after their causes
have fallen away. The ideas, feelings and actions to which these
tendencies lead do not find their full explanation in the life of
consciousness itself. There are always intermediate links that are
skipped, and which only physiological and sociological inquiry discovers.
The conscious motives have fallen away, but their effects remain.
Instinct has therefore been defined as a working toward ends of which one
is not conscious. Conscious intervention is determined in part by
unconscious motives and likewise leaves unconscious effects behind it.
Both in the individual and in nations sudden upheavals avail but little;
subterranean tendencies persist, which are subdued only late. That is why
the
% --- p. 90 ---
\opage{90}Israelites had to wander forty years in the desert.
\emph{Herodotus} relates (IV, 3--4) that while the Scythians were away on
distant campaigns, the slaves had married their women and seized
dominion; when the masters came back, they could not subdue by force of
arms the young generation sprung from these marriages, but brought them
to submission as soon as they cracked the whips with which the slaves
used to be punished. This story can at any rate serve as a poetic
expression of the power of inherited habits. --- In the lives of eminent
and pioneering men we often see how they must struggle to overcome what
childhood impressions and practice have implanted.

Every spiritual upheaval at first shakes only what moves in clear
consciousness; the unconscious undercurrents can often continue their
course for a long time without the movement propagating itself from the
surface down to them. The reaction after a revolution often reveals how
little deep the movement has gone. What has been consciously won by
struggle first becomes rooted when it works unconsciously, or, as one
% footnote *) on p. 90
says, passes into flesh and blood.\footnote{In H.~\emph{Bröchner's}
work »Om Udviklingsgangen i Filosofiens Historie« [On the Course of
Development in the History of Philosophy] (Kbhvn 1869) one will find
many interesting demonstrations of how ideas can unconsciously influence
the successors, and even the opponents, of the age that fostered them.
--- See also his essay in »Nyt dansk Maanedsskrift« [New Danish Monthly]
(1871) on the relation between the conscious and the unconscious.}
Conscious labor breaks the path; but after that the task is to get the
unconscious machinery going. Conversely, one can practice something
purely mechanically which then by degrees gains power over the life of
consciousness. Forced conversion can lead to living faith, if not in the
same generation then in later generations. Compulsion works against what
moves in clear consciousness; forced conversion therefore succeeds only
where there is no clear and developed life of consciousness
% footnote **) on p. 90
present.\footnote{Children's impulse to imitate is so strong that they
take pleasure in joining in movements and ceremonies they do not
understand. \emph{Egger} (Observations et réflexions sur le développement
de l’intelligence et du language chèz les enfants. Paris 1879) relates:
»Un enfant élèvé dans une maison religieuse donnait un jour cette
définition de la \emph{méditation}: On va à la chapelle, on se tient
comme cela, sans bouger, et on ne \emph{pense à rien}«.} But mechanical
practice can by degrees weaken consciousness.
% TR: "On va à la chapelle": the transcription runs "vaàla" together.
% --- p. 91 ---
\opage{91}We are, in \emph{Pascal's} expression, automaton as well as
spirit, for which reason Pascal advises beginning by taking holy water
and going through the customs, and the rest will surely come. That is his
notorious principle: \textit{Il faut s’abêtir!}

\textbf{6.} Simultaneously with a conscious activity an unconscious one
can go on. The spinner turns the wheel and draws out the thread while all
her thoughts are far away. He who reads aloud can be wholly absorbed in
the content, or perhaps even in other thoughts, while he sees the letters
and pronounces the corresponding words. In these examples the subordinate
action at any rate approaches the unconscious, and there is hardly any
question that the boundary can be crossed. And yet what has thus taken
place unconsciously can later make itself felt in consciousness.
\emph{Fechner} relates (Elemente der Psychophysik. II. p.~432) that one
morning in bed he was surprised that a white image of the stovepipe stood
before him when he closed his eyes. While he lay speculating with open
eyes, he had, without being conscious of it, looked at a black stovepipe
with a white wall as background, and it was the negative after-image of
this that now showed itself. The physical impression had thus occurred in
such a way that the visual sensation \emph{could} arise; it had remained
unconscious so long as attention was turned away, but could nevertheless
still come forward into consciousness without further ado. Likewise,
when we listen absent-mindedly to someone speaking to us, we may become
conscious of what he has said only long afterward. It is then only the
express turning of the mind that raises the unconsciously received
impressions above the threshold. That we can remember something is
therefore no decisive proof that we really apprehended it with
consciousness at the time. By its connection with what was consciously
apprehended, an unconscious impression too can be called up again in
memory.\footnote{\emph{Koch}: Vom Bewusstsein in Zuständen sogenannter
Bewusstlosigkeit. Stuttgart 1877. p.~19: ``Memory can be present after
preceding real unconsciousness when, e.g., of a series of processes that
have not come to consciousness, the last link reaches into the conscious
state, and now, starting from this, the earlier links that ran their
course unconsciously are also raised into consciousness by reproduction;
or when at some later time, during the conscious state, a brain state
sets in which has a certain kinship with the one that released processes
in the unconscious state, so that now, starting from it, the same
processes can be reproduced as conscious, as memory.''} To this belongs
also the well-known
% --- p. 92 ---
\opage{92}fact that warriors in the heat of battle do not notice even very
serious wounds, but only later are reminded by the pain of how things
have gone with them. This was already known in antiquity. ``It is
related,'' says Lucretius (De rerum natura III, 641 ff.), ``that
scythe-bearing chariots cut off limbs so swiftly that one sees the
severed part writhe on the ground before the consciousness, or the man
himself, has time to feel the pain.''

\textbf{7.} In the development of the feelings unconscious impressions
play an especially large part. This development is determined not only by clear and
definite sensations and ideas, but also by imperceptible influences
whose sum first makes itself felt in consciousness. Hence the mysterious
and unexplained in the nature of so many feelings; in their first
stirrings especially they are incomprehensible to the individual himself,
since he does not know the definite causes. The vital feeling is due to
the action of the organic functions on the brain; but the individual
impressions do not stand out clearly here, they merely unite in producing
a dim and shifting fundamental mood of well-being or ill-being. The
feeling of love, especially in its first dawning, has a mysterious
character through the awakening of uncomprehended organic instincts and
their influence on the vital feeling and on fantasy. But in other
feelings too there is something of the same, inasmuch as we never become
fully conscious of the influence of our experiences and circumstances of
life on our life of feeling until the feeling takes on a pronounced
character, or perhaps even breaks out in action. It is with such
influences as with the air we breathe in without thinking of it. A quiet
growth thereby goes on in us, often the most important and most decisive
thing in our spiritual life. We are here referred back to the general
presupposition of the life of consciousness and of the nerve process,
that only a more or less sudden change rouses them to activity. A slowly
rising change in heat or electricity can cause the death of a frog
without its stirring from the spot.


% --- p. 93 ---
\opage{93}The inner coherence in the history of the single human being
and of the whole race is preserved by these unconscious increments, whose
results determine so large a part of the content and energy of the life
of consciousness. It is only when we keep to the pronounced states of
consciousness that there seem to be sharp boundaries and sudden
revolutions; in the depths the infinitely ramified transitions are
discovered. Thus the coral animals always build beneath the surface of
the sea, and their building is first discovered when it rises above the
level of the sea.

It is Leibnitz who first, in a brilliant way, drew attention to the
significance of infinitely small elements in psychology (just as in
mathematics and in physics). He has moreover brought this doctrine into
connection with the law of continuity, which he so energetically
maintained. By means of the unconscious impressions (what he called
``petites perceptions'') he explains the connection between past, present
and future: ``En conséquence de ces petites perceptions le présent est
plein de l’avenir et chargé du
% footnote *) on p. 93
passé.''\footnote{Opera philosophica. Ed.\ Erdmann. p.~197.}

\textbf{8.} In the state of dreaming we have an intermediate stage between
the purely unconscious and the conscious state. The analogy that proves
to hold between dream consciousness and waking consciousness can to that
extent throw a light on the relation between the conscious and the
unconscious. Here too we see the same laws prevail, only farther down the
scale.

We are fond of setting sleep and waking over against each other as sharp
opposites. But just as within the waking state there are countless
degrees of energy, clearness and coherence in the life of consciousness,
so there are also many nuances of transition from waking to sleep, and
conversely, and within sleep, too, there are various degrees. The
following descending steps on the scale of psychical energy have thus
been distinguished: 1) reverie with half-slumber, 2) the light morning
sleep with lively dreams, 3) deep sleep with dim, indefinite dream
images, 4) the deepest sleep without dreams [?] and 5) in illness a still
lower degree, the soporous state or
% footnote **) on p. 93
fever stupor.\footnote{P.~\emph{Hedenius}: Om drömmen [On Dreams].
(Vetenskap för alla [Science for All]. Bd.~III. Stockholm 1880).
p.~609--611.} The deepest sleep
% --- p. 94 ---
\opage{94}we have just after having fallen asleep. Whether we always dream
is a question that has been answered differently. Those who assume it
appeal partly to metaphysical grounds, namely that the soul by its nature
cannot refrain from acting, partly to physiological grounds, namely that
movements must continually be going on in the brain and impressions
continually be received. In any case it is clear that the dream stands a
step nearer to waking life than dreamless sleep, if there is such a
thing.

The connection of dream consciousness with waking consciousness shows
itself, in the first place, in the fact that what is the object of waking
interest often occupies dream consciousness; difficulties and vexations
that were insurmountable in the waking state one overcomes, or imagines
one overcomes, in dreams. The elements out of which the dream world is
built up are, next, for the most part taken from the experiences of
waking life, although they are placed in new, often fantastic
combinations. Yet impressions are constantly received in sleep, not only
from the interior of the organism (from the respiratory and digestive
organs) but also from outside (impressions of touch, sound, light, etc.).
The connection with the surrounding world is thus not wholly broken off.
These impressions are now woven together with the after-effects of waking
life into a new world of ideas. But the formation of this world takes
place under other conditions than in the waking state. There is lacking
the firm concentration of attention and the many-sided control which
waking life calls forth or enforces. The individual impressions,
especially the vital sensations, thereby gain a power that pushes unity
and continuity aside. A free and bold interpretation of each individual
impression is the consequence. The dream takes on what has aptly been
called a mythological character. If the breathing is particularly light
and free, one believes one is flying; if it is labored, one is ridden by
the nightmare. If one comes to be cold through being uncovered, one is on
a North Pole expedition or walking naked in the street. A man who had a
hot-water bottle under his feet dreamed that he was walking on the crater
of Etna.

In the waking state too we interpret the individual impressions according
to their relation to our other experiences. Dream consciousness follows
the same method, but cannot get the better
% --- p. 95 ---
\opage{95}of the individual impressions; each sets its own stream of
thought in motion, which then for a moment dominates the whole of
consciousness, until it is replaced by the one roused by the next
impression. Here there is not power of resistance enough against the
individual elements. Hence the shifting and arbitrary character of the
dream, by which it comes to resemble insanity, which is indeed also a
state of dissolution. The shifting of the dream images is, as has
rightly been remarked\footnote{\emph{Delboeuf} in Revue philosophique.
Octobre 1879. p.~341.}, not ``transformation''; it is to be understood as
the emerging of one image after another.
% TR: the print has "phllosophique" (Delboeuf note); corrected.

The state of dreaming, then, shows us the psychological laws at work, but
below the threshold of consciousness proper. It is a station on the way
from the conscious to the unconscious life.

\textbf{9.} The act of waking up sometimes presents circumstances that
can throw a light on the relation between the conscious and the
unconscious. When one is woken, it is not always the physical strength of
the impression that tips the scale, but its relation to the weal and woe
of the individual, his interests in the waking state, \emph{what} the
physiologist
% footnote **) on p. 95
\emph{Burdach}\footnote{Physiologie III. p.~460. --- \emph{Carpenter} also
adduces a great many examples (§ 479--480).} has called the psychical
relation of the impression. An indifferent word does not wake one when it
is spoken softly; on the other hand the mother wakes at the child's
softest movement. A very miserly man was woken by having a coin put in
his hand. A naval officer who slept despite loud noise was woken on
hearing the word ``Signal!'' whispered. From such cases we are not
entitled to conclude with Burdach that ``the soul in sleep distinguishes
among the sense-perceptions''; they point, rather, to the fact that an
individual impression comes to consciousness only through its connection
with other experiences. It releases a whole series of effects in the
brain, and with that consciousness is given. The act of waking, which is
a transition from an at least relatively unconscious state to a conscious
one, takes place through the individual impression's acquiring, by
combination with others, the background it needs in order to become
conscious. It agrees with this that the life of consciousness proves to
be bound to very composite nerve organs, where many currents can meet.


% --- p. 96 ---
\opage{96}This circumstance could perhaps throw some light on a
psychological paradox mentioned earlier, namely that an altogether single
and isolated sensation cannot be conscious, and on the consequent
difficulty of imagining the beginning of consciousness. One could think
of this beginning as conditioned by a single impression's calling up
several elements of consciousness at once. Just as it seems that one can
remember what nonetheless happened unconsciously, so an impression would
be able to rouse consciousness by releasing foreground and background at
one stroke. It is not thereby to be said, however, that the origin of
consciousness can be thought of as a single, instantaneous act.

\textbf{10.} Despite the intimate connection and interaction between the
conscious and the unconscious, the unconscious nonetheless remains for us
a negative concept. The unconscious processes are brain processes just as
the conscious ones are; but whether they, like these, are something more,
we do not know. Instead of unconscious thinking or unconscious feeling it
would --- if one wishes to avoid all hypotheses --- be safest to speak,
with \emph{Carpenter} and \emph{Stuart Mill}, of unconscious brain
activity (unconscious cerebration), were this expression not unfortunate
in that, in the first place, it could give rise to the misunderstanding
that we ever become conscious of the brain activity itself, and, in the
second place, could seem to settle that unconscious activity has nothing
at all akin to what we know from ourselves as conscious states. Precisely
the impossibility of drawing a sharp boundary line between the conscious
and the unconscious, as well as the thoroughgoing analogy in mode of
operation and results, might perhaps make it a justified hypothesis to
maintain the law of continuity within the ideal world, just as everything
in the material world seems to obey it. To all the various material
phenomena of motion there would then correspond various degrees and forms
of the same thing that appears in us as thought and feeling. Just as the
organic world is built up of elements and by means of activities which
also appear in inorganic nature, but more scattered and without unity and
harmony, so we would have in the sensations, feelings and thoughts of
conscious beings higher forms of development of something which, in a
lower degree and form, occurred
% --- p. 97 ---
\opage{97}on the lower steps of nature. We would avoid the paradox that
the life of consciousness should begin wholly without preparation.
Leibnitz drew precisely this conclusion from the law of continuity:
\textit{Rien ne saurait naître tout d’un coup, la pensée non plus que le
mouvement.}\footnote{Op phil.\ ed.\ Erdmann. p.~226. --- Cf.\ already
\emph{Spinoza} Eth.\ II, 13. Schol. --- Among more recent psychologists
\emph{Beneke} must especially be singled out, who in the first volume of
his »Psychologische Skizzen« (Göttingen 1827) has investigated the
relation between the conscious and the unconscious in a clear and
penetrating way.} He set up an analogy between the relation of living
force to tension (potential energy) and the relation of the conscious to
the unconscious. Just as tension (potential energy) is living force that
has come into equilibrium, so unconsciousness could be resting or
neutralized consciousness. It would agree well with this that change, or
the disturbance of equilibrium, is so essential a condition of
consciousness. Just as there is no absolute rest in the external
universe, so --- one could then say --- there is no absolute
unconsciousness either. Unconsciousness would then be not a negation of
consciousness but a lower degree of it, lying in the continuation of the
descending
% footnote **) on p. 97
series of degrees of consciousness.\footnote{\emph{Zöllner}, in his
brilliant book »Ueber die Natur der Kometen« (1872), has taken up the
thought of Spinoza and Leibnitz. One will also find it in \emph{Häckel},
\emph{Nägeli} and other adherents of modern ``monism.'' ---
\emph{Virchow}, who polemicizes against this hypothesis in his address
»Die Freiheit der Wissenschaft« (Berlin 1877), nonetheless makes the
highly significant concession that \emph{if} one is determined to bring
the spiritual processes into connection with what goes on in the rest of
the world, one necessarily comes to extend the domain of psychical
phenomena far down below the domain of human consciousness --- to the
lowest animals, to plants, cells and atoms (p.~27). --- Monistic
speculation cannot be said to be unjustified if it really is the only
logically possible way in which connection can be established between
spiritual phenomena and the rest of the world.}
% TR: the print repeats "Indrømmelse" (concession) in the Virchow note.

Empirically, the life of consciousness appears bound to certain forms of
the functioning of the nervous system. But the nervous system itself has
arisen by differentiation out of homogeneous protoplasm; the properties of
the nervous system must then likewise be higher degrees of something
already attached to organic matter in general. (Cf.\ above p.~43--44).
% --- p. 98 ---
\opage{98}There is nothing here that justifies wholly new points of view
at a certain given step of material development. The nervous system is
only, so to speak, the highest flower of material existence; what
distinguishes it from other forms of material existence is only its
higher degree of development. For this reason recent physiologists (thus
\emph{Claude Bernard}) trace sensibility back to the irritability of
organic matter (the capacity to receive and respond to impressions).
Conscious reaction is thus only a higher form of unconscious reaction.
Bernard draws a proof of this view from the action of numbing substances
(\textit{anaesthetica}). When such substances (opium, chloroform, etc.)
abolish consciousness, it is because they act first and foremost on the
part of the organism most susceptible to impressions, and this is the
nervous system, the most differentiated organic matter. But with stronger
doses or more prolonged action the rest of the vital activity is by
degrees attacked as well. What is thus affected in the same way, only in
different degrees, by one and the same thing can also differ only in
degree. We thus ascend gradually from the lowest manifestations of life
to the highest vital activity, to which consciousness is
% footnote *) on p. 98
attached.\footnote{\emph{Bernard}: Leçons sur les phénomènes de la vie.
p.~280--290. --- See also his essay »La sensibilité dans le régne animal
et dans le régne végétal« (in the collection of Bernard's essays published
under the title »La science expérimentale«) as well as his »Rapport sur
les progrès et la marche de la physiologie générale en France.« Paris
1867. p.~180.}

\textbf{11.} One more important consideration must be made here. In the
foregoing, weight was laid on the law of the conservation of physical
energy as the law which expresses a magnificent connection in the world
of matter, but prevents the assumption of a causal relation between
matter and consciousness. But the law of the conservation of energy is
only the special, precise form the general law of causality takes in the
physical domain. The law of causality is satisfied when physical causes
have physical effects. Now consciousness nevertheless appears as a
surplus, which at certain points is added to these physical effects, as
an extra which cannot be explained from the physical causes. The famous
physiologist \emph{Dubois Reymond}
% --- p. 99 ---
\opage{99}has drawn from this, in his essay »Ueber die Grenzen des
Naturerkennens« (1872), the conclusion that spiritual phenomena stand
outside the law of causality and signify a breach of the principle of
sufficient reason. After all that has gone before we must grant him this,
so long as one takes up a purely \emph{deductive} standpoint and takes
the premises of the deduction from the fundamental laws of physical
mechanics. But we showed in the first chapter that the independence in
principle of psychology in relation to natural science (in the narrower
sense) is marked by its building not merely on consequences of physics
and physiology, but drawing on a new source of knowledge which is not
directly used by natural science, namely inner subjective observation.
If, then, experience is not exhausted with physical experience, we see
the necessity that one --- after having drawn the last consequences of
the fundamental laws of physical experience and seen that they do not
lead us to consciousness --- undertake a new \emph{inductive
investigation}, take up a \emph{new empirical starting point}. Two tasks
here present themselves: the one, to find a lawfulness within the world
of psychological experience itself; the other, to find the relation in
which psychological experiences must be placed to physical ones. The
first task we shall occupy ourselves with in the following chapters; the
second task we have treated in the preceding chapter. It proved
impossible there to insert the life of consciousness into intervals or
gaps in external nature, and we were led to conceive it as another form
of manifestation of the same principle that works in the material world.
Herewith Dubois Reymond's paradox falls away. For now there is the
possibility of a hypothetical assumption of an equally thoroughgoing
connection in the one domain as in the other. It is then only a
semblance when consciousness seems to arise from nothing, just as it is a
semblance that anything in external nature arises from nothing. The
apparent arising of consciousness is then only a transition from one
ideal form to another, just as every new material motion comes into
being by conversion from another form of motion.

Such a hypothesis must be taken for what it is. The unconscious is a
limiting concept in science; and where we stand at such a limit it may
have its significance to try, by way of
% --- p. 100 ---
\opage{100}hypothesis, to survey the possibilities that present themselves
as consequences of our knowledge; but a real extension of our factual
knowledge is here impossible. We proceed here like the philologist who
by conjectural criticism supplements a fragment of an ancient author. The
spiritual world stands --- compared with the physical world --- before us
as a fragment, and only by way of hypothesis is there any possibility of
supplementing it. Such a supplementation we have here indicated from a
purely psychological point of view. Ethical motives \emph{might perhaps}
be able to lead to further-reaching speculations, which would show us the
\emph{law} of the conservation of energy as the means and \emph{bearer of}
the conservation and unfolding of \emph{spiritual} values; but this
would take us beyond the domain we have here staked out for ourselves.

\textbf{12.} This fragmentary character in the position of psychological
phenomena within our empirical world-view entails that psychology will
never be able to become an exact science, as physics has become and
physiology is in the course of becoming. This difference between physics
and psychology \emph{Lotze} has clearly and aptly developed in the
following statement (Mikrokosmus. I. 2 Ausg. p.~218 f): ``The
difficulties of inner observation are increased by the fact that we
cannot lean on a general doctrine, certain in and of itself, of the
necessary interactions in which the states of every being must stand to
one another. Most of the fundamental features we notice in the relations
of spiritual life we can regard only as factual arrangements, whose value
for the higher development we frequently comprehend perfectly, without,
however, being able to show that precisely these forms of operation had
to be the necessary consequences in every spiritual being susceptible to
an indeterminate plurality of external impressions. One easily sees how
unfavorable this order of things is to what belongs to an explanation.
When we are referred to a collection of empirical facts, we may not go
beyond what experience itself tells us; could we, on the other hand,
follow the same facts in their necessary issuing from the nature of the
soul, we would easily be able to give them a stricter and deeper
expression, which would open to us access to a multitude of inferences
now inadmissible. These difficulties one is inclined to rate lower than
they deserve; spoiled by the results of natural science,
% --- p. 101 ---
\opage{101}one is often accustomed to regard propositions that possess
undisputed validity in the explanation of physical processes as general
and necessary truths, and forgets on that account that all unprejudiced
observation of the inner life presents us with quite other forms of
becoming and acting, forms that can scarcely be compared with natural
phenomena. With regard to the motion of matter we possess a sum of
scientifically exact laws, with regard to the manifestations of the soul
a number of empirical views; but there is still lacking the third and
higher thing we should need: a general doctrine that could show the laws
by which the states of beings in general are governed, and from which the
science of the spiritual life could issue as two different
applications.''

The inner imperfection of psychology is connected, as is here rightly
said, with our inability to specify a principle to which both our
psychological and our physical knowledge could be traced back.
Philosophical speculation has sought such a principle, from which both
the world of spirit and that of matter should be derivable. Such a
principle \emph{Spinoza} found in ``Substance,'' the infinite ground-being,
\emph{Schelling} in ``the absolute identity,'' \emph{Hegel} in ``the
absolute Idea.'' But it has always turned out that every such principle
was only apparently of a positive nature, and that the deduction rested
on unjustified presuppositions. (Cf.\ above p.~81). Starting from
experience, we are indeed led to formulate such a goal, but see at the
same time that it cannot be reached.

But although we cannot reach an exact deductive psychology, let alone a
higher knowledge from which psychology as well as physics could be
derived, we can nevertheless by way of induction demonstrate significant
laws which in fact hold both for the life of consciousness and for
external nature. It is \emph{Herbert Spencer's} merit to have been the
first to carry through this
% footnote *) on p. 101
point of view.\footnote{First Principles. (First edition appeared 1861).
Principles of Biology (1864). Principles of Psychology. (First edition
1855).} He has shown that all the phenomena we know, in the domain of
inner as well as of outer experience, are subject to development, and has
attempted to set up common laws for all development. In all domains
