development consists in the transition from an
% --- p. 102 ---
\opage{102}incoherent, indefinite and homogeneous state to coherence,
% footnote *) on p. 102
definiteness and heterogeneity.\footnote{See »Den engelske Filosofi i
vor Tid« [English Philosophy in Our Time]. p.~137--139. --- Among us,
\emph{Sibbern} has laid particular weight on the fact that all
development takes place sporadically from various starting points and
only by degrees leads to coherence, and he has demonstrated this in a very
fine way as regards psychical life in an essay in »Filosofisk Arkiv«
[Philosophical Archive] (1830). p.~263.} By these common laws and forms
psychical life shows itself, from a new side, closely bound up with the
general life of the world; its paths are interwoven with it. Here we will
merely point out that progressive individualization can be named as the
common feature of development in all its forms. Everywhere in nature
smaller wholes form themselves within the great, infinite whole, each with
its own peculiar interplay with the surrounding world.\footnote{See my
work »Om Grundlaget for den humane Etik« [On the Foundation of Humane
Ethics]. (Kbhvn. 1876). p.~110--112.} An individual is a being so
separated from and independent of its surroundings that it can react upon
them with a certain peculiarity of its own. But fully pronounced, as
already touched on earlier, we find individuality only in the domain of
the life of consciousness, where inner centers of suffering and acting
are given. This world-law thus receives its most striking expression in
the spiritual domain --- as a kind of compensation for the
fact that the more elementary law of the conservation of energy cannot
be demonstrated there. If these two laws could be brought into inner
harmony or reduced to a more deeply lying principle, all riddles would be
% footnote ***) on p. 102
solved.\footnote{On the difficulty that lies in the idea of an analogue
to the conservation of force in the spiritual domain, see above
p.~78--79.}

%% ============================================================
%%  IV. THE CLASSIFICATION OF PSYCHOLOGICAL PHENOMENA (pp. 103--119)
%% ============================================================
% --- p. 103 ---
\chapter[The Classification of Psychological Phenomena]{The Classification of Psychological Phenomena\opage{103}}
\label{ch:classification}

\noindent\textbf{1.} The preceding sections have been occupied with the
relation of psychological phenomena to other domains. Before we enter on
an investigation of special psychological relations, it will be useful to
cast a glance at the life of consciousness in its whole extent and at the
main differences that make themselves felt there. We thereby expose
ourselves less to losing sight of the whole for the particulars. Here, to
be sure, as with the psychological developments and results used in the
preceding sections, we must take much for granted and conceded which only
the special investigation can establish. But we gain the advantage that
the exposition can proceed from outside inward and from the main features
to the particulars. Add to this that the special psychological
investigations presuppose an abstraction from the great, living
connection in which every psychical fact arises and subsists; all the
more necessary is it, then, first to establish the totality out of which
psychological abstraction singles out particular elements.

It has not always been clearly recognized that psychological
distinctions and concepts have an abstract character. Reflection early
discovered various elements in the conscious states, but was inclined to
fix them as independent divisions or faculties of the soul, lying outside
one another. Thus already in \emph{Plato} (in the fourth book of the
\textit{Republic}) we find a distinction among various ``parts'' of the
soul, built on an acute analysis of those cases where inwardly struggling
oppositions show themselves in the mind (1)~reason, 2)~feelings of courage
and indignation, 3)~sensual impulse). In modern times
% --- p. 104 ---
\opage{104}there has been talk, in an equally external way, of various
``faculties,'' which could act in dependence on one another and in
opposition to one another. Besides the split thus introduced into
psychical life, between various parts or faculties --- a split refuted by
the thoroughgoing unity of the life of consciousness, without which
precisely the strong oppositions could not be felt and apprehended ---
people were also caught in the illusion that by tracing things back to
various principles an explanation had been reached --- that, e.g.,
knowledge and feeling each became easier to understand when one assumed a
special faculty of knowledge and a special faculty of feeling. It was an
illusion similar to deriving life from a vital force, and one which
Molière mocks \emph{at}, when in the epilogue to \textit{le malade
imaginaire} he has the doctoral candidate explain the soporific power of
opium by ascribing to it a \textit{virtus dormitiva}. What was overlooked
--- as it is still popularly overlooked --- was the purely empirical
character of such distinctions. They state only that certain differences
obtain among certain states of consciousness. But from this there follows
no warrant for assigning them each to its own rubric. It must first be
investigated whether the same elements are not found in all actually
given states of consciousness, so that the differences are grounded in
the preponderance of certain elements and the subordinate importance of
others. It is thus not the phenomena of consciousness or the states of
consciousness themselves that are grouped and classified; it is the
elements we find in them on closer consideration.\footnote{Among older
psychologists this has been impressed with great clarity by
\emph{Fries}: Psychische Anthropologie. Leipzig 1820--21. II p.~XXVIII:
»Die Begriffe von Geistesvermögen klassificiren \emph{allgemeine
Beschaffenheiten} von Lebensäusserrungen aber nicht \emph{die einzelnen
wirklichen Lebensäusserrungen selbst}.« --- When we use the expression
``elements'' instead of ``sides'' or ``properties,'' this must not be
misunderstood as if the states of consciousness could really be resolved
into parts. It is only by abstraction that we distinguish such elements.}
When we therefore set knowledge and feeling in opposition to each other,
this can mean only states with predominant elements of idea over against
states with predominant elements of feeling. It will appear that this
view is the only tenable one, since no state can be pointed to that is
altogether pure
% --- p. 105 ---
\opage{105}ideating or feeling or willing. The question of classification,
then, properly concerns whether we are right to assume different kinds of
psychological elements.

\textbf{2.} The now usual psychological classification is the tripartition
into knowledge, feeling and will. After the bipartition into knowledge
--- will had been followed since \emph{Aristotle}, the German
psychologists \emph{Sulzer} and \emph{Tetens} in the last century took up
feeling as an intermediate member. It was especially Rousseau's insistent
assertion of the right and significance of the life of feeling that here
gained influence on psychological classification. Kant's use of this
tripartition procured it general acceptance. The attempts made since
Kant's time to trace all manifestations of consciousness back to thought
or idea (\emph{Hegel}, \emph{Herbart}) have not succeeded, and besides,
they really recognize the threefoldness as given, but seek to trace it
back to a unity in the principle. The older psychologists conceived
feeling either as obscure thought or as impulse and will. It was natural
that attention should first be directed to knowledge and will and should
overlook the more deeply lying elements. This is analogous to the fact
that attention is directed outward toward the surrounding world before it
is directed toward the inner. For knowledge and will designate the
outward-turned sides of the life of consciousness. In knowledge (in which
in psychology we include sensations, ideas and \emph{thoughts}) an image
is formed of the surrounding world and of the individual himself as a
part of the world. In will (to which impulse, desire and resolution are
reckoned) the individual in turn reacts upon the surrounding world. The
elements of feeling, the inner rhythm of pleasure and displeasure, are
always so intimately bound up with certain images and thoughts or with
certain actions that they are easily confused with them. The element of
feeling is, moreover, so difficult to characterize in and of itself. No
real element of consciousness can be defined, since it is precisely the
expression for the last thing to which investigation leads us. But a
simple sense-sensation, e.g.\ a color or a sound, can nevertheless be
pointed out more directly; the feelings of pleasure and pain lie deeper
and cannot be elements in immediate sense-perception. One could perhaps
define feeling as \emph{that in our} inner states which can never become
an element in a perception or an image. It is an inner illumination that
falls on the stream of sensations and ideas. On the
% --- p. 106 ---
\opage{106}other hand, not all pleasure and displeasure issues in action.
We have given examples in the first section of how difficult it is to
infer inner feelings from outward behavior. Just as we express spiritual
relations by designations taken from the material world, so we express
our feelings by means of the thoughts and actions to which they are
attached.

The independence of the elements of feeling over against the other
elements of consciousness shows itself in this: even if there should be
no state that could be said to be mere feeling without knowledge and
will, feeling is nevertheless not necessarily bound to any definite
theoretical or practical relation. In different individuals, and in the
same individual at different times, pleasure and displeasure are found
connected with different objects. Something that at first arouses
displeasure can later arouse pleasure, and conversely. There appears,
moreover, a clear opposition between states where thought or action lays
so strong a claim on consciousness that the wave of feeling is hardly
noticed, and states where violent emotional commotion pushes back clear
and coherent thinking and deliberate acting. Physiologically the
last-named states are clearly marked by the propagation of the movement
from the central nervous system to internal organs and by reactions from
these on the brain.

\textbf{3.} But although it will thus be justified to take the
tripartition as the basis of psychological investigations, it does not
follow that it must be regarded as original. In our characterization of
the life of consciousness we take it as it shows itself at a higher stage
of development, where it has attained a certain pronounced form. We have
no right to suppose that the threefoldness of the elements should stand
out equally clearly at lower stages of development as well. On the
contrary, it is indeed one of the general laws of development that the
indefinite and homogeneous precedes the definitely and many-sidedly
pronounced. The first germ of the organism is a homogeneous mass in which
no definite structure can yet be distinguished. If the life of
consciousness follows the general laws of life and of development, we
must expect that the three different kinds of elements do not stand out
as distinctly at the lowest stages as later.


% --- p. 107 ---
\opage{107}We are thereby brought to adopt another point of view than
before. Instead of a classification in breadth, according to the
dissimilar but simultaneously active elements, we now get a
classification in height, according to stages, one of which develops out
of the other. Such a point of view, too, was adopted early in
psychology, and it is again \emph{Aristotle}, the founder of empirical
psychology, who must be named first. \emph{Plato} did distinguish between
higher and lower forms of psychical life; but with him it was chiefly
ethical motives that lay at the basis, and he denied that the higher
forms of soul developed out of the lower, since these arose only through
the spiritual being's being implanted in a mortal body. Aristotle, on the
contrary, seeks, with acute use of the material at his disposal, to show
how the one form of psychical manifestations of life forms the basis for
the other. This view has ever since been laid at the basis of psychology
and has received a new foundation in our day through the hypothesis of
evolution and the challenge it poses to find the connection between the
stages of development of psychical life not only in the single
individual, but in the race and in the succession of generations.

\textbf{4.} Great weight has often been laid on learning of what kind the
first manifestation of consciousness is, since it was thought that one
could infer from this which side of the life of consciousness was the
most deeply lying and the most important. Here it was assumed, in the
first place, that the rank of psychical manifestations of life (like that
of noble families) is determined by age, whereas experience shows that
something can have the highest ideal value and yet be the fruit of a long
course of development from an insignificant beginning. In the second
place it was assumed that the first psychical manifestation of life must
be assignable either to knowledge, feeling or will. But this is, as
already mentioned, improbable in view of the general laws of development,
and it is contradicted by experience.

The life of consciousness no doubt begins already in the fetal state (see
I, 4). By all that can be inferred, various more or less definite
sense-sensations (thus elements of knowledge) must already make
themselves felt there. There are likewise surely fluctuations of pleasure
and displeasure, according to the course of the vegetative functions. In
the often very lively movements
% --- p. 108 ---
\opage{108}the fetus makes, one can see an expression of a primitive will,
whether one wishes (with Bain) to explain them by an unconscious urge to
discharge the accumulated tension, or already here to speak of a real
impulse to movement. For even if these movements at first take place
purely unconsciously, they will soon bring consciousness with them through
the sensations they give rise to. We have here, then, elements of all
three kinds; but we have them in close connection. The transition from
impression to reaction is here immediate; even if the movements of the
fetus are more than mere reflex movements, they still do not rise much
above the level of instinct. Instinct differs from mere reflex movement by
containing an obscure urge of feeling, thus a kind of consciousness,
though not consciousness of the actual aim of the action; it differs from
the involuntary discharge of energy assumed by \emph{Bain} by being
directed toward a definite aim useful to the individual or the species.
Instinct need not unfold at a single stroke; it does not exclude the
necessity of certain elementary experiences; but these are made easily
and naturally by means of the original organization. The movement in
which well-being or ill-being issues must naturally go by the paths that,
in accordance with the organization of the individual, are most passable.
This original organization is a given starting point where the
unconscious and the conscious, the inheritance from the race and the
individual's own experience and activity, hang together. --- The newborn
individual, then, is not merely in possession of sensory and motor
apparatus, but has already begun to practice them in the mother's
womb.\footnote{\emph{Kussmaul}: Untersuchungen über das Seelenleben des,
neugeborenen Menschen. p.~35. --- Cf.\ also Cabanis: Rapport du physique
et du moral. éd.\ Peisse. Paris 1843. p.~114 ff. --- \emph{Burdach}:
Physiologie als Erfahrungswissenschaft. 2ter Bd. Leipzig 1828. p.~693 f.}
But a definite distinction between the various elements cannot yet be
made at this stage. The sense-sensations fuse immediately with the
feelings of pleasure and displeasure, which just as immediately break out
in movement.

The experiences from earliest infancy correspond to what can be inferred
about the life of consciousness of the fetus. It is the predominant
importance of vegetative life that prevents
% --- p. 109 ---
\opage{109}differentiation from advancing rapidly. The immediate,
instantaneous transition from impression to movement is characteristic of
the initial stage of the life of consciousness; only by degrees is an
interval formed in which inner differences and oppositions can make
themselves felt. ``The absolute strength of the metabolic exchanges in
the brain substance is considerably greater in childhood than in the
adult. The greater water content and decidedly softer constitution of the
brain substance likewise favor metabolism, whose strong preponderance may
in part explain the greater irritability of the child's nervous
system.''\footnote{\emph{Vierordt}: Physiologie des Kindesalters.
p.~133.} Where adults merely tremble, small children get
% footnote **) on p. 109
convulsions.\footnote{\emph{Darwin}: The Expression of the Emotions.
London 1872. P, 67.} The wholly involuntary and instantaneous character
of the transition from impression to movement shows itself generally in
the slight importance the cerebrum has in the first period of life.
Removal of it or disease in it does not have the same consequence for
% footnote ***) on p. 109
newborn children as for older individuals.\footnote{\emph{Vierordt}
p.~137.} When one remembers the connection between the functions of the
cerebrum and the higher activities of consciousness, one will see the
psychological significance of these facts.

Just as animal life here slowly develops out of vegetative life, so it
also dissolves back into it in the enfeeblement of old age, at the
approach of death and in advanced mental illness. The developed,
diversified life of consciousness gives way again to instinctive and
reflex movements, the mediate transition from impression to movement
gives way again to the immediate. Language, e.g., frequently dissolves
again in such states into mere exclamations of feeling or involuntary
repetition of words heard by chance. Animal life dies before vegetative
life, the brain before the heart, which is \textit{ultimum moriens}, just
as it is \textit{primum vivens}. Instinct stands on the boundary between
the two domains. It is characteristic that taste is one of the
sense-sensations that lasts longest, just as it is one of those that are
first to
% footnote ****) on p. 109
appear.\footnote{Cf.\ F.~\emph{Papillon}: Physiologie de la mort. (In
his book »La Nature et la Vie«. Paris 1874.) p.~379.}


% --- p. 110 ---
\opage{110}The life of consciousness thus describes an arc from the fetal
state to death. Both end points of this arc are relatively simple,
little differentiated and articulated states. It is only in the middle
and at the summit that ideas, feelings and manifestations of will make
themselves felt in their specific peculiarity.

\textbf{5.} What thus holds for the development of the single individual
holds also for the race. A definite difference between knowledge,
feeling and will presupposes a stage of culture where constant and
instantaneous reaction upon the surrounding world is not demanded.
Directly or indirectly the whole life of consciousness is determined by
the individual's position in the universe and its need to orient itself
and to adapt its surroundings to itself, or itself to its surroundings.
Even in the thoughts and feelings apparently most independent of
practical considerations such practical motives can be traced. If it is
not the physical world that motivates the life of thought and feeling,
then it is the social world; if it is not the given physical or social
world of experience, then it is an ideal world built up of elements from
it, of which man in his inner states feels himself to be a member. But
every independent development of the life of thought and feeling
presupposes that the elementary practical demands of life are satisfied.
Art and science, the forms of the life of thought and feeling
emancipated from immediate practical motives, do not unfold in a war of
all against all. Where life is an immediate struggle for existence,
thought is not isolated from feeling, nor feeling from will. It is the
dangers that threaten, or the goods that are hoped for, that fill
consciousness and immediately set the will in motion. The content of
thought is what the impulse (Schopenhauer's ``will to live'') demands,
and feeling is one with the desire to attain it. The more rapidly and
instantaneously the transition from influence to reaction takes place,
the nearer we approach to instinct and reflex action.

\textbf{6.} The formation of an interval between influence and reaction
presupposes sufficient energy, sufficient organization and sufficient
time. --- A certain resistance must be offered to the influence; its
immediate effect must be checked in order that further-reaching inner
activities can be called forth and unfold. And these inner
% --- p. 111 ---
\opage{111}processes themselves lay claim to an energy that could
otherwise have been used immediately to react in response to the
impression. If we start from the assumption that a conscious being at
every stage of development disposes of a certain sum of energy, whose
limit is also the limit of the individual (as regards intensity), then it
is clear that this sum must be greater when it is to be distributed over
various, complicated functions than when it is merely used for the
exercise of a single simple
% footnote *) on p. 111
function.\footnote{The distribution over the various functions will not
always, perhaps even seldom, be even. What the consequence of this is in
psychological respects we shall investigate in later sections.} --- That
these inner activities presuppose a richer organization needs no special
demonstration. --- It is likewise clear that when several different
activities unfold before the reaction takes place, a longer time is
required. A certain independence of the demands of the moment is
therefore, as already remarked, the condition of a higher spiritual
development. In simple and clear form this has been shown by the
investigations of physiological time, i.e.\ the time taken up by the
apprehension of an impression and the response to it. The stronger and
the more familiar an impression is, and the more natural or practiced the
movement with which it is to be answered, the shorter is the
physiological time (or, as it is also called, the reaction time), and the
more nearly we approach the certainty and unconsciousness of reflex
movement and of instinct. The more the individual is prepared for the
kind and strength of the impression, and for the movement with which it
is to be answered, the more quickly the reaction can take place. Already
when he does not know which of two different possible impressions is
coming, the physiological time increases; there is then inserted a
``discrimination time,'' taken up with deciding the nature of the
impression. When, in addition, the different possible impressions are
each to be answered by its corresponding movement, so that it has to be
decided which movement is to be made, a special
% footnote **) on p. 111
``will time'' is required.\footnote{Donders's experiments. See
\emph{Exner}: Physiologie der Grosshirnrinde. (Hermann. II, 2).
p.~278--281. A detailed account by \emph{Wundt} (Physiol.\ Psychologie.
II. p.~206--279) of his own and earlier experiments.} Even quite simple
movements last considerably longer than twitches that are
% --- p. 112 ---
\opage{112}produced by direct stimulation of a motor nerve. When the index
finger of the right hand is set in motion by stimulation from an
induction current, the movement is performed on average in
0.166$''$, whereas a voluntary movement requires
0.296$''$.\footnote{\emph{Wundt}. I. p.~259. (Baxt's experiments).}

\textbf{7.} It must not be forgotten that differentiation merely means the
preponderance of different elements in different states, not complete
separation.

\textbf{a.} Despite all independence of practical demands and of the
claims of the moment, thought is nevertheless always connected with a
certain mood. Elements of feeling are present, which are overlooked so
easily only because they do not push themselves into the foreground, but
subordinate themselves to the play of thoughts and are determined by it.
A wholly feelingless thinking (a ``pure thinking,'' as speculative
philosophers have so often demanded) does not exist. Lotze has aptly
shown that even the most abstract thinking is accompanied by emotional
movements. ``We do not apprehend the concept of unity without at the same
time enjoying the happiness of satisfaction that its content encloses,
nor the concept of opposition without at the same time feeling the
displeasure of hostility.'' (Mikrokosmus. I. 2. Ausg. p.~273). It is by
virtue of these emotional movements, connected with all ideas and
thoughts, that knowledge becomes a power in the soul. When one speaks of
the struggle of reason with the passions, what is really meant is a
struggle between the feelings attached to rational considerations and the
more vehement feelings, united with fewer elements of thought, which are
designated by the expression passion. A feeling can be very strong and
intense without being vehement, but is then more easily overlooked. The
feelings attached to ideal ends and relations are far less able to bring
about instantaneous affect and outburst than the primitive feelings
attached to the physical functions of life. There is in the passions
connected with self-preservation and the propagation of the species an
animal ardor which often cannot be subdued by any other influence. Ideal
enthusiasm is distributed over longer periods of time and works more
hiddenly. And yet step by step it is able to take possession of the
center of the soul and to press into its service the
% --- p. 113 ---
\opage{113}collected energy, originally directed by those primitive
impulses.

\textbf{b.} Just as little is knowledge ever completely emancipated from
will. In all memory and synthesis an activity manifests itself which we
become especially conscious of, and call attention, when for inner or
outer reasons a strong claim is laid upon it, but which in reality plays
a part even in the simplest sense-perception. We must \emph{will} to see
in order to see rightly. All mental labor is connected with a certain
feeling of strain. Already \emph{Leibnitz} has shown that in the
transition from one idea to another a striving or desire makes itself
felt (Monadologie § 15). \emph{Hume} and, in our day, Helmholtz have
traced the principle of causality back to an instinct or impulse
according to which, when something happens, we involuntarily look about
for its cause. But it is with this striving just as with emotional
movement: when it does not grow to a higher degree of strength through
resistance or in some other way, it is as a rule overlooked. This moment
of activity in all knowledge has been especially brought forward in
recent psychology, at first chiefly as a reaction against the attempt of
Condillac and of the one-sided ``association psychology'' to trace all
knowledge back to a mechanical interaction between originally purely
passive sense-sensations and their after-effects.

\textbf{c.} While for a long time there was an inclination to overrate
knowledge at the expense of feeling --- a tendency first overcome through
Rousseau's influence on modern intellectual development --- attempts
have been made, especially in more recent times, to conceive feeling as
the primitive form of consciousness, so that the life of consciousness at
the lowest stage would be a pure life of feeling and the other elements
would only by degrees have developed out of it. Such attempts are
explained partly by the intimacy with which the life of feeling is bound
up with the very conditions of our existence, while the elements of
knowledge seem rather to move on the periphery of our being, and partly,
and above all, by the fact that the elements of feeling stand out more
distinctly and strongly and play a larger part in relation to the
elements of knowledge the more we descend from the higher forms of the
life of consciousness to the more primitive. But this assumption
% --- p. 114 ---
\opage{114}becomes untenable already from the fact that as soon as a state
of pleasure and displeasure endures --- and it must endure in order to be
% footnote *) on p. 114
properly felt\footnote{Patients anesthetized with chloroform often utter
cries of complaint during the operation, without, however, being able
after waking to remember having felt pain. »A vrai dire«, says
\emph{Richet} of this case, »cette douleur si rapide qu’on n’en conserve
pas de souvenir, n’est rien, et c’est un moment presque mathématique dont
il n’y a guère à tenir compte. Ce qui fait la cruauté de la douleur,
c’est moins la douleur elle-même, si intense qu’elle soit, que le
retentissement pénible qu’elle laisse après elle«. Recherches sur la
sensibilité. p.~256. --- \emph{Maudsley} thinks that those cries arise
purely by reflex. He cites a case where a lady, one of whose breasts was
amputated, remembered after waking that she had heard herself scream,
although she declared that she had not felt any pain. (Physiologie de
l’esprit. Trad.\ de l’angl.\ par A.~Herzen, p.~195).} --- memory already
plays a certain part; and as soon as a pulsation takes place --- and
states of feeling always fluctuate in intensity --- an involuntary
comparison will already make itself felt. Here, then, there are already
elements of knowledge that can serve as a starting point for further
development, so that knowledge does not arise through a kind of
\textit{generatio æqvivoca} from wholly formless and blind states of
feeling.

Such a view has recently been advanced by Ad.~\emph{Horwicz}
(Psychologische Analysen auf physiologischer Grundlage. I. Halle 1872.
p.~350 ff.). According to this investigator's very interesting account it
is the movement called forth by feeling that paves the way for knowledge.
Pleasure and displeasure lead to certain movements, which are tried until
the most satisfying is found; this is then practiced and thereby acquires
a special distinguishing mark that makes it an object of consciousness.
But something new is thus added after all, besides the element of feeling
itself: namely the sensation of movement, and one has no right to suppose
that it should always be derived; on the contrary, involuntary movement
occurs as early as consciousness can be assumed to occur at all. At the
basis of this theory, then, lies an abstraction or, if one will, an
anticipation: feeling is conceived as independent and resting in itself,
which it first becomes --- to a certain degree --- when at a higher stage
of development it has emancipated itself from will.


% --- p. 115 ---
\opage{115}It is impulse rather than feeling that is the right expression
for the primitive stage of consciousness. Pleasure and displeasure occur
originally only as elements in desire and striving. Even in the lowest
animal beings, the monera, movements of extension and contraction have
been demonstrated; the former lead to the taking in of nourishment, the
latter to protection against attack. Already here, it seems, elements
of consciousness other than pure feelings of pleasure and displeasure
must be presupposed, such as sensations of touch and of movement, perhaps
also sensations from chemical influence, an analogue of the sensations of
taste. ``In the protozoa's search for nourishment we observe distinctly
that they are capable of a certain discrimination, without which
capacity the feeling-about would have no purpose, could not be called a
feeling-about at all. --- --- With locomotion in search of nourishment
there necessarily goes hand in hand the development of a discrimination
between different directions, i.e.\ a distinguishing of the pleasant
direction (leading to the object of nourishment) from the relatively less
pleasant direction; once this distinction is present, the direction from
which danger comes is also easily distinguished from the
% footnote *) on p. 115
opposite one.''\footnote{G.~H.~\emph{Schneider}: Zur Entwickelung der
Willensäusserungen im Thierreich. (Vierteljahrsschrift für
wissenschaftliche Philosophie. 3. Jahrg.) p.~183. 301.} Even if one
should think that in these little-formed beings, in which no nervous
system has yet been found, the sensations could have no distinctness and
definiteness, the facts adduced show nonetheless that the animal must
apprehend a difference between the impressions. Pleasure and pain would
indeed help the animal very little if they merely drove it to the
performance of movements, without the kind and direction of the movement
being able to be more closely determined by the nature of the impression.

What holds for the lowest conscious beings no doubt holds also for the
higher beings at the first stage of development. (See on this I, 4 and
IV, 4).

In higher and fully developed beings too we have an approximation to a
pure state of feeling, namely in the vital feeling or common feeling, the
fundamental mood that arises from the whole state of the organism, from
the normal or abnormal course of the vital movements, especially of the
% --- p. 116 ---
\opage{116}vegetative functions. Only rarely and imperfectly are we able
to localize the impressions that arouse this feeling. They do not appear
separated, nor with such qualitative peculiarity as the impressions
received through the external senses. Even differences of degree cannot
be apprehended here so definitely as with the senses proper. The vital
feeling consists in an unclear mood, of whose cause we do not, at least,
become conscious at once. Diseases of the heart and of the mind can
produce unrest and melancholy without the sufferer discovering the cause
of these moods. We have seen what part the vital feeling plays in the
fetal state and in the newborn child. In the years of puberty there is
awakened, simultaneously with the maturity of the sexual organs, an
indefinite presentiment and longing, an obscure impulse which,
incomprehensible to the individual, leads him out beyond himself. The
temperaments \emph{manifest themselves} especially in the fundamental
moods that dominate the vital feeling. --- However few elements of
knowledge can be demonstrated in all these states, they nevertheless each
appear with such peculiarity that the transition from one to another,
and with it the difference between them, must come more or less
distinctly to consciousness, and none of them can be as simple as a pure
state of feeling would have to be.

Self-observation shows us at most only an approximation to a state in
which all elements of knowledge have vanished. Such an approximation we
get the more the strength of the element of feeling increases, and this
strength in turn depends on the strength of the impression. Knowledge
and feeling here come to stand in inverse relation to each other: the
more strongly the one manifests itself, the less strength the other
commands. Exceedingly strong joy or sorrow can drive away almost all
ideation, all reflection; but such an ecstasy also stands on the border
of consciousness.

That the higher feelings contain elements of knowledge within them needs
no further demonstration. Feeling acquires a content or an object only
by being attached to memories and thoughts.

\textbf{d.} The intimate relation between feeling and will is given with
the fact that only a strong and living feeling is a motive for the will.
Elements of knowledge in and of
% --- p. 117 ---
\opage{117}themselves do not lead to movement of will. It is from this
point of view that \emph{Sibbern}, who otherwise follows the tripartition
in his psychology, points out that it can be transformed into a duality,
since feeling and will have this in common, that in both there shows
itself ``a personal being-gripped and being-moved by what is known,
whereby we attach ourselves to it and are brought to act and strive for
% footnote *) on p. 117
it.''\footnote{Psychologie. Kbhvn. 1856. p.~150 f.} The close connection
with movement both have in common: feeling finds outlet in movement and
likewise initiates movement. The expressive movements are, to be sure,
in part such as are withdrawn from the direct influence of the will, and
arise through the strong movement in the brain propagating itself further
to larger or smaller regions of the organism. Heart and lungs, intestinal
canal, vascular system and other internal organs thus feel the effect of
the emotional movement. But organs and muscles too that otherwise stand
under the dominion of the will can be set in motion by strong feelings,
and it is difficult, if not impossible, to distinguish between emotional
movement and voluntary movement. What we call instinct and impulse lies
in this intermediate region. Feeling naturally finds vent precisely in
such movements as have frequently (in the same individual and in earlier
generations) been employed in the service of the will. Already the
monera perform contraction and recoil in order to protect themselves
against danger. When beings at a higher stage, too, now start together
and back in sudden fright, it is an old instinct that still stirs dimly
in the \emph{expression} of the emotion. The Greek word φόβος, fear,
originally means (thus often in Homer) flight. In a similar way anger
finds vent in movements of attack, strong sympathetic feeling in the
stretching out of the arms as if to embrace the object, etc. According to
the hypothesis of evolution these phenomena find a natural explanation in
the fact that the involuntary expressive movements were originally
purposively adapted voluntary movements.\footnote{\emph{Darwin}:
Expression of the Emotions. Chap.~1. --- \emph{Spencer}: Principles of
Psychology. II. p.~545 ff. --- \emph{Wundt}: Physiologische Psychologie.
II. p.~417.}

It is only in the course of psychological development that
differentiation between feeling and will sets in.
% --- p. 118 ---
\opage{118}A greater and greater opposition develops between the two ways
in which the inner movement finds vent. We see here distinctly the
psychological significance of the law of the conservation of energy. For
the more energy the individual expends on the one kind of reaction, the
less it can expend on the other. This truth is aptly expressed in Saxo's
well-known story of the different effect that the report of Regner
Lodbrog's murder had on his sons: the one in whom the expressive movement
was weakest had the greatest energy of action. It is a well-known thing
that sentimentality has a weakening effect on the will. In harmoniously
developed natures such an opposition does not come to the fore; feeling
and will each find their proper domain and at the right moments give way
to each other.

Feeling, like knowledge, has an immediately practical character. By a
course of development similar to that in the other case, feeling is
freed from practical impulse and can move freely (thus in aesthetic and
religious moods) without immediate regard to outward action.

\textbf{e.} If any of the three kinds of elements of consciousness were
to be regarded as the original form of the life of consciousness, it
would surely have to be the will. In instinct, raised only a little above
reflex movement, we have the primitive form of consciousness, and there
the side of will is evidently the strongest: the intellectual and
emotional elements acquire significance only as transitional links to
action. The will also forms later, at all stages, the constant
substratum. Activity is a fundamental property of the life of
consciousness, and when we ascribe will to the conscious individual we
merely emphasize this fundamental property in particular. In the narrower
sense, as the capacity to choose between various given possibilities,
will is only a result of psychical development, not originally
co-operative. But if we take will in a wider sense, as all activity
determined by feeling and knowledge, then the whole life of consciousness
can be said to be gathered up in will as its fullest expression.
Consciousness can, in accordance with the foregoing, be defined as
arrested movement. But the transition to movement nevertheless always
stands as the conclusion of everything that goes on in the world of
consciousness. The development of the conscious
% --- p. 119 ---
\opage{119}individual goes from will to will. This development can take
place very sporadically, move through one-sidednesses and oppositions;
but there will always (if not in the individual, then in the race) be an
obscure urge that leads beyond the scattered, one-sided and conflicting
to inner harmony among the fundamental spiritual tendencies. Ethics and
aesthetics are, psychologically speaking, fully within their rights when
they find in such a harmony the highest type for a human life.


%% ============================================================
%%  V. SENSE-PERCEPTION AND THOUGHT (pp. 120--267)
%% ============================================================
% --- p. 120 ---
\chapter[Sense-Perception and Thought]{Sense-Perception and Thought\opage{120}}

\section*{A. Sensation}\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{A. Sensation}

\noindent\textbf{1.} When we consider the phenomena of cognition by
themselves alone, we perform, as we saw in the preceding chapter, an
abstraction, since elements of feeling and will are surely at work in
every actually given state of consciousness. But such an abstraction is
necessary; every inquiry must to a certain degree take its object out
of the living and manifold connection in which it exists. And we must
carry the abstraction even further. The phenomena of cognition
themselves are in a high degree composite. We may, it is true,
provisionally divide them into two groups, sense-perception and thought;
but it will turn out precisely that these two groups do not lie outside
each other, but stand in the most intimate interaction. Consciousness
appears --- at least in the forms in which we know it from immediate
experience --- never as a perfectly single series of inner phenomena.
Manifold series of thoughts and memories, sensations and ideas, interlock
with one another in each single experience, each single inference. But
in order to see the relations and the laws clearly we must, so far as
possible, think of consciousness as forming a single series. And not only
that; we must begin by thinking of this series as consisting of
sensations alone. The difference between sense-perception and thought
rests on the difference between sensations and ideas: between the
immediately given new elements and the residues or after-effects of such
elements that emerge anew. As soon as the elements previously acquired
cooperate
% --- p. 121 ---
\opage{121}in the reception of the new, we have no pure sensation; then
there is already a certain degree of thought involved in the
sense-perception. We shall therefore try whether it is possible to
conceive of a consciousness that consists merely of a series of
perfectly pure and simple sensations, or of simple, immediately given
elements arising in each single moment. Even if it were possible that
there could be sensations entirely without admixture of ideas, it would
not follow that these sensations existed entirely independently of one
another and were each of them absolutely simple. There are thus three
main questions that present themselves to us and indicate the points of
view from which we shall here occupy ourselves with the doctrine of
sense-sensations: namely, the question of their independence of ideas,
the question of their independence of one another, and the question of
their simplicity and indissolubility.

It will easily be seen that if these questions are answered in the
affirmative, the provisional characterization of consciousness on which
we built in the earlier chapters will be incorrect. For we found it to
be the characteristic mark of the life of consciousness that it appeared
in a series of states which did not exist independently of one another,
but determined one another in an inner way, this inner interaction
finding its most typical expression in memory (see II, 5). It is thus a
kind of \emph{test} of the correctness of this characterization that we
now proceed to.

\textbf{2.} By a purely psychological route we can convince ourselves
of the \emph{simplicity} of our sensations only to a certain degree. We
can never be quite sure whether we really stand before something
indissoluble. Where observational psychology leaves off, experimental
sense-physiology takes over, and it has shown in some cases that the
seemingly simple psychological phenomenon presupposes an intricate and
composite physiological process. The possibility that psychological
simplicity is only the result of a composition taking place below the
threshold of consciousness can therefore not be denied.

The more indefinite the sensation, the less probable it is that it
should be uncompounded. Of the vital or general sensations, accordingly,
one will not readily assert it. A sensation such as, e.g., nausea
(sickness) has something composite about it even for immediate
observation,
% --- p. 122 ---
\opage{122}as is seen from the fact that it has been classed now under
the sensations of taste, now under the muscular sensations, while at
other times the wish has been to separate it from both these kinds. Many
of the sensations of taste and smell are so mixed with sensations of
touch that they cannot be called pure or simple. Salt, sour and
astringent taste, pungent and sharp smell, are properly combinations of
sensations of taste or smell with sensations of touch. The enjoyment of
many kinds of food (e.g.\ jelly and the like) is no doubt due chiefly to
their effect on the fine skin of the mouth cavity, and is thus connected
with the sense of touch rather than with the sense of taste. In the wider
meaning in which we are accustomed to speak of the sensation of taste,
the Shah of Persia was therefore right when he reproached the Europeans
(who use knife and fork) for not knowing that the sensations of taste
began in the fingertips. Besides smell, sight too plays a part in taste,
since in the dark one does not judge the taste of a thing so surely as
when one can call on sight for help. An isolation is possible here only
by artificial means, and it then often turns out that the taste-sense
proper has nothing whatever to do with the apparent sensations of taste.

The sensation we have when we lift a burden from the ground is very
composite. Contact, pressure and muscular effort fuse together into an
indefinite whole, which becomes still more intricate from the fact that
several different muscles are tensed, and in different degrees. And yet
it may seem to us as if it were a single sensation that we had.

As regards sensations belonging to one definite sense, the matter might
seem to stand more simply and clearly. Yet here too there can be dispute
whether a sensation appears as simple or as composite in immediate
apprehension itself. \emph{Goethe}, e.g., held that only the sensations
of yellow, blue and red were simple; he called them principal colors and
held that the other colors were composed of them, since in violet he
believed he could trace red and blue, in green yellow and blue,
% footnote *) on p. 122
and in orange yellow and red.\footnote{‚Farbenlehre‘. 1ster Bd.
§ 60.} On the other hand, in our day E. \emph{Hering} asserts that
green is an entirely simple sensation, and that yellow and blue can
never be sensed together as
% --- p. 123 ---
\opage{123}elements in a composite color. He therefore sets up a series
of four principal colors: red, green, yellow and blue.\footnote{Zur
Lehre vom Lichtsinne. 2 Aufl. § 38. --- This fourfold scheme had already
been set up by \emph{Leonardo} da Vinci.} When such able observers
differ so decidedly, it is a proof of how uncertain direct
psychological apprehension must be at this boundary point. As regards
the matter itself, there is evidently yet another possibility left,
namely that all color sensations are simple. He who practices
abstracting from memories and preconceived opinions, and concentrating
his attention on a single narrow band of the color scale, will surely be
able to obtain an entirely simple sensation of each single color; and if
language had formed words enough, he would feel the need to express each
of these color tones by its own independent word. There is the further
% footnote **) on p. 123
point\footnote{V. Krenchel: Om Grundfarver. Et kritisk Bidrag til
Farvesansens Teori [On Fundamental Colors. A Critical Contribution to
the Theory of the Color Sense]. Kbhvn. 1880. p.~11 f.} that the dispute
would begin anew when it came to stating more precisely which shade of
red, green, etc., is the real principal color. Different observers would
each name his own shade and assert that the other shades had arisen by
mixture of it with other colors. When the expressions of language point
to certain principal colors, this is probably to be explained by the
fact that there were certain prominent natural objects whose colors
impressed themselves early and dominated apprehension (the green plants,
the red blood,
% footnote ***) on p. 123
etc.).\footnote{\emph{Wundt}. I. p.~415.}

To be carefully distinguished from the question of \emph{principal
colors} in the subjective-psychological respect is the question whether
there are \emph{fundamental colors} in the objective-physiological
respect. There are certain colored rays which, mixed in suitable
proportions, can produce all possible subjective color sensations. These
colors, by whose objective composition the others can arise, have been
called fundamental colors. They are three: red, green and violet.
According to a theory put forward by celebrated investigators
(\emph{Young} and \emph{Helmholtz}), there are in the optic nerve three
different kinds of fibers, each corresponding to its own ``fundamental
color.'' At every stimulation of the retina all three
% --- p. 124 ---
\opage{124}kinds of fibers are set in activity, and the kind of sensation
therefore depends on which kind of fiber is most strongly stimulated.
Every color sensation will thus --- physiologically regarded --- be of a
composite nature; in order to obtain an entirely simple stimulation, the
two other kinds of fibers must first be dulled by strong stimulation
with the rays corresponding to them. If we want, e.g., an unmixed red
color impression, it is obtained by first looking at a green surface.

This theory, however, is regarded by many as unwarranted and unnecessary
for sense-physiology. It carries physical relations over without more
ado into the physiological domain. ``From the fact that objective red,
green and violet are sufficient to produce all qualities of light, we
evidently may not yet conclude that there also exist only three
physiological processes underlying all sensation of light and color; on
the contrary, we must conclude that, since the qualitative sensations
produced by those three objective colors and their mixtures are very
manifold, the physiological effects of the proportional mixture of the
three fundamental colors are qualitatively of very different
kinds.''\footnote{\emph{Wundt}. I. p.~451.} Dr.\ V. \emph{Krenchel}, in
his treatise cited above, has tried to show that the phenomena before us
can very well be explained without assuming fundamental colors in the
physiological sense, simply by supposing that to each kind of subjective
color sensation there correspond different forms of motion in the brain
molecules to which the sensations must be assumed to be attached.

On the other hand, there seems to be bound up with every \emph{color
impression} a \emph{colorless impression of light}, which, when the
strength of the stimulation is greatly decreased or increased, passes
over into a distinctly colorless sensation (of white, gray or black).
Only with a stimulation of middle strength does the color impression
(the chromatic irritation) have the preponderance over the colorless
impression (the achromatic irritation). This mixture of a chromatic and
an achromatic process in the organ of sight is inferred partly from the
fact that the parts of the retina most remote from the place of
distinctest vision (the yellow spot, \textit{fovea centralis}) are
totally color-blind, partly from the fact that every color sensation,
with
% --- p. 125 ---
\opage{125}sufficient decrease or increase in the magnitude of the
stimulation, passes over into colorless sensation.\footnote{\emph{Wundt}.
I. p.~453.}

As regards the sense of hearing, the sensations are no doubt always
composite. Every tone has its timbre, i.e.\ consists of a combination of
a fundamental tone and weaker overtones, different for each source of
tone. The same tone therefore sounds different when it is produced by
means of different instruments. But just as the practiced ear at a
concert is able to distinguish the contributions of the single
instruments to the resulting impression, so too specially gifted or
trained organs of hearing can separate the partial tones in the clang,
although in immediate sensation this stands as entirely simple. A single
tone is therefore properly an abstraction, since we probably never
apprehend tones that are wholly free of timbre. There is only a
difference of degree between clang and consonance, conditioned by the
weaker or stronger prominence of the overtones in relation to the
fundamental tone.

This theory, set up by \emph{Helmholtz} and generally accepted, shows
how by way of physical and physiological experiment the composite nature
of subjective sensations can be established. The sensation of clang is
resolved into elements on whose varying relations to one another the
character of the sensation depends. But if this is how it stands with
one kind of sensation, should it not stand the same way with our other
sensations? If here we lack the ability to trace the elements of
sensation of which the sensation given to our consciousness is composed,
this might well be connected with the fact that the sense of hearing on
the whole stands so far above the other senses in the fineness with
which it is able to apprehend differences and nuances. The study of the
sensation of hearing has in any case shaken the principle of the
simplicity of sensations and opened a new horizon where the
psychological world accessible to us seemed to close.

There are also certain phenomena that point toward simpler psychical
elements than those we find in our distinct sense-sensations. The
sensations we receive while
% --- p. 126 ---
\opage{126}our attention is taken up in another direction, or in a sudden
surprise, have no definite, qualitative character. We start, notice that
something has happened in or to us, but what it is, whether it is an
impression of light, a blow, an electric stimulation, we do not know ---
in the first moment at least. So the more suddenly the sensations occur
and the shorter they last, the less can they be referred to any of the
definite qualities of sense. The same holds --- as has been shown
experimentally by \emph{Fick} and
% footnote *) on p. 126
\emph{Wunderli}\footnote{Hermann’s Handbuch. III, 1. p.~164. --- III,
2. p.~322.} --- when the impressions are very weak and very limited.
Unless the impression falls precisely on the inner palm of the hand or
on the face, one cannot tell whether a weak and limited stimulation
comes from contact or from warmth. It seems, then, as \emph{Fick}
remarks, that in the distinctly appearing sensations of warmth and
contact we must see the results of a combination by which various
elementary sensations are united into groups that stand before
consciousness as indissoluble unities.

It is all the more natural to conceive the matter in this way since the
nerve process, whatever its nature may otherwise be, takes place in
pulsating shocks or oscillations. It is the highest law of general
nerve-physiology that a nerve process can never be released by a state
of equilibrium, but only by sudden changes in the state of the nerve
proceeding with a certain rapidity. A seemingly continuous nerve process
(a tetanus) comes about only through a series of single alterations in
the equilibrium following rapidly on one another. With this law the
special relations in the individual sense organs also seem to agree,
% footnote **) on p. 126
so far as they are known.\footnote{\emph{Funke} in Hermann’s Handbuch.
III, 2. p.~328 ff. --- The principal law of nerve-physiology referred to
was demonstrated for electrical stimulations by du
\emph{Bois}-\emph{Reymond} in 1845. L. \emph{Hermann}: Allg.
Nervenphysiologie (Handbuch. II, 1.). p.~50.} Sensation as we know it
must correspond to several such shocks, or to the different moments of
the oscillations; there is thus bound together in one moment of
consciousness, in the single momentary sensation, what physiologically
regarded fills several moments of time. And since the structure and mode
of operation of the nervous system
% --- p. 127 ---
\opage{127}seem to be uniform throughout, only one way stands open to an
explanation of the qualitative differences of the sense-sensations,
namely to derive them from the different ways in which those elementary
sensations corresponding to the single nerve pulsations or discharges of
force are combined. Just as the different color qualities can find their
physiological representation in different directions of motion of the
same brain molecules, so one might go a step further and think of the
difference of the \emph{modalities of
sensation}\footnote{\emph{Modality} is a term proposed by
\emph{Helmholtz} for what \emph{Fichte} calls a circle of qualities: the
sum of the sense-sensations belonging to the same class (sight, hearing,
taste, etc.).} as physiologically represented by differences lying still
deeper, in the form and direction of the processes that take place in
the central and sense organs. Only thus will there be a possibility of
understanding how the different senses can have arisen in the course of
the development of organic beings. The further one goes from the higher
animals down to the lowest, the fewer modalities one finds. The modality
that remains as a starting point for the development of sense-perception
is the sense of touch. From it the special senses or modalities
\emph{must} have arisen by differentiation. It agrees with this that in
all sentient organisms the special sense organs develop from the outer
covering of the body.\footnote{Cf.\ \emph{Wundt}. I. p.~280.}

This hypothesis (first set up by \emph{Spencer}) of the origin of our
single sensations by combination of elementary sensations of greater
simplicity thus opens a wide horizon for us. What interests us most
here, however, is that in being led to this hypothesis we find, at the
borders of the domain of distinct consciousness, the traces of a work
which has no doubt gone on below the threshold of consciousness, but yet
according to the same laws that rule within the life of consciousness
(cf.\ chap.\ III). The general characterization of consciousness as
comprehending and unifying proves to hold of the very limiting parts to
which the resolution of composite states of consciousness leads. If,
then, one wanted to assert that consciousness is only a sum of
sensations, it would in any case have to be remarked that
% --- p. 128 ---
\opage{128}the terms of this sum are not absolutely simple, but have
probably arisen by synthesis of still simpler elements. It is thus not
possible to point out psychical atoms.

\textbf{3.} In close connection with the question of the simplicity of
sensations stands the question of their \emph{independence} of one
another. Here too sense-physiology gives us interesting information.

There seems to be a certain resistance to be overcome, both in the end
organs of the sensory nerves and in the nerve centers, before the
impression can produce its full effect; but once it is overcome, the
effect persists for some time after the impression has ceased. It is the
law of inertia, valid for all material phenomena, that appears here in
nerve-physiology too. The different senses, however, do not behave
alike in this respect. The greatest elasticity is possessed by the sense
of touch. If one lays one's finger on a toothed wheel that is turned
with a certain speed, one can have as many as nearly 1000 separate
sensations in one second. If the speed is increased still further, on
the other hand, only one continuous sensation arises. Next after the
sense of touch in this respect comes the sense of hearing. That the
latter is surpassed by the sense of touch is seen already from the fact
that by laying one's hand on a musical instrument one can feel the
vibrations of even fairly high tones as a whirring. In observation with
one ear the crack of two electric sparks is still heard as independent
of each other when the one springs across 0, $_{002}$ second before the
other. In observation with both ears the limit lies higher up (0,
$_{064}$ second). Electric shocks can still be distinguished when they
come at a rate of 35 in a second; if the rapidity becomes still greater,
only one single sensation arises. On the forehead 60 shocks in a second
can still be distinguished. Lowest in respect of elasticity stands the
sense of sight,\footnote{For the sensations of taste and smell no
reliable observations are available. It cannot be definitely
established that these sensations leave after-sensations, since one
cannot with certainty exclude residues of the tasting and smelling
substances in the organs. \emph{Vintschgau} in Hermanns Handbuch. III,
2. p.~221. 284.} which is inferred from the nature of the end organs.
After looking at a clearly lit object
% --- p. 129 ---
\opage{129}an after-image remains when one closes the eye. The new
impression thus does not find the place empty, but is combined with the
after-effect of the preceding one. If a disk divided into equal,
alternately white and black sectors is turned rapidly round, the
impressions fuse and a continuous sensation of gray arises when there
come about 24 stimulations in a second and the intensity of light is
that of ordinary daylight. A glowing sulphur match turned rapidly round
in the dark looks to us like a luminous circle. With slow rotation we
distinctly bring out the single sensations; with increased rapidity a
flickering arises, and with still greater rapidity the fusion into one
sensation then takes place.\footnote{\emph{Fick}. Hermanns Handbuch.
III, 1. p.~211 ff. --- Exner. ibid. II, 2 p.~256--260. --- The strong
and lasting after-effects of light in the eye are an important support
for the photochemical theory, according to which the decomposition of
the so-called visual purple in the retina brought about by the rays of
light is the real stimulus of the optic nerve. \emph{Kühne}: Chemische
Vorgänge in der Netzhaut. (Hermann’s Handb. III, 1). p.~238. 261.}

The man born blind on whom Franz operated still found it very unpleasant
several months after the operation to walk in busy streets. The many
different things and the rapid movements of people, carriages, etc.,
confused his sight so much that in the end he saw nothing at all; the
impression produced by the object last seen had not yet vanished when
the next object was already calling forth a new one. Here, then, the
single impressions did not wholly fuse, but called forth a chaos that
made definite apprehension impossible. At every stage of consciousness
there is a certain tempo that the sensations must observe if they are to
be independent of one another.

The one term in the series of sensations thus does not stand absolutely
independent over against the other; their existence depends on their
distance from one another in time. We move here in a domain where it is
difficult to find the right expressions. For, strictly speaking, one
cannot say that the sensations fuse, since under the conditions stated
they no longer come into being each by itself. One might therefore hold
that these
% --- p. 130 ---
\opage{130}phenomena are no concern of psychology, since psychology has
to keep to what occurs within consciousness. But it is of great
importance for psychology to learn that the independence of sensations
is only conditional and relative. We have now seen that already at the
boundary of consciousness a struggle is waged between the single
elements, and that the significance each of them acquires depends on its
relation to the other elements. The same will prove to hold when we
examine the strength and the quality of sensations.

\textbf{4.} When we listen to a dying tone, we can follow it down to a
minimum of \emph{strength} that could not be apprehended by one who had
not heard the tone struck. So too the eye can follow the flight of a
bird to a distance at which it could not be discovered by an eye that
was only now to apprehend it. What we call the threshold of
consciousness is thus considerably lowered in such cases: it lies
further down when we come to it from above than when we discover it from
below. The strength an impression must have in order to become an object
of sensation thus depends on the preceding state of the sentient
organism. It is easier for consciousness to hold fast a given weaker
impression than to receive a wholly new one, though this may perhaps be
stronger in itself. Here it is active adaptation, or practice, that
makes up for what the impression lacks in strength. Prisoners who had
long lived in dark rooms acquired the ability to distinguish the
smallest objects and the faintest differences in the intensity of light.
When Lavoisier, on the occasion of an academic prize question, wished to
investigate the luminous intensity of different kinds of flame and
noticed that his eye was not sharp enough, he shut himself up for six
weeks in a room hung with black, and thereby became able to notice very
small differences. When sensation is not trained in this way, a higher
degree of strength in the impression is required for the threshold of
sensation to be crossed.

By gentle and successive strengthening an impression can remain
imperceptible even after it has reached a degree of strength at which it
would otherwise call forth sensation. By very slow strengthening of an
electric current a nerve acted on by it is at last destroyed without
any sign being shown of
% --- p. 131 ---
\opage{131}sensation. By successive, very small raisings or lowerings of
the temperature, frogs have been boiled or made to stiffen with frost
without making the least reflex movement. Sensations of warmth and cold
arise only when the temperature of the skin undergoes a change that
proceeds with a certain rapidity. One and the same impression can under
different circumstances produce now a sensation of warmth, now a
sensation of cold. If, e.g., we bring the hand into a vessel in which
the temperature is the same as in the room, to whose temperature the
hand was accustomed, we sense warmth, because the radiation of heat from
the hand is hindered. If one fills a vessel with water of a temperature
corresponding to that of the skin, a vessel to the right with water of
higher, one to the left with water of lower temperature, then dips the
right hand into the right vessel and the left hand into the left vessel,
and immediately afterward both hands into the middle vessel: then the
right hand feels cold, the left warmth in this vessel, where neither of
them before noticed cold or warmth. In the sensation of temperature it
depends merely on the skin (or more exactly the thermal apparatus)
acquiring a temperature that lies sufficiently above or below its
``zero-point temperature'' (i.e.\ the temperature which at the place of
the skin in question is sensed neither as warmth nor as cold). So long
as the current of heat passing through the skin from within outward does
not rise or fall, no sensation of temperature arises, whatever the
constant temperature may be.\footnote{Cf.\ \emph{Fick}: Anatomie und
Physiologie der Sinnesorgane. Lahr. 1864. p.~54. --- \emph{Hering}: Der
Temperatursinn. (Hermann. III, 2). p.~415 ff. --- \emph{Richet}:
Recherches sur la sensibilité. p.~42 ff. 170 ff.}

A certain contrast is thus required between the new impression and the
one it replaces, for a sensation to arise. There must be a background in
relation to which the new impression can assert itself. Every impression
is measured, as it were, by what precedes it. Now if the new impression
is weak in relation to the preceding one, it will be, so to speak,
absorbed by the latter and not noticed. After being electrified for some
minutes with a strong current one does not notice a weaker one that
would otherwise have been sensed.
% --- p. 132 ---
\opage{132}If one receives a strong current through the one hand and a
new current through the other, the latter is not noticed. If one leg of
a pair of compasses is set on a painful wound, the other on the skin
outside it, and both places are pressed equally hard, one gets only one
sensation, even if the distance is double that at which two sensations
would have been felt within the
% footnote *) on p. 132
wound.\footnote{\emph{Richet}. p.~168. 222.} Already
\emph{Hippocrates} taught that when two pains arose at the same time,
but not in the same place, the lesser was obscured by the greater.
Shakespeare has King Lear say in the night of storm on the heath (Act 3,
Scene 4):

\begin{verse}
``Thou findest it hard\\
that the storm presses right in to the skin?\\
for thee perhaps; but where a greater malady\\
has taken root, the lesser is scarcely felt.\\
--- --- --- When the mind\\
is free, the body senses every pain;\\
but the storm in my soul has taken away\\
all other feeling from my senses\\
save that which beats here in the heart.''
\end{verse}
% TR: Høffding quotes Lear III.iv in a Danish verse translation; rendered
% literally here from the Danish, not replaced by Shakespeare's English.

In a strongly excited state of consciousness even impressions strong in
themselves may be unable to win any place in consciousness. The ecstatic
enthusiasm of the martyrs must have weakened the sensations of their
sufferings. Likewise hypochondria and mental derangement can prevent
painful sensations from arising; the fixed idea that takes possession of
consciousness can be so strong that no other impression is able to
attract attention.

\emph{Fechner} has sought a mathematical formula for the relation in
which the effect of every impression is determined by the impressions
previously received. The increase in the strength of the sensation that
is brought about by growth in the strength of the impression is
determined not by the absolute magnitude of this growth, but by the
ratio in which it stands to the impression already present. The stars
send out just as much light by day as by night; but by day their
brightness is for us vanishingly small in relation to the sun's, while
by night they have at most only the moon to compete with. For the
sensation to grow by a certain degree, the impression must grow by the
more degrees the stronger it already is. This
% --- p. 133 ---
\opage{133}Fechner expresses in his ``fundamental formula'': dγ = dβ/β,
where γ denotes the sensation, β the impression. This formula can be
converted into what Fechner calls the ``measurement formula'': γ = log.
β, which means that in order for the strength of the sensation, e.g., to
rise from 1 to 2, and from 2 to 3, etc., the strength of the impression
must rise from 10 to 100, and from 100 to 1000; or in other words, the
strength of the impression must increase in geometric progression for
the strength of the sensation to increase in arithmetic progression.

We disregard here Fechner's presupposition that the just noticeable
increments in sensations are equal (cf.\ I, 8 d), as well as his own
interpretation of the measurement formula as the expression of the
fundamental relation between spirit and matter (cf.\ II, 9 d). The
question here is merely whether his formulas are really confirmed by
experience. Fechner himself at once admitted that Weber's law, expressed
in the fundamental formula, holds only for adequate impressions, i.e.\
those neither too strong nor too weak. There is a definite limit to the
receptivity of the sense organs, and when two unequally strong
impressions exceed this limit, the difference between them will, in the
nature of the case, be impossible to apprehend. The lower limit of the
law's validity is caused by the fact that the internal state of the
organ and of the nervous system comes to play a part with very weak
impressions. In the case of the eye there operates here the so-called
``intrinsic light,'' which arises without stimulation from outside, a
kind of ``normal hallucination.''\footnote{``The black that we see in
the dark and with closed eyes is a sensation of sight that takes place
without external stimulation, and must not be confused with the
not-seeing that holds in the case of the finger or the back of the head,
nor be compared with not-hearing in the absence of external noise. The
black we have in the closed eye is only the same sensation of light that
we have at the sight of a black surface, which can pass through all
gradations over into the strongest sensation of light; indeed, the inner
black of the eye sometimes passes, even from purely inner causes, over
into clear light, and contains, so to speak, interspersed luminous
phenomena. --- On closer attention one discovers in the black of the
closed eye a kind of fine luminous dust, which in different persons and
in different states of the eye is present in different quantity, and in
morbid states can be heightened into vivid luminous phenomena.''
\emph{Fechner}: Elemente der Psychophysik. I. p.~165 f.} Nor do the
other senses seem ever to
% --- p. 134 ---
\opage{134}be entirely without subjective sensations,\footnote{See G. E.
\emph{Müller}: Zur Grundlegung der Psychophysik. Berlin. 1878.
p.~236--239. Cf.\ for the sense of hearing \emph{Hensen}: Physiologie
des Gehörs. (Hermann. III, 2). p.~121 f.} which play a more important
part only when the impressions from outside are very weak. But even for
adequate impressions the correctness of Weber's law has been denied; it
seems to have undisputed approximate validity only for impressions of
middle strength in the domain of the sense of sight, the muscular sense,
and perhaps
% footnote **) on p. 134
the sense of hearing.\footnote{G. E. \emph{Müller}. p.~225.} In the work
in which Fechner has answered his critics, the old man of genius also
declares that just as surely as Weber's law, with the laws following
from it, can within certain limits and in several domains of sensation
provide a useful starting point for judging the relations of measure of
sensation on the basis of its dependence on the impression, just as
surely it cannot provide anything of the kind beyond certain limits and
applied to any domain whatever. The question then is only whether it is
possible to point out special reasons for the deviations from the law
that take place in several domains of sense.\footnote{In Sachen der
Psychophysik. Leipzig 1877. p.~212.}

But whether or not it proves possible to carry through a mathematical
formula, all experience indicates that the strength of sensations is
determined by their mutual relation. Every emerging sensation has a
struggle to go through with other possibilities of sensation, and on the
outcome of this struggle depends the strength with which it appears in
consciousness.

\textbf{5.} From a purely psychological point of view no sharp line can
be drawn between the intensity of the sensation and its
\emph{quality}. The contrast between light and dark, between white, gray
and black, is for subjective sensation just as much qualitative as the
contrast between red, yellow and blue. Just as there are countless
nuances and transitions between white and black, so too there is
continuous transition between the color tones among themselves. Just as
we place gray between white and black, so we place, e.g., orange between
red and yellow; and just as white passes over through a connected series
of nuances into gray, so red into orange.

% --- p. 135 ---
\opage{135}Not from a psychological, but from a purely physical point of
view the sensations of light and dark stand in contrast to the color
sensations. Physically regarded, namely, the sensation of black is
characterized by the eye's receiving no rays of light; black is thus
here something entirely negative, in contrast to which gray and white
express the weaker or stronger action of light. But one has no right to
force this physical point of view on psychology. It was the feeling of
this that drove \emph{Goethe} to so bitter and unjust a polemic against
the physical theory of light. In our day E. \emph{Hering} and Aug.
\emph{Classen} have maintained that, psychologically and physiologically
regarded, Goethe was right. The mere fact that black can become the
content of a sensation at all is enough to decide the matter; if the
physical view exhausted the relation, black would denote the same as
non-sensation (and so non-consciousness). Yet everyone senses something
peculiar in itself when he has the sensation of black. Not to speak of
how deeply the contrast between light and dark reaches into the life of
feeling!

An eminent physiologist, to be sure, says in his polemic against Hering:
``I find in my consciousness only a quantitative change of one and the
same sensation when I gradually diminish the illumination of a white
sheet of paper in the field of vision until at last it looks black, or
when in some other way I let all the gradations follow one another from
gray to black. I find this process entirely analogous to the one in
which a definite clang is heard with decreasing intensity until at last
it vanishes into complete silence.''\footnote{A. \emph{Fick}: Die Lehre
von der Lichtempfindung. (Hermann. III, 1. p.~205.} It would be
interesting to know whether an observer who knew nothing at all of the
results of physical optics would judge in the same way. Psychologically
regarded, \emph{Hering} is fully justified in demanding that the opposite
procedure also be applied, namely to start from black as a positive
starting point and, with increasing illumination, to pass over to gray
and white. The question is, on the whole, whether we do not sense all
differences of strength as qualitative. The difference between a weaker
and a stronger tone, a weaker and a stronger pressure, etc., is for our
sensation of the same
% --- p. 136 ---
\opage{136}kind as that between two colors. He who follows the
gradations from red to orange with strained attention, so that he skips
nothing, will likewise be able to find in his consciousness that it is
one and the same sensation whose quantity merely changes. Let us suppose
that, just as there are eyes that are able to apprehend only the
difference between light and dark, there were eyes that sensed only the
difference between red and orange: would it not, from the standpoint of
such a consciousness, be justified to call this difference a mere
difference of strength?

\emph{Goethe's} great mistake was that he wanted to force the
psychological point of view on physics. He wanted to derive all colors
from combinations of white and black. With increasing illumination all
