colors pass over into white, with decreasing into black. But this shows
only that intensity and quality stand in a definite interaction
physically regarded as well, not that one has a right to derive all
nuances of color from white and black. Physiologically too the
difference between the series of color tones and the degrees of
intensity of light must be maintained, inasmuch as different processes
seem to underlie them. The yellow spot (\textit{fovea centralis}) on
the retina is the proper place for the reception of color impressions;
already a few millimeters from it not all color tones can be
apprehended, but only yellow and blue. Still further out toward the
periphery of the retina no color tones are apprehended, but only the
difference between light and dark. In total color-blindness what
normally holds only for the outermost parts of the retina holds for the
whole retina (the yellow spot included). From this it is concluded that
the processes in the elements of the retina that correspond to the
sensation of colorless light must be independent of those that
correspond
% footnote *) on p. 136
to the sensation of color.\footnote{\emph{Fick}. p.~206--207. ---
\emph{Wundt}. I. p.~432 f.} The lowest animals in which organs for the
perception of light are thought to have been shown can no doubt (like
human beings with congenital cataract) sense only the difference between
light and
% footnote **) on p. 136
dark.\footnote{\emph{Emmert}: Die Organe des Sehens in den
verschiedenen Thierkreisen. Bern 1872. p.~14 ff.} In the first years of
childhood the color sense is very little developed; only glaring color
tones
% --- p. 137 ---
\opage{137}attract attention.\footnote{\emph{Vierordt}: Physiologie des
Kinderalters. p.~154. --- W. \emph{Preyer}: Die Seele des Kindes.
Leipzig 1882. p.~6.} But because the physical difference between
strength and quality also becomes of importance for physiology, it does
not follow that physiology can place the sensations of white and black
(or the corresponding physiological processes) outside the domain of
qualitative phenomena. Even if the organ of sight receives no
stimulation from outside, a process can nevertheless take place in it to
which there corresponds a sensation just as positive as any other. We
have already remarked that inner irritations seem constantly to take
place in the sense organs, which come forward the more, the weaker the
external stimulations are. Here, then, a positive process takes place,
and it is no doubt this process which, by its contrast with the process
arising from stimulation by rays of light, corresponds to the sensation
of black or dark. The purest black is apprehended only beside the purest
white and in contrast to it. During a stay in a completely dark room, on
the other hand, the sensations of the resting eye acquire a fairly
considerable brightness and become almost as much akin to white as to
black. The sensation of black proper therefore arises only under the
influence of external impressions of light, namely as a contrast
sensation to the
% footnote **) on p. 137
sensation awakened by them.\footnote{\emph{Hering}: Zur Lehre vom
Lichtsinne. 2 Aufl. Wien 1878. p.~62 ff. --- Cf.\ above p.~133.}

As it is with the sensation of black, so it is in general with the
sensation of quiet and rest.\footnote{Cf.\ G. H. \emph{Schneider}: Die
Empfindung der Ruhe. Zürich. 1876.} If there were absolute quiet outside
us and within us, we should never get the sensation of rest. It is only
through the contrast with motion and change that the sensation of rest
comes into being. As a sensation it is just as positive as the sensation
of a change or a motion: it is precisely the sensation of the change
itself from the state of motion to the state of rest. When this
transition is made suddenly, it can act very strongly. A sudden pause
in a noisy piece of music startles us. The miller wakes when the mill
stops, the sleeping traveler when the carriage halts. An entirely
% --- p. 138 ---
\opage{138}continuous motion would be sensed as a state of rest, and
would in general be sensed only when it came into contrast with
interrupted and irregular motion.

However it may stand, then, with the relation between the strength and
the quality of sensations, the same fundamental law holds in both
respects. The peculiarities of sensations that are qualitative in the
special sense are also determined by their relation to one another. The
\emph{law of contrast} holds both for quality and for strength. Just as
light is apprehended most sharply in contrast to dark, so red comes out
most definitely beside blue-green, blue beside orange, green beside
purple, yellow beside indigo, violet beside yellow-green. It is the
complementary colors (i.e.\ those whose rays when mixed produce the
sensation of white light) that stand in strongest contrast to one
another and thereby make one another appear in the most pronounced way.
On the other hand, red, e.g., looks different when it is viewed beside
other colors: it here always approaches the complementary color of the
color in question. A gray strip on a colored ground, when covered with
thin transparent paper, takes on a tinge of the complementary color of
the ground. Besides this \emph{simultaneous contrast}, the contrast
effect between two successive impressions (\emph{successive contrast})
also plays a great part. When the eye has dwelt a while on a definite
color impression, it is thereby disposed to an especially pronounced
apprehension of the complementary color. This is seen from the fact that
when the eye, after a strong red color impression, is directed to a
white surface, it sees blue-green. By the union of simultaneous and
successive contrast the most pronounced sensation of a color arises.
Without our noticing it, these contrast effects take part in all our
sensations of color. We seldom keep our gaze fixed on a single point;
even a quite brief fixation costs great effort. After-images from the
one point of the field of vision therefore gain influence on the
apprehension of others. The most intricate combinations of successive
and simultaneous contrast come in. If we keep our gaze firmly fixed on a
single point, the after-images coincide with the objects and make
% --- p. 139 ---
\opage{139}them indistinct; we feel dazzled, and an irresistible impulse
stirs to change the position of the
% footnote *) on p. 139
eye.\footnote{\emph{Helmholtz}: Physiol. Optik. p.~389. ---
\emph{Panum}: Sandserne og de vilkaarlige Bevægelser [The Senses and the
Voluntary Movements]. p.~209 f. --- Examples can also be taken from the
sensation of taste. The taste of cheese heightens, in the opinion of
many, the taste for wine. After bitter or salty sensations of taste,
water tastes sweetish.}

We see how little the single sensation can be isolated: both
simultaneous and preceding impressions determine it. \emph{Helmholtz}
goes so far as to explain the phenomena of contrast by false inferences.
This expression, which he himself moreover declares to be half
figurative, does not fit, since --- as \emph{Wundt} has aptly remarked
(II. p.~457) --- it presupposes that we possess an absolute standard of
\emph{correct} quality of sensation, whereas the truth is that
\emph{every} determination of quality is relative. The determination of
a normal or typical nuance that we fix for each color has only practical
significance. It is difficult to find linguistic expression for
phenomena that, like those brought forward here, lie at the border of
the life of consciousness; the psychological expressions denote higher
and more intricate processes of consciousness. But here in the border
region we nevertheless find processes that form an elementary analogy
to the higher psychical activities. This naturally does not exclude
that these same processes must also be explained physiologically.
According to the fundamental conception at which we arrived in chap.\
II, every psychological phenomenon should, on the contrary, be viewed
from both sides. Physiologically the dispute here can only concern
whether the end organs or the central organs play the larger part;
probably both cooperate.

\textbf{6.} The doctrine of sensations thus confirms the general
characterization of consciousness that we have given earlier (II, 5).
It is impossible to resolve consciousness into a series of single and
independent sensations, each with its own strength and quality. From
the very hour of each single sensation's birth, its existence and its
properties are determined by its relation to other sensations. A
sensation that stands in no relation to any other sensation we do not
know: it would not be an element in any consciousness, and so could
under no conditions be known.

% --- p. 140 ---
\opage{140}Entirely uniform impressions and states therefore do not
become objects of consciousness. The \emph{pressure} of the air we do
not notice, except when it varies. We take part in the earth's so rapid
motion without knowing it, because it is constant. We do not notice that
the blood vessels of the retina cast shadows on the retina itself,
because the retina has always been accustomed to this; these shadows are
noticed, however, when one artificially lets them fall on parts of the
retina that are accustomed to stronger
% footnote *) on p. 140
impressions of light.\footnote{\emph{Helmholtz}: Physiologische Optik.
p.~161,} A substance has a taste only when its taste differs from that
of the saliva. To the saliva itself the tongue is accustomed, and is
thereby dulled to its faint,
% footnote **) on p. 140
saltish taste.\footnote{\emph{Henle}: Ueber den Geschmacksinn.
(Anthropologische Vorträge. 2tes Heft). p.~18.} A sound always ringing
alike, or a color always uniform, that surrounded us from the beginning
of life to its end, we should not sense, if we had no other impressions
against whose background they could stand out. If, as Pythagoras taught,
there were a harmony of the spheres, produced by the motion of the stars
in their orbits, ordinary mortals (and with others psychology cannot
concern itself) would certainly not hear it. In recent years attention
has been drawn to the remarkable circumstance that the writers of
antiquity (the Vedas and Homer, e.g.) name only the radiant and bright
\emph{colors}, and from this it has even been sought to conclude that
the human race in those times still had only the ability to apprehend
the contrast between light and dark, not the differences of the color
tones. This improbable theory (which is already refuted by the fact that
the Egyptians depicted the blue sky) is unnecessary, since the fact
pointed out is far more naturally explained by the law of contrast or
of relation. The blue sky, which spread out before the gaze the whole
day through, must have made less impression than the splendid play of
colors of the dawn and the evening glow; red and yellow fruits must have
awakened stronger sensations than the everlasting green. ``Even our
peasants as a rule hardly show such admiration for the color of the sky
and the meadow as we town-dwellers, who only seldom have occasion to
enjoy in full draughts the blue and the green in nature, and whose color
sense, through the strengthening rest in the colorless rays of the
walls, the pavements
% --- p. 141 ---
\opage{141}and the papers, has acquired an increased
% footnote *) on p. 141
receptivity.''\footnote{V. \emph{Krenchel}: Farvesansen og dens
Tilstand i Oldtiden [The Color Sense and Its Condition in Antiquity].
(Tidsskr. for popul. Fremst. af Naturvidenskaben. 1879). p.~153.}

The \emph{law of relation} agrees with the principal proposition of
nerve-physiology, that not a constant state, but only a change setting
in with a certain suddenness, awakens the nerve process. The preceding
state in the organism and in consciousness thus forms the background for
the following one. The contrast effect in the narrower sense is only a
particularly striking example of what to a certain degree does and must
take place in every sensation. We overlook it so easily only partly when
the degree of contrast is slight, partly when two sensations lie at so
great a distance from each other that we do not put them together: we
have then forgotten the intermediate terms. The sharply and definitely
appearing sensations are built up out of a multitude of small sensations
of difference.

The difference or relation can be either simultaneous or successive.
But the successive relation is the more primitive. Simultaneous
sensations have a tendency to fuse (especially in the domain of the
senses of touch, hearing and smell); and since, on the other hand, our
attention, like our bodily eye, does not keep still but wanders from
point to point, a composite impression too will naturally be apprehended
successively. There is the further point that successive apprehension is
more distinct than simultaneous. Small differences of weight are more
easily apprehended by successive weighing in the same hand than by
simultaneous weighing in both hands; the temperature of two liquids is
better compared by dipping the same hand successively into both than by
dipping both hands simultaneously, each into its own
liquid.\footnote{E. H. \emph{Weber}: Tastsinn und Gemeingefühl.
(Wagners physiol. Handwörterbuch. III, 2). p.~544 ff,} Very faint
shadows are apprehended only
% footnote ***) on p. 141
when the source of light is moved.\footnote{\emph{Fechner}: Elemente der
Psychophysik. I. p.~174.} Finally, it can be said outright to follow
from the general physiology of the sensations that successive
differences must be apprehended more easily and more strongly than
simultaneously existing ones. ``Supposing that the apprehension of
difference rests directly on change in the nerve process or life
process, the intensity of the former must grow with the intensity of the
latter\ldots\ Of
% --- p. 142 ---
\opage{142}simultaneous states, however, one will not say that on account
of the simultaneous difference they indicate a \emph{change} in the life
process of the same kind and degree as successive states; for they can
do so only, first, when each of them forms a contrast to preceding
states, that is, when they have a successive character, and secondly,
when they act modifyingly on new changes. The simultaneous states are,
in their mutual relation, to be regarded as \emph{indirect}
changes.''\footnote{G. H. \emph{Schneider}: Warum bemerken wir mässig
bewegte Dinge leichter als ruhende? (Vierteljahrsschr. für wissensch.
Philos. II.). p.~411.}

No distinction, then, can be carried through between absolute and
relative sensation, or between sensations and differences of sensation.
This consequence of the psychology and physiology of sensation is,
however, still disputed, even by an investigator like \emph{Fechner},
who has done so much to maintain the importance of the law of relation.
``Certainly,'' says Fechner (In Sachen der Psychophysik. p.~114 f.),
``since we never have sensations of a certain kind or strength without
preceding or simultaneous ones of another kind or strength, no
experimental proof can be given that there would be sensations at all if
these did not precede them or go along with them; only I find neither
any theoretical reason nor any reason from experience that could forbid
assuming it; therefore I believe --- but the opposite view too can rest
only on a belief --- that if a child woke for the first time into an
entirely uniform brightness, so that all other sense-impressions were as
far as possible kept away (which, to be sure, is not completely
possible), it would nevertheless sense the brightness of the light.''
Fechner has rightly seen that one must go back to the first sensation,
that is, to the beginning of consciousness, in order to get beyond the
law of relation. Yet his example does not denote any absolute
beginning; for the child that wakes to the brightness of the light has
had general sensations, sensations of movement and perhaps other
sensations before it gets the sensation of light; this sensation thus
gets those more indefinite sensations as its background, and thereby
appears with a characteristic mark different from all subsequent
% --- p. 143 ---
\opage{143}sensations of light. Perhaps it was even prepared by processes
in the organ of sight awakened from within, so that the threshold had
already been crossed. The impression of light, moreover (even if it were
absolutely uniform in itself), does not act alike on the organ of sight
in two successive moments; in the first moment there will set in a
bewilderment which only gradually gives place to seeing proper; --- but
in this successive transition the law of relation asserts itself, since
the state at each moment is determined by the preceding one. So much is
certain, that the more a psychical state approaches absolute unity, or
rather simplicity, the more we approach the border of consciousness. The
phenomena of mysticism, hypnotism and other abnormal
% footnote *) on p. 143
states\footnote{»Ich habe von einer intelligenten und mir nahe
stehenden Kranken, welche zeitweise, in Folge einer durch trockenen
Katarrh bei Emphysem bewirkten venösen Blutüberfüllung, beziehungsweiser
Blutstauung im Gehirn, sehr benommen war und ganz weg zu sein schien,
einen Zustand schildern hören, in den sie dabei wohl gerieth, und der
sehr merkwürdig war. Es war dabei von einem Sich-in-sich-Unterscheiden,
ja überhaupt von einem weiterm Unterscheiden gar nicht die Rede, aber
doch wusste, richtiger gesagt, fühlte oder empfand sich die Kranke
irgendwie noch als etwas, das sie nicht näher definiren konnte. Es sei
eben ein dunkles Etwas gewesen, ein Gefühl etwa von dunkler Existenz
eines runden Klumpens, dem nichts gegenüber stand, nicht einmal das
Nichts.« \emph{Koch}: Ueber das Unterscheidungsvermögen. (Zeitschr. für
Philosophie u. philos. Kritik. Neue Folge. 79ter Bd.). p.~78. --- On
analysis of this description one will easily see that the state was not
entirely simple.} establish this.
In consequence of the law of relation, then,
we must assert that so long as consciousness still exists, every nuance
and every rhythm cannot have vanished. (Cf.\ with this III, 7 and 9).

An important consequence of the law of relation is that it becomes
impossible to distinguish between the matter and the form of our
knowledge in the way Kant attempted. He started from the presupposition
that ``that in which the sensations are ordered and placed in certain
forms cannot itself be sensation,'' and therefore set up the forms, as
the expression of the receptivity of consciousness, in contrast to the
mere sensations as the matter received. But there is no entirely
unformed matter in our consciousness, since there is no
% --- p. 144 ---
\opage{144}entirely pure sensation, uninfluenced by other sensations.
There are only differences of degree in this respect. Only by way of
abstraction do we approach mere sensation, the unformed elements of
matter. One of Kant's most acute disciples, \emph{Salomon Maimon}, had
already shown this. ``Sensation is a modification of the faculty of
knowledge which sets in merely through being acted upon (without
spontaneity); but this is a \emph{mere idea}, \emph{which we constantly
approach by the diminution of consciousness}.''\footnote{Versuch über
die Transscendentalphilosophie. Berlin 1790. p.~168.} Just as there is no
absolute matter in our consciousness, so there is no absolute passivity
either. The mutual interaction between the sensations is at the same
time the most elementary activity of consciousness itself. We see
already here the germ of what at a higher stage of development appears
as memory and thought. \emph{Elementary memory} shows itself in the
preservation of earlier impressions, so that they can modify the later
ones. An \emph{elementary process of comparison} we have in the way in
which every new sensation is referred to other simultaneous or preceding
sensations and assessed by them.

The moment of activity that thus comes forward already in this
elementary domain shows itself still more distinctly when we consider
the significance of movement for sense-perception.

\textbf{7.} We have so far kept to the sensations, or the elements of
sensation, as merely given and received. We must now correct the account
given of the fundamental laws of sense-sensations by showing that we are
not passive in the origin of the sense-sensation. In the first place,
the organism is itself a little world with a certain independence over
against the great one. From the activities in its own interior come (in
the general sensations) important impressions. Nutrition, the
circulation of the blood and respiration go their way to a certain
degree independently of what goes on outside; and these inner processes
awaken stronger or weaker sensations. In the second place, the organism
does not wait for the impressions to come to it from the surrounding
world, but makes movements that bring sensations with them. Even before
actual sensations arise, movements are, in the opinion of some, made as
% --- p. 145 ---
\opage{145}a consequence of the nerve centers' wealth of potential
energy. These movements, psychologically regarded spontaneous or
unmotivated, can themselves become the cause of peculiar sensations:
sensations of energy and muscular sensations.

The change or transition that every act of consciousness presupposes or
consists in can thus be of either an active or a passive nature.
Impressions do not merely come to us, but we ourselves also produce
impressions. And the active changes perhaps even precede the passive in
time, since consciousness itself first arises and develops when the
reflex and instinctive movements undergo an arrest. (Cf.\ IV, 4--6).

The sensations of movement are of different kinds. Psychologically
regarded they fall into two main groups: sensation of effort (sensation
of energy) and sensation of the state of the muscle at the moment. The
first kind of sensation is bound up with an activity proceeding from the
seat of conscious life. The second can have causes that are not
connected with such an activity; for to it belong not only the sensation
of fatigue, but also the sensation accompanying spasmodic contraction of
muscles that are not subject to the influence of the higher nerve
centers (cramp in the calves; colic; labor pains; according to Weber the
contraction of the muscles of the empty stomach calls forth a part of
the sensation we call hunger).

As regards the sensation of energy, it has been much disputed to what
physiological process it is bound. Some have held that the motor
impulse proceeding from the brain became an object of sensation already
in the brain itself, since sensory fibers from the
% footnote *) on p. 145
motor centers\footnote{Whether these are found in the cerebrum or only
in the ganglia lying below it.} immediately bring every movement
initiated to consciousness. We should then have here a kind of
sensations not due to impulses from outside the brain itself. Already
Joh.\ \emph{Müller}\footnote{Handbuch der Physiologie. Koblents 1840.
II. p.~500.} adduced in favor of such a view that we can adjust and
measure out in advance the degree of effort that is necessary for a
certain movement (lifting a weight, climbing a height, etc.), and that
the certainty of
% --- p. 146 ---
\opage{146}powerlessness is different from the sensation of fatigue.
Even if one embraces this view, one need not deny the cooperation of
impressions that derive from the muscle itself and its surroundings.
From such impressions many derive all sensation of energy and muscular
sensation, and this explanation has in the last few years gained
support especially through Sachs's discovery of sensory nerves that run
from the interior of the muscles to the
% footnote *) on p. 146
higher centers.\footnote{C. \emph{Sachs}: Physiologische und
anatomische Untersuchungen über die sensiblen Nerven der Muskeln.
(Reichert und du Bois-Reymond: Archiv für Anatomie, Physiologie und
wissensch. Medicin). 1874.} On this view, then, the sensation of energy
would arise only when word comes back from the muscle to the brain of
the degree of contraction that has taken place; the innervation itself
would not come to consciousness. \emph{Lotze} had already shown the
probability of this view by an apt observation. When a limb has gone to
sleep, so that its nerve trunk has become unable to conduct owing to
compression, we do indeed sense the limb as a passive and heavy mass,
but cannot move it, and despite all effort of will we get no
% footnote **) on p. 146
sensation of movement either.\footnote{Medicinische Psychologie.
p.~310.}

Several facts show that the central sensation of energy is in any case
imperfect and needs support and correction. It can, as pathological
cases establish, be replaced by other senses. Patients who had lost the
muscular sense in the arm could still carry objects when they had their
eyes fixed on them; as soon as they turned their gaze away, they
% footnote ***) on p. 146
dropped what they were carrying.\footnote{\emph{Maudsley}: Physiol. de
l’esprit. Trad. de l’angl. par A. Herzen. p.~455.} The sensation of
energy is, on the other hand, not enough in itself when it is not
supported by other sensations. A person who had entirely lost the sense
of touch asserted, while her eyes were closed, that she had clenched her
hand, raised her arm and the like, without this being in fact the case.
With open eyes, on the other hand, she could close and open her hand
very \emph{well} and surely,
% footnote ****) on p. 146
etc.\footnote{\emph{Exner}: Physiol. der Grosshirnrinde. (Hermann. II,
2). p.~248.} Finally, there are experiments showing that the sense of
energy functions
% --- p. 147 ---
\opage{147}equally well whether the muscle is set in motion by the will
or by electrical stimulation; here, then, all reason for assuming a
special central consciousness of innervation seems to
disappear.\footnote{Cf.\ \emph{Sachs}, op.\ cit., p.~185.}

The sensation in itself tells us nothing about whether its physiological
source is central or peripheral. Whichever of the two assumptions one
embraces, it is at any rate certain that the sensation of energy is
bound up with muscular movement. From the examples adduced we learn only
that in our sensation we have no absolutely sure criterion of what goes
on in reality. In elementary form we come up here against a problem that
will occupy us more fully later. We believe that we have an immediate
sensation of how the impulse of the will flows over into the muscle; but
if the theory of the peripheral origin of the sensation of energy is
% footnote **) on p. 147
correct,\footnote{Cf.\ the misgivings expressed by \emph{Sigm. Mayer}:
Specielle Nervenphysiologie (Hermann II, 1) p.~220--222.} it is not the
impulse of the will itself, but its effect, of which we become
conscious.

\textbf{8.} In almost all sense-sensations the movements of the
organism play an important part, so that sensations of energy or
muscular sensations prove on closer examination to be elements in
states that on superficial estimate we take to be entirely simple. In
taste the movement of the tongue is of importance; solid parts of the
food are pressed against the hard palate and only thereby become
tastable. The sensation of smell arises only when the air is drawn up
through the nose. If one holds back the air, all sensation of smell
ceases, even if one is in a strongly scented atmosphere. In hearing we
move the body, or at any rate the head, until we find the position in
which the sound is heard most strongly. In attentive listening the
muscles of the eardrum seem to contract. It is, however, especially in
sight and the sense of touch that movement is of great importance. The
eye must be accommodated to the distance of the object, which happens by
contraction of the fine muscles by which the surfaces of the lens are
made more convex; this change takes place with a certain effort. In
every definite position of the eye some of the eye muscles are actively
shortened, others passively stretched; with
% --- p. 148 ---
\opage{148}every position of the eye, therefore, a certain sensation of
energy or muscular sensation is bound up. We move the eye, or perhaps
the whole head, until the impression of light we want to apprehend falls
on the place of distinctest vision (the yellow spot). The fineness of
the sense of touch at the different places of the body stands in a
definite relation to the mobility of the part of the body in question;
it is (according to \emph{Weber}) greatest on the tongue, lips and
fingers, least on the chest and back. It is by the help of the movable
parts that the sense of touch becomes of such great importance for
living beings; by them active experimenting becomes possible. Touch and
sight, the senses most bound up with movement, are at the same time our
most important means of orientation with regard to the relations of the
surrounding world.

We are thus not cast out as a purely passive prey to the impressions of
the surrounding world. In the spontaneous and reflex movements that go
on before the awakening of consciousness, an active nature already
manifests itself. The impressions from outside, moreover, soon call
forth movements that serve to hold them fast and follow them. An active
turning toward the impression takes place here, as when the infant
follows or seeks the light with its head or with its
% footnote *) on p. 148
eyes.\footnote{``A male child born in the seventh month of pregnancy I
saw, on the second day of life, late in the evening in the twilight,
repeatedly turn its head, which had been turned away from the window,
toward the window and the light, even when its position was changed. It
was undoubtedly seeking the light.'' \emph{Kussmaul}: Untersuchungen
über das Seelenleben des neugeborenen Menschen. p.~26. --- Only later,
according to \emph{Preyer} (Die Seele des Kindes. p.~31) on the 23rd day
of life, do the child's eyes follow the light without movement of the
head.} An involuntary seeking and adapting conditions the character of
the sense-sensations. How primitive this first form of attention is can
be seen from the fact that a pigeon deprived of the cerebrum turned its
head after a light that was moved. Attention here, then, still bears the
stamp of reflex movement. A conscious concentration of attention
presupposes a certain development of the ability to remember and to
imagine independently of purely momentary impressions, and is therefore
found only at a higher stage than the one we are considering here.

The sensation of attention is closely akin to the sensation of energy or
muscular sensation, and is perhaps connected
% --- p. 149 ---
\opage{149}with the fact that a stronger or weaker contraction takes place
in the muscles belonging to the organ in question. ``When we turn
attention from one domain of sense to another, we have at the same time
a definite sensation of the altered direction, not describable, but
easily reproduced by anyone from experience, which we can designate as
the sensation of a differently localized tension. We feel a tension
directed forward in the eye, one directed sideways in the ears; it grows
with the attention\ldots\ In a corresponding way the sensation is
localized with regard to the different sense organs when we want to
smell, taste, feel something
% footnote *) on p. 149
exactly.''\footnote{\emph{Fechner}: Elemente der Psychophysik. II.
p.~475.}

Even direct, subjective sensation therefore refutes Condillac's theory
that attention is only an exclusive
% footnote **) on p. 149
sensation.\footnote{Logique. II, 7.} When a sensation wholly occupies us
and is about to crowd out everything else in our consciousness, it also
takes possession of our activity. An exclusive sensation thus
presupposes attention; it is not one with it. Besides: how does a
sensation become exclusive? Impressions can come to us from several
sides at the same time. The eye, e.g., apprehends impressions from
several points of light at the same time. Several senses can moreover
act at once. As purely passive, sense-perception would yield a chaotic
multiplicity of sensations in each single moment. But out of this
multiplicity, in each little moment, a single one is taken out, which
becomes the center. Reflexively and instinctively attention moves from
the one impression to the other and endeavors to form an ordered series
of sensations. As we have seen above, succession is apprehended more
easily and earlier than simultaneity; this seems to be connected with
the great importance of movement for
% footnote ***) on p. 149 (continues on p. 150)
sense-perception.\footnote{\emph{Condillac}, despite his own
definition, acknowledges the independence of the element of attention
when he says (loc.\ cit.): «Lorsqu’une campagne s’offre à ma vue, je
vois tout d’un premier coup d’œil, et je ne discerne rien encore. Pour
démêler differens objets et me faire une idée distincte de leur forme et
de leur situation, il faut que j’arrête \emph{mes regards} au chacun
d’eux... Ce regard est \emph{une action par laquelle mon} œil
\emph{tend} à l’objet... par cette raison je lui donne le nom
d’attention; et il m’est évident que cette \emph{direction} de l’organe
est toute la part que le corps peut avoir à 1’attention.« ---} --- The
motive for attention's leaving one
% --- p. 150 ---
\opage{150}impression and turning to another must be sought in a
sensation of fatigue or a feeling of dullness. (Cf.\ above p.~138).
In every such transition an \emph{elementary choice} takes place.


\section*{B. Idea}\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{B. Idea}

\textbf{1.} In the interaction of sensations and in involuntary attention,
the unity and activity of consciousness still manifest themselves only in a
purely elementary way; we there stood before phenomena so simple that we
were even short of psychological terms for them. We now pass beyond the
domain of pure sensations, as we bring forward the fact that new
impressions can be determined and altered not merely by simultaneous and
immediately preceding sensations, but also by sensations that lie further
back in time. This happens through the new impression's reawakening
earlier sensations. The presupposition for this is that the impressions
repeat themselves. A consciousness that passed \emph{over} from A to B,
from B to C, and so on, always to new impressions, would not get beyond the
elementary stage described in the foregoing. But no consciousness is so
constituted. The beings endowed with consciousness have their definite
conditions of life, which not only make existence possible but also narrow
the horizon, since the series of impressions cannot run on to infinity,
but unfolds with a certain rhythm. Both within and outside the organism the
same circumstances recur with more or less regularity. Life pulsates, and
external nature likewise presents a rhythmical alternation. The most
elementary real consciousness would be one in which there was found only a
rhythmical alternation of pleasure and displeasure, with simple sensations
of sense and of movement attached to it. When, now, the series of
sensations had come, e.g., to D, A would enter again --- at least as
A$_{2}$, since the sensation perhaps did not have quite the same strength
or quality. It will then
% --- p. 151 ---
\opage{151}have a tendency to reproduce the same state as took place
before B. It gains by this repetition, as it profits from the traces that
A$_{1}$ has left behind. It is the law of exercise, valid for all organic
life, that is at work here. Every function goes more easily through
repetition and exercise. This holds especially for the functions of the
nervous system; indeed, all practice of compound movements is really
exercise of the central nervous system. The nerve-paths become, so to
speak, more passable the more often they are traveled. The repeated
sensation thereby acquires a mark of its own, and stands with a peculiar
character over against other sensations, which are, so to speak, measured
only against one another, but not against themselves.

\emph{Reproduction} here does not yet become a real and definite
recollection. For the reawakened state fuses immediately with the new one,
does not come forward independently, but serves only to take from the new
impression its stamp of strangeness. It is a kind of \emph{instinctive or
automatic recognition} that takes place here. On such recognition rests all
perception (\textit{Perception}), in so far as it is different from mere
sensation. Perception thus arises through the fusion of new elements of
consciousness with earlier ones of a similar kind. To perception there
therefore belongs an involuntary classification, a referring of new
impressions to older ones, an involuntary and primitive judgment, which of
course is not at all worked out in conceptual fashion. ``This is red!''
means nothing more than: ``this impression of color is of the same kind as
a certain one received earlier''. In so simple a form as we have here
described it for the sake of clarity, perception hardly occurs in any
accessible example. We can, on the other hand, think of the difference
there is between the first and the second reading of a book, or the first
and the second time one hears a piece of music. The partial tones in a
sound are heard more easily the more vivid the memory is of the sensation
of the tones taken singly; if this memory has quite disappeared, the
single tones fuse entirely together for the
% footnote *) on p. 151
ear.\footnote{\emph{Helmholtz}: Die Lehre von den Tonempfindungen.
Braunschweig 1863. p.~110.} When no more than 10 seconds passed between two
sensations of weight, E. H. \emph{Weber} could
% --- p. 152 ---
\opage{152}distinguish between 29 and 30 loth; but after an interval of
half a minute he could perceive only the difference between 24 and 30
loth. With the real sensations there unite the second time --- without our
becoming aware of it --- the after-effects of the first time, and thereby
everything stands clearer and more familiar in the immediate apprehension
itself. Infants and operated persons born blind must therefore learn to
see colors; the first impressions of color can in the nature of the case
be referred only to other kinds of sensations, not to sensations of the
same kind. Well known is the story of the blind man to whom one tried to
describe the color scarlet, and who at last exclaimed: ``It must be
something like the blast of a trumpet!'' As with every entirely new
impression, the element of feeling will therefore also have the
preponderance; only through repetition can knowledge awaken. Repetition is
not the same as continuation, but presupposes an interruption, whereby the
contrast with the intervening sensations (B, C, D) comes to play a part by
bringing out the likeness (of A2 with A) --- thus a kind of contrast-effect
between likeness and difference.

In the account of the person born blind who was operated on by Franz, one
sees clearly both the predominance of the element of feeling at the
beginning and the significance of repetition and of difference. As soon as
the sensitivity to light had decreased so much that the patient could
look at an object without feeling pain long enough to form an idea of it,
the following experiment, among others, was made. Differently colored silk
ribbons on a black ground were used to show him the colors. ``The patient
knew the various colors, with the exception of yellow and green, which he
frequently confused, but which he distinguished when they were presented
\emph{simultaneously}. He could name each single color correctly when
\emph{several at once} were shown him. Gray pleased him most; red, orange
and yellow were painful; violet and brown were not painful, but
unpleasant; the
% footnote *) on p. 152
last-named he called hideous.''\footnote{Philosophical Transactions.
London 1841. I. p.~64.} --- As with newborn children and operated persons
born blind, so it goes with dogs that have been deprived of larger parts
of the surface of the cerebrum, so that they have become ``soul-blind''
and ``soul-deaf'', i.e.\ have lost the ability
% --- p. 153 ---
\opage{153}to combine the new sense-sensations with corresponding
memory-images and thus to arrive at recognition and apprehension. (Cf.\
II, 4). \emph{Hermann Munk} remarks on this: ``One can say that by our
intervention the dog has, as regards the sense of sight, been set back to
the state of earliest youth, the state in which the puppy finds itself
when its eyes have just been opened. Just as the puppy learns to see,
i.e.\ to know what is seen, so a dog must \emph{learn} anew to see, except
that its developed power of movement, the advanced development of its
other senses, etc., can shorten the time of learning\ldots. It is
oriented first, and very soon, about the things most important for its
existence. One need only once or twice have pressed its head down into
the pail, so that the water touched its muzzle, and in future it always
seeks out the pail by itself when it is thirsty. It goes the same way with
the food-bowl. Then it gradually learns to know people, and likewise the
objects in its surroundings, the largest first, the smaller
% footnote *) on p. 153
later.''\footnote{H. \emph{Munk}: Ueber die Functionen der
Grosshirnrinde. Berlin 1881. p.~30. --- Cf.\ above, II, 4d.~e.}

The more frequently the repetition takes place, the more easily
recognition proceeds; the more ready consciousness is for the new
impressions. The rubrics have been laid out in order.

Just as we called the immediate interaction among sensations
\emph{elementary remembering and comparison}, so we may call the fusion of
new sensations with the remains or traces of earlier sensations
\emph{bound remembering and comparison}, because the earlier states do not
come forward anew freely and independently, side by side with the
sensations aroused by momentary impressions. This bound remembering is the
subjective element in perception. The more earlier elements of
consciousness the new impression releases into fusion with itself, the
greater the part played by subjective interpretation in relation to what
is immediately given. In all perception there is an objective and a
subjective pole; but the relation between them varies endlessly.


% TR: "Iagttagelse" covers both perception and observation; it is rendered
% "perception" throughout this section, as Høffding here stipulates.
% --- p. 154 ---
\opage{154}The Danish word \textit{Iagttagelse} means both apprehension by
means of sensation (\textit{Perception, Wahrnehmung}) and voluntary,
inquiring and attentive apprehension (\textit{Beobachtung}). Here we take
it in the first sense. Psychological usage, however, is unsteady.
\emph{Sibbern}, e.g., defines perception as immediate sensation, and so
seems not to distinguish between sensation and perception. Yet he adds that
perception is ``an immediate \emph{consequence} of the affection, or of
the object's stepping forth before, or coming up into or for,
consciousness in consequence of the influence'' (Psychologie. 1856.
\S~50). This ``coming up into or for consciousness'' is precisely what I
designate by sensation, and according to Sibbern perception is only a
\emph{consequence} of this; so that even according to Sibbern sensation and
perception do not coincide. While Sibbern almost merges perception with
sensation, \emph{Helmholtz} puts a greater opposition between the two
expressions. ``Sensations'', he says (Die Lehre von den Tonempfindungen.
Braunschweig 1863. p.~101), ``we call the impressions on our senses in so
far as they come to our consciousness only as states of our body
(specifically of our nervous apparatus); perceptions we call them in so
far as we form from them the idea of external objects.'' With this usage we
should come to lack a word for the simple phenomenon that a sensation is
recognized, or referred to others of the same kind. This simple
psychological operation does not yet presuppose a consciousness of the
source of the impression as something existing outside us. Perception in
the sense of the recognition of a sensation applies just as well to the
domain of internal as to that of external nature. --- As regards the word
impression, I use it in two senses, which the context in each particular
place will suffice to keep apart. Either it designates the emerging
sensation as something given, something received with preponderant
passivity, without, however, containing any hint of how the sensation
comes to be in our consciousness; for of that we know nothing whatever. Or
it designates the physical force that affects the sense-organ, thus the
first link in the chain of effects whose last link we know as
% --- p. 155 ---
\opage{155}sensation; it is in this sense that impression is set in
opposition to sensation (see, e.g., V A, 4).

\textbf{2.} What repeats itself and is thereby perceived is, now, not
merely single impressions, but series of impressions in a certain order.
The repetition then concerns also the order in which the impressions
arise; this order will imprint itself, the elements of consciousness
corresponding to the impressions will enter into a fixed connection, so
that when the first (A) has been reawakened and fused with the new
impression (A2), the elements connected with A (B, C, D) will also have a
tendency to emerge again, and it then depends on whether this tendency is
favored by the following series of impressions. In any case that older
series will always be used more or less for the interpretation and
judging of the new series, and will be able to fuse with it in various
degrees. The subjective element in perception thus consists not merely of
the earlier elements of consciousness with which the new impressions
agree, but also of such elements of consciousness as have entered into
fixed succession with those earlier elements of consciousness. In this
way the effects of a new impression can branch out widely on all sides in
consciousness. It does not then stop, e.g., at the recognition of the
color red; since this color has frequently occurred together with certain
forms, certain sensations of touch, etc., it will have a tendency to draw
this whole series forth after it. When, now, only the first link (A2) is
nevertheless really given, the other links (b, c, d) will not be able
fully to fuse with the given impression. They therefore stand, when they
come forward at all, more freely and independently over against it. They
become, in other words, \emph{free memory-images or ideas}.

By another route, too, we can come from the bound remembering in
perception to the free remembering in the idea. When A2 has reawakened
A1, this latter will, in spite of its fusion with A2, have a tendency to
reawaken other elements of consciousness (Ax Ay etc.), to which it stands
in the same relation as A2 to itself. The presupposition is only that
consciousness has had a sufficient number of similar experiences, and that
these experiences, besides their agreement with one another, present
marked differences which
% --- p. 156 ---
\opage{156}hinder immediate fusion. This strengthening of the subjective
pole in perception gradually removes it from the immediate impression,
which now acts only as a motive that sets many hidden forces in motion.
What thus gathers about the subjective pole will then be able to come
forward as an independent element, not fused with, but in a certain
opposition to, the immediately given impression. The perception of a round
figure can, e.g., call up ideas of other round figures, so that these
successively move further away from the one perceived.

At higher stages of development there forms by these routes an
independent \emph{circle of ideas}, a world of memory, which stands with a
certain independence over against the momentary sensations and
perceptions. In the reading of a book, e.g., the pure sensations are
entirely subordinate; the paper and the black signs hardly come to our
consciousness, but vanish before the ideas and feelings which they --- at
first through many intermediate links --- set in motion.

In a consciousness that has reached the stage of development here
indicated, two different currents take place. The one, which might be
called the \emph{ascending}, is called forth by the sensations we receive
at each moment. However little we may perhaps heed these sensations, each
of them nevertheless has a tendency to set certain definite series of ideas
in motion. I find myself, e.g., in a large hall, adorned with works of
art, filled with talking people and resounding with music. Among these
different impressions a struggle is waged over whose series of ideas is to
win. Shall I hear the music, listen to the conversation, or lose myself
in the contemplation of a painting? As my glance falls on a painting, a
definite feeling is now awakened and a definite, coherent train of thought
called forth. A current has then arisen which, in contrast to the
ascending one, we may call the \emph{horizontal}. But the other impressions
storm on nonetheless, awaken faint beginnings of series of ideas, and it
then depends on whether any of these series can become strong enough to
initiate a new, dominant horizontal current. The following schema can
illustrate this relation.

% --- p. 157 ---
\opage{157}%
% the schema stands at the head of p. 157; the print sets "Ascending
% current" vertically beside the brackets, reading upward
\begin{center}\small
\begin{tabular}{@{}r@{\ }l@{\qquad}l@{}}
 & \qquad Horizontal current.\ $\longrightarrow$ & \\[2pt]
Free flow of ideas & $\left\{\mbox{\begin{tabular}{@{}l@{}}a$_4$\ \ a$_x$\ \ a$_y$\ \ a$_z$\ \ etc.\\ a$_3$\ \ \ ''\ \ ''\ \ ''\\ a$_2$\ \ \ ''\ \ ''\ \ ''\end{tabular}}\right.$ & $\Big\uparrow$\ Ascending \\[2pt]
Perception & $\left\{\mbox{\begin{tabular}{@{}l@{\quad}l@{}}Bound idea & a\ \ \ b\ \ \ c\ \ \ d\\ Sensation & A\ \ B\ \ C\ \ D\end{tabular}}\right.$ & \phantom{$\Big\uparrow$}\ current. \\
\end{tabular}
\end{center}

When we are to follow a piece of music, the point is to hinder
the formation of a horizontal current, so that the mind can be open and
fresh to receive the tone-impressions that ever press in anew. Rigorist
musicians will even have no title on a piece of music, so that it shall
not call up a dominant series of images --- In this example there now
reigns a definite (rhythmical and melodic) connection among the immediate
sensations (A, B, C \ldots). On a walk that is taken for the refreshment
and rest of the mind, we rock ourselves, on the contrary, in disconnected
and changing impressions (the light, the clouds, the trees, the people,
etc.) and let each one initiate its ascending current until this is
relieved by a new one. Such a state approaches the dream, which, as we
know, is among other things often characterized by a constant change of
motives of ideas, since the ``ascending'' currents do not permit any
steady ``horizontal'' current to form. (See III, 8). The total contrast
to this is reflection, self-absorption, where the many impressions vanish
before a single, often narrow, clearly lit current of ideas.

\textbf{3.} Between the two currents, the free course of ideas and the
series of real perceptions, and likewise between the two elements in
perception, the sensation and the bound idea, there reigns an inverse
relation. They strive to inhibit and displace one another. The more energy
the one element lays claim to, the less, in the nature of the case,
remains for the other. Both elements and both currents are present in
every state of consciousness, but with different strength. When they are
equally strong, a rhythmical alternation takes place between them, so that
now the sense-sensation,
% --- p. 158 ---
\opage{158}now the idea has the preponderance.\footnote{Cf.\
\emph{Spencer}: Principles of Psychology. I p.~475. --- II p.~251. ---
\emph{Kant} had already seen the inverse relation between sense-sensation
and perception (Anthropologie \S~19). Later it has been stressed by
\emph{Fries} (Psychische Anthropologie. I p.~96. II p.~30) and
\emph{William Hamilton} (Discussions on Philosophy. p.~63).
\emph{Spencer} has presented the relation most exactly in an interesting
chapter (vol.~II. part.~VI. chap.~XVIII).} They contend for attention;
but an equilibrium between them would presuppose that they could both come
before consciousness with equal clearness, which is impossible, since
consciousness at each moment, like the place of clearest vision on the
retina, concentrates itself on a single side. Wearied of the one current,
consciousness seeks rest in the other. Here too there holds a law of
contrast-effect.

\emph{Spencer} finds in this opposition the explanation of the fact that
we apprehend the tints of color in a landscape more freshly when we look
at it with the head downward. ``When objects are looked at in the usual
way, consciousness is mostly occupied in interpreting the impressions made
on the retina, is filled with the ideas aroused by these, which make up
the knowledge of the objects as this or that; and so long as they are
looked at in this way, it is impossible to prevent acts of recognition
from taking place and claiming a part of the attention. But when the eyes
are placed in an inverted position, or otherwise so placed that all the
relations of form and arrangement by which the objects are recognized
recede, the ideas connected with recognition do not emerge, and
consciousness continues in so much the higher degree to be occupied with
the sensations of color that compose the image received.'' (Principles of
Psychology. II. p.~249).
% TR: Spencer's passage is given in Høffding's Danish rendering and is
% translated from that, not restored from Spencer's English.

The recognition of the composite nature of perception marks a great
advance. The older psychology distinguished sharply between
sense-perception and thinking as two entirely different functions (Plato),
or conceived sense-perception as obscure thinking (Leibnitz), or thinking
as transformed sense-perception (Condillac). \emph{Kant} first showed the
position of perception in relation to sense-sensation on the one side and
thinking proper on the other. Although he is still strongly influenced by
% --- p. 159 ---
\opage{159}the old, Platonic dualism between sense-perception and
% footnote *) on p. 159
thinking,\footnote{Even in his ``Apologie der Sinnlichkeit''
(Anthropologie \S~8) he says of sense-perception that ``in itself it
belongs to the rabble, because it does not think''. --- The dualism
manifests itself in his sharp distinction between lower and higher
faculties of knowledge, especially between intuition and understanding.}
he nevertheless has a clear eye for the fact that perception arises only
through an activity whereby the given impression is taken up into
consciousness (``apprehended'') and worked over by it. It is by the
forming power of consciousness (``the synthesis of the imagination'') that
a coherent image\footnote{As to the definite forms (the forms of space and
time) in which this image woven of sensations appears, reference must be
made to the next section.} is formed out of the manifold elements given in
the sensations. Through this forming power (Kant's ``productive
imagination'') the ``outermost ends'' of our knowledge, sense-perception
and
% footnote ***) on p. 159
understanding, hang together.\footnote{Kritik der reinen Vernunft. 1 Udg.
p.~119--125. (Kehrbachs Udgave p.~130--134).}

Only with the development of a coherent circle of ideas does the
peculiarity of the life of consciousness come clearly forward. So long as
we keep to the series of sensations, it looks as if consciousness were
only the work of the moment. The connection is here elementary and
externally mechanical. But in recognition and in the steady circle of ideas
the unity of consciousness has a clearer expression. The stream of images
that at each moment passes through consciousness is for it what the blood
is for the organism: an inner medium that makes it independent of what the
moments bring. If it stops, life ceases.

\textbf{4.} The question here naturally presses itself upon us, how the
free course of ideas separates itself from the real perceptions. We cannot
ascribe to consciousness any original knowledge of this difference. There
is, to be sure, as a rule a difference of degree in strength between an
idea of memory and a perception; but this difference of degree can be very
small, indeed can disappear entirely. The first time a present impression
calls up the image of earlier experiences, we cannot in any case know what
the difference in strength means. It perhaps conditions a greater
attention to the real impression than
% --- p. 160 ---
\opage{160}to the memory-image; but the latter could for all that just as
well stand as something real. It must be with incipient consciousness as
with dream-consciousness: everything that presents itself is provisionally
taken at face value, and there is no reason to refer the content of
consciousness to two different spheres, the world of possibility and
fantasy on the one side, the world of reality and perception on the other.
On the contrary, this opposition is discovered only through experiences
which are for a great part bitter. We must run our heads against reality
a good many times before it dawns on us where its limits lie.

Let us start from such an exclusive sensation as \emph{Condillac} spoke
of. Every new element of consciousness that is to make itself felt beside
it must share the energy and the interest. It will have a preponderant
tendency to fuse with the given state when this is at all possible, and
the least possible change will in any case take place. But from this it
will in turn follow that the present sensation or perception will cast its
strong and clear light over the less strong elements that emerge, that is,
over the memory-images it awakens and which are firmly attached to it.
Through the connection with the impression of reality by which they have
been recalled, the ideas of memory will then also receive the stamp of
reality, even if they do not appear so definitely as that impression. In
this way what is given is involuntarily supplemented and extended, so
long as no definite contradicting experiences are known.

This power that the present impression exercises is due not merely to the
strength of the sensation itself, but also to the active adherence of all
the organs of sense and movement. The primitive impulse to movement is, in
the nature of the case, directed especially in the direction that the
given sensations indicate. These thereby acquire an effect that reaches
far beyond what their strength and validity would in and of themselves
bring with them. \emph{Bain} has therefore rightly pointed to the original
impulse to movement as an important element in the sanguineness with which
the life of consciousness begins.\footnote{In his earlier presentations
\emph{Bain} laid the chief weight on spontaneous movement as the cause of
belief (\textit{belief}). Later he speaks more unclearly of an original
credulity (\textit{primitive credulitz}), which is gradually checked by the
contradiction of experience. See his interesting presentation in
``Emotions \emph{and Will}''. 3 ed. p.~511--518. --- Even if his earlier
view took too little account of the intellectual side, the later
presentation nevertheless suffers from the essential defect that the
``original credulity'' receives no many-sided psychological explanation.
Such an explanation might well lie in the power of attention to
concentrate all the activity at its disposal upon its object.}
% TR: "credulitz" (for "credulity") and the letterspacing of "and Will"
% are as printed.
% --- p. 161 ---
\opage{161}We stand, as it were, ready to set off at the first signal that
comes, and once we have set off, much is needed to change the direction.
So long as we are not stopped, we suspect no danger. The animal follows
the scent of its prey, and only when the trap snaps shut over it does it
turn out that one and the same chief impression does not always bring
with it the same series of circumstances.\footnote{Another example from
the animal world I take from Lyell's ``Travels in North America''
(Deutsch von Wolff. Halle 1846. p.~66). At the Lehigh Summit mines the
coal is conveyed down in wagons which by their own weight run down rails
on the mountainside. To draw the empty wagons up again, mules are used,
which are then driven down again each evening, comfortably enough
consuming their fodder on the way. When now these mules come into other
service, they are very willing to draw heavy loads uphill, but refuse
obstinately to take a wagon downhill, halting at the least slope, and
there is no moving them from the spot.} The child puts everything it gets
hold of into its mouth and begins to suck, but often it thereby suffers
bitter disappointments. It learns that there are other things and
relations than it had suspected in its tender philosophy. It is thrust
back after its first sanguine assault upon reality.

There is greater reason here to speak of ``unconscious inferences'' than
in the case of the immediate sense-sensations. Formulated logically, it is
a positive inference in the second figure that incipient consciousness
constantly draws, and by which it comes to have experiences that often
confirm in painful fashion Aristotle's warning against such a way of
inferring. A is C, B is also C --- from this it is inferred that A is C.
% TR: so printed; the conclusion intended is presumably "A is B".
But because a finger or a comforter has something in common with a nipple,
it cannot for all that take the place of the latter in every respect.
Not every smiling face announces food or play soon to follow. Because the
sheep gladly took the leaves that were held out
% --- p. 162 ---
\opage{162}to it, it does not follow that the bird does the same. A little
boy even stated outright the claim that snow was sugar: ``for snow is
white, and sugar is white too''. The sight of the white calls up the
memory of the sweet, and for the time being this union of white and sweet
reigned in consciousness; experience now mercilessly tears this
connection apart, the consequence of which is that the idea of the sweet
acquires a peculiar stamp, withdraws as it were into a special corner of
consciousness, whither a whole series of other ideas with which things
have gone the same way are gradually shown (the idea of play in relation
to the smiling face, of the ability to eat grass in relation to being an
animal, etc.) --- in other words: the first foundation is laid for the
opposition between possibility and reality. Only now do the \emph{free
ideas} enter into a definite relation of opposition to sensation and
perception. The original sanguineness is broken.

This now has an influence also on the immediate state itself under the
impression of reality. The ideas awakened by that impression no longer
fuse so closely with it. A certain \emph{doubt} and unrest make
themselves felt; the state is not so closed and uniform as at first. The
idea of what has earlier been found to accompany or follow the given
impression does not now become an object of full assurance. When the child
has learned that satiety does not always follow hunger, there enters into
its consciousness an opposition between the present feeling of
displeasure and the idea of the satisfaction of the want. Formerly the
two fused together; the transition from the one state to the other was
continuous. Now, on the other hand, there is something vibrating, as it
were, about the ideas connected with the present perception. They do not
play the same part as that perception and do not produce the same effect.
In so far as the connection nevertheless holds fast, hope stirs, since the
ideas of memory still constantly have a tendency to approach reality, even
if this tendency meets resistance. Only when this resistance becomes
considerable is the sanguineness still lying in expectation also subdued
at certain points, and \emph{hope and memory} become opposites. The
possibilities no longer lie over the real perception, waiting for it to
give place to them, but stand as pale shadows with which
% --- p. 163 ---
\opage{163}the idea of full presence no longer connects itself. Their
tendency to take on the stamp of reality has been completely overcome, and
with this the first step is often taken toward their complete
disappearance. A higher stage of consciousness is required to preserve
ideas that can no longer be realized and acquire practical significance.

In this process it is of course also of significance whether the
perception (B) corresponding to the awakened idea used formerly to precede
or to follow the present perception. If A used to precede B, it will,
when B calls it up, have a tendency to stand as memory; if A used to
follow B, it will, when called up again, more easily stand as expectation.
One might think that every element of consciousness has two poles: by the
one it is attached to the preceding, by the other to the following element
of consciousness (B thus hanging together with A by $\alpha$, with C by
$\gamma$). If it is now C that recalls B, B will place itself \emph{before}
it in the series, because in this case it is the pole $\gamma$ that first
emerges in consciousness. If, on the other hand, it is A that recalls B, B
will place itself \emph{after} A, because the pole $\alpha$ first emerges.
The hungry (A) child is calmed by being taken up, because this is the
introduction ($\alpha$) to satiety (B); B places itself as of its own
accord after A in the series.

% footnote *) on p. 163
This theory,\footnote{It is found in outline in \emph{Robert Zimmerman}:
Philos. Propädeutik. 3 Aufl. p.~223, worked out in \emph{Taine}: De
l'intelligence. Livre III. Chap.~7 \& 9. Cf.\ also \emph{James Sully};
Belief, its Varieties and its Conditions. (Sensation and Intuition. London
1874. p.~89 f).} which of course uses figurative expressions, brings out
elements of importance, but only of supporting, secondary significance. The
chief cause of the distinction between hope and memory is the same as the
cause of the definite opposition between perception and idea: experience
and the disappointments it brings with it. After the ideas have been
separated from the perceptions, there is now a new distinction to be
practiced, that between memory and expectation, and it is supported, but
not exclusively effected, by the definite place that the perceptions
usually occupy in relation to one another. This is seen
already from the fact that originally the
% --- p. 164 ---
\opage{164}forward pole of the idea always emerges first: consciousness is
originally practical, strives forward, and notices only what can be means
to its ends. Backward reproduction is always more unnatural to us, and at
the lowest stage of consciousness it probably does not occur at all. Here
the original sanguineness comes forward again; life strives forward, and
it is only by being stopped that it is led to look back. As soon as it
comes about that a perception (C) awakens the idea of its predecessor
(B), this will nevertheless provisionally be placed not as predecessor but
as successor, and expectation will arise. Only when experience has
exercised its purifying influence can the difference between $\alpha$ and
$\gamma$ become of significance.

We see from this presentation how little it is in reality possible to
carry through the abstraction of the elements of knowledge from the other
kinds of elements of consciousness. Yet we here leave aside many
questions that might be raised concerning the influence of feeling and
will on the course of development here described; they belong in later
sections of our inquiry.

That the free world of ideas is gradually separated off, through
disappointed expectations, in opposition to reality and to the
expectations attached to reality, has not always been clearly seen.
According to \emph{Plato} the transition from expectation (or impulse,
Eros) to memory takes place through an evenly ascending development, in
the course of which the primitive objects of impulse recede before ideal
ideas, until expectation comes to rest in
% footnote *) on p. 164
contemplation.\footnote{\emph{Plato's} Phaedrus and Symposium.} That
modern psychology sees the matter otherwise is due to the more penetrating
experience that the development of life everywhere proceeds through a
struggle with opposites. --- The examples we have used in the foregoing
were taken from primitive and elementary stages.\footnote{Children do not
understand the difference between poetry and history, fairy tale and
reality. They know nothing as yet of criticism, and wonder that one does
not know about everything. --- Cf.\ \emph{Egger}: Sur le développement de
l'intelligence et du langage chez les enfants. Paris 1879. p.~60 f. (In
his eighth year Felix was given a book of fairy tales and wondered that
his parents seemed to doubt the truth of its contents. --- He learned a
little history, and it interested him very much; but he did not like there
being anything that people doubted).} But it will easily be seen that the
same
% --- p. 165 ---
\opage{165}process repeats itself every time experience exercises its
criticism of sanguine and preconceived assumptions and hopes. Here is an
ordeal by fire that all theoretical and practical endeavor must pass
through. ---

One element is still lacking to give memory its full clearness. We have
distinguished between elementary remembering, bound remembering and free
remembering, and have tried to show how free remembering emancipates
itself from perception and expectation. With this free remembering there
can further be connected a definite consciousness that the idea has its
origin in an earlier time. The idea of time and its process of
development we shall come to examine more closely only in the next
section. Here it need only be pointed out that this definite referring of
the idea to a certain point of time marks a chief difference between
memory and free \emph{fantasy}. Fantasy alters the relations and
combinations of ideas and produces new orderings and groups, while memory
in the narrower sense follows the series of real perceptions step by step.
Here too the criticism of experience is the most essential cause of the
distinction; for real memories have more practical significance than the
products of fantasy, which cannot so easily lead to controlling
experiments.

\textbf{5.} Already in the first sections of our inquiries we found in
memory, and in the intimate way --- standing alone within the circle of
our experiences --- in which various elements are there connected into
unity, a typical expression of the nature of consciousness. My
remembering falls in the present moment, but what I remember belongs in
an earlier moment. In the unity which embraces and comprehends the various
sensations and ideas and makes interaction between them possible lies the
germ of the concept of the ego or the self. The difficulties that have
been found in this concept derive for a great part from the fact that the
ego has been sought as something entirely simple, which could therefore be
given in a certain definite state, a certain definite
% --- p. 166 ---
\opage{166}sensation or idea. If one starts from the presupposition that
the ego itself must appear as a single element of consciousness in
opposition to other elements of consciousness, it is no wonder that one
seeks in vain. Thus \emph{Hume} says, in attempting to prove that the idea
of the self contradicts experience: ``If any sensation gives rise to the
idea of self, it must continue invariably the same through the whole
course of our lives, since the self is supposed to exist after that
manner. But there is no constant and invariable sensation. Pain and
pleasure, grief and joy, passions and sense-sensations succeed each other,
and never all exist at the same time. The idea of the self cannot
therefore be derived from any of these sensations, or from any other; and
consequently there is no such idea. \ldots\ For my part, when I enter most
intimately into what I call myself, I always stumble on some particular
sensation of heat or cold, light or shade, love or hatred, pain or
pleasure. I never can catch \emph{myself}
% footnote *) on p. 166
without a sensation,\footnote{The \emph{expression} Hume uses here
(\textit{perception}) designates with him both sensation
(\textit{impression}) and idea (\textit{idea}).} and never can observe
anything but the
% footnote **) on p. 166
sensation.''\footnote{Treatise on human nature. Vol.~I. Part.~IV.
Sect.~6.}
% TR: Hume is given in Høffding's Danish rendering (which puts
% "Fornemmelse" for Hume's "perception") and is translated from that. The
% print also has an asterisk after "sensation" in "some particular
% sensation*", dropped here.
In this Hume is quite certainly right. But he seeks in the wrong place.
The nature of the ego manifests itself in the connection of sensations,
ideas and feelings, and in the forms and laws of this connection, that is,
in memory and comparison, from their purely elementary and automatic forms
up to the highest and clearest forms they are able to take on. Hume
cannot see the forest for the trees. His polemic is justified against the
spiritualistic view that makes ``the soul'' a simple substance behind
and separated from the single elements of consciousness. But he sins
against psychological experience itself when he mockingly declares that,
setting aside some few metaphysicians, the rest of mankind are nothing
but bundles or collections of sensations (\textit{perceptions}), which
succeed each other with an inconceivable rapidity and are in a perpetual
flux. He overlooks the inner bond between these elements of consciousness,
% --- p. 167 ---
\opage{167}by which they become precisely elements in one and the same
consciousness and not in several. And yet he would naturally have to come
to ask what holds the elements of consciousness together and makes a
``bundle'' of them. There must surely be a uniting force here, but for
Hume it receded entirely behind the single members of the bundle. He even
went so far as to ascribe independent or substantial existence to the
single perceptions. It therefore became for him an insoluble riddle how
there can be a connection between the perceptions when each of them
stands by itself as independent. ``I must confess'' --- thus he ends the
cited chapter of the ``Treatise'' --- ``that this difficulty is too hard
for my understanding.'' It is also certain enough that once the single
elements of consciousness have been set up as entirely independent, it is
impossible to build a bridge between them. Hume of course did not overlook
the actual connections between ideas; on the contrary, he lays strong
weight on them; he also shows how the easy transition that such
connections bring about causes us to pass imperceptibly from the one idea
to the other. It is, after all, precisely the actual connection in spite
of the presupposed independence that occasions the great problem.

The more recent English school has not entirely worked its way out of this
difficulty. \emph{Stuart Mill} halts at the same paradox as Hume. ``A
memory of a sensation, even if it is not referred to any definite date,
nevertheless involves the suggestion and belief that a sensation, of
which it is a copy or representation, existed in the past; and an
expectation involves the belief that a sensation or other state of
consciousness, to which it points, will exist in the future\ldots. We are
faced with the alternative of either believing that the mind, or ego, is
something different from any series of
% footnote *) on p. 167
sensations\footnote{Mill's expression is ``feelings'', which is more
comprehensive than the expression sensation in the sense in which I use
this word.} or possibilities of sensation, or of accepting the paradox
that something which is supposed to be a mere series of sensations can be
aware of itself
% --- p. 168 ---
\opage{168}as a series''.\footnote{Examination of Sir William Hamilton's
Philosophy. 2 ed. p.~212 f. --- Cf.\ my work ``Den engelske Filosofi i vor
Tid'' [English Philosophy in Our Time], p.~43 f.}
% TR: Mill likewise is translated from Høffding's Danish rendering; the
% print's asterisk after "a mere series of sensations*" is dropped.
Mill rightly stresses that memory presupposes a self.\footnote{See
especially his notes to \emph{James Mill's} ``Analysis of the human
mind''. London 1869. I. p.~229. 339 f. --- II. p.~174 f.} But he is still,
like Hume, caught in the idea of consciousness as a mere series or bundle,
and therefore the connection and the unity stand for him as a paradox.
When all psychological experiences point to connection, it is surely most
reasonable to take this as fundamental, as \emph{Kant} did in setting up
the concept of synthesis as the fundamental one in the domain of the life
of consciousness. If there is any substantiality in the spiritual domain,
it must not be sought in the single elements, but in the unity that makes
their mutual relation possible, through which each of them first is what
it is, and which reveals itself most clearly in memory.

This is not to say that Hume's demand for constant elements of
consciousness as the condition of the unity of the self is entirely
unjustified. The synthesis or unity is in itself purely formal; it gets its
individual and practical shape from the content it comprehends. If no
sensations at all were repeated, no memory would take place either, and
the life of consciousness would dissolve into momentary flashes. (Cf.\ V
B, 1). Of a person who hastens from the one impression to the other,
always seeking something new, we also say that he does not get time to
collect himself or to be himself. There must be dominant sensations, ideas
and feelings, if there is to come true unity and connection into
consciousness and a peculiar self is to be formed. Through a sudden
change, especially in the life of feeling, we can become strangers to
ourselves. The fundamental feeling that animates us is the real side of
the ego, just as the synthetic unity of memory is the formal side. If the
real connection is shaken, the \emph{formal} one is only a weak bond that
in the end is torn apart. An insane person in a state of partial
derangement often describes his earlier state as insane, or declares
himself dead, or speaks of himself as a third person.
% TR: the print closes a parenthesis here that it never opened; dropped.
Total change in
% --- p. 169 ---
\opage{169}the general sensation, in connection with hallucinations, seems
to be the cause of this want of ability to recognize
% footnote *) on p. 169
oneself.\footnote{See \emph{Griesinger}: Die Pathologie und Therapie der
psychischen Krankheiten. 2 Aufl. p.~334. 241. --- Quite recently the
French physician \emph{Krishaber} has described cases where the power of
reflection was entirely unimpaired, but where all inner and outer
sensations took on another character. When the patient had grown used to
the new way of sensing, he declared himself to be another; in the period
of transition he doubted his own existence. Cf.\ Revue philosophique. I.
p.~289 f.} The strongest feeling and the most coherent and comprehensive
group of ideas act attractively and determiningly on everything else that
emerges in consciousness. It is therefore no wonder that consciousness
becomes a stranger to itself when they undergo a radical change. In the
remarkable cases of so-called ``double consciousness'' two different
existences are even lived, each with its own circle of experiences and
memories. The patient can sometimes read and write in the one state, but
