not in the other. When the dream of one night, as is sometimes the case,
is continued the next, there is already an approach to such a double
existence. Yet there are hardly any examples of the continuity between such
different existences in the same individual being entirely broken. A lady
who suffered from ``double consciousness'' could nevertheless make herself
familiar with the fact that there was a difference between her ``old'' and
``new'' state. In most examples one sees memories and feelings pass over
from the one state to the other. A zealous abstainer
(\textit{teatotaller}), who in his somnambulistic state was brought to the
conviction that he was drunk, nevertheless kept obstinately maintaining
the impossibility of his having drunk ``gin and water''. A young woman who
had been on the point of drowning, and after being brought back to life
had a fit after which she lost for a long time her memory, the power of
speech and several of the senses, nevertheless still hung together by dim
recollections with her earlier state; she could not bear to see water, not
even in a painting, while on the other hand the presence of a young man who
was the object of her affection always exercised
% --- p. 170 ---
\opage{170}a calming influence on her.\footnote{The examples are found in
\emph{Brierre de Boismont}: Des Hallucinations. 3e éd. Paris 1862.
p.~339 ff. \emph{Carpenter}: Mental physiology, p.~459 ff. --- A French
medico-legal writer, \emph{Fodéré}, even goes so far in holding fast the
connection between the normal and the somnambulistic state that he
maintains the imputability of sleepwalkers' actions: »Il me semble, qu'un
homme qui aurait fait une mauvaise action durant son sommeil ne serait pas
tout à fait excusable, puisque, d'après le plus grand nombre des
observations, il n'aurait fait qu'exécuter les projets dont il se serait
occupé durant la veille.« Cf.\ against this \emph{Tardieu}: Etude
médico-légale sur la folie. 2 éd. Paris 1880. p.~236 ff.} --- A
\emph{real} double consciousness would be the beginning of the dissolution
of the life of consciousness.

The very fact that there is something else in consciousness than the
strongest feeling or mass of ideas shows again that the self is not
exhausted by it, and that \emph{Herbart} is not right when he lets the one
group of ideas perceive (apperceive) the other. The ego is just as much in
the weak as in the strong feeling and mass of ideas, and is determined just
as much by the effect of the strong group on the weak as by that of the
weak on the strong. The ego is precisely that which by its unity makes it
possible for such an interaction among the elements of consciousness to
take place.

Meanwhile we must grant \emph{Hume} and \emph{Stuart Mill} that there is a
difficulty here; but it lies at another point than where they found it.
The difficulty lies in the fact that memory itself, and with it the unity
of the life of consciousness, develops successively and is never absolute.

We have, it is true, tried to find an analogy to memory already within the
circle of mere sensations; but we saw that the more we approached the
limit of consciousness, the more elementary the connection became, the
more multiplicity gained the upper hand over unity. Could this not lead to
the view, held by some physiologists (\emph{Vierordt} and \emph{Preyer}),
that the unity of consciousness is not original? One of the most important
laws of development is, after all, that we first have a growth out from
scattered starting points, and that these sporadic beginnings only
gradually unite into a common stream. Thus
% --- p. 171 ---
\opage{171}one might think that psychical life begins with scattered and
independent sensations, which are only gradually comprehended together
and brought into mutual connection.\footnote{\emph{Vierordt}: Physiol.
des Kindesalters. p.~157. 169.} \emph{Preyer} even goes so far as to
speak of several different egos in the infant (a cerebrum-ego, a
spinal-cord-ego, and besides these an ego for each of the central
sense-organs), which later fuse together.\footnote{Die Seele des Kindes.
p.~368 f.} One cannot express more strongly the fragmentary and sporadic
character of the earliest manifestations of the life of consciousness in
comparison with the later ones. It is certainly also both useless and
unjustified to assume a hidden unity where the experiences point in the
direction of incoherence. Psychologically regarded, there is nothing else
to answer to the question raised than that we here stand at a limit, where
understanding fails. We have repeatedly pointed out that entirely single
states are psychologically unintelligible. A flash of sensation that is
not remembered is as if it did not exist; it may be a preparation for the
clearer appearance of later flashes; but so could unconscious states. A
coherent consciousness seems just as well able to form itself even if
those sporadic sensations did not precede. Synthesis can never come about
through a mere pushing together of single elements. It is precisely in
this that spiritual connection differs from bodily connection.

Later on, too, we meet the sporadic in the life of consciousness, and, as
F.
% footnote ***) on p. 171
C. \emph{Sibbern} has shown,\footnote{Filosofisk Arkiv. p.~265.}
precisely in especially fruitful periods. Various promptings, ideas and
impulses stir in chaotic fashion, especially in times of ferment and
transition, and it is only through long work and reflection that order and
harmony are brought into this material pressing forward from various
sides. And the sporadic is never entirely overcome. The unity of
consciousness is always a struggling unity. Life, after all, consists
precisely in the constant taking up of new material that is to be worked
over. But at such stages dispersion does not mean absolute separation and
isolation. This is seen already from the fact that the sporadic and
chaotic itself is \emph{felt}, becomes an object of consciousness,
precisely through its opposition to the connection reigning in other
% --- p. 172 ---
\opage{172}regions and periods of the life of consciousness. This could not
happen if there were not a unity of consciousness embracing both the
sporadic and what has been worked together, any more than two colors
contrast when each is apprehended by a consciousness of its own.

\textbf{6.} Sensations arise, as we have mentioned earlier (III, 1.
11--12), psychologically regarded, out of nothing. They are the primitive
elements out of which the life of consciousness is woven, but whose
explanation cannot be found within this web, if it can be found at all.
Ideas are, in their simplest form, renewed or recalled sensations. But
since we do after all distinguish between idea and continued sensation,
so that there lies an interval between the original sensation and the
idea in which it is renewed, the question arises whence the ideas come. Do
they too rise out of a psychological nothing, or do they have an
existence beneath consciousness? --- What can be answered hypothetically
to this question in connection with the general problem of the relation
between the unconscious and the conscious, we already know (see III). We
cannot avoid ascribing to ideas we have had a closer relation to our
consciousness than to such as have never appeared in it. To this is added
the remarkable circumstance that ideas, when they emerge again, may have
undergone changes, may have been arranged in new groups or fused
together. It is both the preservation of ideas, although they are not
always in consciousness, and their working over, without consciousness
taking part in it, that justify ascribing a positive validity to the
concept of unconscious psychical activity, in spite of all the
difficulties that are bound up with carrying through a law of the
conservation of energy in the spiritual domain. When one speaks of
traces, residua or dispositions that remain after sensations and ideas
have vanished from consciousness, the assumption lying at the base is
precisely that there must be in the spiritual domain an analogy to the
conservation of energy in material nature. The difference between material
and spiritual nature is here this: whereas in the former one can point out
potential energy as a definite given state of equilibrium, something
corresponding is not possible in the spiritual
% --- p. 173 ---
\opage{173}domain. Purely physiologically there is no real difficulty here,
for it is in itself easy to understand that there can arise a brain
process of the same kind as that which took place on an earlier occasion,
and that this can happen all the more easily the more frequently it has
been repeated and practiced. One can imagine that through repetition the
molecules concerned are arranged in such a way that they are brought out
of equilibrium with greater ease. On the other hand we lack any image to
make clear what spiritual tension (potential energy) means.

The reason we here return to this consideration is the way in which the
theory of the independence of ideas, criticized in the foregoing, has led
to a conception of the preservation of the idea. Some psychologists
(especially of \emph{Herbart's}
% footnote *) on p. 173
school\footnote{Cf.\ however already \emph{Fries}: Neue Kritik.
(Heidelberg 1807). I. p.~96.}) assume that the faithful and steady
preservation of the idea is the rule, and forgetting the exception. The
problem would thus not be remembering, but forgetting. Every idea is then
assumed to have a tendency to self-preservation, to assert its existence
in consciousness, a tendency that is checked only by other ideas coming
forward with the same tendency. The single idea can therefore easily be
displaced from consciousness by other ideas; but as soon as these vanish,
it comes forward again of itself, without needing any help or any motive,
just as a compressed watch-spring flies out at once as soon as the
pressure is removed. A distinction is therefore made here between two
kinds of reproduction: the immediate, by which an idea comes forward again
by its own force as soon as the hindrances are cleared away, and the
mediate, by which an idea is drawn forth again in consciousness by means
of its connection with other ideas. An immediate reproduction takes place,
e.g., after sleep, in that the ideas of the preceding day present
themselves at once, as soon as the inhibiting influence of sleepiness has
fallen away. Even if an idea seems to be entirely forgotten, one may not
therefore regard it as entirely vanished; it lies below the threshold of
consciousness and can on a given occasion come forward again. Memories
that one did not
% --- p. 174 ---
\opage{174}oneself suspect one still possessed may arise when one sees
again the surroundings in which their content was lived through. In old
age and shortly before death, memories of childhood can be awakened and
long-forgotten ideas assert themselves. A great sorrow awakens what has
vanished to a melancholy but clear memory. It is also known from a number
of remarkable case histories how in a state of fever and other
pathological states things and words are uttered which the patient in a
healthy state would not be able to remember --- which can only be
explained by earlier overlooked ideas finding occasion, under the abnormal
conditions, to
% footnote *) on p. 174
press forward.\footnote{Some special examples are cited in
\emph{Mynster}: Om Hukommelsen [On Memory]. Kbhvn. 1849. p.~17 f. 21 f.
--- A young lady in a somnambulistic state maintained that a rare plant,
whose Latin name she gave, and which was to be found in the botanical
garden, would cure her. The plant was found there, but could not, for the
rest, cure her. That she knew its name could only be explained by her
having heard it in her childhood from her father, who was versed in
natural history. --- A peasant in an illness uttered some Greek words that
he had heard many years before during a short stay at the parsonage. ---
Erasmus of Rotterdam tells of an Italian who spoke good German during a
fit of raving brought on by a painful illness, while otherwise he did not
understand this language. The only possible explanation is that in his
youth he must have learned German, but later forgotten it. --- A Venetian
lady who had been brought up in French later forgot this language for
Italian; but in her old age she could speak French, the language of her
childhood. --- See also \emph{Carpenter}: Mental physiology.
p.~436--439. --- Through the taking of opium, too, long-forgotten
recollections can suddenly emerge. De \emph{Quincy} tells of this (En
Opiumsæders Bekendelser [Confessions of an Opium-Eater]. Oversat af
Hazeland. Kristiania 1878. p.~161): ``The minutest incidents of childhood,
or forgotten things of later years, could suddenly emerge. Remembered
them in the ordinary sense I surely did not; for had I been told of them
when waking, I should not have been able to acknowledge them as belonging
to my past life. But placed as they stood before me in my dreams, like
revelations, clothed in every little accompanying circumstance and every
corresponding feeling, I recognized them at once.''}
% TR: De Quincey is quoted from the Danish translation and is translated
% from that.

That we naturally may not say of any idea whatever that it has entirely
vanished from consciousness follows of itself. We do not know all the
threads by which the single, apparently accidental and isolated idea can
% --- p. 175 ---
\opage{175}be attached to other elements of consciousness. The close
connection between conscious and unconscious psychical life makes it
possible that what stands as isolated, so long as we keep to clear
consciousness, may have its subterranean connection. Precisely for this
reason our self-knowledge is most often so purely empirical; we frequently
discover an actual connection between certain ideas and feelings without
being able to find the intermediate links. Thoughts emerge, feelings
assert themselves, whose previous history we do not know, although it
must after all be attached to the circle of our experiences. Here we can
often discover something in ourselves that we did not suspect.

But it does not follow from this that the single ideas have a kind of
individual immortality. The independence of the single idea is grounded
only in its being a peculiar form of the activity of ideation, in its
marking one of the paths that psychical activity enters upon under
certain definite conditions. When the life of consciousness has gained new
strength through sleep, it is quite natural that it should first and most
easily enter upon the accustomed ways. Our habitual train of thought is
for the life of our consciousness what the type is for the organism: the
form in which development moves under usual conditions, and from which it
lets itself be removed only with a certain resistance. The chief weight is
thus to be laid not on the single ideas as such, but on the general
activity of consciousness. No single idea is preserved as such and
recalled except when the conditions of the course of the life of
consciousness as a whole bring it with them. There is here, as it were,
an inner psychical world-order that bears the single manifestations of
life. The single ideas may very well help to determine the further
development of the life of consciousness without being themselves
preserved. It is thus in reality about the conditions of the
\emph{preservation} of ideas that psychology must ask. The Herbartian
psychology introduces an anarchy into the life of consciousness by
ascribing to the single ideas an imperishable existence. Consciousness is
not merely a stage on which the ideas wage their struggle for existence;
it is itself at work in and through the single ideas.

Our spiritual states, our thoughts, feelings and resolutions are very far
from being imperishable. It is
% --- p. 176 ---
\opage{176}precisely here that we have the bitterest experiences of the
perishability of everything that has developed, when, as the poet says,
``even the paradises of fantasy, the spirit's inner possession, blow
away.'' It must therefore be regarded as a victory when memory is
preserved, and it becomes of interest to dwell on the conditions and the
laws to which experience shows us this victory to be attached.

\textbf{7.} \textbf{a.} The \emph{free} memory-image is different not
merely from immediate perception and from the immediate after-effects of
sensations (after-images, after-sounds, etc.), but also from certain ideas
that emerge from within without any external occasion, the so-called
hallucinations. Hallucinations present themselves, like perceptions, with
the stamp of reality, and it can often be difficult, if not impossible,
for the individual himself to separate them from real perceptions. Many
differences of degree make themselves felt here. While the insane person
firmly holds fast to the reality of his hallucinations, there are cases in
which the individual knows clearly that what he sees is only phantoms. A
man of whom
% footnote *) on p. 176
\emph{Bonnet}\footnote{Analytischer Versuch über die Seelenkräfte. Aus dem
Französ. übers. von Schütz. 2ter Bd. Bremen und Leipzig 1771. Kap.~23.
p.~68.} tells saw before him from time to time, without the slightest
external occasion, men, women, birds, carriages, buildings, etc. These
figures moved, grew larger or smaller. Sometimes all the walls of his rooms
seemed to him hung with landscape paintings. All this made just as vivid
an impression on him as if the objects themselves had been present. And
yet he did not take these apparitions for realities; in any case he always
knew how ``to correct his first judgments''. They were for him a play at
which he was a spectator, without knowing what scene the next moment
would bring. --- By this \emph{stamp} of involuntariness and externality
(exteriority) the hallucination, like the \emph{dream-image}, stands close
to sense-perception. The dream-image is no doubt due in part to causes
similar to those of the hallucination, namely the stimulation of the
sensory brain-centers from without (especially by the altered state of the
blood),\footnote{Cf.\ \emph{Wundt}: Physiol. Psychol. I. p.~178--181.}
thus an
% --- p. 177 ---
\opage{177}automatic irritation, by which the brain is affected in the way
it is normally, and in the waking state, affected only by impressions from
the surrounding world. Yet in dream-images the real impressions received
in sleep play a very essential part (see III, 8), so that they are rather
to be put alongside \emph{illusions} than hallucinations. By illusion is
understood a wrong subjective interpretation of an objective impression,
as when a white towel in moonlight is taken for a figure clad in white, or
bits of wreckage on a beach for human beings. According to the schema we
used earlier, illusion is psychologically characterized by the bound idea
gaining the upper hand over the element of sensation in perception (a
over A, so that a draws b c d along with it).\footnote{An illusion on the
basis of a hallucination is depicted by \emph{Benevenuto Cellini} in his
Life (2nd Book, 12th Chap. --- Goethe's transl. I. p.~323). In his
underground prison he had long ardently wished and prayed to see the sun,
at least in a dream-vision. One morning he at last had a vision of the
sun. But the disk of the sun soon changed into a golden disk, in the
middle of which Christ on the cross, and a little later Mary with the
Child, appeared as work in relief. As Goethe already remarks, he behaved
toward the visionary sun as a plastic metalworker. Had his fantasy been
accustomed to work in another direction, he would have got other things
out of the same vision.}

The hallucination has this in common with the memory-image, that it can
sometimes be called up at will. An English painter possessed the ability to
imagine persons whom he was to paint, and who had sat for him only half an
hour, with even more vivid colors and forms than in reality, so that he
could paint from this idea. From time to time he raised his eyes from the
painting to look at the imaginary person and compare the picture with it.
It ended, to be sure, with his losing the ability to distinguish imaginary
persons from real ones, and having to spend thirty years in an asylum for
the insane. But the ability to call up images with the stamp of reality
can also occur without any pathological
% footnote **) on p. 177
character.\footnote{\emph{Brierre de Boismont}: Des hallucinations.
p.~27. 462.} \emph{Goethe} was thus able, with closed
% --- p. 178 ---
\opage{178}eyes and bowed head, to see a flower out of which ever new
flowers unfolded, as long as he wished.

Although memory-images, like most hallucinations, can arise automatically,
they are nevertheless for the most part called up by their connection with
other ideas or with given sense-perceptions. While the hallucination
therefore stands as something foreign, psychologically unmotivated, it is
as a rule possible to trace the motives for the emergence of a memory-image
in consciousness under the given circumstances. The chief forms under
which this motivation appears, the laws of the association of ideas
(\textit{Ideassociation}), we shall go into more closely in the next
paragraph (8).

As regards strength and vividness, there is only a difference of degree
between hallucinations and memory-images. In this respect great individual
differences make themselves felt.\footnote{Cf.\ \emph{Fechner}: Elemente
der Psychophysik. II. p.~469--491. --- \emph{Galton}: Statistics of mental
imagery. (Mind 1880).} While some people have very vivid and colored
memory-images, with others these are colorless and indefinite in their
outline. With some the memory-image approaches real perception or
hallucination. They see things in the idea just as clear and vivid as
they appear in reality, so that the memory of the sun can even have a
dazzling effect. Others, on the contrary, declare that in memory they
cannot see individual objects, but have only general and uncertain ideas
of what was seen; their memories are not so distinct as to deserve the
\emph{name} of images. --- Not only original endowment, but also age and
practice are of significance here. Memory-images are most vivid in
childhood and youth. A child said, after it had been absorbed in the
memory of a person it was very fond of: ``Now I have dreamed.'' Poets,
naturalists and travelers have more vivid memory-images than abstract men
of science. There is on the whole an opposition between imagination and
abstract thinking of the same kind as that between the free course of
ideas and real perceptions, and as that between the two \emph{elements} of
perception (the sensation and the bound idea). (See V A, 2--3).


% --- p. 179 ---
\opage{179}Those who do not possess individual and vivid memory-images may
therefore very well have a good and sure memory. They remember that they
have lived through something, although in memory they do not see it before
them. Very strong and uncompound sensations are no doubt all referred to
remembering in this way. A cannon shot, a sudden blow or a flash of light
one remembers more indirectly than directly. Even colorless and abstract
memories can well be exact and fully sufficient to form the basis for the
description of what was lived through and for the appraisal of it.

Some psychologists, such as \emph{Bain} and \emph{Lotze}, deny that ideas,
like sensations, each have a certain strength of their own. They derive
the so-called strength or vividness of ideas from the feeling and
interest attached to them. The strongest and most vivid idea is thus the
one connected with the greatest
% footnote *) on p. 179
emotion.\footnote{\emph{Bain} in a note to the 2nd edition of \emph{James
Mill's} Analysis. I. p.~116 ff. --- \emph{Lotze}: Grundzüge der
Psychologie. 1881. p.~18 f.} Against this, however, speaks the fact that
memory-images, without being especially attached to a feeling, can
approach real perceptions in distinctness and firmness. The difference
between the full individuality and splendor of color of some
memory-images and the misty indefiniteness of others cannot be better
designated than as a difference in strength and life. When Lotze says
that the idea of a bright luster is no greater work of the activity of
ideation than the idea of a dull glimmer, and that the idea of thunder
requires no greater effort than the idea of a sound, he seems to confuse
the idea that emerges involuntarily (if one will: the idea in itself) with
the idea that one calls up and holds fast with voluntary attention. It can
even at times cost greater effort to imagine a faint glimmer than a strong
light. But we are speaking here of the idea merely as such, apart from the
special difficulties to which its arising and its being held fast may be
subject.

Just as illusion and the dream-image lie between sense-perception and
hallucination, and just as very strong
% --- p. 180 ---
\opage{180}and vivid memory-images approach hallucination, so on the other
side we have an intermediate link between sense-perception and the
memory-image in what \emph{Fechner} has called
% footnote *) on p. 180
``memory-after-image''.\footnote{Anfr. Skr. p.~491 ff.} This is a
memory-image that one calls up immediately after a sense-impression, while
the after-effect is still making itself felt. Even those who otherwise do
not have vivid and colored memory-images can produce them in this way, at
least for a moment. Their power of memory thus needs, as it were, a
helping hand from the real sensation. Here too many individual differences
make themselves felt. With some the after-image itself, with others the
concentration of attention on the after-image, plays the greatest part.
Fechner, after looking at an object in ordinary daylight, got a
complementary after-image; but on concentrating the activity of memory on
it, there arose a memory-after-image with the object's own color and
without complementary after-effect. --- An observation I have made shows
that the after-image itself can be called up anew. One morning, without
thinking further about it, I had looked at an oval photograph surrounded
by a white border. When I closed my eyes, a light oval emerged with dark
surroundings. This negative after-image vanished after a little, but a
whitish patch of mist remained at the place in the field of vision of the
closed eye where it had vanished, and when I concentrated my attention on
this mist, the after-image arose again. This was thus really a
memory-after-image of an after-image. The further the memory-image lies in
time from the immediate sense-perception, the more difficult it is for it
to take on a vivid character.
% TR: "Anfr. Skr." = anført Skrift, "work cited" (Fechner's Elemente).

As regards the chief conditions of the preservation and arising of
memory-images, there are especially three things to stress: the
circumstances in which the original experiences were lived through, the
circumstances of the recall, and finally the nature of the memories
themselves.\footnote{Cf.\ as regards what follows: \emph{Spencer}:
Principles of Psychology. I. Part.~II. chap.~5--6. --- \emph{Carpenter}:
Mental physiology. p.~441 ff. --- \emph{Ribot}: Les maladies de la
mémoire. Paris 1881.}


% --- p. 181 ---
\opage{181}\textbf{b.} Since memory has its physiological expression in the
organism's ability to preserve the traces of impressions received, it
follows of itself that the fresher and more energetic the life-process as
a whole, the better will one also be able to learn, i.e.\ the more lasting
and deeper will be the traces that sense-perceptions leave behind.
Therefore childhood and youth are the proper time for learning, and what
is learned then is preserved more easily than what is experienced at a
later age. In old age, therefore, what one lived through in childhood is
better remembered, while the experiences of later years and of the last
days pass into oblivion. ``The old man's spectacles are ground for seeing
near''. His brain process lacks the energy to preserve the new
impressions. This \emph{quicker} dissolution of what was \emph{later}
acquired is a general physiological law (cf.\ also III, 4).

What is lived through and experienced in an especially vigorous and elated
state is preserved better than what is appropriated in a slack and
depressed state. The heightened vitality benefits also the newly received
elements of consciousness. In cases of apoplexy and epilepsy the same
relation sometimes shows itself as in old age: while older memories are
preserved, the most recent are effaced. Exhausted by fatigue, one is
\emph{not} able to gather up material for memory.

Time and repetition are needed if memories are to be founded. What one has
appropriated in haste as a rule also vanishes in haste. Actors who have
learned a part at short notice do not remember it so well as one they have
really studied. Drilling for examinations (\emph{what} the English call
\textit{cramming}) does not lead to such thorough results as real study.
Here belongs also the remarkable fact that in pathological weakening of
memory the words first forgotten are those that express concrete and
individual objects, while designations for abstract concepts and relations
are better preserved. Most frequently, therefore, proper names, and nouns
in general, are forgotten; later verbs, adjectives and pronouns.
% footnote *) on p. 181
\emph{Kussmaul}\footnote{Die Störungen der Sprache. p.~163--165.} derives
this from the fact that, while we can easily imagine persons and things
without words, abstract concepts and relations first gain firmness for our
consciousness with the help of words.
% --- p. 182 ---
\opage{182}Far more numerous processes and combinations in the cell-network
of the cerebral cortex must therefore no doubt be needed to produce an
abstract than to produce a concrete concept, so that the organic bonds
that connect the abstract concept with its name are far more numerous than
in the case of the concrete concept.

It might seem to conflict with this that one remembers so little from the
very first years of childhood, when the brain must surely be freshest.
Memories, after all, seldom go further back than the third or fourth year.
\emph{Preyer}, by way of explaining this, has aptly remarked that the
experiences of the very first years of childhood are very different from
those of the later
% footnote *) on p. 182
years.\footnote{Die Seele des Kindes. p.~226.} What the child learns in its
first years --- to hold itself upright, to walk, to talk --- is like a
closed course by itself; when it has gone through it, the way is paved to
the great circle of experience common to all adults. There is thus lacking
continuity and agreement between the experiences of the first years and the
later ones, and therefore, as a rule, interest in keeping fresh the memory
of those older experiences.

Yet one would also be able to point out other contributing causes. The
first sense-perceptions still have a chaotic and sporadic stamp, are little
ordered and organized; and a certain definiteness and order is, as will be
developed later, a condition for the preservation of experiences in memory.
In the consciousness of the infant the ``ascending'' currents reign, just
as in dream-consciousness. Dreams too, after all, are seldom remembered.
The cerebrum, to which the activity of memory is attached, plays on the
whole a not very active part at this stage (see III, 4). The impressions
have a greater tendency to discharge themselves in reflex movements than
to deposit themselves as memories.

\textbf{c.} Just as a sound and fresh brain is required for gathering up
material for memory, so it is also a condition for the recall of this
material that there be sufficient energy in the organism, and especially
in the brain. An English physician, Sir \emph{Henry Holland}, through
overexertion during a visit to the mines in the Harz, suddenly forgot his
German, and it came back only when he had rested and refreshed himself. In
an elated mood, and in general in a strong emotion,
% --- p. 183 ---
\opage{183}memories emerge that are not at one's service under ordinary
circumstances (cf.\ 6). The taking of opium and similar means can have the
same effect. In some way or other there must, besides, be something in the
organic state and in the reigning fundamental mood that corresponds to the
state and mood that were present when the experience was lived through;
though at times precisely the contrast can here be of great significance.

It depends, besides, on what sensations are pressing in at the moment. The
stronger these are, the more difficult it is for the memory-images to
unfold (see 2). The more akin they are to the memory-images, the more they
will hinder the latter's free appearance. We can have the memory of colors
or tones while we touch an object, but hardly the memory of red while we
sense blue, or of other tones than those we are hearing at the moment.

\textbf{d.} Not everything that we live through is equally suited to being
remembered. The more simple and uncompound it is, the more easily it
vanishes. What is many-sidedly marked and articulated is better
remembered. Feelings and moods are therefore remembered, as we shall see in
a later connection, only with the help of the ideas to which they are
attached, and we recall the fluctuations and transitions in emotion more
easily than the feeling itself. The general sensations, the dimmest and
least articulated of our sensations, cannot easily be recalled either. We
can remember that we have felt hunger or thirst under these or those
circumstances, but of the hunger or thirst itself no memory-image forms.
Taste and smell, too, cannot really be remembered. The higher senses, on
the other hand --- touch, hearing and sight --- yield clear and vivid
memory-images, and for seeing people the world of memory is no doubt even
peopled chiefly by visual ideas. We remember relations better than we
remember the single members that stand in the relation, the form better
than the content. Of the forms, again, those are best remembered that are
most distinctly marked. Thus the spatial relation is remembered better
than the temporal relation, and this better than the general relation of
opposition and difference. Because of the easier preservation of the
spatial relation, it was made the basis in the so-called art of memory, as
a frame (\textit{memoria localis}) that could enclose and support all
material for memory.


% TR: in this paragraph "Hukommelse" is rendered "memory" and "Erindring"
% "recollection", to keep the distinction Høffding draws.
% --- p. 184 ---
\opage{184}\textbf{e.} Memory can be of different kinds. --- Already
\emph{Plato} and \emph{Aristotle} distinguished between memory and
recollection, so that the former is the mere mechanical preservation of
experience, the latter the ability freely to recall it. Aristotle remarks
that some people are more gifted in the direction of memory, others in the
direction of recollection. In Danish literature the same opposition has
been set up between \textit{Hukommelse} and
\textit{Erindring}\footnote{A corresponding distinction is made in German
between »Gedächtniss« and »Erinnerung«.} by \emph{Treschow},
\emph{Sibbern} and S. \emph{Kierkegaard}. According to this usage, which
is not observed in everyday speech, one can remember much without
recollecting it. Recollection is more akin partly to poetic reproduction,
partly to logical thinking, while memory keeps to the external connection
in which what was lived through and learned happened to occur. Many
features and details may have vanished from memory without recollection
having suffered anything thereby.

The ability to preserve (memory) is with most people restricted to
particular domains, where in return it can often express itself with a
remarkable power. An Englishman by the name of \emph{Baxton}, who had not
even learned to write his name, could reckon the hardest problems quickly
and surely. Once he undertook to multiply 39 figures by one another, and
finished this calculation in two and a half months; the result consisted
of 78 figures, and the whole operation took place in his head. A Danish
memory artist, \emph{Carl Otto}, could repeat a number consisting of 162
digits after merely having heard it named once. He let himself be examined
in all directions within the number and could, e.g., say without
hesitation what the 121st digit was. (See O. \emph{Borchsenius}:
\emph{Fra Fyrrerne} [From the Forties]. I. p.~115). A man of science
remembers best what belongs to his subject. The botanist \emph{Brown} knew
25,000 names of plants, \emph{Knuth} 20,000; when Knuth tried to impress
more on his mind, others were in return
% footnote **) on p. 184
lost.\footnote{\emph{du Bois-Reymond}: Ueber die Uebung. Berlin 1881.
p.~28.} I have heard a man of science tell that when he learned English at
an advanced age, it was at the expense of his French; ``the bag was full''.
% --- p. 185 ---
\opage{185}Such phenomena confirm the assumption, often put forward, that
a certain sum of energy stands at the disposal of every individual. This
is of course not constant; it varies with age and practice; but it has a
maximum. Within this maximum the point is to make the best possible
distribution, and as regards memory this will be determined by the
individual's essential interest and activity.
% TR: "Baxton" (Jedediah Buxton) as printed; "halvtredie Maade" in the
% print is read as "halvtredie Maaned", two and a half months.

\textbf{8.} \textbf{a.} When we give ourselves over to the course of our
ideas, the emerging images seem to come ``of themselves'', like
sensations. We have in any case a feeling that we produce them as little
as we produce the immediate impressions. Both we must take as they are and
as they come. Especially when there is great difference and opposition
between the emerging ideas and their predecessors, they stand as
inexplicable. The effect here seems to stand in no relation to the cause.
If there is added to this the suddenness with which ideas sometimes
present themselves, it is no wonder that even many who assume no break in
the causal series in the physical world nevertheless think that the
psychical world is not subject to fixed laws. We have already seen (see
III) that the world of consciousness is not a closed whole; it is
understood only by assuming an interaction between conscious and
unconscious activity. All the conditions for the arising of a psychical
state are not given in the conscious life of ideas and feelings;
unconscious dispositions and instincts, inherited or self-acquired, often
play the most essential part, and the observer comes to know them only by
their effects. The laws for the interaction of conscious ideas are
therefore only guiding threads that can guide us in our attempts to
understand the changes of the phenomena of consciousness --- empirical
rules by which we order the chaos of experiences. But in so far as we are
able to find these rules confirmed by closer inquiry into psychological
phenomena, the justified presupposition that the inquirer brings to inner
as well as to outer nature is also confirmed. And in so far as a
phenomenon cannot be satisfactorily explained by these rules, we conclude
only that there must either be laws we do not know, or that the connection
must be so intricate that it cannot be traced back to simple points of
view.


% --- p. 186 ---
\opage{186}\textbf{b.} The laws that hold for the connection of ideas we
have already come upon in the foregoing. We have seen how a sensation fuses
with the traces of earlier sensations (A with a), and how a fixed and
repeated connection between sensations (\emph{ABCD}) also brings with it a
fixed connection between the corresponding ideas (a b c d), so that when
one of them is reawakened it will have a tendency to draw the others after
it. In the interaction of sensations, in the arising of sense-perception
and in the liberation of memory-images, the same laws are at work that
hold for the connection of free ideas. The difference is that here we know
the single members as independent elements of consciousness before the
connection. In the connection between sensations and bound ideas we know
only the product. It is only analysis that demonstrates the composite
character of sense-perception. Yet associations of ideas too can become
so fixed and steady that it is forgotten out of what elements they arose.
Some of the greatest mysteries in the psychological respect are due to
such ingrown associations of ideas, whose origin and history have been
forgotten. The theory of \emph{inseparable association} is the chief
weapon of the more recent English school against the appeal to a priori
and original forms and ideas of consciousness. This theory starts from
the correct presupposition that what stands before us as a unity and as
necessarily belonging together may nonetheless well have arisen through the
fusion of independent elements. It therefore demands a more deep-going and
longer continued psychological analysis than dogmatizing psychology
engages in. This applies especially to connections that have not formed in
the individual's own consciousness, but are an inheritance from earlier
generations, either through innate organization or through tradition and
language. That associations of ideas can be inherited is shown, e.g., by
the fact that young hunting dogs that are out hunting for the first time
and have never yet heard a shot rush forward eagerly when the first shot
sounds, even if they see no game fall. The connection between the sound of
a shot and the spring after prey must, in the time since the invention of
gunpowder, have become hereditarily fixed in the dog's brain, or have
become a so-called instinct. Birds in uninhabited lands are
% --- p. 187 ---
\opage{187}easier to come near than birds that for generations have grown
used to human enemies.\footnote{\emph{Sigm}. \emph{Exner}: Physiologie der
Grosshirnsrinde. (Hermann's Handbuch. II, 2). p.~282 f. --- Cf.\ above
III, 5. --- See besides the chapter on instinct in Darwin's ``Arternes
Oprindelse'' [The Origin of Species] and Spencer's Principles of
Psychology. I. p.~423. 432 ff.} The fundamental psychical phenomena can in
such circumstances be found only with comprehensive regard to sociological
and physiological conditions. But even then it is the simple psychological
laws that furnish the principles for understanding the influence of the
historical and physical circumstances pointed out upon the life of
consciousness.

If a certain idea (a) is given, it will be able to call up other ideas in
two directions. It will either awaken ideas of a similar kind and content
to itself (a$_{2}$ a$_{3}$ a$_{4}$ \ldots), or ideas whose objects usually
occur in connection with its own object (a b c d corresponding to A B C
D). The two chief rules are thus the \emph{laws of similarity} and \emph{of
external connection} (of contact, of contiguity). What is akin in nature
belongs together, and what occurs in connection belongs together for
consciousness. We shall consider briefly the most essential relations that
make themselves felt here.

I) \emph{Association of ideas by similarity}.

1) The first relation to be named here is identity, \emph{complete
sameness}. It is the relation that makes itself felt in sense-perception,
when the sensation awakens a bound idea and fuses with it. Here, then,
lies the starting point for all the influence an impression can exercise
in consciousness. For whatever states and further-reaching effects it may
otherwise be able to call forth, the first condition is still that an
instinctive recognition take place, i.e.\ that the impression have a point
of contact and a point of attachment in consciousness. This point of
attachment (a) then becomes the starting point for further effects. Once
recognition has taken place, various relations of similarity can then
make themselves felt.

2) The next simplest connection by similarity consists in recognition's
leading to the idea of an earlier experience of the \emph{same} kind. If I
have recognized a man, there naturally comes
% --- p. 188 ---
\opage{188}before me the image of him from the last time I saw him. At the
portrait of a person we come to think of the person himself. A step
further off lies already the idea of people who resemble the one I see,
i.e.\ who have features in common with him, or who resemble him in
character and fate (as when at Napoleon I come to think of Alexander and
Caesar, or at the Bourbons of the Stuarts).

3) Through the last examples cited we are naturally led to remote
relations of similarity, analogies, parallels, \emph{metaphors} and
allegories, which play a great part, especially at primitive stages of
consciousness. We have already mentioned earlier that all the expressions
of language for spiritual phenomena are transferred from analogous
material experiences (see I, 3). There is, according to \emph{Max Müller},
a period in the development of the race that may be called the
mythological, because ``all thoughts that went beyond the narrower horizon
of our everyday life had to be expressed by metaphors, which had not yet
become what they are for us, namely mere expressions made familiar by use
and tradition, but were felt and understood half in their original, half
in their modified character.'' From roots that meant to shine, to glitter,
there were thus formed names for sun, moon, stars, the eyes of man, gold,
silver, play, joy, happiness and love. \emph{Max Müller} distinguishes
between \emph{radical} and \emph{poetical metaphors}. It is a root
metaphor when a root that means to shine is used to form expressions not
only for sun and fire, but also for spring, the light of morning, the
clearness of thought and the hymn rising with glad rejoicing. The poetical
metaphor arises through the transfer of a word whose meaning is already
fixed to designate another object, as when the sunbeams were called the
hands and feet of the sun, the rain-clouds cows with heavy udders, the
lightning an arrow or a serpent, etc.\footnote{\emph{Max Müller}:
Vorlesungen über die Wissenschaft der Sprache. Deutsch von Böttiger. II.
p.~339 f.} --- Even to this day the poet finds by this route a connection
where the prosaic eye is blind. In the power of rhythm over the dancing
couples he sees, e.g., a symbol of the world-law that the globes follow in
the heavens (\emph{Schiller}: Der Tanz). --- In recording a series of
associations of ideas from my own
% --- p. 189 ---
\opage{189}experience it has struck me how easily and quickly the
metaphorical meaning presented itself, even where it did not lie quite
near (e.g.\ with the words swelling, fog, gild).
% TR: the Danish words are "Svulst" (swelling; also bombast), "Taage"
% (fog, mist) and "forgylde" (gild).



4) The idea of a \emph{whole} calls up the idea of its
% footnote *) on p. 189
\emph{parts}, and conversely.\footnote{\emph{Wundt} refers this
relation to association by external connection (Physiol. Psychol. II.
p.~300). It seems to me that \emph{Sibbern} with greater right refers it
to association by similarity (Psychologi. 1856. p.~230).} The whole and
the parts have, after all, certain features in common, which furnish
motives for a reproduction. --- By looking at the embers in a stove one
may, e.g., come to think of a smithy. --- The mad Lear wants to comfort
the blind Gloster in his misfortune; the comfort turns into a sermon,
during which, in the manner of Puritan preachers, he holds his hat in
his hand; from the hat he comes in turn to think of a possible
stratagem: to cover the horses' hooves with felt, so as to fall silently
upon his
% footnote **) on p. 189
enemies.\footnote{% TR: the verse is the Danish translation of King Lear IV.vi, rendered literally.
\begin{verse}
Be content; we came weeping\\
into the world, you know well --- ---\\
I will preach you a sermon; listen!\\
--- --- That hat-crown is good. --- A splendid stratagem\\
to shoe a troop of cavalry horses with felt!\\
I will try it, though; and when I then\\
have come upon your sons-in-law from behind:\\
cut down! cut down!\\
\hspace*{\fill}(King Lear. Act. IV. Sc. 6).
\end{verse}} --- In complete dementia (\textit{démence}), where an
almost complete dissolution of the circle of ideas prevails, since not
even fixed ideas prevail any longer, the association of ideas is
determined only by the like sound of the words
% footnote ***) on p. 189
(assonances).\footnote{\emph{Griesinger}: Pathol. u. Therapie der
psych. Krankheiten.} Even in the normal state every word awakens certain
associations by its sound alone.\footnote{\emph{Tegnér}: Språkets
makt öfver tanken [The Power of Language over Thought]. Stockholm 1880.
p.~25 ff.}

5) Ideas of \emph{qualities and actions} call up the ideas of the
\emph{things or persons} that possess the qualities or perform the
actions, and conversely. The word treason awakens the ideas of
Ephialtes, Judas, etc. With the idea of a gambler there naturally comes
forward
% --- p. 190 ---
\opage{190}the idea of pallor (perhaps under the influence of
reminiscences from novels).

6) The idea of the \emph{cause} calls up the idea of the \emph{effect},
and conversely. We refer this relation to the relation of similarity,
since --- as will appear in a later section --- the concept of cause is,
on closer analysis, traced back to the principle that like conditions
bring like consequences, so that the effect is really only a
continuation or prolongation of the nature of the cause. The planetary
motions lead us naturally to think of the law of gravitation. --- When
Lear sees the mad Thomas, he asks at once: ``Have his daughters brought
him to this pass?'' His idea goes back from phenomenon to cause --- the
only cause he can conceive of. --- The idea of an end leads to the idea
of the means, which is, after all, the cause of the end's realization.

II) \emph{Association of ideas by external connection}
(\emph{contiguity}).

1) Sensations that constantly occur together also produce connected
ideas. In this way \emph{the idea of a single object} is formed. Certain
visual ideas (yellow color), olfactory ideas, tactile ideas (smoothness)
and gustatory ideas are combined into one idea (of an apple). What has
occurred together in our experience in time and space will on the whole
naturally occur together in our ideas, even if it does not shape itself
into a closed whole. The idea of a man leads naturally to the idea of
his house, his friends, etc., the idea of the gambler to the idea of the
green table, the idea of a wreck to the idea of a beach, etc. There is,
moreover, a natural connection between an event and the time and the
place at which it took place.

2) A special kind of association by external connection is that between
the \emph{thing} and the \emph{sign} of the thing. An emotion and its
external expression are naturally associated in our ideas. If, e.g., one
looks up the word terror in a dictionary, the imagination involuntarily
forms the picture of a start, a blanching, or the like. The Greek word
for flight later came to mean fear. Language is such a sign, originally
perhaps called forth as an involuntary outburst at
% --- p. 191 ---
\opage{191}the observation of a phenomenon that was characteristic or
that affected the individual's weal and woe, but thereafter employed as
a means of mutual understanding between individuals. ``Just as the
subject itself, in its state still poor in ideas and in self-determined
forms of utterance, had a tendency to return with the same idea to the
same utterance (sound), so the sound also became common to others by
being repeated as a response, and through the impulse to imitation,
which is active precisely at this stage of life and before the sound had
yet acquired significative determinations; but thereby there was at once
formed a connection, common to several, between this sound and the idea
affecting the mind \ldots{} The means of communication and understanding
had been found for this
% footnote *) on p. 191
idea.''\footnote{\emph{Madvig}: Om Sprogets Væsen, Udvikling og Liv
[On the Nature, Development and Life of Language].
(Universitets-program). Kbhvn. 1842. p.~9 f.} Why it is precisely sound
that becomes the universal sign for all sensations and feelings can
perhaps be explained by the fact that it commands the greatest wealth of
nuances for expressing feelings. Already in the animal world the cry is
at once the involuntary effect of what strongly affects the individual
and a signal to other individuals. The cry of pain warns, and mating
calls bring the sexes together. According to \emph{Darwin}, the capacity
to produce tones developed among man's ancestors in the course of sexual
selection, and was thereby bound up with the very strongest emotions:
burning love, jealousy and triumph. This capacity would thus have arisen
before the capacity for
% footnote **) on p. 191
articulate speech.\footnote{Expression of the Emotions. London 1872.
p.~87.} The various sense-sensations have this in common, that each in
its own way affects feeling; it is no wonder, then, that they are all in
the end translated into the language of feeling.

\textbf{c.} The two chief laws of the association of ideas, the law of
similarity and the law of contiguity, it has sometimes been attempted to
reduce to a single one. In English psychology the prevailing tendency
for a time was to take external connection as the basis, and to explain
all connection within consciousness by habitual connection in time and
space. So already in the founder of English psychology, \emph{Thomas
Hobbes} (Human Nature. 1640), and later, e.g., in \emph{James Mill}.
This is
% --- p. 192 ---
\opage{192}the principle of the extreme
% footnote *) on p. 192
``Association-psychology''.\footnote{The expression association of ideas
was introduced by \emph{Locke}, who, however, designates by it only
certain individual peculiarities, and does not, as Hobbes and the later
school do, make the ``association of ideas'' the sole governing
psychological principle.} It conceives consciousness as a series or
bundle of sensations and ideas, and so cannot consistently admit any
connections other than those which rest on external coincidence.
Association by similarity then becomes only a special case of
association by external connection, since, namely, similar and related
experiences will, by the nature of things, often occur simultaneously or
in immediate succession. The artificiality of this view is easily seen.
It conflicts, moreover, with the experience that it is often the
relation of similarity itself which first brings us to put together
things remote from one another in time and space. Caesar, Alexander and
Napoleon certainly occur together readily in our imagining; but that is
just because a comparison between them has so often been made. The
steps of a mathematical proof follow one another with ease in our memory
once thought has linked them together.

It would be more justified to trace all association of ideas back to the
relation of similarity. Recognition is, as we have seen, a condition of
sense-perception itself. It is further a presupposition for the relation
of external connection being able to produce associations of ideas. For
A to awaken the ideas of B, C, D, together with which it usually arises
in consciousness, A must itself first, as it were, establish its
identity. A must therefore call up a, and it is only then a which in
turn brings b, c and d with it. The relation of similarity is thus the
innermost kernel of every association of ideas; external connection can
act only on the presupposition of the internal.

But because the relation of similarity thus underlies association by
external connection, the latter does not lose its independent
significance. Mere recognition and identification would not carry the
life of ideas further. External connection is the acquisitive,
material-absorbing principle; the material absorbed is then gradually
ordered according to the principle of similarity.
% --- p. 193 ---
\opage{193}Thus two laws work together in every association of ideas, a
centrifugal and a centripetal tendency. The two laws or tendencies
appear in different degrees in different individuals according to their
nature and gifts. Some strive to gather a manifold material of ideas and
observations; the aspiration of others, on the contrary, is to gain as
simple and clear points of view as possible, for which reason they
concentrate their interest on the common and the typical. Historical
learning and specialization in the special sciences point in the one
direction; mathematical and philosophical inquiry in the other. It is
only artistic genius that is able to unite the particular and the
typical at once.\footnote{The two most eminent English psychologists of
the present day (\emph{Bain} and \emph{Spencer}) conceive the two laws
as working together in every association of ideas, but differ from each
other in that \emph{Spencer} regards the relation of similarity as the
most deeply operative, the really decisive one, in relation to which
every other motive of association is secondary, while \emph{Bain},
conversely, takes association by external connection as the basis and
regards similarity as a presupposition of subordinate importance. Cf.\
Bain's latest contribution: Mind. April 1881. p.~267.}

Yet there is a psychological standpoint from which the two laws can be
united under one and the same fundamental law.\footnote{Cf.\
\emph{Fries}: Neue Kritik der Vernunft. I. p.~114 f. ---
\emph{William Hamilton}: Lectures on Metaphysics. I. p.~233. (See ``Den
engelske Filosofi i vor Tid'' [English Philosophy in Our Time]. p.~114).}
However many different sensations and ideas may occur simultaneously or
in immediate succession in our consciousness, they do not remain
entirely separate; they work together to produce a total state or total
activity. The various sense-sensations unite in the perception of a
definite object; the various ideas are combined into more or less
definite products of thought. Now when one of these sensations or ideas
is renewed and draws the others along with it, what is really operative
is the tendency to reproduce \emph{the common state or activity} to
which all the ideas belonged. The deepest ground of all association of
ideas would thus be to be sought in the unity with which every mental
state and activity appears,
% --- p. 194 ---
\opage{194}and which stamps all the single elements with a common mark.
Seen from this standpoint, then, the association between the parts and
the whole would be the typical form of all association. ---
Schematically this can be expressed thus: x = (a$_{x}$ b$_{x}$
c$_{x}$). ax calls up bx and cx because they are parts of the same
whole. Here similarity and external connection work inseparably
together.

This consideration takes us a step further. For what gives each single
mental state its peculiar character is not merely the particular
combination of sensations and ideas, but it is above all the
\emph{mood} that prevails during it, determines it and is determined by
it. As with immediate sensations, so also in the course of ideas,
interest and the \emph{attention} determined by interest play an
essential part. We are never entirely passive in the associations of
ideas, any more than in our sensations (A, 7--8). The connection between
our ideas is therefore conditioned at every single moment not only by
relations of similarity and connection, but also by the prevailing
feeling. An approximation to pure, absolute validity of the stated laws
of the association of ideas we have only in moments when the mood is
roughly neutral, or rather, when it is so adapted in a certain respect
that in a given domain it contributes just its part to allowing the
ideas to order themselves according to their kinship and connection.
Originally, definite practical ends and interests weigh down the scale
in favor of definite series of ideas. A kind of selection thus takes
place among the possible ideas, and here the way stands open for many
unconscious influences, which assert themselves even where we think we
are following the stream of our thoughts and not directing it. Through
interest we come back to impulse, instinct and temperament, hidden
sources which are often known only through their effects. The connection
between feeling and knowledge (see on this VI E) lies deeper than the
connection of the ideas among themselves. If all associations of ideas
rested on the individual's actual experiences and their combination
according to the laws of the association of ideas, consciousness in each
person would be far more clear and transparent than it actually is.
This is not to be understood as if it were only the lack of intelligible
connection that had
% --- p. 195 ---
\opage{195}to be explained by influence from the side of feeling.
Feeling can, on the other hand, also bring about a firmer connection
than there would otherwise be. The strongest feeling is that with which
man embraces his ideal or practical ends; this feeling drives him to seek
the means for the realization of these ends, and thereby lays the
foundation for a firm connection among a whole circle of
% footnote *) on p. 195
ideas.\footnote{\emph{Hobbes} already stressed the fixing and ordering
power which the thought of an end exerts on the association of ideas.
(Human nature. Chap. 4. Leviathan cap. 3). In more recent times
\emph{William Hamilton} has set up the law of interest (law of
preference) as supplementing the laws of similarity and contiguity.
(Cf.\ \emph{Mansel}: Metaphysics. Edingburgh 1875. p.~241 f.). In
Wundt's theory of apperception (the concentration of consciousness) as
an act of will (Physiol. Psychol. II. p.~205 ff.) the law of interest is
combined in an acute way with the concept of apperception set up by
Leibnitz and Kant. Cf.\ already \emph{Fries}: ``Neue Kritik der
Vernunft'' and ``Psychische Anthropologie''.}

The closer investigation of the influence of feeling and will on
knowledge must, however, be postponed to a following section (VI E).
Here it need only be added that when association by \emph{contrast} has
sometimes been set up as a special case of the association of ideas, the
phenomena belonging here can naturally be explained by the influence of
feeling, in so far as they do not quite simply become intelligible by
the laws of similarity or of connection. It is characteristic of the
life of feeling to move through opposites; it is determined from first
to last by the great contrast between pleasure and pain, which is
stronger than any contrast-effect in the domain of sensations. A strong
tension in one direction is readily followed by a slackening, if not by
a tendency to turn interest in the opposite direction, just as the eye
saturated with one color seeks the contrasting color. This could explain
the urge to pass from the idea of light to the idea of darkness, from
the idea of the great to the idea of the small. Yet it is not in all
cases necessary to go back to feeling's propensity for states of
contrast; often the explanation lies in a relation of similarity or a
% footnote *) on p. 196
% --- p. 196 ---
\opage{196}relation of connection.\footnote{Cf.\ \emph{James Mill}:
Analysis of the phenomena of the human mind. 2 ed. London 1869. I.
p.~113 f.} Contrasts fall under the same common concept, like two poles
that move away from one and the same center, each in its own direction.
Dwarf and giant both deviate from the ordinary standard. Further, the
course of life brings with it that opposites succeed one another, touch
one another and pass over into one another, as day succeeds night, joy
sorrow. Here, then, external connection can act associatively.

\textbf{d.} Even if one cannot agree with those psychologists who hold
that forgetting is what is difficult to explain, whereas remembering is
self-explanatory, one cannot, conversely, maintain that ideas are
forgotten merely because there are no special reasons for their being
remembered. Forgetting can be just as great an art as remembering, which
is also why Themistocles, on Simonides's offer to teach him the art of
memory, is said to have answered that he would rather learn to forget:
``for I remember even what I do not wish to remember, but I cannot
forget what I wish to forget.'' What is indifferent or of little
significance disappears as of itself; but precisely the painful ideas
are as a rule bound up with such far-reaching experiences and
circumstances that the involuntary course of ideas does not wash them
away. Besides, there may lie in the individual's very nature a tendency
to hold on with a certain stubbornness to the painful ideas. In other
circumstances, naturally, the task may be precisely to forget ideas with
which pleasure is connected. --- Here we shall only briefly consider the
ways in which an idea can be more or less completely displaced from
consciousness. These are the \emph{laws of forgetting} in contrast to
those of remembering.

1) A refractory idea naturally cannot be counteracted quite
% footnote **) on p. 196
directly.\footnote{``Oblivion is like the heather; it grows only of
itself, and all the world's protection and care adds not an inch to its
growth.'' J. P. \emph{Jakobsen} (Marie Grubbe).} The art of forgetting
(or, as it has also been called, of abstraction) can consist only in
displacing certain ideas with the help of others. Whoever wants to
forget must seek strong and great series of ideas in which
% --- p. 197 ---
\opage{197}his thought can be absorbed. Which he seeks (amusements or
penances, work or fantasies) will depend on his character and on what is
at his disposal in a mental respect. --- The capacity for self-education
depends in great part on whether one can practice the art of forgetting.
Fortunately, as what follows shows, nature comes to the aid of art.

2) In many cases an idea is from the first very closely bound up with
another of such strength and significance that the latter easily
obscures or displaces the former. When one points at something for a
small child and then withdraws one's hand, the child's eye for the most
part follows the hand instead of fixing itself on the object. But when
the object for one reason or another is able to attract attention, the
child does not care where the hand goes. This is the history of all true
education (see III, 4): authority leads the ward to a truth which
gradually acquires independent validity and significance, so that the
original authority is forgotten, just as the scaffolding is taken away
when the house is built.

3) In other cases the first idea does not disappear entirely, but enters
as a subordinate element into the one it awakens. When we read, the
letter-signs awaken a multitude of ideas and feelings, but the signs
themselves do not on that account disappear entirely from our
consciousness. In transferred and figurative designations the original
meaning often still glimmers in the background. When, e.g., there is
talk of the fire of enthusiasm, there still remains, for those who are
not all too accustomed to rhetoric, a gleam of the idea of real fire.

4) An idea can thus be displaced either by an entirely foreign idea,
arbitrarily awakened from other regions of consciousness (a < x); or by
an idea which it itself awakens (a < b); or it can enter as a
subordinate element into the victorious idea (ba). But there is yet a
fourth possibility. It can preserve itself in its independence over
against the other idea to which it attaches itself, and yet enter into
so intimate a connection with it that a new idea arises which is
determined by both of those ideas, though neither of them is definitely
noticed in the new idea. The schema for this is: ab = c. Here there
thus shows itself
% --- p. 198 ---
\opage{198}a kind of psychical chemistry\footnote{\emph{Hartley} first
called attention to this phenomenon in his ``Observations of man''.
(Cf.\ ``Den engelske Filosofi i vor Tid''. p.~19--20).}: in chemical
composition, after all, the product has other properties than the
substances of which it is composed. The history of compound words
furnishes examples of this. At the word \textit{Vindu} [window] only the
linguist now thinks that it comes from
% footnote **) on p. 198
\textit{vind-øje} [wind-eye]\footnote{\emph{Tegnér}: Om språkets makt
öfver Tanken. p.~49. --- According to \emph{Molbech}, the first part of
the word is rather the Old Swedish \textit{wind}, loft, than
\textit{Vind}, \textit{ventus}.}; % TR: English "window" (Old Norse vindauga) has the same history.
but there must have been a time when the etymology asserted itself
immediately in the idea. In the relation between the agglutinative and
isolating languages on the one side and the inflectional languages on
the other, this transition stands out clearly, if indeed \emph{Max
Müller} is right that agglutinative languages maintain the consciousness
of their roots and do not let them be affected by phonetic weakening.
``Even if they are no longer conscious of the original meaning of their
endings, they still feel distinctly the difference between the
significant root and the modifying elements. It is otherwise in the
inflectional languages. There the various elements that are joined to
the word-form can fuse with one another.''\footnote{Vorlesungen über die
Sprache. I. p.~281. --- Whether the transition from agglutination or
isolation to inflection is always an advance is another question. At
one time it may be good to concentrate interest, at another to disperse
it. In the expression ``amavi'' three ideas act as a collected mass; in
the expression ``I have loved'' the emphasis will on each occasion be
laid on one of the three ideas.} --- The idea of distance seems to us to
be simple and immediate, and yet it is certainly (as we shall see in the
next section) the product of sensations which no longer assert
themselves individually. The same holds of every apprehension of a whole
that we have gained by laborious work with the details; the whole stands
as the object of immediate intuition, of an ``intuitive knowledge'', in
which all discursive elements and processes have vanished. We seem to
know the thing ``in itself'', apart from all relations and comparisons.
And yet in reality we know what the thing is only because we have
investigated all the relations in which it stands
% --- p. 199 ---
\opage{199}to other things, and compared it with the things with which
it has points of similarity.

Such a psychical synthesis can take place more or less mechanically. It
may be the linguistic expression that goes first and draws the thought
after it, as seems to be the case in the development of language at the
transition from
% footnote *) on p. 199
agglutination to inflection.\footnote{\emph{Tegnér} p.~54: »Flexionen
har sin väsentliga källa ej i tankan utan i \emph{tungan}; den uppkommer
ej dårigenom att man tänker snabbare utan därigenom att man talar
snabbare.«} But it may also be a fusion of the ideas themselves that
takes place, and which is essentially conditioned by the need for unity
and intimate connection among the elements of consciousness which lies
in the very nature of consciousness. In the phenomena of psychical
chemistry there appears --- even more clearly than in the other forms of
association of ideas --- the general law of synthesis, which we have
traced at every stage of the development of conscious life. Seen from
this side, forgetting is a necessary condition of remembering and
thinking. We could neither remember nor think if we could not abbreviate
our experiences by eliminating intermediate links and forming new,
compound unities. The material would overwhelm us. \emph{Ribot} has
therefore aptly remarked that remembering and forgetting work together
like assimilation and disassimilation in the organic process of
life.\footnote{Les maladies de la mémoire. p.~46.}

\textbf{9.} The laws of the association of ideas must by the nature of
the case be determinative for \emph{the formation of compound ideas}.
This is namely the place to point out that we have hitherto used the
word idea with a certain indefiniteness. We introduced it originally
(see 1--2) to designate reproduced sensations. But the sensations each
have their definite content, which appears to us as something simple
(see on this A, 2); the corresponding ideas must therefore also have a
simple content (color, light, etc.). Yet in the foregoing we have also
spoken of ideas of objects, persons and relations. The question then
arises how such compound ideas are formed out of those single ideas.

% --- p. 200 ---
\opage{200}The idea of an individual object or an individual relation is
formed chiefly by a combination of single ideas according to the law of
external connection. (Cf.\ 8 b). When a series of single sensations has
frequently arisen, the corresponding single ideas also enter into a firm
connection, so firm that we are inclined to assume that there exists as
its counterpart a certain mystical unity, the thing itself, distinct
from those of its properties that are given us in our sensations and
ideas. Yet such total \emph{or} individual ideas, as we might call them
to distinguish them from the single ideas, are by no means entirely
fixed and unchangeable. They constantly change elements, and are not at
one moment what they were at the preceding one. My idea of the table at
which I sit is formed by a combination of various single ideas (a
certain color, a definite degree of hardness, form, position, etc.). But
each single time I have seen the table I have seen it in a different
way; point of view, lighting, position have never been quite the same;
now one element, now another (now color and hardness, now form and
position) has, owing to special circumstances, attracted my attention.
When I nevertheless ascribe to myself an idea of the table, and when I
recognize it by means of this idea, then my idea, if it is to fit all
the single experiences of the table, can contain only certain common
points or features that recur every time I have the table before me.
Only when we have observed an object or a relation a single time can we
have an entirely individual image of it. If several observations have
taken place, the differences will more or less assert themselves; we
imagine, e.g., our home now in one lighting, now in another, and there
can arise a certain struggle among the elements to determine the
character of the idea, and this all the sooner, the richer and more
many-sided our experiences are.

Since we are unable to hold one and the same memory-image fast for any
length of time (which is why even what we call dwelling on a memory is
in reality a continual letting go and recalling of the image), one and
the same individual idea
% --- p. 201 ---
\opage{201}will have a tendency to change its form every time it emerges
anew. The elements that prevail at one moment will, by virtue of the law
of contrast, give way to others that now have greater freshness. The
question then is: do we really have ideas of individual objects and
relations that are more than mere repetitions of the single experiences
and observations? Do we have \emph{common or typical ideas} that hold
for all the single observations we have made?

The difficulty of this question lies in the fact that every real and
complete idea of an object must give us the object with all its features
and properties; now if these vary in each single experience, then I have
not one individual idea of the object, but many. We therefore
distinguish between a \emph{concrete} individual idea (of \emph{the
table in this} lighting, seen from \emph{this} side, etc.) and a
\emph{typical individual idea} (of this table as opposed to other
tables).

The psychological difficulty that lies in this was noticed long ago. Yet
it was not the individual ideas but the \emph{general ideas} that drew
attention to the difficulty. It was not observed that our ideas of
single objects and relations which occur more than once in our
experience are just as much abstract ideas as the general ideas proper.
My idea of a table is related to the various tables I have seen as my
idea of this particular table is related to my various observations of
this table. The general idea arises by a continuation of the same
process by which the typical individual idea is formed. Just as the
concrete individual ideas struggle to determine the typical individual
idea, so the various individual ideas struggle to determine the general
idea. If I want to imagine a triangle, it becomes now an isosceles, now
an equilateral triangle, etc. The common element is not enough to
constitute a real idea. Just as little as one can eat fruit in general,
but always only apples or pears, etc., just so little can one imagine
fruit in general. --- But then do we, psychologically speaking, really
have general ideas?

% --- p. 202 ---
\opage{202}\emph{Berkeley} was the first to call urgent attention to
this psychological difficulty (in the Introduction to his ``Principles
of human knowledge''). The current theory of abstraction ascribes to us
without further ado a faculty of ``extracting'' common properties and
laws and of forming from them new, ``abstract'' ideas. Now Berkeley
flatly denied that he had such ideas, although it was adduced by other
philosophers, e.g.\ Locke, as one of man's chief advantages over the
animal that he was able to form them. ``It is impossible for me,'' says
Berkeley, ``to form the abstract idea of a motion without a moving body,
a motion that is to be neither swift nor slow, curvilinear nor
rectilinear, and the same holds with regard to every other abstract
general idea.'' Every idea concerns something quite individual and
single. Only in the sense that we can let a concrete individual idea
serve as an example or representative of a whole series of individual
ideas are there typical or general ideas. The generality of an idea thus
means nothing other than its usefulness for being set up as an example
or representative.

In this \emph{Berkeley} has certainly pointed to the decisive point. But
it must still be asked by what psychological process an idea comes to be
set up as a type in this way.

In the formation of the idea of a single object the law of external
connection is at work. In the formation of the typical individual idea
and of the general idea the law of similarity is at work. The single
experiences and the single individuals have common, pervasive elements,
which stand in contrast to those peculiar to each single experience,
each single individual. Let A = ax, B = bx, C = cx be three experiences;
then, according to the law of similarity, the single idea x will stand
differently before consciousness than a, b and c. The light of
recognition falls more strongly on it. x will form a fixed and bright
kernel, around which a, b and c will move as dimmer and looser elements.
The error of the old theory of abstraction was the assumption that x
could be taken out and imagined by itself. x must be imagined either
together with a or with b or with c; which of these possibilities occurs
depends on the individual circumstances.

% --- p. 203 ---
\opage{203}The simplest psychological explanation of this matter might
seem to be the one that makes the interaction of the ideas themselves
decisive. With repetition x will by the nature of the case come far more
frequently than a, b and c, and will therefore attain greater strength
and firmness. a, b and c, on the other hand, will inhibit and obscure
one another. The common elements keep themselves up, while the struggle
