goes on among the other elements at the threshold of consciousness. In
this way, then, the individual features would be worn off and only the
general retained. --- But this explanation would lead us back to the old
theory of abstraction. The process would have to end with the common
elements alone being left, which conflicts with the individual character
of all distinct ideas.

Often, to be sure, we behave so passively that the involuntary play of
ideas itself takes care of the separation between the clear kernel and
the dim periphery. Many confused and accidental ideas can approximately
be said to be formed in this way. But for the most part we ourselves
have an interest in the course the ideas take. We have a definite
practical end whose means we seek, or a definite theoretical problem
whose solution we seek. Our attention will therefore concentrate on the
elements that point in the direction of what we seek, and will not take
more account of the other elements than the apprehension of the image of
the idea itself makes necessary. And this concentration will strengthen
and bring out these elements, just as attention in general strengthens
sensations and memories. This will not least be the case where the
points of similarity are to be held fast in spite of strong differences.
For a, b and c will also draw attention to themselves, and only when the
goal and motive of the movement of thought is strong enough are
obstructing associations of ideas kept away. In the idea ax we thus take
special note of x. The general theorems about the triangle we prove by
means of any triangle whatever, taking care not to take account of the
special peculiarity of the triangle drawn (whether it is right-angled or
obtuse-angled, etc.). General and typical ideas therefore exist in the
sense that we are able to concentrate attention on certain elements of
the individual idea, so that a
% --- p. 204 ---
\opage{204}weaker light falls on the other elements. We therefore form
only those general ideas that we have an interest in forming. Such
individual ideas as exhibit in a particularly striking way the elements
decisive for us we choose as representatives. How comprehensive and
correct the general idea becomes depends on the wealth of experiences
and the exactness of comparison. The following example, in which child
psychology and folk psychology meet, will be illuminating with regard to
the way in which general ideas are involuntarily formed.

A small child who had learned the letters saw a book with Greek on one
side, Latin on the other. It then exclaimed: ``that is
% footnote *) on p. 204
Greek, but that is letters.''\footnote{\emph{Egger}: Observations et
réflexions sur le développement de l'intelligence et du langage chez
les enfants, Paris 1879. p.~22.} Under the concept letter it thus
included only the signs it knew. Here the general idea was closed and
did not yet possess sufficient power of expansion. Peoples in their
childhood have fared similarly. They regarded only their own language as
real speech; the languages of foreign peoples seemed to them mumbling or
stammering, childish babbling or animal bellowing. It is well known that
the Greek word for all non-Hellenes, barbarians, really means those who
speak roughly and inarticulately. Corresponding words and ideas have been
shown among the ancient Indians, Hebrews and Arabs; indeed, even the
Herero people (a Kaffir tribe) regard only their own language as fluent,
all others as stammering.\footnote{L. \emph{Geiger}. Ursprung und
Entwickelung der menschlichen Sprache und Vernunft. I. Stuttgart 1868.
p.~300 ff. --- In Greek authors ἄγλωσσος (speechless) occurs in the same
meaning as ἑτερόγλωσσος (speaking another language). Thus, e.g., in
\emph{Sophocles's} ``Women of Trachis'' ἄγλωσσος as the opposite of
Ἕλλην (v.~1060)! --- The word ἄθεος (atheist) has a similar history.}
--- It is only through closer acquaintance that the general idea is
widened. Thus \emph{Strabo} (in the 1st century A.D.) says, apropos of
the word barbarian: ``With extended intercourse and dealings with the
barbarians it was seen that it was not a matter of \emph{defects} in the
organs of speech, but of \emph{peculiarities}
% --- p. 205 ---
\opage{205}of the languages.'' (Quoted in Geiger.) --- While linguistic
differences thus stood out so strongly for the Greeks that they were
long inclined to restrict the concept of language to their own language,
they had, conversely, a striking tendency to rediscover their own gods
in foreign deities --- a tendency which, especially in the imperial age,
``so completely dominated the pious believers that it let them perceive
only what was really or apparently alike in the various religions, and
made them completely blind to the sharpest and most glaring
% footnote *) on p. 205
contrasts.''\footnote{\emph{Friedländer}: Sittengeschichte Roms. 5te
Aufl. Leipzig 1881. III. p.~501.}

Just as the naive consciousness, as regards the single sensations and
observations, must pass through a purgatory of disappointments before it
has learned by practice the difference between observation, memory and
expectation (see 4), so too the involuntarily formed general ideas,
which by the nature of the case are built on the nearest-lying cases,
are purified and sifted by advancing experience. New experiences are
always measured by those one has already had, and on which one has built
one's general idea. In the first example cited, the new phenomenon (the
foreign language) was found unworthy of coming under the previously
given general idea. In the second case one was precisely too quick to
classify under given rubrics (likewise when the whale is counted among
the fishes and the bat among the birds, or when the Bushman calls the
European traveler's wagon ``the white man's big animal''). --- In both
forms there appears the sanguine presupposition of being sufficiently
equipped to understand and judge any phenomena whatever that may occur.

\textbf{10.} Not even such primitive general ideas as those just
discussed could have formed and fixed themselves in consciousness
without the help of \emph{language}. To association by similarity
between the homogeneous elements in observations and ideas there is thus
added association by contiguity between the idea and its sign (8 b).

Even if language is from the first essentially a means of communicating
finished ideas and not a means of forming
% --- p. 206 ---
\opage{206}and retaining ideas, and even if associations of ideas can
develop to a certain degree without the help of language, there
nevertheless comes a point in mental development at which language
becomes a necessary means of getting further.

As proof of the independence of the process of ideation one might appeal
to the fact that infants have ideas and think before they learn
language, --- to the fact that the faculty of speech can be lost in
morbid cases without the understanding having suffered at all, --- and
finally to the fact that certain languages lack expressions for ideas
which nevertheless cannot have been lacking in the consciousness of the
peoples concerned.

Even if one cannot ascribe a language to the infant, it nevertheless
does not lack signs and marks that can serve as means of fixing ideas,
by entering into association by contiguity with them. It is instinct and
feeling that rule in the child at this stage. They find vent in
involuntary outbursts, and these, when the child can notice them itself,
will soon become a kind of sign, whereby the observation or idea that
calls forth the feeling acquires a stamp that distinguishes it from
others. The very first formation of ideas is thus not entirely without
support, even if it is not the word that furnishes the support. --- The
child does begin its life with a cry, but this cry it cannot itself
hear. It is not long, however, before it finds great pleasure in the
tones it can itself produce. Out of such tones, at first involuntarily
produced, the child forms its first language. Already when it is half a
year old it is often able to answer in this language what is said to it.
Then comes a period in which it uses and alters this instinctive
language with greater freedom, so that imitation of the language it
hears around it also
% footnote *) on p. 206
asserts itself.\footnote{This characterization I base on a record of a
two-year-old child's vocabulary. --- Cf.\ also \emph{Egger} op.\ cit.\
p.~20. \emph{Preyer}: Die Seele des Kindes. p.~259 ff. --- In the list
given by H. \emph{Trier} in ``Vor Ungdom'' [Our Youth] 1881 p.~207,
imitation of sounds (onomatopoeia) and of adults' speech stands out more
than in my list.} At this transitional stage language is entirely
individual for each single child. Only at a third stage does the
historically developed language gain power
% --- p. 207 ---
\opage{207}over the child, and now the association of ideas also goes
more easily and more briskly.

It is no wonder that people can do without language after they have been
able to make use of it for a whole life. The ideas must then naturally
be able to be strong enough to do without the help of the symbol, at
least for a time. They have, moreover, facial expressions and actions by
which the ideas can be fixed. A remarkable example of the independence
of ideas from the word, once they have been formed, is a 70-year-old
woman whom \emph{Kussmaul} mentions.\footnote{Die Störungen der Sprache.
p.~22.} As a result of apoplexy she had lost the ability to speak and
write; she could only utter exclamations when she became agitated. Yet
she could read, and she showed great energy and intelligence in regaining
the right of disposal over her fortune, which had been taken from her in
the mistaken belief that she was insane.

When a language lacks an expression for an idea which the people must
nevertheless be assumed to have had --- as, e.g., the Samoyeds,
according to \emph{Castrén}, have no expression for gratitude --- this
can be explained in several ways. It may be that the feeling exists as
an instinctive, involuntary stirring which has not become an object of
consciousness; it is a trait of savage nations that they are children of
the moment in their kindly as in their egoistic feelings. Castrén adds,
too, that the Samoyed will go to his death for the one who has given him
a present. ``I have,'' says the famous traveler, ``also begun to believe
that the word to thank was invented by someone who wanted to get off his
obligations as cheaply as possible.'' --- More plausible, though, is
perhaps the explanation that the Samoyeds have not formed quite the same
general idea as we have, so that the elements of what we call gratitude
are found expressed in the designation for a related virtue. We have,
after all, e.g., no single word to express the Latin
% footnote **) on p. 207
\textit{pietas}.\footnote{\emph{Tegnér}: Språkets makt öfver tanken.
p.~100--101. --- The Hawaiians are thus said to have only one word for
love, friendship, gratitude, benevolence and esteem; all these feelings
they designate by the word \textit{aloha}. \emph{Max Müller}:
Vorlesungen. II. p.~295.}

The primitive functions of cognition, sensation and observation, do not
need the definite mark of language. Already
% --- p. 208 ---
\opage{208}the memory-image may need the word when it is not entirely
fresh and vivid. The more memory and idea renew reality for us and
approach intuition (cf.\ 7 a), the more independent of the word they
become. But with paler and especially with typical and general ideas the
word is an essential help. In some people thinking is to such a degree
an inner speaking that they become hoarse from strenuous thinking.
Thinking has therefore been called ``a process of speech running its
course imperceptibly in the central organs'', which stands in the same
relation to actual speaking as the will stands to actual
movement.\footnote{L. \emph{Geiger} op.\ cit.\ I. p.~58.} This agrees
with the fact that our word-ideas consist chiefly in motor ideas,
corresponding to an activity in the nerve centers governing the muscles
of articulation, while the auditory and written form of words asserts
itself only in a subordinate way, and in some people not at
all.\footnote{\emph{Stricker}: Studien über die Sprachvorstellungen.
Wien 1880. p.~20. 33.} When lively thinking leads to speaking aloud,
this is thus all the more natural, since with every idea an innervation
of the muscles of articulation seems to take place.

With the general idea the word becomes of special significance. For
intuitability is here entirely lacking, and the very need for
intuitability contains a temptation, since it can lead to confusing the
typical with the individual. It is only with the help of the word that
the bright and fixed kernel can avoid being mixed up with the dim and
shifting periphery. The word is, as it were, a substitute for the
impossible intuition, and it is only by misuse that it also replaces the
general idea itself.

Deaf-mutes who have not learned finger-language nevertheless express
objects and experiences in the most vivid and individual way by facial
expressions and imitative movements. But precisely this individual and
concrete character of their designations makes it impossible for them
to form clear and definite general ideas; these are not properly freed
from the individual ideas. Thus they designate meat and food by pointing
to their own body, red by touching their lips. Thereby the lack
% --- p. 209 ---
\opage{209}of hearing and speech cuts so deeply into the mental
% footnote *) on p. 209
development of deaf-mutes.\footnote{\emph{Oehlwein}: Die natürliche
Zeichensprache der Taubstummen und ihre psychische Bedeutung. Weimar
1867. (Quoted in \emph{Preyer}: Die Seele des Kindes. p.~406).}

The abbreviation or condensation of ideas which the general idea makes
possible is secured and fixed by the word. If the word is a symbol, then
the general idea is already a symbol too. The idea of an animal is a
symbol for a series of ideas (mammal, bird, fish, etc.); the idea of
multiplication is a symbol for a certain process of addition, the idea
of a power a symbol for a certain process of multiplication, etc. The
general idea takes a certain series of ideas for granted, but precisely
thereby frees consciousness from now running through this series. While
observation and the memory-image still have the character of intuition
(\textit{cognitio intuitiva}), the general idea is in itself blind and
becomes seeing only with the help of the word (\textit{cogitatio coeca
vel etiam
% footnote **) on p. 209
symbolica}).\footnote{\emph{Leibnitz}. Meditationes de cognitione,
veritate et ideis. (Op. philos. ed.\ Erdmann p.~80). --- Leibnitz
already called attention to the fact that with symbols what matters is
not agreement between their original meaning and the content of the
ideas, but the connection and relation of the symbolic signs among
themselves. The single signs can be chosen arbitrarily, provided only
their use and connection are not arbitrary. Dialogus de connexione
inter res et verba. (Op. Phil. p.~77).} Once such a symbol has been
gained, it in turn makes possible further-reaching general ideas and
combinations of them. We then get symbols for symbols, as in mathematics
letter-signs for number-signs, or as paper money symbolizes silver and
gold coin, which in turn symbolizes the immediate necessities of life.

\textbf{11.} Through the definite delimitation made possible by the
word-symbol, the transition from the general idea to the \emph{concept}
in turn becomes possible. The concept states the fixed characteristic
marks that hold for all the phenomena to which it is to be applied. The
concept has in common with the general idea that it needs an individual
idea, a concrete image, as its psychological representative; it differs
from the general idea in standing as the conclusion of a more or less
arbitrary
% --- p. 210 ---
\opage{210}search for an exact determination of the universally valid
characteristic marks within a given circle of phenomena. The
psychological peculiarity of the concept as opposed to the mere general
idea thus lies in this, that it now presents itself to consciousness as
a task and an ideal to make its ideas precise and to ground them.
General ideas are wild-growing concepts, which are now brought under the
garden shears. This presupposes a turning point, a special awakening of
attention and interest. And such a turning point presupposes in turn
that so rich and provisionally ordered a content has been involuntarily
formed in consciousness that consciousness comes to stand more freely
over against its single elements. The naive, primitive consciousness
feels no need to form concepts, but goes passively from disappointment
to disappointment and forms only general ideas that act instinctively.
The formation of concepts presupposes an ability to look away from what
lies immediately at hand and to take account of \emph{more remote
elements} and relations. Here, then, there must be exercised on the one
hand a self-control that keeps down the involuntarily intruding ideas
when they are not relevant to the matter, and on the other hand a
positive striving to call up and gather everything \emph{that} belongs
under the problem set.

It was \emph{Socrates} who first put forward the demand for definite and
conscious concept-formation, supported by examination of all the
relevant cases. He thereby became the founder of logic. But his real aim
was practical: definite concept-formation was necessary for
self-knowledge and for knowledge of the difference between good and
evil. The close connection between thinking and will stands out clearly
here.

We stand here at the boundary between psychology and logic. Logic
investigates the validity of concepts, the ideal demands made on
concept-formation. Psychology has to do only with the psychical
activities on which the formation of concepts rests. It therefore takes
no account of the validity of concepts, but of the conditions of their
origin in the soul. The transition from the general idea to the concept
coincides with the transition from \emph{association of ideas} to
\emph{thinking}. It is this transition, then, that must be illuminated
somewhat more closely.

% --- p. 211 ---
\opage{211}a) Just as the will (in the narrower sense) presupposes the
original spontaneous and reflex movement and can already be said to be
present in it as a germ, so thinking is a special development and
elaboration of an activity which already manifests itself in sensation,
sense-perception and involuntary association of ideas. In the
interaction and mutual combination of sensations, in automatic
recognition and in the grouping of ideas according to the laws stated
above (8), we have activities that can rightly be called elementary
thinking. Here recognition and association by similarity especially
stand out as what is central and most characteristic of conscious life.
Thinking differs from the association of ideas only in that it tests,
measures and makes precise the relation of similarity. It is thus a
critical activity; it seeks to dam in the associative process, which
runs its course involuntarily and often beyond all bounds. But the goal
is the same, for thinking too seeks what is common; it wants to
establish the valid principles and the prevailing laws. It only
distinguishes more definitely than the association of ideas left to
itself between identity, homogeneity and analogy.

The motive for seeking an exact determination of the relation of
similarity is here again a disappointment, a disturbance of the
equilibrium of consciousness. The involuntarily produced associations by
similarity must be shaken in their accustomed dominion by conflicting
experiences. The original sanguine tendency (see 4) leads consciousness
to cling to the first automatic generalizations. Throughout the whole
history of human knowledge this tenacious holding on to general points
of view once gained can be traced. Precisely because the need for unity
and connection is, psychologically speaking, so deeply grounded in the
nature of human consciousness, it is intelligible that it so often
seizes and holds fast, with blind and ruthless energy, ideas that offer a
simple and clear ordering of the state of consciousness hitherto given.
That is why \emph{Bacon}, the philosopher of empiricism, maintained that
what the human understanding needed was not wings but leaden weights to
check its leaps and its flight (Nov. Org. I, 104). Such leaden weights
are experiences that conflict with what has hitherto been accepted. A
king of Siam would not believe that water could
% --- p. 212 ---
\opage{212}take on solid form, so that one could walk and drive upon it.
If he was now convinced of it by observation, the equilibrium of his
consciousness was disturbed, and the task was to reconcile the new
phenomenon with what is otherwise known of water. He who for the first
time is astonished that a stick put into water looks as if it were bent
has thereby acquired a problem that can lead to the study of the laws of
refraction. It is the differences that awaken doubt; they shake the
previously given unity and connection; but thereby they also awaken and
nourish. Difference and change are, as has often been remarked, a
fundamental condition of consciousness. But equilibrium is always sought
to be restored anew by the striving to find a deeper-lying similarity
and unity that can embrace the new phenomena as well as the old. Doubt
and criticism are thus natural fruits of widened experience, and they
are justified where they are borne by faith in a deeper-lying truth than
the one hitherto found (\textit{alte dubitat, qvi altius credit}). A
great example is modern astronomy, which broke down the ancient and
medieval world-view and set up in its place a magnificent unity in the
universe, the same forces that act on the earth's surface guiding the
course of the globes in infinite space.

b) The striving by which consciousness seeks to restore its equilibrium
and connection, disturbed by new differences, is a striving for
\emph{grounding}. If a phenomenon A is given, and we, on going further,
find B under conditions where we should expect A to repeat itself,
astonishment is awakened, and we are satisfied only when we find in
hitherto unknown or unnoticed phenomena (C) a bridge between A and B
(A = C = B). If we are again puzzled at the transition from A to C, we
should have to seek a D, such that A = D = C, etc. Grounding is found
only where similarity overcomes and embraces the given differences.

The extreme ``Association-psychology'' (see 8 c), which would trace all
association of ideas back to external connection, is therefore incapable
of setting up a logic in the sense of a doctrine of the norms of
thinking. And yet \emph{Stuart Mill} founded his famous ``inductive
logic'' on the theory that inference from particular to particular is
the primitive form of inference,
% --- p. 213 ---
\opage{213}from which all other has developed, and to which it in turn
points back. The foundation on which logic rests thus becomes
accidental, becomes a mere ``association of ideas'', a facticity that is
not itself grounded. And yet Mill himself expressly demands more than
mere
% footnote *) on p. 213
associations of ideas.\footnote{See especially III, 21, 1: There are
many things which generate \emph{belief besides evidence. A mere strong
association of ideas} often causes a belief so intense as to be
unshakeable by experience or argument. Evidence is not that which the
\emph{mind does or must} yield to, but that which it \emph{ought} to
yield to.} --- Only when, in accordance with what was developed above
(8), one regards the relation of similarity as fundamental, can one find
within psychology the salient point from which the logical principles
and norms can develop. It is by similarity, by unity, that we measure
everything else; similarity is what brings light and clarity into the
chaos of ideas.\footnote{In his later writings (the book on Hamilton's
philosophy and the notes to James Mill) Stuart Mill has recognized
association by similarity as the fundamental one; but this recognition
did not lead him to any change in his logical theory. --- Cf.\ further
on this question: ``Den engelske Filosofi i vor Tid.'' p.~26--27. 32.
37 f. (In the last-cited place, p.~37 line 3 from the bottom, should
read: ``Sammenfattelsen er Betingelsen for Sammenligning''
[Comprehension is the condition of comparison].)}
% TR: the print closes the parenthesis inside the quotation marks.
That inference from particular to
particular does not in reality exist. When ``a burnt child dreads the
fire'', i.e.\ when the sight of fire awakens the memory of the pain that
touching fire has previously caused, this is a justified train of thought
only if this fire (c) which the child now sees is related to a certain
possible sensation (x) as the fire (a) which the child once saw before
is related to the pain (b) it felt
% footnote ***) on p. 213
that time.\footnote{Cf.\ \emph{Spencer}: Princ. of psychology. II.
p.~101 f.} But whether this logical proportion is correct does not
depend on external, accidental connection. It depends in the first place
on whether c is essentially of the same kind as a (whether c is =
a$_{c}$); only then must x also be of the same kind as b (be b$_{x}$).
It is possible that the child is mistaken --- that, e.g., what it starts
back from is a shiny metal plate; but only continued and exact
\emph{comparison} can decide that. The principle of similarity or
identity thus underlies it clearly enough.

% --- p. 214 ---
\opage{214}Without going more closely into what it is the business of
logic to set forth, we must nevertheless briefly bring forward the forms
of thought and principles that assert themselves when instinctive
association is clarified into logical thinking in the school of
experience and doubt. In other words, the task is to show how \emph{logical
necessity} develops out of merely psychological necessity.

Let the relation between A and B be the object of our consideration.
Here there are four possible cases: 1) A can be connected with B (which
we express by AB); 2) A can be found without B (which is expressed by
A (÷B)); 3) B can be found without A (which is expressed by B (÷A));
4) finally, both A and B can be absent from our experience (which is
expressed by ÷ A ÷ B). --- Now if, e.g., the first and the fourth case
(AB and ÷ A ÷ B) are given, it follows that A = B. The presupposition
naturally is that we have convinced ourselves that the 2nd and 3rd cases
(A without B and B without A) do not occur in experience. --- If the
first, third and fourth cases, but not the second, occur in experience,
then A must be AB, i.e.\ A presupposes B, but B does not necessarily
presuppose A. In this way one infers, e.g., that man (A) is a rational
being (AB), in such a way that the existence of other rational beings is
not excluded, since reason (B) may also be ascribed to the animals (so
that B can occur without A). He, on the other hand, who finds the third
possibility excluded in experience, so that only AB and ÷ A ÷ B are
given, must infer that man is the only rational being. --- If all four
cases are given in experience, it is impossible to demonstrate any law,
any definite relation between A and
% footnote *) on p. 214
B.\footnote{\emph{Jevons}; Principles of Science. 2 ed. London and
New York 1877. p.~134--136. Cf.\ G. \emph{Boole}: An investigation of
the laws of thought. London 1874. p.~81.}

The experiences are each given by themselves, but the combination and
working-up of what is given in experience is carried out according to a
law which consciousness must acknowledge as its own law.
\emph{Induction} itself, by which it dawns on us what experience
contains and signifies, presupposes the activity of consciousness just
as much as \emph{deduction}, which is the reverse process.
% --- p. 215 ---
\opage{215}If I know that A = B, I can infer from this that only the
first and the fourth of the stated possibilities can be found in
experience.

Here the difference stands out clearly between \emph{logical thinking}
and \emph{the immediate course of ideas}. The former fixes its gaze on
definite relations and seeks to exhaust and measure them. Every
investigation aims at making a choice among the possibilities that the
nature of the case offers. A logical compulsion takes the place of the
psychological necessity by which the ideas order themselves according to
the laws of the association of ideas. Psychologically speaking, every
association of ideas is \emph{possible} that accords with the laws of
similarity and contiguity. Logically, the question is whether the
transition from one idea to the other is \emph{justified} and
\emph{necessary}. More than the four combinations just cited
\emph{cannot} be given as regards A and B; if they are \emph{all} given,
no necessary inference can be drawn; if certain definite combinations
are given, then there also follows inevitably the path the train of
thought must take if it is not to become entangled in
self-contradiction. For logical necessity differs from merely
psychological necessity in being a \emph{demand} addressed to
consciousness, an ideal that is set up but is not always fulfilled.
Psychologically, inconsistency and self-contradiction can be just as
intelligible as clear, consistent thinking.

Logic is a doctrine of art, psychology a doctrine of nature. But art
grows out of nature and develops nature further. The demand that logic
makes is grounded in the very nature of consciousness and of the
activity of ideation. This shows itself when we consider what laws
consciousness follows in the logical working-up of experience.

When we set up A B and A (÷ B) above as two different cases, the
presupposition underlying this was that A cannot be anything but B or
÷ B, but must be one of the two. Here we come to the logical principle
which states that between two mutually exclusive determinations there is
no third (\textit{principium exclusi medii inter duo contradictoria}),
the principle of exclusion or, as it has been called most recently, the
\emph{principle of duality}.

% --- p. 216 ---
\opage{216}This principle, on closer consideration, includes in turn two
still simpler principles. --- The first is the principle of
\emph{self-contradiction}: A cannot at once be B and ÷ B, or: B is not
÷ B. --- This principle plays a part in all thinking. It is from the
first contradiction that sets thought in motion, and all our reflection
is in a way a hunt for contradictions, for eliminating the cases that
conflict with the presuppositions (e.g.\ A (÷ B), when A = B). On the
other hand, everything may be assumed that does not lead to
self-contradiction, and everything must be assumed whose negation leads
to self-contradiction. A discussion is a trial of strength in
consciousness between the various associations of ideas, a
systematically conducted
% footnote *) on p. 216
process of elimination.\footnote{Cf.\ Spencer's essay ``Sandhedens
Kendemærke'' (Test of truth) in ``Mindre Afhandlinger af Herbert
Spencer'' [Minor Essays by Herbert Spencer]. Danish transl. Kbhvn.
1877. p.~148.}

In the second place, the principle of duality presupposes that every
thing is what it is. This is the principle of \emph{identity} (A = A),
the most abstract of all principles. Every concept must, wherever and
whenever I apply it, be identical with itself, preserve its essence
under all forms and relations. This most abstract and formal principle
is the inner bond that makes transitions of thought and inferences
possible. Whether the proposition A = B holds we cannot always directly
experience. They perhaps stand so far from each other that they cannot
be immediately compared. The task then is to find a connecting middle
term. If A = C and C = B, then A = B must also hold. For when C = B, B
must everywhere be substitutable for C --- thus also in A = C. The
inference thus rests on substitution by virtue of identity.

\emph{Aristotelian logic}, which has been dominant right up to the most
recent times, did not yet grasp this simple and fundamental point of
view. It took the relation between the whole and its parts as the basis
of the theory of inference. When A is a part of C, and C is a part of B,
then A must be a part of B --- that was the model inference (inference
in the first figure) of Aristotelian logic. But alongside this form of
inference several others were set up, whose validity and
% --- p. 217 ---
\opage{217}scope were determined by laborious and intricate rules. One
had seen the difference and collision between psychology and logic, but
had not found the connecting and common element. Its warnings against
invalid inferences set up boundary posts, but these boundaries are not
traced back to a clear and general principle. A single example, already
hinted at above (4), will be illuminating at once with regard to the
relation between psychology and logic and with regard to the relation
between Aristotelian logic and the logic of identity.

For the immediate course of ideas any similarity and any contiguity is
enough to bring about a connection between ideas. The mythologies,
dreams, children's fancies, the deliria of the insane, and the
transitions between the different meanings of one and the same word
seem to show that there is no impossible association of ideas. Only
under compulsion does the immediate course of ideas submit to logical
control. When A is C and B is C, it promptly decides that A is B --- as
when the child asserts that snow is sugar. \emph{Aristotle} (Analyt. pr.
I, 5) had already pointed out that one cannot infer positively in the
2nd figure: from the fact that A and B both belong to the same whole
(C), nothing can be inferred as to their mutual relation.\footnote{Cf.\
S. \emph{Heegaard}: Den formelle Logik systematisk fremstillet [Formal
Logic Systematically Presented]. Kbhvn. 1871. p.~78 f. --- Erasmus
Montanus's inferences, by which he turns his mother into a stone and the
parish clerk into a cock, are positive inferences in the 2nd figure. He
has thus not studied his ``Logica'' properly.} Aristotelian logic first
sets up a form of inference and afterwards determines the limit of its
application. \emph{The newer logic of identity}, on the other hand,
whose principle was stated by \emph{Leibnitz}, and which has been
developed in our day by English investigators (\emph{Boole} and
\emph{Jevons}), takes the principle of identity as its basis, whereby
the unjustified inferences are cut off from the first: When A = C and
B = C, then A = B as well. The various ``figures'' and the various
warnings against their misuse thereby fall away. The concept snow and
the concept white do not coincide, nor do the concepts sugar and white;
substitution is therefore impossible. The newer logic thus rests on the
principle of substitution, while the older logic rests on the principle
of subsumption. The logic of identity
% --- p. 218 ---
\opage{218}throws a clear light on the fundamental activity of thinking,
and thereby on the inner connection between logic and psychology.

c) Thinking is distinguished from the immediate course of ideas not only
by more exact generalization and by the striving for grounding, but also
by seeking \emph{new differences}. Just as little as thinking relies on
the exactness of the agreements that immediately present themselves,
just so little does it rest content with the completeness of the given
differences. This is just as important a point in the process of thought
as those cited hitherto; for logical inference itself presupposes a
completeness in the given experience. A more comprehensive point of view
would perhaps in many cases discover combinations that one had not
regarded as given. What matters, then, is to widen the horizon of
experience, to cast the net that seeks for facts out over deeper
grounds. But the motive for seeking can lie only in the connection of
what is already given. When this has gaps or discrepancies, the need for
filling in and clearing up asserts itself. If the full explanation does
not lie in the given elements, then other elements must be sought,
through whose combination with the given ones the explanation can be
reached. It is Herbart's merit to have shown that the ground cannot be
anything single, but always consists in a relation among
% footnote *) on p. 218
several factors.\footnote{Cf.\ already Tschirnhausen's criticism of
\emph{Spinoza's} metaphysics. (My book ``Spinozas Liv og Lære''
[Spinoza's Life and Teaching]. p.~69 f.). --- Later this consideration
was urged by \emph{Fries} against \emph{Reinhold}, who opened the era
of German speculation with the demand that all scientific propositions
should be derived from one single principle. (See Fries's work:
``Reinhold, Fichte und Schelling''. Leipzig. 1803. p.~11 f.)}

The new nuances that are sought contribute to a closer determination of
what is already given in consciousness. The given ideas are seen in new
lights, new relations. This process has its expression in
\emph{judgment}. Psychologically speaking, it is more correct to
conceive judgment as a nuancing, a making precise or an analysis of a
given idea than as a combination of different independent concepts. In a
judgment such as: The man is good, the concepts ``man'' and ``good'' are
not fetched from different
% --- p. 219 ---
\opage{219}regions of consciousness in order only then to be combined.
There stands from the first before consciousness a total action, an
event, to which both the man and his good sides or actions belong, and
the mental operation carried out in the judgment is a division, a
distinguishing bringing-out of the elements that were given in
unresolved unity for the immediate apprehension. --- \emph{Hegel} has
already aptly pointed to the etymology of the German word
\textit{Urtheil}, by which it is signified that the unity of the concept
is the first and precedes the distinction between the parts of its
content.\footnote{Encyclopädie der philos. Wissenschaften. §~166. ---
Cf.\ also \emph{Wundt}: Logik. I. Stuttgart 1880. p.~49 ff, 135 ff.
Wundt develops this conception of judgment in a most instructive way.}
% TR: Urtheil, "judgment", read as Ur-theil, "original division".
In the course of the development of language we are pointed to a time
when the root-words each by itself expressed an event, a relation, or in
other words were ``sentences in the larval state'', so that, e.g., the
root that meant to strike designated the agent, the action
% footnote **) on p. 219
and the patient all at once.\footnote{L. \emph{Geiger}: Ursprung und
Entwickelung der menschlichen Sprache und Vernunft. I. p.~205.} Only
when attention was directed to each single one of these parts of the
fundamental idea could the need for a distinct designation of them
arise. The form of judgment as a combination of subject and predicate is
thus the expression of the fact that the unity lying in the fundamental
idea is not to be abolished by the distinguishing bringing-out, but is
to persist in and through it. --- At later stages of the development of
language, where --- as in the modern European languages, especially in
English --- the inflectional language is in turn replaced by the
% footnote ***) on p. 219
isolating language\footnote{\emph{Tegnér}: Om Språkets makt öfver
tanken, p.~54 f. --- Cf.\ \emph{Wundt}: op.\ cit.\ p.~36 f.}, a
\emph{process} takes place of the same kind as that which expresses
itself in every judgment. What was before comprised in one word is now
distributed over two or three (\textit{amavi} --- \textit{j'ai aimé}).
--- The child's first words are likewise judgments or sentences ``in the
larval state''. \textit{Vov-vov} [bow-wow] means both ``the dog'',
``there is a dog'', and ``the dog barks''. Preyer's child uttered its
first judgment in the 23rd month. ``The child was drinking milk that was
too hot for it, quickly \emph{put the cup down}
% --- p. 220 ---
\opage{220}and said loudly and firmly, with great seriousness: `hot!'
This one word was to mean: `The drink was too hot.'\,''\footnote{Die
Seele des Kindes. p.~310. --- Cf.\ p.~279: »Tuhl bedeutet: 1) Mein Stuhl
fehlt; 2) Der Stuhl ist zerbrochen; 3) Ich möchte auf den Stuhl gehoben
werden; 4) Hier ist ein Stuhl.«}

The thinking search for and setting-up of differences thus leads partly
to new observations, partly to the analysis of ideas already given. By
both paths the multiplicity of the content of consciousness is
increased.

d) As regards material, thinking is dependent on the course of ideas,
and it cannot produce anything new out of itself. It shapes and seeks,
but only by the laws of the course of ideas itself do the new forms and
ideas arise. While from this point of view thinking is only the correct
association of ideas, the contrast between thinking and the association
of ideas comes out in this, that the former has the stamp of
\emph{activity}, the latter that of \emph{passivity}. Thinking is a
matter of will, as already remarked above. Certain ideas are to be
inhibited and others strengthened; this happens according to the laws of
remembering and forgetting; but the active interest in the solution of
definite problems pushes accidents aside. The willing that manifests
itself in thinking is no lawless arbitrariness; it aims precisely at
being freed from the accidental and becoming bound by a \emph{valid
law}.

Thinking is not content with what the involuntary streams of experiences
and ideas bring along. It uses this as material or as motive. Many
discoveries are owed, in a sense, to
% footnote **) on p. 220
chance\footnote{\emph{Jevons}: Principles of Science. p.~529--532.
(Accidental Discovery).}, in so far as it was a coincidence of
circumstances that awakened attention; but once this has happened, the
role of chance ends. Thinking is not content with passive observation,
but undertakes experiments and constructions by which it demands of
experience definite answers to definite questions.

That we ascribe the character of activity to thinking must not be
understood as though we always consciously directed our ideas toward a
certain object. Thinking, when it is free and energetic, runs its course
with such \emph{speed} that we
% --- p. 221 ---
\opage{221}forget ourselves. When we really think, we ``fall into
thought'', are overwhelmed by our thoughts. But will is not the same as
self-reflection and self-constraint either. We can quite well forget
ourselves entirely and yet guard against accidental and unjustified
associations of ideas, avoid straying onto side-paths and into
inconsistencies. Practice is needed for thinking as for all other
activity. And before practice has been gained there is resistance to
overcome, so that a special exertion must take place. In the effort to
hold a train of thought fast the element of will comes out clearly; when
the practiced thinker surrenders himself to the course of his thoughts,
the element of will is present in a hidden way, unites its energy with
the energy of the connection of thought itself, and comes out specially
only when difficulties and resistance arise.

\textbf{12.} While the development from the concrete individual idea
through the typical individual idea to the general idea and the concept
leads from mere association of ideas to thinking proper, another process
of development can take place which does not lead away from the concrete
individual ideas, but whose result is precisely the formation of new
concrete individual ideas. It is \emph{the imagination or fantasy} that
develops here, from the same root as thinking, but in another direction.

To understand this process of development it must be held fast that the
concrete individual idea itself is of a compound nature, is the product
of a psychical synthesis. Herein lies the possibility that the same
elements can also be put together in other ways, occur in other
combinations. If only some of the elements are exchanged for others, the
whole individual idea can acquire a different stamp. Now this happens
involuntarily in all our remembering. Single features are effaced and
replaced by others without our noticing it. Precisely when we hold the
essential features fast with certainty, such a rearrangement and
shifting of subordinate features can take place without attracting
attention, if we have no opportunity to compare the memory-image with
reality. The dream, both the dream proper and the waking dream, goes
further still and transforms the very elements that rule and determine
the individual idea, thus producing ideas of
% --- p. 222 ---
\opage{222}individual persons, things and events that have never
presented themselves in our experience. We can dream of persons who
stand before us quite clearly and vividly, and whom we have nevertheless
never seen.

This \emph{free power of combination}\footnote{It is hardly found at the
lowest stage of human existence. The imagination of savages is
reminiscent, not constructive. --- \emph{Spencer}: Princ. of Sociol.
§~39 and 47.} we make use of daily when we try to get into a matter
whose full and entire connection we do not know. When we understand a
half-sung ballad, we supplement the scattered elements given us into an
individual whole.
% TR: "forstaa en halvkvædet Vise", a Danish idiom for taking a hint.
The \emph{discoverer} of a new mechanism combines given elements, whose
laws of action he knows, into a whole and a connection that has no
complete counterpart in experience. The scientific discoverer likewise
tumbles the elements of experience about, trying their possible
combinations in order to find the one that agrees best with other
experiences. During all this a series of individual ideas forms in his
consciousness, which are successively rejected until that one presents
itself which best accommodates and arranges the given elements. The
admirable thing about scientific genius is the freedom of mind with
which it is able to look away from experience and let various
possibilities tumble about and unfold their consequences for the
imagination --- in order thereby to find a new reality that was not
accessible to direct experience. \emph{Kepler} stressed this freedom of
mind as a significant trait in Copernicus's
% footnote **) on p. 222
gifts.\footnote{Copernicus vir maximo ingenio et, qvod in hoc exercitio
magni momenti est, \emph{animo libero}. (\emph{Reuschle}: Kepler und die
Astronomie. Frankfurt 1871. p.~119).}

This free combination has its psychological motive in the feeling of
disharmony, of being overwhelmed by the chaos of the isolated elements
of ideas. What drives thinking proper forward is outright
self-contradiction, the spots on the bright surface of similarity and
inner identity. Thinking is analytical; it seeks to make differences
into disguised similarities. Free combination lets the differences stand
as they are, but arranges the manifold elements in a new
% --- p. 223 ---
\opage{223}harmony. Where it works in the service of scientific inquiry,
it nevertheless always needs thinking proper as a corrective. The formal
or abstract sciences (logic and mathematics) therefore form the basis
and corrective for the real or concrete ones (natural science and
history). The development of scientific knowledge aims at finding so
deep-lying a unity and similarity that all differences can be
accommodated within it, and on the other hand at ordering all
differences in such clear and definite forms that the laws of similarity
come forth as of themselves.

As long as fantasy stands in the service of knowledge, it is only a
detour which the process of ideation takes where the straight path is
inaccessible. Sometimes this detour cannot be walked to its end; it may
nevertheless be justified when its general direction, the bends it
makes, point toward an agreement with experience. Knowledge then ends in
a \emph{hypothesis}. But there is also a use of free combination in
which such a return to given experience is not possible, and where what
is striven for is precisely an independent and new creation, just as
the dream involuntarily produces such a one. \emph{Artistic fantasy}
forms new individual ideas with more or less consciousness. It can bear
most resemblance now to the involuntary combinations of the dream, now
to the scientific search for the solution of a problem. In the poet of
genius the combination often takes place unconsciously, so that an
% TR: the print has "efte" for "ofte" (often).
image finished in its main features suddenly emerges in consciousness.
The joining together of the single elements has taken place below the
threshold of consciousness. Or the combination is the work of the
moment, as in improvisation, where the given theme acts through the
movement it at once awakens in the life of ideas and feelings, just as
in a lively conversation one remark draws the other after it.
``L'improvisation est pour moi comme une conversation animée'', Mme de
\emph{Staël} has Corinne say. After first having praised ``the glory and
happiness of Italy'', which her hearers had given her as a subject,
Corinne feels, from the expression on the stranger's face, an urge to
strike up more serious tones and ``to speak of happiness with less
certainty'' (Corinne II. c.~2). In contrast both to the instinctive
creation that does not know
% --- p. 224 ---
\opage{224}what it is doing and to the free unfolding of images
according to the mood of the moment, there stands the energetic labor of
forming a refractory material into a new form. The poet tolerates the
self-contradictory and the incoherent just as little as the inquirer
does. But for the poet the greatest contradiction lies in the elements'
not shaping themselves into an individual figure. Every man receives his
impressions, lives through his moods; but that they can be used as
stones for the erection of a new building dawns on few. Most let the
single experiences stand as they are. The poet's fantasy, on the other
hand, goes on working with them until they shape themselves into an
image, and \emph{only} then does he feel free and clear. Just as
\emph{Newton} found an inner agreement between the motion of the moon
and the fall of a stone, so the poet discovers a whole event, a piece of
world history, where the ordinary eye sees only scattered features of
everyday life.

Even where the poet rises highest, he works with elements of experience
and according to the general laws of the association of ideas. Every
poet is in so far a realist. When \emph{Lavater} wanted to depict the
entry of the Antichrist, \emph{Goethe} rediscovered in his description,
step by step and point by point, the entry of the Electors into
Frankfurt at the coronation of Joseph the Second, ``so that even the
tassels on the heads of the isabella-colored horses were not missing''
(Aus meinem Leben. Fünftes Buch). It is well enough known how
\emph{Goethe} used real events and persons in his works. Of his
``Tasso'' he says himself (in a conversation with Eckermann): ``I had
Tasso's life, and I had my own life; and in joining together two such
strange figures with their peculiarities, the image of Tasso arose for
me, to whom I opposed Antonio as a prosaic contrast, for whom I did not
lack models either. Court, life and love relations were, moreover, to be
found in Weimar just as well as in Ferrara, and I can rightly say of my
presentation that it is bone of my bone and flesh of my flesh.'' ``I
received,'' he continues, ``in my inner self impressions of a sensuous,
lively, colorful and manifold kind, such as a lively imagination offered
them to me; and as a poet I had nothing else to do but artistically to
round off and develop such intuitions and impressions within me and, by
a
% --- p. 225 ---
\opage{225}lively presentation, to let them come forth in such a way
that others received the same impressions when they heard or read my
presentation.'' \emph{Schiller} got the idea for ``Lied von der Glocke''
through frequent visits to the bell foundry at Rudolstadt, and he did
not neglect, as a basis for the composition, to acquaint himself with
the technique. Walter Scott, on a visit to a Scottish mountain glen that
he wanted to depict in one of his novels, noted down even the names of
the most inconspicuous flowers that grew there. The main features of the
Sistine Madonna have been rediscovered in a portrait of a Roman woman
whom Raphael also painted.

The artist everywhere seeks the individual, while the inquirer seeks
what is common to the individual phenomena. The concrete individual
idea, which for the thinker is only an example or symbol, often a
disturbing symbol, is for the artist the highest thing. But neither of
them lets the given be what it is. All art differs from a mere
impression or duplication of reality in bearing the stamp of the spirit
whose work it is. This stamp does not rest on the artist's personality
coming forward immediately before the beholder; it rests on his having
made a definite \emph{choice}, conscious or unconscious to himself, of
what he will give and how he will give it. The element of will thus
comes forward here, just as in the psychology of thinking. And just as
little as there does this element of will signify arbitrariness. It is
the expression of the striving, led by a ruling feeling, that brings the
artist to his \emph{work}. How this feeling develops does not concern
the psychology of knowledge, any more than does the question on what the
value of the work of art for the beholder depends.

In the choice of material and mode of treatment lies the idealistic
element in all art, an element from which so-called realism only
apparently frees itself. It is the open side of art, on which it stands
in connection with the stirrings of spiritual life in other domains.
However deep the movements that arise here, what matters artistically is
what result they lead to. The poet may, e.g., be a thinker and a man of
science; but poetically what counts is whether his work becomes more
than abstract thoughts translated into verse --- whether image, feeling
and thought really fuse together from first to last. It is
% --- p. 226 ---
\opage{226}an unpsychological idealism that would individualize what has
lost its living connection with concrete, individual reality. On the
other hand, in that idealistic element which is present in all artistic
production there may lie the germ of an \emph{idealization} of what is
actually given. \emph{Goethe} found it apt when \emph{Merck}
characterized his striving, in contrast to that of the brothers
\emph{Stollberg}, in the following way: ``Your endeavor, your
unshakeable direction, is to give the real a poetic form; the others
seek to realize the so-called poetic, the imaginative, and that yields
nothing but foolish stuff.'' (Am meinem Leben. 18tes Buch).
% TR: "Am meinem Leben" sic in the print, for "Aus meinem Leben".
Through the poetic form a light can be cast on the real which the real
does not have in itself or at all times, and the poet's ruling feeling
discovers a world-order in which his ideals find their satisfaction. In
this way art seeks to spin further on a thread that can already be
traced in nature; it becomes an ideal continuation of natural
development. Thus, e.g., the visual arts complete the subordination of
vegetative life to animal life which appears clearly on comparing higher
and lower forms of organic
% footnote *) on p. 226
life.\footnote{J. C. \emph{Schiødte}: Det vegetative og det animale i
den dyriske og menneskelige Form [The Vegetative and the Animal in the
Animal and Human Form]. (Nordisk tidsskrift, udg. af den
Letterstedtske Forening. 1878. p.~345).} Precisely through its idealism
art comes anew into kinship with nature; for nature too makes its
choice (qualitative and sexual selection), directly or indirectly,
whereby the transition takes place from lower to higher forms. A
conception of artistic striving that would restrict it to giving a
mosaic of single observations of real things would come into conflict
with nature itself, which is constantly engaged in ``idealizing'',
inasmuch as forms and characteristic marks are, in the struggle for
life, always stamped more distinctively, in close connection with the
whole sphere within which the struggle is waged.


\section*{C. Perception of Time and Space}\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{C. Perception of Time and Space}

\textbf{1.} Already in the preliminary characterization of the life of
consciousness it was mentioned that psychical phenomena appear
% --- p. 227 ---
\opage{227}in \emph{the form of time}. Change, transition, alternation ---
and an inner connection through all alternation --- these were the most
important characteristic marks of consciousness. But in this the form of
time is already given. Psychology must therefore stop at it as something
originally given, a psychological ultimate, which is implied in all
phenomena of consciousness and which cannot itself become an object of
explanation.

It is another matter when we speak of the \emph{idea of time}, the idea
of the relation of time. This idea, like every other, has its
psychological history of development. Psychical states may go on
succeeding one another without there therefore arising in consciousness
an idea of this succession. The more consciousness approaches to being a
series of different, mutually independent sensations and ideas --- which,
as mentioned earlier, is the same as an approach to the dissolution of
the life of consciousness --- the less possibility there is of an idea of
time. The moments change, but each moment lays full claim to
consciousness, without there being energy to spare for what lies before
or what comes after. Not only in its dissolution, but also in its
beginning, consciousness approaches to being a series (cf.\ B, 5), which
is why the idea of time can hardly be traced in the child before the
third year. We have seen in an earlier connection how expectation and
free recollection only gradually arise as special states in the struggle
of experiences with the exclusive sensation and the original sanguine
disposition (B, 4). This account of the development of memory we now
complete by adding an important moment in its nature, namely the
referring of the content of memory to definite points of time.

The simplest form of consciousness would be one in which two states (a
and b) succeeded each other without intermediate links. Now so long as a
and b each by itself occupy consciousness while they are present, no
idea of time can arise; a is forgotten when b arises, and conversely.
There must be something which so encompasses a and b that their
alternation can appear as a different filling-out of one and the same
schema. This common encompassing element can be nothing other than a
sensation or feeling which remains constant while a and b alternate, and
which therefore
% --- p. 228 ---
\opage{228}furnishes the fixed background in contrast to which the
change, the succession, can stand out clearly. Besides the alternating a
and b we must therefore have a third, relatively unchanging element x,
which can make an effect of contrast possible. We are reminded here that
the unity of the self, of consciousness, is borne not only by the formal
connection and interaction among everything in consciousness, but also
by a dominant feeling (B, 5). This fundamental feeling, which in large
part, and in the case of the lowest conscious beings exclusively, is
determined by the general or vital sensations, is thus a necessary
presupposition for the rise of the idea of time.

\textbf{2.} The idea of time gains greater clearness when intermediate
links are inserted between a and b, so that consciousness, in order to
get from a to b, must pass through m and n, constantly with x as the
background for the whole series of alternating states. The presupposition
is, however, that m and n do not themselves appear with such great
strength and interest that they obscure a and b, but that a and b
continue to stand as the main points. Let a, for example, be the
sensation of hunger, b the sensation of satiety, so that m and n
designate the means by which hunger can be replaced by satiety (the
sight of prey, capture, etc.). A fixed connection a-m-n-b will thereby be
formed, and a rhythmic alternation will take place, with which
consciousness gradually becomes familiar and which it easily surveys. The
more manifold the states become with higher development, the more
necessary it is that there be certain points which constantly recur,
like elevated places in the succession, from which the rest can be
surveyed and measured.

For the idea of time presupposes two things: 1) \emph{consciousness of
the change}, the succession; this arises through contrast with a
constant sensation; --- 2) an order which makes \emph{a certain measuring
and grouping} possible in the series of changes; this arises through
sensations which recur according to a rhythmic law.

A single constant sensation or feeling would not make an idea of time
possible. The more we become absorbed in a single thought, the more we
are, as it were, removed from time, which is also why the mystics call
eternity a ``standing now.'' On the other hand, the mere succession of
sensations would not
% --- p. 229 ---
\opage{229}make an idea of time possible either; there would be lacking
something that could lead to surveying and measuring the succession.

The more rhythmic series form themselves, and the more practice
consciousness gains in surveying them, the more clearly does the idea of
the time-series stand out, in a certain contrast to the sensations that
fill it. Just as many parts of time as are filled by the series a-m-n-b
may under other circumstances be filled by the series c-p-q-d, and so
on. With this the conditions are present for the formation of a general
idea of time. The limits of the time-series are pushed ever farther out,
both backward and forward, the further experience and practice in
handling the idea of time advance. We try in vain to imagine a first
beginning and a last end. Homer is right that rational beings are those
that ``look before and after''; we do this involuntarily, even when we
speak of the beginning or end of time. It does not follow from this that
we can have an idea of an infinite time. We merely cannot cut the
time-series short. Our imagination may grow tired of continuing the
succession in idea, of adding link to link; but reflecting consciousness
recognizes that it is only fatigue that compels it to stop. That time is
infinite can thus have the correct negative meaning that it cannot end.
--- Infinite time is therefore not the same as eternity. Succession and
change conflict with the concept of eternity. The popular view conceives
eternity as a continuation or a forerunner of time. The view of the
philosophy of religion, on the other hand, has always maintained that
time and eternity cannot stand in this external relation to each other;
for it, eternity is an inner concentration of what, in the form of time,
is spread out in an endless series of succession. Thus \emph{Master
Eckart} says: ``The day that passed a thousand years ago is no farther
from eternity than the hour in which I stand here; and the day that is to
come in a thousand years is no farther from eternity than the hour in
which I am now speaking. --- --- He who in the midst of time has set his
heart on eternity, in him is the fullness of time.''\footnote{---
Augustine defined time as ``the distension of the mind'' (\textit{distentio
animi}. Confess. XI, 26). Distension is the opposite of concentration.
The mystical and speculative philosophy of religion postulates an
absolute concentration, in which all ``distension,'' all alternation and
becoming, is annulled; here psychology is not able to follow it. Cf.\
\textit{Spinozas Liv og Lære} [Spinoza's Life and Teaching].
p.~142--144.} The
% --- p. 230 ---
\opage{230}concept of eternity in the philosophy of religion brings out
\emph{the value of the content of time} as independent of the
differences of time. It comes into conflict with the psychological idea
of time when it confuses this determination of value with an actual
annulment of the differences of time, which is psychologically
unthinkable.

\textbf{3.} But even the limited idea of time is not without difficulty
for psychological understanding. That general schema or pattern, which we
think of as filled out in different ways, we cannot imagine in and for
itself. It shares the lot of all general ideas, that of needing an
individual representation. But the time that we can immediately imagine,
or encompass in a single moment, is very short. Now when the idea is
nevertheless to have as its content a time-series stretching far beyond
the present moments, this content must undergo an abbreviation. Quite
clearly I imagine only the transition from one moment to another; the
idea of spans of time that include myriads of moments can come about only
by a symbolic route. The vanished days, weeks, months and years --- not
to speak of longer periods --- pass before our consciousness, so to
speak, with diminished dimensions. Were we to remember past time as
clearly as we imagine the minutes that make up what we call the present
time, memory would become an impossibility. We therefore always imply a
different scale for the past than for the present and the nearest
future. With the more distant future it goes with us just as with the
past. Only when we really want to put ourselves back vividly into the
past do we seek to expand the differences of time in memory, though never
so much that the extent of the present time is reached. It goes, then,
with remembered time as with the strength of remembered sensations (B, 7
a); both the duration and the strength of our original experiences are
remembered only indirectly. --- On this symbolic element in the idea of
time, once it has reached
% --- p. 231 ---
\opage{231}a further development, depends the fact that it is formed only
comparatively late, and long remains within certain limits. It first
reaches its full clearness when it becomes possible to present it in a
distinct symbolic form, to fix the fleeting rhythms of ideas in a resting
image. And this happens only when the perception of space comes to its
aid. It is only through the form of space that an intuition of time
becomes possible. We then conceive time as a straight line, infinitely
prolonged in both directions.

The idea of time is a typical individual idea. Wherever we observe time,
it is the same time whose parts we have before us. It is like a river
that we observe at different stretches. It often hides itself from us, as
when we sleep, are unconscious, or for other reasons do not know ``where
the time has gone.'' But as soon as attention is aroused again, we
reconstruct the missing course of time. Time is thus related to the
particular times not only as a general concept is related to the
individual cases included under it, but also as our dominant idea of an
individual is related to our various observations of this individual.

\textbf{4.} So long as the idea of time rests only on the alternation of
our inner states, the \emph{estimation of time} is very uncertain. In
particular, the estimation of the content of what is experienced plays
in and diverts attention from the succession itself, its stages and
landmarks. Even a long time filled with monotonous experiences can pass
quickly when what is experienced lays claim to our interest. The seven
years went for Jacob as a few days, because he loved Rachel. If we
experience many important events in a very short time, the time may seem
to us to have been very long, since we involuntarily infer from the
weightiness and richness of content of the experiences to a long extent
of time. This is especially so when what is experienced has brought about
a far-reaching change in our fate; what lies before the turning-point is
pushed back in time, even if the turning-point lies very near the
present.

If we disregard entirely the significance of what is experienced, the
estimation of time is determined merely by the quantity of the features
experienced. Time that is divided up and studded with salient points
seems
% --- p. 232 ---
\opage{232}longer to us than time that has flowed by uniformly.\footnote{Here
perhaps the answer might be sought to a question which a contributor to
the first volume of the Revue philosophique raises: why the content of a
recollection which is itself remembered seems to us to lie farther back
in time than when we first had the recollection. The first moment of
recollection forms, in relation to the later one, a station, an
intermediate link, which serves for division and thereby makes the
perspective more distinct.} Here every individual has his own scale,
taken from the speed with which his ideas are accustomed to move. If he
is now compelled to fall into a much slower tempo, through the
impressions slackening their pace, he feels unsatisfied, hampered,
impatient. A feeling of boredom and weariness arises. A much quicker
succession of impressions than usual can have the same effect, since
such a quantity of ideas streams into consciousness that it cannot order
and master them, so that it soon becomes unable to keep up. --- When the
time of waiting seems very long, it is not merely because it is not
filled with experiences, but also because the idea of what is expected
constantly comes up and must constantly be pushed back again. A painful
feeling is thereby aroused, which gives rise to an unfavorable judgment
about the time.

In states that are especially favorable to recall in memory (cf.\ B, 7
c), such manifold events can unfold before the imagination that it seems
to us as if long times had been unrolled before us. Persons who have been
on the point of drowning have in a single second seen their whole past
life pass by in memory, often with distinct appearance of the smallest
details. The taking of opium can bring about the same thing. \emph{De
Quincey} describes how time, as it were, swelled up for him. ``I often
seemed to have lived 80 or 100 years in a single night; indeed it was
sometimes as if a millennium must have passed from the one day to the
next, or at least a span of time which stretched far beyond the limits
of a single human being's
% footnote **) on p. 232
experience.''\footnote{\textit{En Opiumæders Bekendelser}
[Confessions of an Opium-Eater]. p.~161.} A dream which could be
ascertained to have lasted only 2 or 3 minutes represented a procession
of bloody figures which seemed to the dreamer to last five
% footnote ***) on p. 232
hours.\footnote{\emph{Brierre de Boismont}: Des hallucinations, 3 ed.
p.~267.} This also explains
% --- p. 233 ---
\opage{233}how visions can unroll time and eternity for the ecstatic
seer, although not so much time elapses as it takes for the hourglass to
empty.

The estimation of time thus stands in a certain relation to the organic
state, just as memory does. If the measure of time originally depends on
the alternation of our sensations and ideas, and if the vital or general
sensations, as the most constant sensations, form the background
necessary for consciousness of succession and change, one must
necessarily conclude that the manner in which the organic functions take
place in the individual organism will condition the estimation of time.
Many animals seem to possess an instinctive estimation of time
(\textit{unconscious chronometry}), which is explained by regular organic
changes
% footnote *) on p. 233
in the body.\footnote{\emph{Carpenter}: Mental physiology. p.~347 ff.} Of
no less importance must be the individual's habitual active movements.
These stand out more distinctly and more objectively, and therefore
furnish more definite points of support. Beings whose vital functions and
active movements take place quickly must therefore be supposed to have a
finer scale for time than beings in whom the corresponding activities
take place with greater slowness. A gnat's wings make (according to
Marey) 330 strokes a second; if these wing-strokes are noticed by the
gnat as we notice the movements of our arms, one and the same time must
seem far longer to the gnat than it seems to us.

Experimental investigations point in the same direction as these general
experiences and inferences. In Vierordt's investigations of the ability
to estimate differences of time it turned out that with very small spans
of time there was a tendency to overestimate, with larger spans a
tendency to underestimate, the length of time. Later investigations by
\emph{Wundt} led to the result that at a difference of time of 0.72, or
nearly ¾, of a second the actual difference of time and the reproduced
one coincide. This, then, is the length of time that we most easily
apprehend and retain in memory and use as a scale. What lies below it we
overestimate, what lies above it we underestimate. Remarkably, the value
stated agrees with the time we need to form simple associations of ideas;
% --- p. 234 ---
\opage{234}the course of ideas thus proves to be our original clock. And
further, the same value agrees approximately with the time the leg needs
to swing in brisk walking. It is thus a psychical constant, valid for
both mental and bodily
% footnote *) on p. 234
movement.\footnote{\emph{Wundt}: Physiol. Psychol. II. p.~286. ---
\emph{Julius Kollert}: Untersuchungen über den Zeitsinn. (In Wundt's
»Philosophische Studien«. 1 Bd. 1 Heft. Leipzig 1881).}

The need to put an objective measure of time in place of the subjective
one, whose uncertainty was bound to be easily noticed, made itself felt
early. The great, regularly recurring phenomena of nature furnished a good
schema. The movements of the sun and moon, day and night, morning, noon
and evening served as a basis. For finer division the sand in an
hourglass was used, the water in a clepsydra, or else a burning candle.
But only with the pendulum and the chronoscope has greater exactness
become possible. \emph{Wheatstone} measured the speed of an electric
spark and found it to be 1/115,200 of a second. With Siemens's
chronoscope even
% footnote **) on p. 234
1/1,000,000 of a second can be determined.\footnote{\emph{Jevons}:
Principles of Science. 4 ed. p.~307 f. --- A. \emph{Paulsen}:
Naturkræfterne. III. p.~129.}

This exactness we can imagine continued to ever higher degrees. But a
conclusion is as little thinkable here as with the length of time. We
measure time by means of uniform movements in nature. But this very
uniformity must then be proved --- and here we thus come to move in a
circle. An absolutely uniform time is an ideal; it is the demand that
every estimation of time whatever shall again be subjected to control.
Every scale that has been tried in order to proceed from complete
uniformity has proved to vary. Only in the symbolic presentation of time
as a line is absolute uniformity present. But there, precisely, the
idealizing abstraction has done its work.

\textbf{5.} That the form of time is present from the beginning of
consciousness there can be no \emph{doubt}. The psychological
investigation of time therefore concerns only the idea of time and the
estimation of time. On the other hand, it is a matter of dispute whether
\emph{the form of space} is also original. That in any
% --- p. 235 ---
\opage{235}case it cannot stand in the intimate relation to consciousness
that the form of time does follows from the general character of
consciousness. The states of consciousness follow one another in time;
but no meaning can be attached to their spreading out in space. What
appears in the form of space can then only be objects for consciousness,
not consciousness itself. Already in this it lies that the form of time,
psychologically regarded, is more original than the form of space; the
latter does not seem to be absolutely necessary for consciousness. The
question now is whether experience points to the form of space being in
reality a psychical product, formed according to the general
psychological laws. There would then be, or have been, a stage in the
development of consciousness in which sensations and ideas appeared only
with a certain strength and a certain quality, without their content
being shaped into extended images.

It is difficult for us to think ourselves into a consciousness without
any perception of space. We think, after all, constantly in images. Even
our moods and feelings we cannot apprehend and express except by means
of intuitions and images. From this one might conclude that the form of
space must be original; the symbol might even seem to lie nearer to us
than what is symbolized, so that the intuition of space would possess
greater originality than the intuition of time. An acute thinker,
\emph{Albert Lange}, has in fact drawn this conclusion. ``The empirical
observation of our inner state cannot,'' he says (Logische Studien.
Iserlohn 1877. p.~139), ``be accomplished in the mere form of time. We
always find a plurality of sensations simultaneously, which can only be
combined into a synthesis in the form of a spatial image. \ldots\ All
our empirical ideas of time rest on ideas of space. A line forms our idea
of the course of time. Movements in space give us occasion to measure
time. Should one not conclude from this that the idea of time is
secondary in relation to the idea of space?'' --- To this it must first
be remarked that because the higher and clearer development of the idea
of time and of the measurement of time presupposes the idea of space
(see 4), it is not thereby said that the latter is also the more
original. \emph{Lange} infers too much from the necessity of symbolism.
Thought must after all be more original than the word, although it needs
the word for its full
% --- p. 236 ---
\opage{236}clearness and exactness. However intimately feeling is bound
to its expression, it would still be to stand the matter on its head to
take feeling for secondary in relation to its expression. It is, after
all, only at the higher stages of the life of consciousness that a wide
circle of symbols stands at its service. Only with two of our senses,
sight and touch, do sensations arrange themselves for us in the form of
space; with hearing, smell and taste localization is not originally
present; there we have to do only with the strength and quality of the
sensations. The proper, definite intuition of space is, as we shall see
in what follows, bound up with the sensations of sight. Visual images
play so great a part in our world of ideas that we abstract from them
only with difficulty. The more we fix our attention on tones, and on
impressions of taste and smell, the more we shall approach a
consciousness endowed merely with the form of time, and see the
possibility that an inner manifoldness may be given in consciousness
without its being combined into unity in the form of space.

This shows itself still more clearly in the states of feeling. These are
certainly most often accompanied by local sensations (in the chest, in
the heart, e.g.); but inner observation makes it easy to distinguish
between the feeling proper and its organic consequences. Pleasure and
pain, joy and sorrow, hope and fear can stir in us without being directly
symbolized. There is indeed in every feeling something inexpressible,
incommensurable with every expression. The feelings drive us to seek
forms and expressions, but they are not themselves ordered under the form
of space. When, in a despondent mood, a gleam of hope emerges, we do not
imagine the hope as above, or to the right or left of, the despondency,
as we do when we simultaneously have sensations of different colors.
Different elements of consciousness can thus assert themselves
