simultaneously without being ordered in the form of space. The obscurity
and difficulty of immediate psychological observation is connected in
part with this (see I, 8 a). We lack a form of intuition for
simultaneous inner \emph{phenomena}, while we possess one for
\emph{simultaneous outer phenomena}.

\textbf{6.} We perceive space in three \emph{dimensions}: up and down,
right and left, forward and back. This threefoldness can be reduced to a
twofoldness: distance (or depth) and extension in surface.


% --- p. 237 ---
\opage{237}That the \emph{perception of distance} cannot be due to any
single sensation is easily seen. Every sensation presupposes,
physiologically regarded, that a physical impression comes to the sense
organ. But distance itself makes no physical impression on us. We
measure distance by a line from the object to ourselves; but no
impression can inform us of the existence of this line. It designates
the direction of the impressions, but itself calls forth no sensation.
We perceive distance only when we can in one way or another measure out
this line; but such a measuring-out is not any single sensation; it is a
process of comparison, which either already presupposes a certain idea of
space or else can build only on the strength and kind of the sensations
we get by moving ourselves, or parts of our body, toward the object.

Such a measuring-out by means of movement we do in fact perform in every
perception of an object. The eye adjusts itself involuntarily so that
the impression of light strikes the yellow spot on the retina. The two
eyes are set so that the visual axes meet at the point that attracts
attention. We reach for the object or move toward it in order to feel
it. We thereby get sensations of movement which stand in a definite
connection with the position of the object in relation to us. With the
sight or the touch of the object there now combines, through association
and practice, the idea of the sensations of movement that are a
necessary presupposition for the most distinct perception of the object.
More or less clearly, distance means for us the greater or smaller
series of sensations of movement that we would have if we moved from our
standpoint to the object. In agreement with this is the fact that the
perception of distance is clear and distinct only with near objects, and
that among distant objects it is the more distinct the more familiar they
are to us. We have the most distinct perception of space of what our own
hands have immediately measured. Greater distances we understand (like
greater periods of time) only symbolically, by regarding them as a sum of
small, immediately measurable distances.

The sense of touch and the sensations of movement bound up with its
activity are the most important basis of the perception of distance. The
proper measure of the object we get by active feeling
% --- p. 238 ---
\opage{238}of it. The distances we perceive in this way we always imply
in visual perception. Distant objects, which to sight look smaller, we
nevertheless estimate immediately according to the size they would have
for touch. Only secondarily does the size of known objects for sight in
turn become a means of estimating their distance.

This theory, which was first put forward by \emph{Berkeley} (Theory of
% footnote *) on p. 238
vision. Dublin 1709),\footnote{\emph{Berkeley}, however, does not yet
lay such great weight on the sensations of movement as has been done in
the account given here. It is especially \emph{Bain} and \emph{Spencer}
who have brought out the importance of the sensations of movement for
the perception of space. --- \emph{Helmholtz} has expressed a similar
view in his famous »Physiologische Optik«.} is confirmed in part by
observations of newborn children and of those born blind who have been
operated on. Although the child turns toward the light quite early, it
hardly has a perception of distance. The movements of the two eyes are
as a rule not coordinated, so that the visual axes do not from the first
necessarily intersect at the point that is the object of perception,
which would be a necessary presupposition if the perception of distance
rested on an innate
% footnote **) on p. 238
mechanism coming into action at once.\footnote{\emph{Descartes} already
held this view, which in our day is maintained by the so-called
\emph{nativism}. Yet Descartes grants that in the immediate perception
of distance, which seems to be simple, there lies a comparison as
presupposition (\textit{ratiocinatio involuta, similis illi, qva Geometræ
per duas stationes diversas loca inaccessa dimetiuntur}. Dioptrica. Cap.
5. §~13). Here, then, the theory of ``unconscious inferences'' is already
intimated, and that not in an empiricist but in a nativist.} Only
gradually (in the course of the first three months) does practice in the
coordination of the eyes arise, so that squinting no longer occurs so
frequently. And even after the adjustment has been brought about, the
sure perception of distance is lacking, which shows itself in the child
reaching for things it cannot reach. Even in the 2nd and 3rd years of
life the estimation of distance is
% footnote ***) on p. 238
imperfect.\footnote{\emph{Preyer}: Die Seele des Kindes. p.~24--29; 38
f; 112.}

Nor even at a later age is the cooperation of the two eyes entirely
harmonious. With practice one can see double images of objects. This
happens most easily when one directs the eyes
% --- p. 239 ---
\opage{239}at a point out in the background of the field of vision, but
fixes attention on an object lying in a straight line in front of it;
this is then seen double.

It is not quite certain whether it goes with newborn animals as with
newborn human beings. \emph{Spalding's} experiments with newly hatched
chickens and newborn pigs prove that these animals are at once able to
find their food with great sureness. The chickens dart off after a grain
or an
% footnote *) on p. 239
insect,\footnote{\emph{Adam Smith} already (»On the external senses« in
his »Essays en philosophical subjects«, edited by Dugald Stewart)
remarks that this sure ability of orientation immediately at birth is
found only in birds that build their nests on the flat ground, not in
those that build in trees and in other less accessible places.} and the
pigs after their mother's udder. A pig that was set on a chair ten
minutes after the bandage it had been given over its eyes immediately
after birth had been taken off seemed to measure the distance from the
ground, knelt, and jumped down. Here, then, a perception of distance
seems to assert itself at once, although one must surely beware of
reading too much into it, since an immediate instinct might be supposed
to assert itself, which under the guidance of the visual impression led
the animal in the direction of the food, and something similar might be
the case with the jump. It is therefore hardly justified to posit a
perception of distance prior to experience and practice, even if this
course of experience is here, as in so many other fields, far shorter
for the animal than for man.

The experiences of those born blind who have been operated on also
present features that can be interpreted according to the theory founded
by Berkeley. The man born blind who was operated on by
\emph{Cheselden} (1728) was, when he had gained his sight, so unable to
estimate distances that he believed that all objects ``touched his
eyes'' (as he himself put it), just as what he felt touched his hand.
The one operated on by \emph{Franz} (1840) took a cube for a square, a
sphere for a disk, and a pyramid for a triangle; it was only by feeling
them that he learned to understand what they were. All things seemed to
him perfectly flat. The man born blind who was operated on by
\emph{Dufour} (1876) could not judge distances without the help of his
hands. With
% --- p. 240 ---
\opage{240}outstretched hands he walked toward the shiny door-handle to
which his attention had been drawn, but stopped two steps from it, and
missed several times before he succeeded in grasping it. --- Should one
say, apropos of the last example, that he who moves toward an object must
have an idea of its distance, this is an unjustified inference. He need
only adjust his movements so that the guiding visual sensation does not
lose but successively gains in strength, and for this no idea of
distance is required. There is a game in which one is to find an object
by music, in such a way that the music sounds the louder the nearer one
comes to the object, the fainter the farther one moves away from it. Here
the seekers are obviously not guided by perception of distance; the
movement is adjusted immediately to the strength of the music.

Most of the operated persons born blind mentioned in the literature
could already before the operation distinguish between light and
darkness and be guided in their movements by the impressions of light.
But it does not follow from this that the impressions of light
themselves immediately brought with them a perception of distance.

\textbf{7.} \textbf{a.} That the idea of distance or depth arises
through a psychological process is granted even by investigators who
regard the perception of \emph{extension in surface} as original and
innate. But even if it is the case that the perception of distance is
due \emph{merely} to association and practice, the question of the
originality of the perception of space would thereby only have been
pushed a step back, so long as we have not settled it with regard to the
perception of height and breadth. One might, after all, maintain that we
become able to measure the distance between an object and ourselves only
because we already have the idea of space as regards lines and surfaces.

With regard to the perception of extension in surface, too, attempts
have been made to explain the perception of space by association of
visual sensations with sensations of touch and movement. What is
immediately perceived would then here too be sensations with a certain
strength and quality, and the perception of space would arise as a
certain ordering of the sensations. It is natural to assume, and
observations of newborn children confirm it, that what is first
perceived by sight is light and colors. The clear and
% --- p. 241 ---
\opage{241}bright, yet not too dazzling, impressions are sought out and
sought to be held fast; only later is the form of objects
% footnote *) on p. 241
perceived.\footnote{\emph{Preyer}. p.~36. 41.} It is owing to
observations and experiments by means of touch and movement that the
particular objects mark themselves off. The language of sight becomes
perfectly distinct only by the help of sensations of movement and of
touch. Those born blind who have been operated on must therefore
gradually learn to know by their appearance the same objects with which
they were very familiar by their tangible properties. The question then
arises whether the blind man, who remains restricted to sensations of
touch and movement, really has any intuition of space like that of the
seeing. We, the seeing, think at once, when we think of space, of a
visible surface at some distance from us; but what can the blind man
really imagine by space? --- Here the real paradox comes out in the
assumption of space as a psychological product. For if, by the exclusion
of visual images, we keep back only something that is not of the same
kind as visible space, we would not be able to say what space is in and
for itself, since no definition could be set up that fits both the space
of sight and that of touch. According to \emph{Berkeley} there is no
more natural connection between visual space and tactile space than
between the word and the thing it designates (Theory of vision
§~121--143).

There is in fact a definite observation on record in this direction.
\emph{Ernst Platner} writes in his »Philosophische Aphorismen« (Erster
Theil. Leipzig 1793, p.~446 f.): ``As regards the sightless idea of space
and extension, the observation and examination of a man born blind,
which I undertook in the year 1785 and continued for three whole weeks,
has convinced me that the sense of touch by itself alone is wholly
ignorant of everything that belongs to intuition and space, and knows
nothing of a being-outside-one-another in space, and, to put it briefly,
that man without sight perceives nothing at all of the external world
but the existence of something acting, which is different from the
feeling self acted upon by it, and for the rest merely the numerical
difference of the impressions or things. Time serves the man born blind
in place of space. Nearness and distance
% --- p. 242 ---
\opage{242}mean for him nothing but the shorter or longer time, the
smaller or greater number of sensations, which he needs in order to get
from one sensation to another. That the man born blind makes use of the
language of sight [just as the color-blind have learned to use the names
of colors] can deceive, and at the beginning did deceive me too. But in
reality he knows nothing whatever of things that are outside one
another, and if the objects and the parts of the body touched by them did
not make impressions of different kinds on his nerves of touch, he would
(this I noticed especially clearly) regard everything external as one
and the same thing, which affected him more strongly when he laid his
hand on a surface than when he put his finger on it, more weakly when he
merely stroked with his hand or passed lightly over a surface. On his
own body he distinguishes head and feet not by the distance, but merely
by the difference, perceptible to him with incredible fineness, in the
sensations he has of these two parts, and for the rest by time.
Likewise he distinguishes the figure of bodies merely by the character
of the impressions of touch, the cube, e.g., affecting the sense of touch
differently with its corners and edges than the sphere does.''

We have an approximation to the perception of space of the man born
blind when we try to find our way in a dark room. Only we then have the
advantage that visual space lies ready-made and can be called upon to
help in interpreting the sensations of touch and movement. Something
similar holds when we concentrate our attention on the tongue and ---
with the greatest possible abstraction from visual ideas --- observe
what perception of space it gives us. For the tongue is like one born
blind, and yet is excellently oriented in its surroundings.

\textbf{b.} Here, however, a possibility still shows itself for
maintaining the originality of the perception of space. The sensations
of movement are, it is true, always successive. But through the sense of
touch we can get several impressions simultaneously. Likewise several
impressions of light can be received simultaneously on the retina. Might
there not lie in this the possibility of an immediate perception of the
impressions as ordered in space? It might even seem necessary to assume
this. For the sensation of a color means, after all, the sensation of a
colored surface; if, namely, the colored thing were only a mathematical
point,
% --- p. 243 ---
\opage{243}it would make no impression. Even if larger objects can be
perceived only by movement of the organs of sight and touch, one would
still think that small objects could be perceived immediately, without a
successive process. There must surely be an immediate difference between
the impression of a krone and the impression of an øre. The perception
of small surfaces would then be the minimum to which \emph{nativism}, the
view that maintains the originality of the perception of space, would
restrict itself, the last position from which it could not be
dislodged.\footnote{Cf.\ \emph{Stumpf}: Der psychologische Ursprung der
Raumvorstellung. Leipzig 1873. p.~56--71. --- Professor \emph{Mahaffy} of
Dublin reports, in a letter printed in Mind 1881. p.~278 f., on a
conversation with the man born blind who was operated on by Franz. This
man, who was a physician in Kingstown, stated that immediately after
gaining his sight he saw and distinguished figures, and that outlines and
figures corresponded to what he had expected from the sense of touch.
Mahaffy regards this communication as the testimony of a competent judge
to the originality of the ability to distinguish figures by sight alone.
But neither this interpretation nor the communication itself agrees with
Dr.\ Franz's report, which was printed shortly after the operation in
»Philosophical Transactions« (1841). As stated above, the patient took a
cube for a square and a sphere for a disk. When Franz asked him to
describe the impressions the objects had made on him, he said that he
had at once noticed a difference between the cube and the sphere, but
was not able to form the idea of a square or a disk from them
\emph{before} he \emph{had perceived a sensation in the tips of his
fingers}, as if he were actually touching the objects. (Phil. Transact.
1841. I. p.~65). He thus really had to carry out a translation from the
language of the one sense into that of the other before he could
recognize the figures. There remains only the fact that he at once
noticed a difference between the figures, which speaks in favor of
nativism. But here too the experience of the sense of touch may have
helped him. He did not stand before the figures as a consciousness
without any experiences, i.e.\ a beginning consciousness, would stand.}
% TR: "a krone" and "an øre": Danish coins, the one large, the other
% small.

It naturally cannot be denied that we can get, and constantly do get, a
multitude of simultaneous impressions of light and touch, and that we
immediately perceive them as ordered in space. But it is not certain that
the beginning consciousness has the same perception. A manifoldness of
simultaneous impressions will from the first rather act as a collected
mass, give one single chaotic
% --- p. 244 ---
\opage{244}sensation. Just as quality and strength cannot from the first
be distinguished, so too the relation of space could not from the first
be perceived by itself, but would, so to speak, hide itself in a higher
degree of strength, or, to use Bain's expression, a greater massiveness
of the impression. Platner's account quoted above points in this
direction, since it shows that it was only qualitative differences in the
impressions that led the blind man to assume a manifoldness outside
himself. One can here also refer to Weber's experiment, which proved
that when the whole hand is dipped in warm water, the degree of heat
seems to be higher than when only the finger is dipped. Since no innate
idea of the difference between the fingers and the hand can be assumed,
that difference in strength must from the first have been the only one
that stood out.

A purely passive perception of simultaneously given impressions can only
be quite momentary. Activity is aroused at once; the eye moves over the
surface or the hand feels it. At once, then, succession begins to take
the place of simultaneity, the discursive that of the intuitive. We
perceive moving objects more quickly and easily than resting ones, and if
they do not move themselves, we put ourselves in motion in relation to
them. Two successive impressions on the skin can be distinguished at a
smaller distance from each other than two simultaneous ones. The lowest
animals and newborn children do not notice simultaneous differences in
their surroundings, while they can perceive successive differences
% footnote *) on p. 244
(changes).\footnote{G. H. \emph{Schneider}: Warum bemerken wir mässig
bewegte Dinge leichter als ruhende? (Vierteljahrsschr. für wiss. Phil.
II. p.~412).} By movement, then, is discovered and perceived what would
otherwise go unnoticed. The first chaotic sensation is thus analyzed more
closely by the help of a series of succeeding sensations, in which the
sensations of movement, in the nature of the case, take a prominent
place.

One might even maintain that successive and discursive perception is a
necessary presupposition for real intuition of space. Space means a
relation. That something is in space means that it occupies a certain
position in relation to other things, and that its particular parts
occupy certain positions in
% --- p. 245 ---
\opage{245}relation to one another. Instead of speaking of space
\textit{en bloc}, as a complete unity, one therefore does better to use
the more elementary determination \emph{position}. The perception of
space is then seen to rest on a comparison or a combination. It cannot
therefore be present at the first immediate observation.

\textbf{c.} It is not a matter of indifference which place on the body
is struck by the impression. Since we must learn to know our own body
through successive experiences, just like the world surrounding us, one
cannot assume an original knowledge or sensation of the place at which an
impression strikes us. Now if the impressions nevertheless act
differently at the different places, this difference can only appear to
consciousness as a certain nuance, a qualitative subsidiary determination
of the impression. The peculiar stamp the impression receives by striking
a single definite place Lotze has called its \emph{local sign}. The
conditions are different at every place of the skin and of the retina;
there must therefore be a manifoldness of local signs. In the case of
sight, these local signs might consist either in the impulses of
movement, different for each point, which tend to turn the eye so that
the impression of light falls on the yellow spot (Lotze), or in the
different quality of the sensations on the different parts of the retina
(\emph{Wundt}). In the case of the sense of touch, Lotze finds the local
signs given by the different secondary sensations which one and the same
touch brings with it at different places on the skin, on account of the
skin's differing density and tension and the different substratum it
% footnote *) on p. 245
encloses.\footnote{\emph{Lotze} has given several presentations of his
theory of local signs, most recently in Grundzüge der Psychologie
(1881). For Wundt, see Physiol. Psychol. II. p.~25. 28. 163.}

These local signs can from the first assert themselves only in successive
perception. Consciousness cannot sense them all at once; only after
having run through their series can it assign to each of the sensations
furnished with local signs its definite place. In order to fulfil their
function, the local signs must therefore make up a coherent system.
Stimulation of a single place on the skin
% --- p. 246 ---
\opage{246}or the retina can then lead to an orientation, a local
determination, without the whole series of local signs needing to be
gone through anew; for the idea of the series as a given whole serves as
an abbreviated symbol of the original successive experiences.

\textbf{8.} With all this the perception of space proper has not been
reached. We have obtained a coherent group of sensations of movement,
local signs, and impressions of light and touch, connected according to
the laws of the association of ideas. But the ordering of all this in
such a way that there results an intuition of an image whose particular
parts are \emph{outside} one another, and which is itself \emph{outside}
us (or rather outside the image of our organism), is not thereby given;
for all those sensations were qualitative and intensive, not extensive.

Here it becomes apparent that all theories of the perception of space
come into difficulty. The so-called nativistic theory regards the
perception of space as given with the very first
impressions.\footnote{The most important representatives of nativism at
present are, on the psychological side, \emph{Stumpf} (Der psychol.
Urspr. der Raumvorst.), on the physiological side, \emph{Hering} (whose
latest work is: »Der Raumsinn und die Bewegungen des Auges«. Hermann's
Handbuch. III, 1). Hering's general standpoint is indicated in the
following statement: ``That the complete exclusion of the motives of
experience is unthinkable is acknowledged by all\ldots\ Where the boundary
between the innate and the acquired lies, we do not know. But it seems
inadmissible to assume that the spatial properties of sensations and
their localization are merely the product of experience, partly because
many newborn animals directly prove the contrary, and it cannot be
assumed that the newborn human being is in this respect more than in
degree different from the animals; partly because visual sensations
without any spatial properties are something inconceivable.''} According
to it, any psychological explanation of the development of the
perception of space is both superfluous and impossible; the perception of
space must here be taken as something given from the first. The
experiences cited above (6. 7 a and b) seem, however, to speak against
this theory. The \emph{genetic} theory therefore regards the perception
of space as a psychological product, arisen through the combination of
intensive and qualitative sensations in accordance with the general
psychological laws of the association of ideas. Now, however, an
unexplained remainder is left over, since the psychological
% --- p. 247 ---
\opage{247}product has other properties than the elements from which it
is supposed to have arisen, and that precisely the property with which
the problem is concerned, namely the extensive form itself.
\emph{Herbart} and \emph{Spencer} have indeed tried to explain the
transition from the perception of successive impressions to the
intuition of what is simultaneous and extended in this way: that when it
is a matter of indifference from which end we begin the reproduction of
a series of impressions, an intuition of space forms itself; for while
time has only one direction, space has several. In the end the whole
series will then stand before us at once, wherever we begin. But by this
we arrive only at \emph{simultaneity} (coexistence), not at space. We
can, e.g., repeat a series of tones forward and backward without the
tones arranging themselves for us in space. --- Here, therefore, a
transformation must be assumed to have taken place, a psychical
synthesis analogous to the chemical syntheses by which compound
substances arise with quite other properties than the elements possess
(cf.\ B, 8 d 4). In this synthesis, in the seeing human being, the
sensations of sight play the most important part and become the dominant
language into which are translated the contributions to the perception
of space that other senses can make. Whether a corresponding synthesis
also takes place in the case of the blind, such that the sense of touch
becomes the dominant one, seems doubtful according to Platner's account.

Even if it should be the case that tangible and visible minima are
perceived as extended, a synthesis would still have to be posited. For
our images of space are continuous, uninterruptedly coherent; but neither
the retina nor the surface of the skin furnishes a basis for such a
continuity. The organs of sight and touch give the image of a mosaic,
not of an unbroken coherence. On the retina there is even a place quite
insensitive to impressions of light, ``the blind spot,'' where the optic
nerve enters the eye; but in our visual image there is no corresponding
gap; we therefore involuntarily fill out the hole in the series of
sensations. Every theory which does not hold that the perception of
space is given at one stroke from the first must therefore, in one form
or another, appeal to the constructive capacity of consciousness.


% --- p. 248 ---
\opage{248}Such an appeal is not without its drawbacks. For a
psychological view which finds the very nature of consciousness expressed
in synthesis, in the gathering into unity and coherence (II, 5. --- IV,
5), there might seem to be something quite reasonable in this
fundamental form asserting itself also in the particular psychical
processes. But it is one thing to hit upon the right characterization
of consciousness as a whole, another to apply the characteristic mark
thus found as a \textit{deus ex machina} to special psychological
problems. The way is easily opened to arbitrariness; into such a
psychical synthesis one can, after all, put whatever one likes. Lotze
therefore surely proceeds most cautiously when he declares that the
purpose of his theory of local signs is not to explain the intuition of
space itself, but only the motives and aids the soul uses when it forms
its images of space. That the soul forms images of space out of certain
of its sensations Lotze regards, on the other hand, as a factual
capacity or impulse that cannot be an object of further explanation.

The \emph{genetic} theory too, then, must acknowledge an original element
in the perception of space. This original element points back to the
general property of the activity of consciousness; what was not
explained by the special sensations was, after all, precisely the unity
and continuity of the space intuited. But unity and continuity there
could be, even if the sensations were combined into other forms of
intuition than our space. Here something always remains that must be
regarded as merely factual or historical.

The genetic theory stands in complete opposition to the nativistic only
when it assumes that all the conditions for the development of the
intuition of space are given with the experiences the single individual
can have in the course of its development. But this view is improbable
on account of the unexplained remainder that is left when we compare the
elementary sensations with the fully developed intuition of space. If in
the synthesis to which the intuition of space owes its definitive rise we
see the manifestation of a constructive capacity acting instinctively,
then the question of how this capacity came to be directs us beyond the
single individual to the natural context within which it arises. The
experiences
% --- p. 249 ---
\opage{249}which in the life of the single individual cannot lead to the
goal indicated may, in the course of the development of the race, have
gradually adapted the organization in such a way that inherited
dispositions supplement what is insufficient in individual experiences.
The hypothesis of evolution, which was first applied in this field by
\emph{Herbert Spencer} (1855), opens the prospect of following the
problem further back than was possible so long as one kept to the
experiences of individual life.

How near the genetic and the nativistic theories can in this way come to
each other may be seen from the fact that, while on the one side it is
granted that what is immediately given in the perception of space may be
vanishingly small in relation to what is added by association, and that
in the original sensations there is really given only a possibility of a
definite intuition of space (\emph{Stumpf}), on the other side it is
intimated that everything may be so arranged in the organization that
the time between the first impression of light on the retina and the
rise of the idea of space becomes vanishingly small
(Wundt).\footnote{\emph{Stumpf}: Der psychologische Ursprung der
Raumvorstellung. p.~114 ff. 184. --- \emph{Wundt}: Physiol. Psychol. II.
p.~164. Cf.\ also \emph{Spencer}: Principles of Psychology. II. p.~203
ff.}

\textbf{9.} Whether one adheres to a nativistic or a genetic theory, it
is in any case a necessary presupposition for the rise of the intuition
of space that a definite organic basis be present. The dispute of the
theories concerns only (or ought to concern only) whether the functions
conditioned by the organization come into force at once or need some
time of preparation and practice.

The more precise demonstration of the organic structures that are of
importance for the rise of the intuition of space is the task of the
physiology of the senses to
% footnote **) on p. 249 (continues on p. 250)
give.\footnote{On this see Panum: \textit{Sanserne og de vilkaarlige
Bevægelser} [The Senses and the Voluntary Movements]. p.~234--238, and
Wundt's very detailed presentation (II. chaps.~11 and 13). --- In the
preceding investigation only the most general psychological points of
view have been taken into account. Whoever wishes to be introduced to
the special psychological and physiological questions which the theory of
the perception of space has led to discussing is referred to Wundt's
work, where the whole material is found collected and critically worked
up. --- The question whether the geometrical axioms are grounded in the
capacity of intuition determined by our organization, or whether with
the necessary practice we should be able to imagine and intuit space of
another character, belongs to the theory of knowledge, not to
psychology. Purely logically such a possibility naturally cannot be
denied; but in fact we are surely bound to our space, characterized by
the axioms of Euclidean geometry, so long as our organization has not
undergone a change through adaptation to other conditions of
existence.} Besides what has been indicated in this
% --- p. 250 ---
\opage{250}respect in the foregoing, we shall here only point to the
importance of the central mechanism by which a close connection is made
possible between the sense-impressions and the muscular movements of the
sense organs. In the case of the sense of touch the optic thalami, in the
case of sight the corpora quadrigemina, seem to be the reflex centers by
which this connection, and with it the physiological expression of the
psychological synthesis, is brought about; though the cerebrum probably
also plays a part.

The apparatus that have here been made ready are perhaps (cf.\ 6) in some
animals able to function immediately after birth, so that the
experiences necessary for the perception of space can be had at once and
easily. In man, as it seems, several months pass before this point is
reached.

\textbf{10.} Hitherto we have spoken of the form of space itself, the
capacity to intuit images of space. As regards the idea of space as a
general idea, or rather a \emph{typical} individual idea, it develops in
a way corresponding to the idea of time (1--3), as attention is directed
to the schema common to all particular images of space and to its
capacity for extension. From the first the idea of space is limited. The
man born blind who was operated on by \emph{Cheselden} could not imagine
lines in space beyond the limits of his field of vision. He knew that the
room he was in was only a part of the house; but he could not comprehend
that the whole house could look larger than the room. He thus still
lacked the ability to use the individual idea in a symbolic way. When
this ability develops, it is discovered that no limit can be posited for
the division or the extension of space, any more than for that of time.

% --- p. 251 ---
\opage{251}%
\section*{D. The Perception of the Real}\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{D. The Perception of the Real}

\textbf{1.} Sensations, ideas, concepts, time and space are forms under
which the content of consciousness appears and orders itself. We have
followed this ordering from its simplest stage, in the grouping and
interaction of sensations, to its highest stage, in thinking directed
toward definite problems. The laws in force were everywhere the same.
The motive for the advance from the involuntary ordering in the play of
sensations and ideas to scientific thinking and artistic imagination lay
in the criticism that advancing experience necessarily brought with it.
New differences and contrasts demand new activity, in order that
connection and unity may be maintained and chaos overcome. The logical
principles and the aesthetic laws, which it is not the business of
psychology to set forth, nevertheless develop according to psychological
laws of nature, and thereby prove their intimate connection with human
nature; they do not suddenly come forward as something forced upon it
from outside.

But here the question arises how it comes about that consciousness, in
the content of its sensations and ideas and in the connection among them
brought about by thinking, sees a \emph{reality} independent of itself.
Because our knowledge develops according to definite psychological laws
of nature, it is not thereby said that it leads to a reality. The
fantasies of the insane and the images of the dream obey, after all, the
same laws, which is why we have so often been able to use them for
orientation. How then does it dawn on consciousness that in its content
it has a reality and not a dream? Can any definite psychological activity
--- observation or act of thought --- be indicated by which we are led
to the conviction that we stand before a reality?

It is not a question that first arises at an advanced standpoint in the
development of consciousness. From the first beginning of consciousness
it is really posed without interruption. The individual is not allowed
to order his ideas as he pleases. There goes on a constant education
through disappointments, which may be hard and painful. (Cf.\ V B, 4.)
The first disappointment lays the first foundation for the contrast
% --- p. 252 ---
\opage{252}between the possible and the real, or between dream and
reality. --- Here we return to this point in order to carry the train of
thought further.

\textbf{2.} The impulse to movement which stirs so early in conscious
beings leads them involuntarily to intervene in the surrounding world.
But they soon learn that their movements are not allowed to proceed
unhindered. At certain points they meet a resistance, and in the
sensation of resistance, in the sensation of hampered movement, the
individual meets something foreign, something which --- whatever else it
may be --- is not itself. It tries perhaps again and again to overcome
the resistance; yet it never quite succeeds, so long as life lasts. New
barriers are set up in place of those overcome.

Seen from one side, \emph{every} sense-sensation is a sensation of
resistance. Every physical impression acts only by reaching the outer
surface of the organism, in particular those parts of it where
receptivity to the kind of impression in question is especially
developed (thus the eye for rays of light, the ear for waves of air,
etc.). It is an old thought that the sense of touch is the universal
sense, and that the other senses have developed out of it. But precisely
on account of the fineness of the special senses they do not play so
great a part in the development of the firm conviction of a reality.
They cooperate, but presuppose a firm kernel about which they can
gather, and this firm kernel is given in the sensation of resistance to
our movement. A being that possessed merely sight, hearing, smell and
taste would lead, as it were, an ethereal existence, would be placed
outside tangible reality. The very word \textit{Genstand} [object] means,
after all, resistance; an object is what stands against us.
% TR: Danish "Genstand" (object) = "gen" (against) + "stand"; the
% etymology is Høffding's point, so the Danish word is kept.

No single observation whatever can be relied on, however. We have seen
that observation is always of a composite nature, since new sensations
immediately fuse in it with memories. Here lies the source of a
multitude of \emph{illusions}, which arise through false interpretation
of the given sensations (B 7, a). Observation is an attempt at
interpretation; but how is the correctness of the interpretation
guaranteed? Sensations may call forth incorrect memories. And besides,
the brain can, through processes in the interior of the organism, be put
into a
% --- p. 253 ---
\opage{253}state similar to that when impressions are propagated to it
from the surrounding world. Then \emph{hallucinations} arise, false
sensations and observations, which one is often unable to distinguish
from normal sense-observations. Not only sight, but the other senses too
can suffer under such abnormal states. It is remarkable, but in keeping
with what was noted above about the relation between the sensation of
resistance and the other senses, that while hallucinations of sight and
hearing do not always bring with them hallucinations of touch and
resistance, the latter, conversely, seem as a rule to bring with them
hallucinations of sight or
% footnote *) on p. 253
hearing.\footnote{\emph{Brierre de Boismont}: Des hallucinations. 3 éd.
p.~597 f.} False sensations of resistance thus give the most forceful
illusion of a reality. They therefore also act in a more harrowing and
destructive way on mental health.\footnote{Cf.\ E. \emph{Kraepelin};
Ueber Trugwahrnehmungen. (Vierteljahrsschr. für wiss. Phil. V.
p.~365).}

But the question is precisely how, in the particular case, a distinction
can be made between mental health and mental illness. When all the
sense-sensations join together, and when the hallucinating person
moreover rejects with great acuteness all the objections that can be
directed against the imagined world into which he has lived his way,
how can it then be decided who is right?

Particular sense-observations as such cannot decide the matter. Each of
them may rest on illusions or hallucinations. The only decision that is
possible is reached by looking to the connection among the different
sense-observations. The particular points at which the sensation of
resistance (to keep to it) occurs are not isolated, but prove to stand in
connection with one another. The task is then set for the individual of
ordering his ideas according to this connection. If he does not find the
right ordering, he will run up against contradictions, and in the end he
will suffer a practical disappointment or pain. If he perhaps found the
right ordering among the ideas as they presented themselves, but
nevertheless came into conflict with new experiences, then doubt is
raised about the correctness of the observations themselves. If doubt is
not raised, and the interaction
% --- p. 254 ---
\opage{254}with the surrounding world is continued, destruction is
certain. That is why the child and the insane are withdrawn from the
struggle of life: they are not able to correct their ideas according to
experience.

The real is what we perceive as real --- what, in spite of all
resistance on our part, we must in the end nevertheless let stand as it
is --- what we cannot but acknowledge. This ``cannot but'' is a negative
and subjective criterion, and there can be no question here of any
other. For the dreamer his dream is reality; if he wakes, he discovers
that the dream was only an illusory reality, which is conditioned by a
more comprehensive reality and finds its explanation within it. As far
as we can go within the dream without running up against glaring
contradictions and conflicting experiences, so far we believe in the
reality of the dream. But there comes a point where the thread breaks.
Even the most systematic dream is only a fragment in comparison with the
totality into which advancing experience leads. In this way all those of
our ideas that have no root in reality are corrected; sooner or later
their limitation will show itself, and it is discovered that there is
more in heaven and on earth than we thought of in our philosophy.

By this route the knowledge of the single individual finds its
completion in a total picture of the reality of which he is a member. But
the individual's own powers can do little here. He cannot correct his
own illusions when he stands alone. Just as little can the single people
and the single age. The total pictures which the single individuals,
peoples and ages form in turn carry on a great struggle among themselves,
and through this struggle the world-view of the race slowly approaches
reality. The psychology of the individual here leads over partly to the
psychology of peoples, partly to the history of the sciences.

Whether the goal can in itself be reached is not thereby decided. But
before we touch on the great problem that arises here, we must bring
forward an important concept to which the foregoing development has led
us, and whose psychological basis it is the place here to point out.

\textbf{3.} Reality, according to what has just been developed, shows
itself in the fixed connection among observations. Of
% --- p. 255 ---
\opage{255}particular observations, of the reality of particular things
or events, we can therefore never be so firmly convinced as of connected
series of things and events. The more ``method'' there is in the
``madness,'' the more dangerous it therefore is.

The fixed bond by which things and events are joined into the connection
of reality we call the \emph{causal relation}. The real, we may say, is
that which is effect and has effect. Without having effect it would not
exist for us, would not meet us, would not stop us on our way as a
non-ego. Without being and having effect it would not fill one definite
place in the series of things and events. That we know reality therefore
means the same as that we apply the concept of cause to our observations.

According to the popular view of the causal relation, the cause is one
thing and the effect another thing. The difficulty that might lie in the
fact that things which exist each by itself nevertheless have so much to
do with one another as cause and effect must have is easily solved from
this standpoint by ascribing to the thing one calls the cause a
producing or compelling power. One personifies causes, attributes to
them something analogous to a personal exertion of will.

\emph{David Hume} was the first to carry through a criticism of the
popular concept of cause. What does one really mean --- he asked ---
when one says that one thing is the cause of another? If the answer is
that the cause \emph{produces} the effect, what then does one understand
by ``production''? Can one give any explanation of this concept without
its becoming the same as --- causation, so that one is thus moving in a
circle? --- Let us take a thing by itself alone. Can one by any
reasoning whatever infer what effect it will have? All our distinct ideas
can be held apart from one another, and it is easy for us to imagine an
object as non-existent at this moment and existent at the next, without
needing for this any idea of a cause or a producing principle. When we
consider things each by itself, there is none of them that
\emph{necessarily} presupposes any of the others. --- Nor by the route of
% --- p. 256 ---
\opage{256}experience can we arrive at the concept of cause. We do not
discover an inner bond between things and events. We see the one
phenomenon exist together with or follow after the other, but we do not
see it \emph{follow} from the other. The producing or causing itself we
do not see. --- The fixed connection that we posit between two things
which we call cause and effect thus does not belong to the objects
themselves. Necessity exists only in consciousness, not in things. But
what then can it be that thus binds our ideas together? This is in
itself just as mysterious as the connection between the objects. The
only possible explanation is that constant experiences produce a habit,
an instinct, an inclination to pass from one idea over to other ideas
with which it is usually connected. This subjective compulsion, which we
experience in the course of our ideas, we then transfer
anthropomorphically to nature.\footnote{Treatise on human nature. Book
I. Part 3. Sect 3--14.}

Hume dissolves the popular view of the concept of cause by carrying
through its presuppositions. His criticism is built on the cause being
one thing and the effect a quite different thing. This isolation of the
particular members of the causal relation stands in close connection with
his psychological theory, which regards consciousness as a sum or series
of independent ideas (see above B, 5).

Instead of saying with Hume that we cannot see in a thing, or infer from
its concept, that it is the cause or effect of another thing, we must
conversely maintain that we know a thing at all only in so far as it is
cause or effect. Hume has not hit upon the real basis of the popular
view. Things are always given to us as members of a connection. The
popular view takes them out of this connection, in which they live and
move and have their being --- and then it certainly becomes wonderful
that they have anything to do with one another. Hume says that a thing
continues to be what it is whether or not it is a cause. But the thing
that is a cause, i.e.\ from which a change proceeds, must surely be in a
different inner state than when no change is determined by it. It
cannot bring about
% --- p. 257 ---
\opage{257}any change without itself being changed. Instead of beginning
with the idea of independent things and wondering at the causal
relation, it would be at least as justified to begin with the causal
relation and wonder at the independent things.

Hume saw clearly the connection between the theory of the concept of
cause and the psychology of knowledge. ``The uniting principle among our
internal perceptions,'' he says (I, 3, 14), ``is as unintelligible as
that among external objects.'' If Hume's psychology is corrected, the
theory of the concept of cause will also undergo a corresponding
correction. \emph{Kant} attempted both, after Hume's criticism itself had
awakened him from his dogmatic slumber. Kant's theory of psychical
synthesis as the typical characteristic mark of consciousness lies at
the basis of his theory of the causal relation.

The objects of experience form --- such is Kant's train of thought ---
the connection, fixed and universally valid for all thinking beings,
which we call nature, only through the particular sensations and
observations being ordered in a definite way. This ordering in turn
becomes possible only through the combining or synthetic activity of
consciousness. The laws of this activity therefore determine our
conception of nature. In them are given principles according to which we
seek a definite place for each observation, until there forms a
connection that is continuous and yet at the same time marked out in the
finest nuances.

Kant thus agrees with Hume that all necessity is in the end subjective.
But he does not regard it as mere habit or instinct; for him it stands as
the expression of a law of the understanding grounded in the nature of
consciousness. Of this law of the understanding we naturally become
conscious only when we reflect on the involuntary thinking we employ in
all our experience; but it proves to be closely connected with the
fundamental peculiarity of consciousness, to be a special expression of
it.

Kant's theory comes out clearly in a statement such as the following.
``That something happens is a perception which belongs to a possible
experience, which becomes actual through my regarding the phenomenon as
determined as to its place in nature, and thus regarding it as an object
which can always be found according to a rule in the connection of
perceptions. But this rule, according to which something is determined
with respect to the succession in time, is: that in what
% --- p. 258 ---
\opage{258}precedes, one can meet with the condition under which the
event always (i.e.\ necessarily) follows. The principle of sufficient
reason is therefore the ground of possible experience, namely of the
objective knowledge of phenomena with respect to their relation in the
sequence of time. --- --- If, on the other hand, I posit what precedes,
and the event does not necessarily follow upon it, then I regard it only
as a subjective play of my imaginings, and, if I nevertheless represent
it to myself as something objective, I call it a mere
% footnote *) on p. 258
dream.''\footnote{Kritik der reinen Vernunft. ed Kehrbach. p.~189--190.}

The imperfection of Kant's theory is that it often looks as if the
principle of causality were an independent, originally given form of
consciousness. From a bent for scholastic formalism he has let himself be
led into setting up a whole series of coordinate forms in which the
original synthesis of consciousness was supposed to show itself. If we
keep to a statement such as the one quoted, the theory amounts merely to
this: that the concept of cause arises through the application of the
law of sufficient reason to what is given.

In an earlier connection (B 11, b) we have seen how the law of sufficient
reason is connected with elementary thinking. Here it is only a matter
of stating the relation between ground and \emph{cause}. We ask about
grounds also when we have to do merely with our own thoughts and seek
nothing other than what is \emph{thinkable}. There is talk of cause when
the question concerns the \emph{existence} of \emph{real} phenomena. The
cause is then that in the series of phenomena which satisfies our asking
for the ground of the existence of these or those phenomena. It goes
here, then, as with all grounding: a difference, a contrast, is to be
reconciled with the rest of the content of consciousness. The
equilibrium, the connection, has been disturbed by a new experience and
is now to be restored. The concept of cause thus does not lie ready-made
in us; in the particular case we do not know whether the cause can be
found. Purely empirically, the universal validity of the law of
causality can to that extent be denied --- a sign that it cannot derive
from experience. The concept of cause designates a demand we set up; it
contains the \emph{postulate} that everything in our experiences shall
stand in mutual connection. To that extent
% --- p. 259 ---
\opage{259}Hume is right that it is an instinct that guides us; only he
did not see the origin of this instinct in the very nature of our
consciousness. Consciousness anticipates experience, sets up an ideal
which it follows in its treatment of experience. This ideal would
certainly die away if it were arbitrary and were not confirmed by
experience. But the concept of cause is not merely a postulate; it is
also a \emph{result}. It is no foreign power to which our sensations and
observations are subjected when we apply the law of causality to them.
The receptive and the active side of our nature, the predominantly
passive taking-in of experiences and the predominantly active working-up
of them, play more and more into each other's hands. It might, regarded
purely abstractly, be supposed that nature never presented quite similar
cases. The ideal satisfaction of the postulate of causality rests on the
presupposition that like conditions are followed by like consequences.
But if reality now consisted of nothing but differences, if it varied
infinitely, then the concept of cause would find no application, or
rather would never have arisen at all. It would in any case soon die of
atrophy, like organs that are not used.

Reality consists of a multitude of mutually intersecting series of
phenomena. If we want to understand a phenomenon, the task is to find the
series to which the phenomenon belongs. Were there only one single series
of phenomena, there would be no difficulty. Now there are not only many
series, but they intersect one another in manifold ways. From this it
comes that the law of causality stands as an ideal, because the
circumstances of the past never quite repeat themselves. We can learn
from the past only when the future to a certain degree resembles it. The
sanguine tendency in the human mind can lead to overestimating both the
similarities and the differences. In the first case it builds bold
inferences on few and superficial similarities. In the second case it
holds, even where the circumstances are essentially alike, that it is
after all ``another matter''; that is why it is, e.g., so difficult to
learn from history. But this does not shake the general validity of the
law of causality; on the contrary, that validity is confirmed by the
fact that that sanguine tendency can be corrected.


% --- p. 260 ---
\opage{260}A thought-experiment such as that of a reality of absolute
differences is, however, surely impossible to carry through.
\emph{Stuart Mill} held that by sufficient exercise of one's power of
abstraction one would be able to imagine a world where events followed
one another in a chance manner (\textit{at random}), without definite
law.\footnote{Logic. III, 21, 1.} At every moment, then, something
wholly new would enter which had no connection with anything preceding.
Even in our dreams and our wildest fantasies there is nevertheless a
certain connection, however unstable and arbitrary. The law of
causality, and through it the general nature of consciousness,
necessarily asserts itself. Stuart Mill's thought-experiment we can carry
through only in so far as we can imagine the dissolution of
consciousness itself. What he postulates as ``thinkable'' cannot be
distinguished from the ``\textit{Ideenjagd}'' of the insane, the last
embers before the fire of consciousness is wholly extinguished.

Just as the concept of cause is an ideal, so reality or nature itself is
something coming to be for us. There is no original harmony between our
thinking consciousness and nature. But our consciousness, in accordance
with its fundamental peculiarity, constantly seeks greater connection
--- i.e.\ seeks nature, reality; and since the history of the sciences
shows us that this striving succeeds at least in some fields, nature may
be said to come to meet our striving. But a conclusion of this process of
development is in itself unthinkable. The connection can always be
brought to encompass more phenomena, and within the connection new
nuances and transitions can always be sought out.

\textbf{4.} The complete development of the concept of cause belongs to
the philosophical theory of knowledge, not to psychology. In the
foregoing we have in part overstepped the boundary between these two
disciplines. We now return to the psychological field in order to point
out that the determination of the concept of cause which Hume and Kant
agreed in striking out --- the anthropomorphic conception of every causal
relation in nature as an activity of will analogous to the human ---
contains a hint as to
% --- p. 261 ---
\opage{261}\emph{the psychological origin of the concept of cause}. For
with regard both to \emph{motive} and to \emph{form} the concept of cause
proceeds originally from a practical interest. Only at an advanced stage
of development does man take an interest in the connection of nature
apart from whether it can serve his own purposes. It is the impulse of
self-preservation that from the first leads to knowledge of the
surrounding world: necessity teaches thinking, just as it teaches
spinning. Knowledge that cannot immediately be made useful is not
esteemed. When a missionary told an Indian about the creation, the latter
answered: ``My father, my grandfather and my great-grandfather used only
to look at the earth to see whether it gave grass and water for their
horses. They never troubled themselves about what went on in the
% footnote *) on p. 261
heavens.''\footnote{\emph{Lubbock}: Origin of civilisation. 3 ed.
p.~370.} And later on, too, knowledge and learning stand as means by
which man adapts nature to his purposes. Thus \emph{Baco}, the prophet
of modern natural science and of industrialism, says: ``Human knowledge
and power coincide, for ignorance of the cause makes the effect
impossible. For nature is conquered only by obeying her. And what in
inquiry stands as cause appears in operation as
% footnote **) on p. 261
rule.''\footnote{Novum Organon. I, 2.} The cause is thus originally the
means, a detour that man is compelled to take in order to reach his
goal. It is only in pure instinct that the dim impulse immediately gives
rise to the action. Reason (in the widest sense of the word) raises
itself above instinct in that there joins itself to the impulse an idea
of what must first take place in order that the impulse may be
satisfied. In this idea of unavoidable intermediate links lie the germs
of the concept of necessity and of the concept of cause; and when this
idea acquires a more comprehensive content and becomes an object of
independent interest, the concept of cause emancipates itself from the
concept of purpose.

In close connection with this originally practical motivation of the
formation of the concept of cause stands the original form under which
it is conceived. The concept of cause, on account of its intimate
connection with the nature of consciousness, asserts itself at all
stages of human development; but that
% --- p. 262 ---
\opage{262}in which the cause is sought may be extremely various. What
at the different stages of mental development is regarded as good and
sound causes depends on the whole standpoint one occupies. When the
Australian native sees one of his tribe die without having been struck
by a shot or other external injury, he concludes that there must be
sorcery at play, and in order to discover who by his magic power has
killed his comrade, he follows the direction in which the first insect
that comes along moves away from the place of the death; the one whom he
then meets must be the
% footnote *) on p. 262
murderer.\footnote{Fr. \emph{Müller}: Allgemeine Ethnographie. 2 Ausg.
Wien 1879. p.~214.} On his presupposition this is good, rational
thinking. That similar presuppositions in part still hold sway to this
day may be seen from the fact that millions of people in the most
civilized countries derive the so-called spiritistic phenomena from the
influence of
% footnote **) on p. 262
spirits.\footnote{Cf.\ \emph{Girard de Rialle}: La Mythologie comparée.
I. Paris 1878. Chap.~14: Le Fétichisme chez les peuples civilisés.} So
long as the gods of mythology, or similar beings, stand as realities for
consciousness, they furnish excellent and easily applicable means for
satisfying the causal impulse. This impulse is, moreover, quickly sated
at such a standpoint. When imagination has taken a couple of steps back
in the series, it needs rest and posits a conclusion. The Greeks
regarded the gods as the originators of the phenomena in nature (in any
case of all important or conspicuous phenomena). Where did the gods in
turn come from? This question \emph{Hesiod} answers in his Theogony by
describing how the dynasties of gods gradually developed out of chaos.
But where chaos came from he did not ask, although he expressly declares
it to have come into being. (``First of all chaos \emph{came into
being},'' v.~116.) --- From the mythological to the scientific
explanation of nature there is a continuous series of stages. The
anthropomorphic form is broken up gradually as more and more intermediate
links become necessary, and the more the conviction arises of the
constancy of these intermediate links and their independence of personal
caprice. The scientific concept of cause is characterized by its finding
the explanation of a natural phenomenon in tracing it back to a circle
of
% --- p. 263 ---
\opage{263}other natural phenomena. Nature is explained by itself, not
by something outside it. The medically trained observer of a sudden
death gathers information about the man's constitution, way of life,
descent, etc. He perhaps finds at the post-mortem an obstruction
(embolism) in one of the arteries, and now explains the death to himself
either as an effect of the supply of blood to the brain having been cut
off, or by the heart having stopped. In this way he forms the picture of
a connected process in which link is joined to link; the mysterious and
sudden phenomenon then stands as the natural conclusion of this process.
The law of causality is thus closely connected with the law of
continuity. The scientific causal series too must in each particular
case stop somewhere (as in the example just given, at the obstruction of
the artery), either from fatigue or because there is no way of getting
further. Thus the modern hypothesis of evolution stops at the primeval
nebula, without therefore regarding it as an absolute first. For the
conviction arises, through reflection on the nature of the concept of
cause, that the series of causes --- like the time-series and space ---
has only relative limits, and can always be thought of as continued
further. The path of thought is prolonged before our gaze with every
advance. What seemed to be the outermost proves to be a transition to
something still more remote.

\textbf{5.} But even if a conclusion of this process could be thought,
what \emph{validity} then has knowledge? This again is a question for the
theory of knowledge; but it arises naturally at the close of the
psychology of knowledge, and we will not pass it by without answering
it as well as we can.

All our sensations and ideas are subject to \emph{the law of relativity
or the law of relation}. In the case of sensations this has been shown
in particular in an earlier section (A, 6). As regards ideas, it follows
from the development we have given of the ideas of time, space and
cause. These ideas formed series in which every member is supported and
determined by the other members. Every totality is in turn enclosed by a
higher totality. In its most abstract form the law of relation shows
itself in the fact that all grounding requires a plurality of ideas, and
that thinking always moves forward through
% --- p. 264 ---
\opage{264}the choice and rejection of possibilities (B, 11).\footnote{Just
as there has been speculation about the possibility of more than three
dimensions in space, so one could also, purely formally, set up the
possibility of a thinking that moved forward through a division into
three instead of through a division into two. Instead of resting on the
principle that A must be either B or (÷ B), thinking would then function
according to the principle that A must be either B, (÷ B) or C (which is
neither B nor (÷ B)). Such a thing is as impossible for us to
\emph{think} as it is impossible for us to \emph{imagine} a fourth
dimension. --- Cf.\ \emph{Boole}: An investigation into the laws of
thought. p.~50 f.} Knowledge is conditioned by the reciprocal relation
between the similarities and differences of our ideas, or, to use
Spencer's expressions, which recall the general laws of evolution,
integrations and differentiations. Yet there is one relation which lies
deeper than all others, and which becomes of special importance for the
question of the validity of knowledge, namely the relation between
consciousness and object, \emph{subject} and \emph{object}.

The popular answer to the question of what conditions the validity of our
knowledge is: ``our knowledge is correct when it agrees with the
object.'' But how can we convince ourselves of this? ``The object'' we
know only through our sensations and ideas! --- On the basis of our
\emph{sensations} we ascribe certain properties to objects (light and
darkness, colors, sound, heat, cold, smell and taste, etc.). But these
properties do not belong to the objects themselves; they are a language
in which we express them according to the way in which they affect our
organism. Purely physically, color is only vibrations which --- perhaps
in an exceedingly fine substance, the ether --- are propagated from the
objects to us; sound is waves of air, etc. Were there no eyes and no
brain, light as we sense it would not exist. We thus do not really sense
things; but our sensations correspond to the state into which our brain
comes when effects from the objects are propagated to it. Even the
sensation of resistance does not help us beyond this; we always measure
the resistance by our own exertion of force; what it is in itself we
cannot sense. There remain, then, the relations of space themselves, in
which the objects (among which our organism itself, the brain included,
must here be reckoned) appear. But
% --- p. 265 ---
\opage{265}the relations of space we know only through the intuition of
space, and the intuition of space is a psychological activity. Whether
we follow the nativistic or the genetic theory, the intuition of space
belongs to the subjective forms in and through which objects are given to
us, and apart from which we know nothing about them. --- In
sense-observations \emph{ideas} at once play in as well; it is by virtue
of psychological laws that we form the idea of the particular object as a
whole. Our knowledge is and remains, however logically we proceed
according to the law of sufficient reason, a play of ideas. And just as
impossible as it is to perceive anything about objects except through
sensations, just so impossible is it to think anything about objects
except by means of ideas and concepts. In order for that popular
criterion of the validity of our knowledge to be usable, we would have
to be able to go behind our consciousness and compare the object with
the image or the concept of it that we have in our consciousness; but
this is impossible, because it is self-contradictory.

In everyday life we do not stop at this difficulty, because we are
constantly occupied with letting our ideas and sensations mutually
control one another. When we say that we correct our concepts by
comparison with ``reality,'' ``reality'' does not mean anything
independent of consciousness, but more definite and more comprehensive
observations than those we had hitherto had. But now we take the sum of
all actual and possible sensations and ideas and ask how they are related
to reality. Here we stand at the limit of knowledge. We cannot positively
refute \emph{Fichte} and \emph{Stuart Mill} when they teach that the
non-ego, the sum of all barriers to our action and all objects of our
knowledge, is the product of a mental activity unconscious to ourselves.
We cannot, then, if we want to push the matter to its extreme, know
whether life is not a dream, a great, coherent and consistent dream.

It does not help to exclaim that such a doctrine is bottomless
skepticism. If one will not, with \emph{materialism}, hold fast with
blind dogmatic defiance to the absolute validity of the content of
sensations, although this content exists only in and for consciousness,
then one must, with \emph{speculative}
% --- p. 266 ---
\opage{266}\emph{idealism}, teach that appearance presupposes essence,
and that behind the world of phenomena there rises an ideal world, which
is the true reality. But however high one may wish to climb in the
airy realm of speculation, the same fundamental relation between subject
and object will come into force. \emph{Kant} posited an ideal world
(\textit{noumena} in contrast to \textit{phaenomena}) as the true
reality. But this ideal world itself exists only for a consciousness.
Therefore we cannot even form any concept of an absolute knowledge, still
less possess one. For an absolute knowledge the subject and object of
knowledge would have to coincide; it would have to be a knowledge that
itself produced its objects, and that knew no barrier, no resistance.
But to it our logical and psychological laws, and thus our concept of
knowledge, would not apply; and in spite of the many speculative and
theosophical attempts, it has not yet been possible to point out and
carry through other (``higher'') psychological and logical laws.

But just as materialism and speculative idealism sin against the law of
relation, and in an illusory leap go beyond the limits of knowledge by
positing an absolute reality, so subjective \emph{idealism} is wrong in
assuming an absolute dream. Dream too is a concept of relation, which
falls away when it does not have a reality as its contrast. Fichte's pure
ego is just as dogmatic as the pure being of the dogmatists. What we call
the reality of waking life is a greater connection than the reality of
the dream, and if it is itself a dream, it must be so in relation to a
still more comprehensive reality.

When it proves impossible to apply the popular definition of truth as the
agreement of knowledge with the object, since the object itself
presupposes our knowledge, we must seek the criterion of truth
\emph{within} the world of consciousness and not \emph{outside} it. And
it can then be nothing other than the inner harmony and consistency among
all thoughts and experiences (cf.\ 2). If we cannot get outside the
dream, we can still, as \emph{Calderon} demands in \textit{Life Is a
Dream}, ``live rightly in the dream''; that is to say, here, we can
constantly extend the realm of our experiences and thoughts and found a
deeper
% --- p. 267 ---
\opage{267}and firmer harmony among them. The law of relativity thus by
no means deprives our concepts of their validity. That ``everything is
relative'' does not mean that it is all equally true, but that all truth
holds in quite definite relations --- that all truth is a harmony. If
there is a higher truth than that which human knowledge can reach, then
all the truth we can know is a part of it. By using the means and the
scale that are given us in the nature and organization of our mind, we
can therefore really advance in the knowledge of objective truth.


%% ============================================================
%%  VI. THE PSYCHOLOGY OF FEELING (pp. 268--364)
%% ============================================================
% --- p. 268 ---
\chapter[The Psychology of Feeling]{The Psychology of Feeling\opage{268}}
\label{ch:feeling}

\section*{A. Sense-Sensation and Feeling}\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{A. Sense-Sensation and Feeling}

\noindent\textbf{1.} When we set feeling in contrast to knowledge, we do
not thereby mean, as was already explained earlier (IV), to assert any
contrast between different faculties or powers in the soul. The
psychological distinctions concern only the elements of which psychical
states prove, on closer consideration, to be composed, and it has been
shown earlier with what right we distinguish between elements of feeling
and elements of knowledge in every psychical state. It proved impossible
to derive all other forms of conscious life from a pure state of
feeling; even if the elements of feeling were altogether predominant at
the primitive stages, closer consideration nevertheless established the
presence of elements of knowledge. The task now is to show by what laws
and along what paths the higher forms of the life of feeling are
developed out of the primitive feelings attached to the immediate
sense-sensations.

Some have wished to deny altogether that any such development takes
place. Just as within the domain of knowledge there has been an
inclination to maintain a sharp contrast between sense-perception and
thinking, so it has been regarded as a degradation of the higher, ideal
feelings to be akin to the primitive feelings. Here an ethical valuation
is unjustifiably allowed to determine the psychological view. As an
example of this tendency we may refer to the work, able in other
respects, by \emph{Nahlowsky}: »Das Gefühlsleben« (1862). This
psychologist,
% --- p. 269 ---
\opage{269}who belongs to the Herbartian school, distinguishes between
the way in which the sense-sensations determine our general organic
condition and the way in which inner movements and stirrings in our
circle of ideas affect us. Only the latter kind of affection will he
call feeling. The sense-sensation has, to be sure, its peculiar
``tone,'' but this concerns only the bodily state, not the soul. One
sees here how a spiritualistic theory of the relation between soul and
body can interfere in a special psychological question. According to
Nahlowsky, physical pain is a sensation transmitted from the body to the
soul, while psychical pain is a real feeling, an expression of the
soul's own inner state during the interaction of the ideas with one
another. Sensations are therefore explained by the relation between soul
and body, feelings by the relation of the ideas to one another.

From the standpoint we have taken up, it is impossible to draw a
definite boundary between physical and psychical pain. All pain is
psychical, since psychical life is known to us only as conscious life.
The psychological difference can consist only in this, that so-called
physical pain, i.e.\ that which is due to immediate sense-sensations,
is less composite, contains fewer elements, than so-called psychical
pain. Toothache is a single, primitive feeling, while sorrow and remorse
are feelings that presuppose ideas and memories. On the other hand, the
higher feelings, just as well as the lower, have their corresponding
physiological process. The difference can consist only in this, that
the central processes, those taking place within the brain, play a
greater part in the higher feelings than in the lower, which are
determined predominantly by the single impression.

It thereby becomes possible that the ``tone'' of the sense-sensation, or
the way in which it immediately attunes us, may psychologically be a
germ out of which higher feelings develop. Before we go further into
this development, we shall consider somewhat more closely the relation
between the sense-sensations and the feelings of pleasure and
displeasure, or pain, connected with them.

\textbf{2.} Feeling stands out clearly as an element distinct from the
sense-sensation proper in certain experiences which establish that the
pain of an impression needs a longer time for
% --- p. 270 ---
\opage{270}its arising than the sensation itself, and that sensation can
arise without corresponding feeling and vice versa.

According to \emph{Beau}, when one strikes a corn with a stick, one to
two seconds pass between the sensation of touch and the feeling of pain.
E. H. \emph{Weber} found that when one dips the hand into very cold or
very hot water, one first has a very lively sensation; this then
decreases, but only to increase again at once and become pain. He sees
an example of the same thing in the fact that when one starts at a
sudden sound (e.g.\ at the sudden blare of kettledrums and trumpets
after a pause in the music), a measurable time passes between the
impression and the startled movement; and since the propagation of the
impression through the sensory and motor nerves takes no noticeable
time, he explains the phenomenon by saying that the activity of the
brain is a condition of the arising of feeling. The same slowness in the
arising of the feeling of pain in the sensations has been established
for electrical stimulation and for pinching the skin with a pair of
tweezers, as well as under pathological
% footnote *) on p. 270
conditions.\footnote{E. H. \emph{Weber} in Wagner's Physiol.
Handwörterbuch. III, 2. p.~565--571. --- \emph{Richet}: Recherches
expérimentales et cliniques sur la sensibilité. Paris 1877. p.~290--293.
--- \emph{Funke}: Tastsinn und Gemeingefühle. (Hermann's Handbuch. III,
2. p.~298--300).}

In a discussion of the relation between feeling and knowledge carried
on between \emph{Horwicz} and \emph{Wundt} in the »Vierteljahrsschrift
für wissenschaftliche Philosophie« (3rd and 4th volumes), the
first-named investigator appealed to sudden and violent blows or knocks
as examples in which the feeling of pain arose before the sensation.
With very strong impressions this may perhaps be so; but with
impressions of moderate strength one can easily observe that Beau and
Weber are right. I experienced this very clearly once when, with my
hands behind my back, I stepped a couple of paces backward, so that I
touched a hot stove which I had not believed so near; I then noticed
quite definitely the sensation of touch before the feeling of pain.

Pain must both spread and have a certain duration in order to be felt.
\emph{Richet} even says that without memory and
% --- p. 271 ---
\opage{271}without radiation pain would not be pain. (See above,
p.~114.) It is therefore not so simple in its nature as the
sense-sensation; it presupposes the overcoming of a certain resistance
in the central nervous organs.
