In certain cases the feeling of pain is abolished while the
sense-sensation is unimpaired. After section of the gray substance of
the spinal cord, the part of the body lying behind the cut can be
maltreated in every way, cut, crushed, burned, without any sign of pain
being shown. Anaesthetics, cold, intoxication and hypnotic sleep have
the same influence. A chloroformed patient who had a leg amputated did
indeed feel the operation, but it seemed to him as though it were being
performed on a wooden leg. After the battle of Eylau the operations were
almost painless, because there were 10° of frost. Hypnotized persons
feel no pain when their teeth are drawn. Just as there can thus be
analgesia (freedom from pain) without anaesthesia (or apselaphesia, lack
of the sensation of touch), so there can also be apselaphesia without
analgesia (\textit{anaesthesia dolorosa}). On section of the posterior
columns of the spinal cord the sensations of touch in the hind part of
the body disappear, while
% footnote *) on p. 271
the feeling of pain persists.\footnote{C. \emph{Lange}: Rygmarvens
Patologi. p.~11. 92 f. 111. --- \emph{Richet}. p.~118 f. 258 ff. ---
\emph{Preyer}: Die Entdeekung des Hypnotismus. p.~44.}
% TR: "Entdeekung" (for Entdeckung) left as printed.

The feeling of pain can be of different character. It can run through a
whole scale, from mere tingling or ``falling asleep,'' pricking,
formication (\textit{fourmillement}), and prickling, to pain proper. The
pains themselves differ in their immediate appearance. The feeling of
fatigue is different from the feeling of displeasure in faintness and
dizziness. There are burning, cutting, pressing, gripping and boring
pains. In the opinion of some, these differences are not to be regarded
as differences in kind, but rest on the different strength, extent and
duration of the pain. Here too the more central character of pain in
relation to the sense-sensation comes out. It is only at its peaks that
pain appears as altogether simple, while the sense-sensation from the
first appears predominantly with the stamp of simplicity (but cf.\ on
this V. A, 2). But those
% --- p. 272 ---
\opage{272}differences in the immediate appearance of pain contain at the
same time a new proof that in every state of feeling there are also
elements of knowledge. The difference between cutting and boring pain is
just as much of an intellectual character as the difference between the
sensation of yellow and the sensation of blue.

As regards the feeling of pleasure, it was already maintained by
\emph{Aristippus} that it presents no differences in kind. Pleasure was
not different from pleasure; only the circumstances, the causes, were
different. But Aristippus pushed this so far as to make the feeling of
pleasure something altogether simple, setting up the purely momentary
pleasure, unmixed with all remembering and looking forward, as the end
of life. Such altogether simple and instantaneous feelings do not exist.
Aristippus's moral principle would presuppose a dissolution of
consciousness into flashes; it would then be a matter of possessing such
virtuosity that one could move from flash to flash, and at each moment
be wholly concentrated in the single flash. But as soon as Aristippus
maintains that insight and practice are necessary for attaining such
virtuosity, he admits that the one moment has something to do with the
other. And even if the general character of the feeling of pleasure is
the same through all its forms, it nevertheless acquires in each
particular case certain definite nuances in consequence of the way in
which it is called forth. Thus the feeling of pleasure in muscular
movement has other elements in it than the feeling of pleasure in taste
or smell. It is on this composite, nuanced character of even the
simplest feelings of pleasure that the possibility rests of pleasure
being developed and transformed through connection with elements of idea
and thought. Aristippus therefore also consistently ranked physical
pleasure highest, because it comes nearest to being altogether momentary
and simple.

\textbf{3.} In so-called physical pleasure and pain, then, there are
already elements of knowledge besides the elements of feeling proper,
although the latter decidedly predominate. We shall now briefly consider
the relation between these two kinds of elements in the domains of the
different senses. It will appear that the domains of sense can be
arranged in a series, such that at one end the elements of feeling have
a decided preponderance over
% --- p. 273 ---
\opage{273}the elements of knowledge, while at the other end a more even
development of both kinds appears.

\textbf{a.} Characteristic of the general sensation is the lack of
definite and local characterization of the particular sensations. These
vanish in a general feeling of well-being or indisposition, which
constitutes, as it were, the result at which the brain arrives from the
reports it receives from the various parts of the organism. We have here
a feeling of our existence as a whole, of the general course of the
life-process; this feeling, attached to the general sensations, we shall
call the \emph{feeling of life}. The quality and quantity of the blood,
the liveliness of the circulation, the tension of the vessels (tonus),
the more abundant or more sparing secretions of the glands, the
slackness or tautness of the muscles (both the involuntary and the
voluntary), the brisk or labored course of respiration, the normal or
abnormal course of the digestive process --- all this helps to
determine it, without any of the factors mentioned needing to stand out
by itself. The general sensations form a chaos, which receives its
stamp from the contrast between well-being and ill-being, and whose
special nuances are, in the nature of the case, determined by one organ
or another playing a particularly predominant part, without there always
arising on that account an express consciousness of it as the source of
the sensation. On the contrary, it is characteristic of the general
sensations that they often ``radiate'' (irradiate) or are projected to
places quite other than those where the cause really lies. In this
little-articulated state of conscious life, the state of the organ
predominant at the given time becomes determinative for the general
fundamental mood.

This fundamental mood can be characterized only by certain general
features, which stand in close connection with the easy and free, or
hindered and heavy, course of the organic life-process. Thus the feeling
of freedom, security and strength stands in contrast to the feeling of
inner resistance, unrest, anxiety and faintness. In the contrast between
the \emph{feeling of strength} and the \emph{feeling of faintness}, the
muscular sensation plainly plays an important part. Even if we do not
consciously stretch our muscles, they are nevertheless always in a
certain degree of tension; resting muscles (in sitting, lying, etc.) are
not wholly slack, but partly contracted; the muscles of mastication
always involuntarily draw
% --- p. 274 ---
\opage{274}the lower jaw up against the upper; the \emph{upper} eyelid is
always held up, etc. During sleep this ``latent innervation,'' as it has
been called, is diminished; the posture of the body then conforms more
to the laws of gravity; --- and yet there is still a difference between
the posture of the dead and
% footnote *) on p. 274
that of the sleeping.\footnote{C. \emph{Lange}: Rygmarvens Patologi.
p.~344 ff.} The feeling of lightness and of \emph{freedom} is connected
especially with the course of respiration and of the nutritive
functions. Hindered respiration produces a feeling of painful unrest and
anxiety. If the first cry of the child is called forth by the hunger for
air that sets in as a consequence of the interruption of the placental
circulation, life begins with anxiety. A physician from the West Indies
who consulted Charles Bell reported that ``when he fell asleep, just as
will and consciousness ceased, the involuntary movements also stopped,
with a sensation of death, during which he as a rule woke up in
convulsions. His medical friends have sat up and watched over him, and
they found that when sleep took hold of him, the respiration became
slower and weaker. The heart and pulse also slacken and cease to beat
when sleep comes over him, and a moment later he wakes up in terror.''
Incubus or nightmare seems (according to \emph{Laycock}) to be caused by
the
% footnote **) on p. 274
sleep of the respiratory centers.\footnote{\emph{Laycock}: On the reflex
function of the brain. (British and Foreign Medical Review. 1845. 19er
Bind). p.~306.} Many abdominal diseases bring with them the same
feeling. It seems to the patient ``as though nature had stopped its
activity in him.''\footnote{\emph{Richet}: Recherches sur la
sensibilité. p.~312.} With nervous pains in the pit of the stomach
(cardialgia) there may be connected --- perhaps on account of
disturbances of the circulation --- a terrible feeling of anxiety and
powerlessness, which makes these pains some of the very
% footnote ****) on p. 274
most dreadful sufferings.\footnote{C. \emph{Lange} p.~152 ff.}

In their first stages the feelings of \emph{hunger} or of \emph{thirst}
also have the vague character of the general feeling, expressing
themselves as an indisposition, an unrest. With this, however, definite
local sensations soon connect themselves: in the case of hunger, of
pressure and gnawing in the stomach; in the case of thirst, of dryness
and burning in the tongue and throat. --- We must here disregard the way
in which these feelings appear when definite
% --- p. 275 ---
\opage{275}ideas of their significance attach themselves to them, and
when the habit has been formed of taking nourishment at fixed
% footnote *) on p. 275
times.\footnote{This ``psychical factor'' (\emph{Ranke}: Physiol. des
Menschen. 3 Aufl. p.~220) no doubt also plays a part in the sensation of
cold, which is why the adult suffers more from cold than the child, in
whom no further ideas are yet connected with it (\emph{Perez}: Les trois
premières années de l'enfant. Paris 1878. p.~8 f).}

\textbf{b.} The sensations of touch and of \emph{movement} stand so near
to the general sensations that they are often absorbed into these
without standing out independently, and, as with the general
sensations, the strength of the impression plays a greater part than its
quality. But when the strength does not exceed a certain degree, these
sensations are nevertheless fine enough to be connected with feelings of
pleasure or displeasure which have a certain independence in relation to
the general feeling of organic well-being or ill-being. In active
movement a peculiar sense of comfort can be felt, and one kind or form
of activity can be preferred to another, just as one color is preferred
to another. Likewise there is a comfort in touching soft and smooth
surfaces and a displeasure in touching rough and hard ones, which may
already be said to have a certain aesthetic character.

\textbf{c.} \emph{Taste} still stands near the general sensation. It is
closely bound up with the function of nutrition, as a kind of gauger and
rejecter of what is to be taken up and worked over by that function. The
feelings of comfort or of disgust which according to some are due to
the part of the organs of taste lying on the last third of the tongue
have quite the same stamp as the general feeling of life determined by
the general sensation. But alongside this, the differences of quality
acquire a definite significance here. Even newborn children seem able
to distinguish between the different qualities of taste. To each of
these qualities (sweet, bitter, sour and salt) certain nuances of
feeling attach. In their simplicity these are indescribable; but that
they are present is seen from the fact that expressions from the domain
of taste are used to designate higher states of feeling. Only for the
salt taste there seems to be no analogy in the domain of the
% --- p. 276 ---
\opage{276}higher life of feeling; on the other hand we have sweet
memories, bitter experiences and sour looks (biting into the sour
apple).
% TR: "bide i det sure Æble", Danish idiom for having to do something
% disagreeable.

\textbf{d.} The \emph{sensations of smell} also present qualitative
differences, but these have not attracted so much attention that
language has formed separate words for them. In animals smell plays a
predominant part among the senses; by it the prey is tracked, danger is
avoided, and the sexes are brought together. This deeply penetrating
significance smell has not quite lost in man. It is to the process of
respiration what taste is to nutrition, and it also works together with
the sensation of taste as the guardian of the alimentary canal. Quite
apart from associations of ideas and idiosyncrasies, the sensations of
smell moreover have a great, often violent, influence on the mood. But
since this sense nevertheless stands almost as a relic in man, at any
rate in civilized man, it is easily loosened from its close connection
with the feeling of life and acquires its own scale of feeling.

\textbf{e.} The higher senses, \emph{sight and hearing}, seem almost
wholly emancipated from the immediate connection with the feeling of
life. And yet they too are originally only outposts for it. Smell and
taste make it possible to carry out a preliminary examination, so that
nothing shall get into the alimentary canal that could be harmful to the
continuance of life; smell and the sense of touch give warning that the
enemy or the prey is approaching. In a similar way sight and hearing
stand from the first in the service of
% footnote *) on p. 276
instinct.\footnote{Through this connection with instinct, sight and
hearing, as well as touch, are able to call forth certain elementary
stirrings of what, under the influence of definite ideas, becomes hope
and fear. See below (B, 2 d).} Just as the sensation of taste is
followed by an impulse to swallow, so the sight of a grain or an insect
immediately awakens in the newly hatched chick the impulse to peck it
up, or the sound of the hen's cluck makes it dart off toward the source
of the sound. One can eat and enjoy with the eyes.

What places the higher senses more freely over against the feeling of
life is, in the first place, their definitely marked scale of qualities.
As long as differences of strength play the chief part,
% --- p. 277 ---
\opage{277}the sensations intervene more directly in the feeling of life
itself. This is seen especially at the peaks of pleasure and pain, even
where purely intellectual and aesthetic feelings are in question. The
special forms of tone and nuances of color awaken a finer play of
feelings than impressions which by their strength affect the very
processes to which the continuance of life is bound. Next, it is also
of importance that impressions of light and sound on the whole do not
belong to the stronger influences on our body, and that there are means
in the sense-organs for damping impressions that are too violent.

In the developed consciousness so many secondary ideas attach themselves
to colors and tones that it becomes difficult to discover what effect on
feeling the elementary sensations produce in and for themselves.
Practically, we use colors and tones as means of orienting ourselves; we
do not think so much of them themselves as of what they signify. Their
immediate effect is as a rule unconscious to us, and only when the mood
they awaken comes into a certain contrast with other moods do we become
% footnote *) on p. 277
aware of it.\footnote{»Von einem geistreichen Franzosen wird erzählt: Il
prétendoit que son ton de conversation avec Madame étoit changé depuis
qu'elle avoit changé en cramoisi le meuble de son cabinet qui étoit
bleu«. \emph{Goethe}: Farbenlehre § 762.} In order to feel such effects
fully in their peculiarity, \emph{Goethe} used to look through colored
glass and so make himself one with the color, see the whole world green,
yellow, etc. His remarks on the feeling-tones of colors are still
classic. H. C. \emph{Ørsted}, in his fine treatise on this subject,
follows Goethe for the most part.\footnote{To Capitler af det Skjønnes
Naturlære [Two Chapters of the Natural Theory of the Beautiful]. Kbhvn.
1845. --- This point is also interestingly treated by \emph{Nahlowsky}
and \emph{Wundt}.}

The connection with the feeling of life itself is seen most clearly when
we keep to the differences of strength. A lively sense of nature has, as
Ørsted remarks, always connected light and life, darkness and death,
with one another. In the teaching of Zoroaster this connection was even
made the basis of the whole view of the world. Light is a condition of
life for all organic life. Quite apart from all associations of ideas,
the contrast of feeling attached to the contrast between light and
darkness is therefore
% --- p. 278 ---
\opage{278}easy to understand. But the comfort in light and the
displeasure in darkness are not merely part of the general feeling of
life; light is the condition of the functioning of the organ of sight,
and the joy in light is the expression of this function unfolding in a
healthy and normal way, just as the displeasure in darkness is the
expression of a hindered impulse to activity.

The eye needs \emph{colors} just as much as light. ``Let one recall,''
says \emph{Goethe}, ``the refreshing feeling that arises when on a dark
day the sun shines on a single part of the landscape and the colors
become visible out there. That healing power was ascribed to colored
precious stones may perhaps have arisen from a deep feeling of this
inexpressible comfort.'' --- With regard to their effects on feeling,
Goethe divides the colors into two series: the positive (the plus side)
and the negative (the minus side). The positive series, which consists
of yellow, orange and vermilion, has a rousing, lively and striving
effect; the negative series, which consists of blue, violet and purple,
calls forth a restless, soft and yearning mood. Yellow and blue in
particular appear as opposites to each other. \emph{Yellow} stands
nearest to white. In its highest purity it has the stamp of clearness,
and has a cheering and warming effect. This is experienced especially
when one looks at a landscape through yellow glass on a dark winter day.
``The eye rejoices, the heart expands, the mind is cheered; an immediate
warmth seems to stream toward us.'' \emph{Blue} has the stamp of
darkness. It gives a feeling of cold, recalls black, and shows us
objects in a mournful light. --- The two series meet on the one side in
red, on \emph{the other side} in green. In \emph{red} Goethe sees the
highest of all color phenomena. It gives an impression both of
seriousness and dignity and of beauty and grace, the former in its
darker, the latter in its lighter nuances. A well-lit landscape seen
through a purple-red glass appears in a terrible light: ``such must be
the tone of color spread over heaven and earth on the Day of
Judgment.'' \emph{Green} stands midway between yellow and blue, while
red unites both effects. The impression of green is rest and
simplicity. »Man will nicht weiter und kann nicht weiter.«

These elementary moods are of course altered by different lighting.
Diminished intensity of light gives greater \emph{seriousness} and damps
the energy of the positive series. Likewise it follows
% --- p. 279 ---
\opage{279}of itself that the elementary moods can enter into
combinations and modifications when the colors are combined in different
ways. Here we have wished only to use Goethe's account in order to
characterize these simple nuances of feeling.

Corresponding to the contrast between light and darkness, the contrast
between \emph{sound} and \emph{silence} is at work in the domain of
hearing. There is a natural pleasure in all sound merely because it sets
our organs of hearing in activity. The ear-splitting music of children
and savages satisfies only this need for vigorous functioning of these
organs. --- Corresponding to the positive and negative series of
color-nuances, the contrast between high tones and deep tones has been
set up. The former have a cheering and rousing effect, the latter a
depressing effect, or they call forth seriousness and longing. The
timbre of the instruments has then been arranged according to the same
relation of contrast. Here too, then, it is seriousness and elation,
energy and rest, that mark the chief nuances of the elementary
feelings.\footnote{Cf.\ \emph{Nahlowsky}: Das Gefühlsleben. p.~142 ff.
--- \emph{Wundt}. I. p.~471 ff. --- As \emph{Panum} has remarked, the
physical and the physiological approach here lead to different results.
If one takes account of wavelength and frequency of vibration, red will
correspond to a deep tone, violet to a high one; according to the
physiological impression, on the other hand, red corresponds to a high,
violet to a deep tone. (Sanserne og de vilkaarlige Bevægelser [The
Senses and the Voluntary Movements]. p.~198 ff.).}

That there can be no complete contrast between the feelings attached to
the differences of quality and those attached to the differences of
strength is seen already from the fact that all differences of strength,
psychologically regarded, appear as qualitative differences (see V A,
5). And if Goethe's account is correct, the great fundamental contrast
in feeling, motivated by light and darkness, is a frame into which the
feeling-effects of the particular colors fit. That feeling thereby
undergoes alterations follows of itself. But this alteration may well be
connected with purely quantitative relations. For darkness to awaken
real displeasure, it must appear overwhelming and dominating. As a
subordinate factor in a landscape it has the effect of setting off and
enlivening, and it is then a question whether one is right in saying
that an element of displeasure is present in the impression. What
caused the displeasure was, after all,
% --- p. 280 ---
\opage{280}the hindrance of the activity of seeing; but now this activity
is precisely furthered by the dark shadow. The higher senses are
distinguished precisely by being able in this way to apprehend
combinations in which what, in and for itself and alone, would awaken
dislike can serve to heighten liking. Something of this is already
present in the lower senses, since, e.g., small quantities of bitter
and sour substances heighten the agreeableness of a taste. But only the
higher senses command such a wealth of combinations that new and
peculiar nuances of feeling, partly independent of the fluctuations of
the feeling of life, can develop.

What holds for the quality of sensations holds also for their
\emph{form}. By the very way in which the particular tones and colors are
combined, liking and dislike can be awakened. Here belongs the feeling
of pleasure in symmetry, in definite relations of form (the golden
section), in rhythm and harmony. These feelings too detach themselves
more or less from the general feeling of life.

\textbf{4.} The feelings attached to immediate sense-sensations thus
form a graded series from the feeling of life to the finely wrought
nuances of feeling in the qualitative sensations of the higher senses.
This graded series marks the natural course of development of the
elementary feelings. Before the definite appearance of special organs
and functions, in the individual and in the race, feeling can only be a
chaotic mass, a summary expression of the course of life. But it is
necessary for the very continuance of life that the feeling of life be
differentiated, take on special forms. In order to maintain its own
existence, the individual must be able to feel the significance of the
existence of other things. The special development of the sense-organs
makes this possible, but it becomes actual only through a simultaneous
development of higher, intellectual functions.

As regards the general relation between feeling and sense-sensation, we
can now set up the following result. In respect of strength they stand
in inverse relation to each other, such that the stronger the element of
feeling becomes, the more the properly sensing or cognizing element
vanishes. The sense-sensations that awaken the strongest pleasure and
displeasure teach us least about relations outside us, however great
their practical significance may otherwise be as warning or
% --- p. 281 ---
\opage{281}enticing. But where the qualitative character of the sensation
can assert itself at a strength suited to the sense-organ, there feeling
is formed and specified together with the sensation. What it loses in
violence it gains in richness and manifold nuance, as well as in
independence of the immediate struggle to exist.

The same sum of energy that in the feeling of life is concentrated on
the one question of being or not being, on organic weal and woe, is
distributed in the qualitative feelings and led out into several
different streams.

\section*{B. The Development of Feeling through Knowledge}\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{B. The Development of Feeling through Knowledge}

\noindent\textbf{1.} The feelings attached to immediate sensations
acquire a peculiar character through the qualitative character of the
sensations. The mere fluctuation between pleasure and displeasure is
developed into a series of states of feeling, each of which receives its
individual stamp by being attached to a definite content of sensation.
Already here, then, one may speak of the development of feeling through
knowledge; for the sense-sensations, in so far as we can distinguish
them from the feelings of pleasure and displeasure, belong to the domain
of knowledge. But we come to the most decisive relation between
knowledge and feeling only when we examine the influence of ideas on the
feelings. As we have seen earlier, the pure sensation is only an
abstraction; there will always be connected with the impression of the
moment more or fewer, stronger or weaker residues or memories of earlier
sensations. The point at which the circle of ideas and the associations
of ideas gain influence on feeling must therefore lie at the beginning
of conscious life, even if this influence stands out more clearly only
later.

Since we start from the assumption that feelings of pleasure and
displeasure are present in the most primitive psychical states and are
given prior to any \emph{definite} and distinct sense-sensation, we
cannot use the common definition of feeling as the effect of sensations
and ideas on consciousness. As a primitive element of consciousness,
feeling is already given before sensation and ideation can exert any
influence; this influence only develops and modifies what
% --- p. 282 ---
\opage{282}is already given beforehand. If we have nevertheless (IV, 7 c)
declared ourselves against the assumption that all conscious life has
developed out of pure states of feeling, this was partly because it is
not probable that feelings occur altogether without sense-sensations (at
any rate general sensations and sensations of movement), partly because
an intellectual element is already given in the differences of strength
of feeling and in the fluctuation between pleasure and displeasure. It
is thus only by abstraction that we speak of mere feeling without any
elements of knowledge. Yet it is justified theoretically to take such an
abstraction as a basis, since it facilitates insight into the laws that
hold by presenting the relation in a still simpler form than actual
experience can.

\textbf{2.} \textbf{a.} When the feeling of \emph{displeasure} or of
\emph{pain} is connected with the idea of its cause, it passes over into
\emph{aversion} or \emph{anger}. Before such an idea can arise, the
feeling as yet has no content, no definite direction. The violent
movement that pain causes in the newborn child is a general discharge,
but is not directed against any definite object.

``It was'' --- says \emph{Darwin} (Biographical Sketch of an Infant.
Mind 1877. p.~287 f.) --- ``difficult to decide how early anger was
felt. When the boy was eight days old, he wrinkled the skin around his
eyes before he screamed; but this may derive from pain or distress, not
from anger. At about ten weeks old he was once given rather cold milk,
and a slight frown came on his forehead while he drank, so that he
looked like a grown person who is vexed at having to do something he
does not like. But when he was four months old, one could see clearly
that he fell into a great passion. The blood rushed strongly to his face
and scalp. A small cause was sufficient. Thus, when he was seven months
old, he screamed with rage because a lemon slipped away from him. At
seven months old, when he had been given a wrong plaything, he pushed it
away and struck at it. I believe that the blow was an instinctive sign
of anger, just as a young crocodile, just crept out of the egg, snaps
its jaws; but I do not believe that he thought he was doing the plaything
harm. At two years and three months
% --- p. 283 ---
\opage{283}old he was very skillful at throwing books, sticks, etc.\ at
anyone who offended him.'' --- A child of three months which had been
given bitter medicine from a blue cup afterwards always turned its head
away when it saw this cup, made a wry face and cried.
% TR: Høffding gives Darwin in his own Danish rendering; translated here
% from the Danish, not restored to Darwin's English.

The presupposition of aversion and anger, then, is that a fixed
connection forms in consciousness between the feeling of displeasure and
a certain observation or idea. The content of this idea will in the
nature of the case be something that plays an essential part in the
arising of the feeling of displeasure, even if it is not the real cause
(as the blue cup, e.g., was not). This feeling can arise both toward
lifeless things and toward personal beings; everything that hinders and
pains is its object. With further development, and on the presupposition
of an ability to enter into the feeling of another individual, anger
becomes pleasure at the personal cause of the pain coming to feel pain
itself (what \emph{Bain} has aptly called \textit{malevolent sympathy}).
There arise \emph{desire for revenge} and hatred, while mere aversion
and anger in themselves lead only to removing ourselves from the object
or removing it from us.

\textbf{b.} By a similar metamorphosis the feeling of pleasure becomes
\emph{joy} and \emph{love}. The idea of that which stands in essential
connection with the feeling of pleasure fuses with it and determines it
in a certain direction. There arises an involuntary need to hold fast
and protect what awakens pleasure. Joy is this need seen from the
intellectual side; it denotes pleasure in dwelling on the object; love
denotes the active side, the impulse to an action that can secure the
object or at any rate secure it to us. At higher stages of development
there arises \emph{the sympathy of love}, pleasure in the pleasure of
others.

\textbf{c.} From this account it is seen that aversion and joy, anger
and love, cannot be separated from impulse or \emph{desire}. Every
pleasure or displeasure sets the organism more or less in motion. The
form and direction of this movement are determined by the original
structure of the organism. While it is often an ineffectual, if not
directly harmful, process of discharge for the energy roused to
activity, in other cases (in the so-called instinctive actions) it is
a
% --- p. 284 ---
\opage{284}purposive approach to or turning away from the object. An
impulse arises when this involuntary initiation of a movement asserts
itself in consciousness with a certain idea of the goal to which it
leads. If the movement proceeds easily and immediately, no impulse
arises; then the whole takes place below the threshold of consciousness
as a simple reflex movement. The movement must meet a certain
resistance, which, however, need not be so strong as to call forth
downright displeasure. There is a certain unrest (what \emph{Locke}
called \textit{uneasiness}) in all impulse; but this lies simply in the
fact that impulse goes beyond the present and resting state, in that it
either holds fast the cause of pleasure or removes that of displeasure.
The stronger the resistance becomes, the more the unrest passes over
into displeasure, in its simplest form displeasure at hindered movement.
With this there soon follows displeasure at the object of pleasure not
being able to be held fast, or at the means of removing the cause of
displeasure not being procurable. In this way impulse becomes more and
more composed of feelings of pleasure and displeasure, and thereby
gradually moves more definitely away both from the simple reflex
movements and from the instinctive movements called forth by immediate
sense-sensations. Impulse then acquires a content of ideas, is connected
with the thought of what hinders or furthers its purpose. --- If one
wishes to distinguish between impulse and desire, it will be most
natural to regard desire as impulse guided by distinct ideas. --- If
satisfaction is delayed very long or fails altogether, impulse, if it
has a deep ground in the nature of the individual, passes over into
strong pain.

\textbf{d.} Impulse is originally sanguine expectation. A rising idea is
at first not distinguished from a real observation; they have, to be
sure, as a rule different strength, but of the significance of this
difference there is no innate knowledge; only experience, and that here
means disappointment, impresses the difference between the possible and
the actual. (Cf.\ V B, 5.) Now when the idea of disappointment asserts
itself with greater or lesser strength alongside the idea of
satisfaction, so that the mind dwells now on the one, now on the other,
there arise \emph{hope} or \emph{fear}. Let α be a feeling of
displeasure, a the idea of that which can remedy it; or α a feeling of
pleasure and a the idea of what can
% --- p. 285 ---
\opage{285}preserve and heighten it. Let b be an idea by which a is
favored, c another by which a is cancelled. Both b and c stand in
connection with a and are therefore called forth by it according to the
laws of the association of ideas. As long as neither b nor c is given as
actual experience, consciousness will pass now from a to b, now from a
to c. The question now is what influence these alternating transitions
between the ideas have on feeling.

It has been shown (A, 2) that feeling on the whole arises more slowly
than sensations. By observation one will easily convince oneself that
feeling also arises and moves with greater slowness than ideas. It takes
longer to pass over from joy to sorrow than from the idea of something
joyful to the idea of something sorrowful. Even in the sanguine, thought
and fancy move with greater rapidity than mood. Now when consciousness
passes over from the one of the viewpoints indicated (ab) to the other
(ac), the idea c will have a tendency to awaken a new mood (γ); but
since the mood (β) awakened by the first idea (b) still persists, the
two moods will meet each other and enter into a combination. It goes
here as with the waves that beat against the shore; the newly arriving
one takes up into itself the backwash of the preceding one. There thus
arises a mixed mood: when b β keeps the upper hand, hope, --- when c γ
gains the upper hand, fear. Both feelings presuppose a certain play of
possibilities.

The moods of hope and fear appear in countless gradations and nuances,
according to the relation of the possibilities to one another. The
greater the possibility of attaining the goal is assumed to be, the more
will hope approach \emph{confident expectation}, in which the mind rests
in the idea of the bright future with no other unrest than that which is
inseparable from the consciousness that the present is to give place to
a future, --- the smaller, the more fear approaches \emph{despair} or
\emph{resignation}. If the possibilities are regarded as equal, so that
fancy is attracted equally strongly from both sides, the mind feels
itself divided. Two different moods strive to spread in consciousness,
but neither of them can gain the mastery. Hence arises the mood of
\emph{doubt}, whose chief peculiarity is the painful unrest that
% --- p. 286 ---
\opage{286}can awaken so violent a need for decision that it seems a
matter of indifference in what direction the decision goes, if only the
pain of dividedness
% footnote *) on p. 286
ceases.\footnote{Othello (Act III. Sc. 3) says to Iago, when the latter
has awakened doubt in him about Desdemona's fidelity:
\begin{verse}
You have set me on the rack!\\
Far better to be wholly deceived\\
than only to know a little.
\end{verse}}
% TR: Høffding quotes Othello in Danish; translated from his Danish.
% Shakespeare: "Thou hast set me on the rack: / I swear 'tis better to
% be much abused / Than but to know't a little."
People who are tormented by sudden promptings or fixed ideas sometimes
suffer so much from them that they follow the prompting to murder or
suicide merely in order
% footnote **) on p. 286
to have peace.\footnote{Cf.\ \emph{Ideler}: Biographien Geisteskranker.
Berlin 1841. p.~134. \emph{Dagonet}: Nouveau traité des maladies
mentales. Paris 1876. p.~311.}

As we have here described the feelings of hope and fear, they
presuppose a certain development of the life of ideas, a certain ability
to weigh possibilities. But just as there is a more \emph{elementary
form of hope} in the original sanguine tendency, which only gradually is
checked by experience, so there is also an elementary fear at sudden and
strong impressions.\footnote{This contrast between hope and fear can be
followed still further back, namely to the feelings, conditioned by
certain general sensations, of lightness and freedom on the one side,
anxiety and oppression on the other. Cf.\ A, 3 a.} This kind of fear is
akin to astonishment at the new and unexpected and at first not to be
distinguished from it. The unknown and unexpected can awaken both
feelings, and both feelings find their expression in one's starting,
standing motionless or shrinking back. The lower animals contract at a
touch just like the higher ones and just as men still involuntarily do.
We are startled by a sudden sound, a rustling in the leaves or an
unexpected address, without accounting to ourselves for what affects
us. It is one of the first feelings that can be traced in infants. It
has something of the character of instinct, and the will cannot always
check it. In so far as it is due merely to the unknown, it falls away
with the development of knowledge. One of the most beneficial effects of
science has been to stop up false sources of fear. But on the other
hand the development of the life of ideas and of reflection has
% --- p. 287 ---
\opage{287}favored fear by leading to and giving practice in occupation
with possibilities. The play of free ideas makes possible a dividedness
and cleavage in the mind which the unreflective state does not know,
however much it may suffer from the instinctive terror of the unknown.
Here too, however, scientific insight can exert a beneficial influence,
in that by its criticism it restricts the possibilities and in its
knowledge of laws builds a firm ground for the conviction as to what
cannot become an object of knowledge.

\textbf{e.} When two conflicting feelings press forward at once and
simultaneously assert themselves with equal strength, the unbearable
dividedness mentioned above arises. This, however, will be a rare case
and not of long duration. When \emph{Shakespeare} has King Claudius
describe his mood at his marriage to his brother's widow as a complete
\emph{equilibrium}
% footnote *) on p. 287
of joy and sorrow,\footnote{\emph{Shakespeare's} Hamlet. Act I. Sc. 2.
\begin{verse}
--- with subdued joy, with one eye\\
wet with tears, one in the gleam of hope,\\
with delight at the funeral, dirge at the wedding,\\
while sorrow and pleasure held balance in the soul.
\end{verse}}
% TR: Høffding quotes Hamlet in Danish; Shakespeare's text reads "With an
% auspicious and a dropping eye, / With mirth in funeral and with dirge in
% marriage, / In equal scale weighing delight and dole."
it was surely the intention of \emph{the great poet-psychologist} to
mark him thereby as a hypocrite, who betrays himself by the
unnaturalness of the state he attributes to himself. Where the one
feeling does not displace the other or reduce it to a subordinate factor
(cf.\ A, 3 d), they will relieve each other rhythmically. \emph{Plato}
describes in this way the mood of the disciples of Socrates during their
last conversation with their teacher. ``I found myself,'' he has Phaedo
say, ``in a truly strange state, in an unusual mixture of joy [at the
content of the conversation] and sorrow, when I considered that he was
now soon to die. And all those present were in almost the same mood; now
they laughed, now they wept.'' Such alternation is the natural state
where different motives assert themselves. It will not be able to last,
however, since the mind seeks equilibrium and converts the successive
differences into simultaneous ones; the two feelings will thereby fuse
into a new feeling, sorrow and joy, e.g., into wistful melancholy. In
gentler natures this conversion will take place more quickly than
\emph{in more} passionate ones. \emph{Homer} represents Andromache
``smiling
% --- p. 288 ---
\opage{288}through tears'' (δεκρυόεν γαλάσασα), when Hector hands her
their little son in order himself to hasten out to the battle.
% TR: the Greek is as printed; Il. VI 484 reads δακρυόεν γελάσασα.
% "Vemod" (wistful melancholy) is the Danish word for this blended feeling.

\emph{Sibbern} has therefore rightly distinguished between the mixture
or alternation of different or even conflicting states of feeling and
mixed feelings in the proper sense. To a mixed feeling it belongs that
the difference between the elementary feelings is no longer noticed,
both working to form one single total feeling --- ``as when fear is
overcome by boldness with regard to the very thing feared, or when
strength in its struggle and exertion feels itself inflamed or furthered
precisely by the hindrances or difficulties. A certain contentment with
life, indeed blessedness, by virtue of overcome and subdued affliction
or of some other effect of adversity, belongs here.''\footnote{Psychologie.
Kbhvn. 1856. p.~380.}

\textbf{3.} We have analyzed some of the simplest states of feeling in
order to discover what relation obtains between the elements of feeling
and the ideas connected with them. We shall now see what this analysis
teaches us about the law of the development of the feelings.

In the older psychology there appears a tendency to see in knowledge the
main thing and what is properly constitutive in consciousness. For
\emph{Plato} the immortal part of the soul was one with thought or
reason; the feelings of pleasure and displeasure, like the
sense-sensations, arose only through the soul's being enclosed in a
material body. In modern idealistic philosophy down to \emph{Hegel} and
\emph{Herbart} a similar view can be traced. \emph{Kant} and
\emph{William Hamilton}, e.g., could conceive of spiritual beings with
reason without feeling and will, but not the reverse. For them the
essence of consciousness coincides with knowledge. \textit{Consciousness
is a knowledge}, says Hamilton.

It was therefore usual to apply without further ado to the feelings the
laws found for the development of ideas. This has happened especially
with the laws of the association of ideas. The natural growth of the
life of thought rests on these laws; but do they also hold for the
feelings among themselves? Some psychologists (among the older ones,
e.g., \emph{Spinoza} Eth.\ III, 14) assume this and hold that related
feelings and
% --- p. 289 ---
\opage{289}feelings that have earlier arisen together will later call
each other forth. But it is a great question whether a feeling \emph{in
itself} has the power to call forth another, however great the kinship
and likeness may be. Feeling \emph{as such} rests in itself. One idea
leads to another; therefore there can be a coherent system of thought.
But the feelings lead beyond themselves only indirectly, namely with the
help of thoughts and of the will, and are not connected by any
dialectical coherence. It has therefore been wrongly objected to the
expression ``association of ideas'' that it does not include the
feelings, which after all are also members in the
% footnote *) on p. 289
series of states of consciousness.\footnote{\emph{Bouillier}: Du
plaisir et de la douleur. 2 éd. p.~173 f.} There are, as we have seen,
hardly any quite pure states of feeling without elements of knowledge;
at any rate they must be relegated to the very first beginning of
conscious life or to its cessation.

The question is: can the element of feeling of one mental state attract
the element of feeling in another mental state, or does the transition
always take place through the connection of the elements of knowledge?

1) A state of consciousness (A) consists in the connection of an element
of feeling (α) with an element of knowledge (a). Now if there are other,
related elements of knowledge (a$_{2}$.. a$_{3}$.. a$_{4}$ etc.), which
a has a tendency to call forth, some of these will be able to enter into
a connection with a, and through a with α. While α was earlier
determined only by a, it is now determined by a + a$_{2}$ + a$_{3}$ +
a$_{4}$. The mood is modified; the state A becomes A$_{4}$. We thereby
obtain no wholly new kind of feeling, but the given feeling spreads over
a larger part of the content of consciousness. I love a human being ---
and thereby acquire interest in and love for everything that concerns
him and is connected with him. Love is always an expansion of our being;
it joins the lover and the beloved into a whole. But what we love is a
part of the world, and since the world is a coherent whole, our feeling
will extend further, when special causes do not prevent it. --- It goes
similarly with other feelings: it is according to this rule that the
devil takes the whole hand once he has got the finger.


% --- p. 290 ---
\opage{290}2) The original feeling undergoes a greater change when the
new element of knowledge is not so immediately connected with the first.
If we have A = a + α, and a proves to be closely connected with b, then
A becomes A$_{b}$, i.e.\ the feeling is still of the same kind, but more
special. When, e.g., a certain quality (a) has great value in my eyes,
and I discover, or believe I discover, that I myself (b) possess it, my
admiration (A) becomes pride (A$_{b}$).

3) When, finally, the new idea (b) itself brings with it a feeling (β)
which it would have awakened had it reigned alone, a new kind of feeling
(B) arises. Instead of A = a + α we get B = α + (a + b) + β: i.e.\
through a and b, α and β are connected. This is the schema for the
development of hope, fear, wistful melancholy and similar composite
feelings.

No sharp boundary can of course be drawn between these three cases,
since already a$_{2}$ and a$_{3}$ etc.\ must call forth new nuances of
feeling, which fuse with the feeling given beforehand.

The association of ideas is thus the channel through which the feelings
mingle with one another. Through the relation of thoughts to new
thoughts, the feelings pass over into new feelings. But since the
movement of feeling proceeds more slowly than the movement of thought, it
is no wonder that intellectual progress as a rule has the lead over the
development of the life of feeling. Thought is the most mobile part of
our being; feeling forms the foundation, to which the effects from the
more mobile surface are propagated only gradually.

Although the laws of the association of ideas cannot be applied to the
relations of the feelings, they are nevertheless, on account of the
connection between feelings and ideas, of the greatest significance for
the development of the life of feeling.

% footnote *) on p. 290
\textbf{4.} If this view\footnote{It is already hinted at by
\emph{Hume} (Treatise II, 1, 5. 8), more definitely by \emph{Bain}
(Emotions and Will. I, 5) and \emph{Kirchmann} (Erläuterungen zur Kant's
Anthropologie. p.~447).} is correct, it must be confirmed by the way in
which feelings are recalled in memory; for the laws of the association
of ideas are the laws of memory.
% --- p. 291 ---
\opage{291}Now it at once appears that ideas are more easily recalled
than the feelings connected with them. Pictures and situations from our
past life we can recall to ourselves, but only most imperfectly the
moods that animated us.\footnote{\emph{Longfellow} has expressed this in
the following fine lines:
\begin{verse}
Ah, our memory can take up again\\
all that belongs to the outward;\\
it builds up, as we would most have it,\\
every fairy palace of the vanished days.\\
Only ourselves it cannot draw in there!\\
Our heart's strange play of strings\\
never gets its first tuning back.\\
\hspace*{3em}(The Golden Legend. Tr.\ Thor Lange)
\end{verse}}
% TR: "Stemning" in the last line means both mood and the tuning of an
% instrument.
The more nuances, the more definitely marked features and relations a
mental state presents, the better can it be called back in memory. But
nuances and relations presuppose comparison and belong to the region of
knowledge. The smaller the part the elements of knowledge play in a
state, the more imperfectly is it remembered. We thus recall the
alternation and succession of feelings more easily than the single
feeling itself in and for itself. In this respect it goes with the
feelings as it goes with the immediate sense-sensations (V B, 7 a.\ d.);
we can remember that we have had them without really recalling them.
Special circumstances are needed for the state of feeling to be
reproduced. Feelings are remembered with the help of the ideas to which
they were originally attached, and together with which they once made up
a certain state of consciousness. Only when one really immerses oneself
in the memory, lives oneself into it, can the feeling be awakened. This
follows directly from the slower movement of feeling; the thought comes
back in a moment, but
% footnote **) on p. 291 (continues on p. 292)
the unfolding of the feelings takes time.\footnote{In some cases of an
almost pathological character, the memory can \emph{be} connected with
quite the same fresh feeling as the original experience. \emph{Littré}
relates from his own experience a remarkable example of such
``automnésie affective.'' At ten years old he had lost a little sister
under especially moving circumstances and grieved much over it. »Mais le
chagrin d'un garçon ne dure pas beaucoup.« Yet he always kept the event
in lively memory, although the fresh pain had vanished. Then, in his
old age, he suddenly felt, without \emph{external} occasion, the same
pain again. »Tout à coup, sans que je ne le voulusse ni le cherchasse,
par un phénomène d'automnésie affective, ce même événement s'est
reproduit avec une peine présente non moindre, certes, que \emph{celle}
que j'éprouvais au moment même, et qui alla jusqu'a mouiller mes yeux
de larmes«. This recurred several times in the course of \emph{several
days}, \emph{after which it} ceased and gave place to the usual memory.
(\emph{Revue} philos. 1877. p.~660 f.).} There will always be a
% --- p. 292 ---
\opage{292}hindrance given in the feeling that dominates us at the present
moment; it will in any case modify the earlier feeling more or less, and
a new feeling will arise which becomes the resultant of the two
(according to the schema a + (a + b) + β). From this source stem many
illusions that we form with regard to the past.

The feelings attached to the senses of sight and hearing and to free
intuition and activity of thought are recalled more easily than those
due to the lower senses, and especially than those due to the exercise
of organic functions. The former therefore stand most at our free
disposal, and external hindrances can less easily cut us off from them,
which is of great significance, since the aesthetic, intellectual, moral
and religious feelings are of this kind.

\textbf{5.} So far we have spoken of feeling in general, and we have
seen how the primitive feeling of pleasure or displeasure can be
developed by being directed toward definite objects through the ideas
that fuse with it. The further development of feeling now receives its
decisive stamp according as it is regard for the individual himself or
regard for something lying beyond the individual that comes to furnish
the \emph{center of gravity of feeling}.

At first this contrast, which in its full development becomes the
contrast between egoism and sympathy (altruism), cannot stand out. In
the incipient life of consciousness the ideas are little clear and
definite; the idea of one's own ego therefore does not yet come into
contrast with the idea of something outside the ego or of other egos. It
is therefore psychologically meaningless to speak of an original egoism.
It is another matter that no individual can feel joy or displeasure at
anything that does not make itself felt in
% --- p. 293 ---
\opage{293}him. Whatever he may attach his pleasure and his pain to ---
he can have no other pleasure and pain than his own. At the primitive
stages it is as good as exclusively the feelings attached to
sense-sensations (especially general sensations) that reign. The
impulse to suck and to bring to the mouth everything that is grasped
shows itself early; it can certainly be interpreted as a tendency to
refer everything to oneself as center; but it must then be added that
the child itself does not know this center.

A long process of development (described from another point of view in
I, 4) is required before the consciousness of one's own ego as center
arises. The child is unconscious of itself, feels no \emph{shyness or
bashfulness}, but stares calmly and fixedly at a new face, provided
this has nothing about it that frightens. One could just as well speak
of innate impudence as of innate egoism. Only when the child has the
sensation of being observed and judged does it become bashful, turn its
eyes away and blush when one looks at it. This feeling of being an
object of observation and judgment contains the germ of feelings such
as \emph{humility}, \emph{remorse} and \emph{self-contempt}, which have
in common that we feel ourselves to stand far below the standard that we
ourselves or others apply to our person. These feelings presuppose such
a development of the circle of ideas that a distinct idea of our person,
of a demand that is to be fulfilled by us, and of the relation between
the two can arise. --- On the other hand, feelings such as \emph{pride},
\emph{feeling of power} and \emph{self-satisfaction} form when our
person in our own eyes possesses those advantages and reaches up to the
height of the ideal in question.

The feelings that concern ourselves are the first to be awakened and the
strongest that for the time being stir in us. The feelings of pleasure
and displeasure, at first impersonal so to speak (not concentrated or
integrated), naturally gather about regard for self. This, in the nature
of the case, comes forward so frequently that it grows together with
feeling more firmly than any other experience and idea. The more regard
for self excludes other resting points for feeling, the more there
arises egoism proper, which makes everything a means to the self's
feeling of pleasure.


% --- p. 294 ---
\opage{294}\textbf{6.} How can it be explained at all that the individual
can feel pleasure and displeasure at something that does not intervene
in its own existence? --- This question has seemed so difficult to
answer that some have even denied that what is asked about takes place.
All sympathy has then been explained as hidden self-love. ``Self-love,''
says La Rochefoucauld, ``never comes to rest outside itself, and stops
at foreign objects only as the bees do at the flowers, in order to
extract from them what it can use.'' --- Others have recognized that
there is a pleasure and a displeasure at other things for their own
sake, and have sought to explain the coming into being of such unselfish
feeling according to general psychological laws. They even undertake to
show that a psychological bridge can be built between absolute regard
for self and absolute self-forgetfulness, between self-preservation and
self-surrender. The most interesting and most original accounts in this
direction have been given by \emph{Spinoza} and \emph{Hartley}. Later
James \emph{Mill} and \emph{Stuart Mill} developed the same theory
% footnote *) on p. 294
further.\footnote{Cf.\ also \emph{Sibbern}: Psykologisk Pathologi
[Psychological Pathology]. Kbhvn. 1828. p.~97--108.}

It is the laws of forgetting discussed earlier (V A, 8 d) that find
application here. An idea that has been the occasion of another idea's
arising may itself vanish, so that this other idea comes to work
immediately and alone. An often used example of this is the independent
value attached to money, although it is only a means of procuring
certain goods. In the miser, the middle term, without which the value
cannot be grounded and with whose help it originally arose, has passed
entirely into oblivion. He loves money for its own sake, indeed even
entirely renounces the goods it could procure. The feeling has shifted
from the end to the means, or rather has made the means an end. In the
same way, persons and things which at first became objects of joy and
love only because they were causes of pleasure will be able to become
immediate objects of these feelings. We love them for their own sake,
having forgotten the original why.\footnote{\emph{Paul Friedmann}, in
his essay »The Genesis of Disinterested Benevolence« (Mind 1878), lays
weight on the fact that people who live together do each other good
turns even without expressly willing it. The feeling of having thus been
a cause of joy to another awakens interest in him; one takes care that
the benefit is not undone, because one feels one's \emph{power} in thus
helping others. The interest thus awakened can \emph{then} come to
embrace the whole person, and the original occasion is forgotten. ---
Aristotle already said something similar. ``Benefactors seem to love
those they \emph{have} benefited more than these love their benefactors.
\ldots\ The same we see in artists: every one loves his work more than
he would be loved by it, \emph{if} it were ensouled.'' Aristotle
explains this phenomenon by the fact that the deed is a part or an
expression of our being. Eth.\ Nicom.\ IX, 7.} This
% --- p. 295 ---
\opage{295}will happen all the more easily the more other persons have in
common with ourselves in nature, appearance, circumstances of life and
interests. We then become accustomed not to separate ourselves from
them; in everything that happens to them we involuntarily put ourselves
in their place, suffer and feel with them. Involuntarily, by virtue of
the law of similarity, interest glides from ourselves over to others. It
is not only sympathy that can arise in this way, but also envy and
ambition. These feelings too arise through one's vividly representing to
oneself the feelings of other persons.

Sympathy presupposes that the common interests have the upper hand over
the conflicting ones. It therefore also develops first in narrow circles
and afterwards radiates out into larger circles. Each of the narrower
circles (family, class, nation, sect) stands as an egoist in relation to
more comprehensive circles. In the end sympathy must be able to extend
to all living beings, to the whole of nature; it then finally acquires a
religious character, becomes what Spinoza called ``the intellectual love
% footnote *) on p. 295
of God.''\footnote{Cf.\ »Spinozas Liv og Lære« [Spinoza's Life and
Teaching]. p.~124--140. --- »Den engelske Filosofi i vor Tid.«
[English Philosophy in Our Time]. p.~57.}

This view, however, surely overrates what can be attained in the course
of a single individual's life. The feelings are, as we have seen, heavy
masses to operate with; they do not shift so easily once they have
found a center of gravity. More experiences than the single individual
can have in his life are needed to complete such a metamorphosis,
however aptly and naturally it is otherwise described.


% --- p. 296 ---
\opage{296}The theory moreover presupposes that there is a possibility of
receiving help, or at any rate beneficial influence, from others. The
individual thus does not stand isolated, not from the first sharply
separated from other individuals. A society is presupposed within which
the individual develops. The problem is then only pushed further back,
and the question is whether the society in which relations are so
arranged that a development such as the one described becomes possible
can itself be conceived as having arisen through the union of
individuals each of whom begins by himself with an unconditional impulse
of self-preservation.

In so far, finally, as the theory lays weight on the motive lying in
other individuals' likeness to the individual himself, it presupposes an
original impulse of imitation or an instinct to feel and suffer with
one's like. So \emph{Spinoza}, who here hints at a theory later
developed by \emph{Adam Smith}. He who sees another burn himself
involuntarily draws back his hand. The glad or sorrowful appearance of
others at once attunes us in the same way. Some even hold that this
ability to be attuned by the appearance of others is grounded in an
inherited disposition. But in any case this ability or impulse then
becomes an independent element alongside the impulse of
self-preservation and requires its own explanation.

All this indicates that one must go further back than the individual
impulse of self-preservation if one wishes to understand how individuals
come to attach an independent value to something that lies beyond
themselves.

\textbf{7.} The boundary of psychology lies where the question of the
origin of the individual arises. Nor shall any attempt be made here to
solve this problem. But it \emph{will} be of interest to point out that
what does not find its full explanation in what can be acquired in the
course of the individual's life may become more intelligible by seeing
the individual, with his whole nature, proceed from the race. However
it may stand with psychological individuality (the center of pleasure
and pain, of memory and self-consciousness), physiology shows us that
the separation between individuals takes place gradually, and that the
stage at which the mother organism and the new organism stand
independently over against each other is preceded by a stage at which
they make up one single living whole or organism.


% --- p. 297 ---
\opage{297}Reproduction stands in the same relation to the race as
nutrition and renewal (regeneration) to the individual organism. The
series of living beings presents every possible transitional form
between individual self-preservation and the production of new
individuals from the substance of the mother organism. In plants and
lower animals, single cells from one part or another of the organism can
at once lead an independent organic life as soon as they are separated
from the mother organism. The higher we go up in the series of beings,
the more intricate become the conditions for such a development or
prolongation of the organism's life in new organisms. But also in the
beings that reproduce by seed and egg, and in which therefore the new
organism arises from cells derived from two different organisms, the
same fundamental relation holds, since egg cells and sperm cells have
formed within the mother and father organisms and at their expense.
Whatever the mode of reproduction, the organic individual nevertheless
lives the first period of its life as part of another organism.
According to the assumption now general, the egg is formed in the mother
organism before the latter's birth, according to some even in
% footnote *) on p. 297
the egg of the mother organism.\footnote{\emph{Ditlefsen}: Menneskets
Histologi [Human Histology]. p.~244. --- \emph{Claude Bernard}: Leçons
sur les phénomènes de la vie. p.~311.} Although the origin of the
individual is thus finally lost in the distance for physiology just as
for psychology, we have nevertheless obtained the important information
that it must be sought within earlier organisms, and that the separation
between independent organisms is only the result of a process of
development. The origin of the individual thereby falls under a more
general physiological point of view. --- ``Growth,'' says v.\
\emph{Baer},
% footnote **) on p. 297
\footnote{Entwickelungsgeschichte der Thiere. II. P.~4.} ``is nutrition
with the formation of new elements, thus a continued generation; and
generation is nothing but the beginning of an individual growth. ---
Growth we find quite comprehensible; it is precisely the beginning that
we should like to know. Above all we seek a really definite beginning, a
sharp boundary between being and not-being. But do we then really find
anywhere in nature such an absolute beginning?''


% --- p. 298 ---
\opage{298}Since self-preservation and reproduction thus pass over into
each other, as \emph{Plato} already taught (Symposion. Hejse's transl.\
p.~73), we get here a physiological foundation for the transition
between the feeling of pleasure in what concerns the individual himself
and the feeling of pleasure in what lies beyond his own life-process.
Sympathy seems here literally to grow out of self-preservation. Even
after the physical connection between the mother organism and the new
organism has been broken off at birth, \emph{instinct} maintains a firm
connection. The most wonderful instincts of living beings are precisely
those that lead the one generation to prepare the way for the other.
Many insects prepare food and safety for the larvae that are to develop
from their eggs, and which they never get to see. In none of its forms
does mother-love know any \emph{why}; but it appears most obscurely and
most instinctively where the mother does not have the child over against
her as an independent organism. Only when this is the case can the
general psychological laws begin to work, so that the maternal instinct
can be developed into real \emph{maternal feeling}. That instinctive
care for beings of which the individual itself can have no idea is
guided in its particulars by sense-sensations. Instinct consists in the
union of a strong feeling with certain sense-sensations and involuntary
motor impulses. When an observation of the child and an idea of it can
take the place of the mere sense-sensation, the feeling acquires a
higher form. The child's caresses and smiles, its helpless situation,
the feeling of belonging together which constant benefits call forth in
the very one who confers them (see above, p.~294**), develop the
originally blind and instinctive feeling into clearness and inwardness.
The feeling now separates itself definitely from the general feeling of
life and can enter into definite contrast to it. How strong maternal
feeling is in comparison with the impulse of self-preservation is seen
from the courage with which animals defend their young and the grief
they feel at losing them. Swallows fly into burning houses to save their
young. The mother whale does not leave her calf when it has been
harpooned, as long as it is still alive. When sea-bears have had to
abandon their young in flight, they soon come back again, search for
them, shed (according to
% --- p. 299 ---
\opage{299}\emph{Brehm}) great tears, and swim about in their
lamentation for several days along the shore. Many animals seek to draw
the attention of attackers from their young to themselves.

In animals and in the lowest races of men, mother-love fades when the
child's helpless age is past. The child is left to itself when it no
longer sucks. Where, on the other hand, there is a lasting relation
between mother and child, the feeling will attain a still higher form by
embracing not only the child's physical but also its spiritual
individuality. When the vivid idea of an independent spiritual life in
the child has formed, the psychological doubling in which sympathy
consists is fully complete. Pleasure and pain are felt because another
being feels pleasure and pain, just as the vibrations of one string make
another vibrate in the same way.

In the foregoing only mother-love has been spoken of, not father-love,
since the latter first shows itself with strength at higher stages.
Mother-love is not only the strongest sympathetic feeling; it is also
--- when we regard the series of living beings as the expression of a
great process of development --- the one that reveals itself earliest,
and which, by founding the most original human social relation, at the
same time lays the foundation for all the means and forms by which
sympathy can be developed further and higher. The relation between
mother and child is the most primitive family and the most primitive
human society. It makes a pure ``state of nature,'' an absolute
individualism, impossible.\footnote{Cf.\ »Grundlaget for den humane
Etik« [The Foundation of Humane Ethics] p.~27 f.\ 58.} --- In the animal
world the male seldom takes part in the care of the offspring. In some
species of fish the male takes over the whole work with great fidelity
and
% footnote **) on p. 299
watchfulness.\footnote{\emph{Darwin}: Menneskets Afstamning og
Parringsvalget [The Descent of Man, and Selection in Relation to Sex].
II. P.~19.} But otherwise the father is often even a danger and an
enemy to his own offspring. A remarkable example at once of egoism on
the part of the male and of self-sacrifice on the part of the mother is
related by \emph{Darwin} in his ``Voyage round the World'' (Danish
transl.\ p.~213). The wild stallions on the Falkland Islands roam
constantly from place to place and force the mares to accompany them,
whether the young foals are able to
% --- p. 300 ---
\opage{300}follow or not. A man saw a stallion for a whole hour violently
kick and bite a mare until it forced her to leave the foal to its fate.
Male egoism shows itself also in the human world, since here too at the
lower stages the care of the children is left to the mother. Only where
marriage takes on fixed forms, and \emph{this happens}, as
\emph{Lucretius} already taught (V, 1008), only when \emph{people live}
in fixed dwellings, --- only then can the relation of father become a
source of the development of sympathetic feelings. Paternal tenderness
then takes its place beside maternal tenderness.

A sign that the half-unconscious instinct in the development of sympathy
% footnote *) on p. 300
within the human race\footnote{A detailed account of this course of
development is found in \emph{Herbert Spencer's} Psychology (Part 2),
Sociology (Part 1) and in his »Data of Ethics«. --- In our own
literature we now have an account of the primitive family and social
relations in \emph{Claudius Wilkens's} work »Samfundslegemets
Grundlove« [The Fundamental Laws of the Social Body]. Kbhvn. 1881. IV,
3--4.} precedes the psychological development proper, which is
determined by the laws of the association of ideas, is that sympathy
develops in those who give sooner than in those who receive. The love of
parents for their children is as a rule greater than the love of
children for their parents. During Sulla's proscriptions sons sometimes
betrayed their fathers, never the reverse.

In the shelter of the bulwark that paternal and maternal sympathy
builds, brotherly and comradely sympathy can now develop. It spreads
also beyond the family by virtue of the psychological process described
above (6). It reaches its consummation in the feeling that all men are
brothers, of like nature and subject to like conditions.

\textbf{8.} There is yet another mighty feeling that grows out of a
natural instinct and forms an important foundation for the development
of sympathy. \emph{The feeling of sexual love} is in its purely
primitive form, like maternal feeling, a factor in the general feeling
of life. Here too the first stirrings are connected with upheavals in
the interior of the organism, by which the feeling of life acquires a
character hitherto unknown. New, inexplicable presentiments and
sensations arise. Something stirs in the individual that drives it out
beyond itself. But at the
% --- p. 301 ---
\opage{301}primitive stages the individual regards the object to which
the impulse binds it only as a means. The feeling of sexual love is for
the time being like a prolongation of the feeling of self.
\emph{Aristophanes} therefore explains it in Plato's »Symposion« by the
gods' having cut human beings in two, which is why the two halves now go
about full of longing and seek each other. The comparative physiology
of reproduction in a way confirms this humorous explanation. It shows us
various transitional forms between sexless and sexual reproduction. In
the lowest forms of sexual reproduction the corresponding organs are
still found in one and the same individual; such a hermaphrodite
represents in itself the whole species, while in the higher beings the
species requires two different individuals in order to be represented.
In this double representation it is --- as in the contrast between the
mother and the child born --- as though one ego were divided in two.

Here too it is only when the idea of the object works determinatively
on the feeling that that \emph{doubleness} becomes significant. At the
lowest stages the object does not stand as another ego. An approach to
it, however, is found already within \emph{the animal world}. \emph{The
courtship of animals} is, \emph{as Darwin has} shown in his well-known
work on sexual selection, not so simple a thing as is generally
supposed. An individual choice is often met with; beauty and other
attractive qualities are taken into account, and often a \emph{touching
fidelity} is shown. With this, motives are already given which in the
human world develop the feeling of sexual love from an unbridled,
sensual craving, in which the individual seeks only its own, into
inward devotion to and joy in another individual. Regarded purely
egoistically, the sexual impulse is a deception; it looks as though it
were the individual's own satisfaction that is at stake, and yet \emph{it
is the preservation of the race} that is secured. \emph{Schopenhauer},
who sees the essence of the feeling of sexual love exhausted in the mere
sexual impulse, \emph{therefore preaches revolt} against it out of
indignation at the deception that ``the will to live'' here practices.
But this impulse, which in its lowest forms need not know the object
that satisfies it, is purified and ennobled the more it is attached to
and determined by the image of another, independent individual, an
image that can awaken joy and admiration, not merely immediate desire.
Instead of \emph{coming forward with a demand} and with a word of power,
% --- p. 302 ---
\opage{302}the feeling can now be satisfied only by free devotion.
``Pleasure can be stolen, love must be a gift''
% footnote *) on p. 302
(\emph{Schiller})\footnote{In »Mauprat« \emph{George Sand} has
described how brutal instinct can undergo a metamorphosis into ideal,
human feeling.}. And that the means has become the end is seen from the
fact that resignation is possible, that desire can be given up without
the feeling ceasing.

\textbf{9.} In the instincts now discussed we have the helping hands
that from the first lead man out beyond himself and bring him into
relations under which the educative laws of the association of ideas can
work. This was already emphasized by \emph{Shaftesbury} in opposition to
the individualistic explanation of sympathy. In our day this point of
view has been asserted especially by \emph{Spencer} and \emph{Darwin}.
--- Once a feeling is attached to an idea, and this idea to other ideas,
the way will be opened (according to 3) to the expansion and
modification of the feeling. And as such a metamorphosis slowly takes
place during the life of the race, it deposits its results in the
organization that the new individuals receive. The laws of heredity make
it possible that what earlier generations have acquired can become a
capital with which later generations can begin. Together with heredity,
tradition and upbringing are at work, in that the forms in which earlier
generations have given expression to their sympathetic feelings meet the
later generations as awakening and nourishing in a sympathetic
direction. The sympathetic instinct unfolds differently in a Christian
than in a Greek atmosphere, differently within modern humanity than
within the medieval mixture of asceticism and barbarism. How much rests
on tradition, how much on inheritance, and how much must be acquired and
developed in the course of the individual life on the basis of the
constantly working instincts, differs for every individual, every sex,
every race and every age. Our analysis is here only qualitative, and
only points out that these three forces are at work.

If one starts from the impulse of self-preservation as the original
foundation, which is successively altered by the influence of
experiences working through the laws of the association of ideas, one
will be able to construct a dominant feeling of hatred,
% --- p. 303 ---
\opage{303}envy and malice just as well as a dominant feeling of
sympathy. \emph{Spinoza} already called attention to this. If it is
asked, then, why in psychology we do not follow the development of a
disinterested malice, but dwell instead on the development of
disinterested love, the answer lies in the first place in the fact that
the formal laws are the same in both processes of development; one need
only, as it were, change the sign. But the most important reason is that
disinterested malice (\textit{malevolent sympathy}) does not find the
nourishment in the conditions of life that the opposite feeling does. It
can arise under certain individual and unhappy circumstances (cf.\
Shakespeare's Richard the Third, and Miss Wade in Dickens's ``Little
Dorrit''), but it cannot strike root in nature, because it is hostile to
life, not life-preserving and life-furthering. Even if it finds
favorable conditions in the conflict between the interests of
individuals, families, races and confessions, historical development on
the whole nevertheless tends to even out and cancel such disharmony, to
lead the conflicting interests out into a common stream. Often the
decisive help comes here from the sympathetic feelings grounded on
organic instincts (the story of the rape of the Sabine women;
\textit{connubium} between patricians and plebeians; Romeo and Juliet).

The parallel mentioned between the psychological development of
disinterested love and of disinterested malice explains why they
sometimes so easily turn into each other. In both places ideal forces
are in motion, and lukewarmness and narrowness have been left behind. It
depends only on whether the railway train, with the speed it has
attained, may come at a switch to rush off in another direction.

\textbf{10.} Even where instinct finds no direct satisfaction, it still
exerts its mighty effect. The movement it causes in the feeling of life
must have an outlet, and if this cannot be found in reality, it is
sought in an ideal. For the ascetic and the mystic the ideal becomes
``the heavenly Virgin,'' who takes the place of an earthly woman.
\emph{Plato} already (in the »Symposion«) described Eros with genius as
