the ideal impulse that is satisfied either in sexual love or in the
striving for honor or power or knowledge. Eros is the great teacher who
leads man to attach his heart to something that lies
% --- p. 304 ---
\opage{304}beyond his own ego. As a counterpart to this ideal effect of
instinct there is no lack of examples of an uncanny mixture of
sensuality and mysticism. In instinct itself there lies nothing but that
the need for devotion has been awakened; it then depends on how this
need is satisfied. That is why the age of transition from childhood to
youth is so significant and fateful a time in every human being's life.
The power of the obscure but strong feelings leads the individual out
beyond his limitation, sets thought and fancy in motion, and awakens an
idealizing need. It is a time when everyone is a genius, even if he
never is otherwise.\footnote{\emph{Cabanis} remarks (Rapports du
physique et du moral de l'homme. V, 10): »J'ai vu nombre de fois la plus
grande fécondité d'idées, la plus brillante imagination, une aptitude
singulière à tous les arts, se développer tout à coup chez des filles de
cet âge, mais s'éteindre bientôt par degrés, et faire place au bout de
quelque temps à la médiocrité d'esprit la plus absolue. La même cause
n'a souvent pas moins de puissance chez les jeunes garçons; souvent
aussi les heureux effets n'en sont pas plus durables.«} \emph{Goethe}
in his poem »Der Schäfer« has described with unsurpassable humor this
all too often merely brief ideal flaring up, which ``draws toward the
distance,'' but, after instinct has had its physical satisfaction, gives
place to the normal prose.

The most significant crisis in the development of a feeling occurs when
its object passes over from the sphere of sensation and observation to
that of idea and memory. The source of all poetry, all morality and all
religion lies where the sympathetic feeling no longer has its object
immediately present. The immediate connection with the object is now
replaced by a certain distance, and it depends on whether the feeling
can bridge this distance, so that ``its power is felt at a distance''
(\emph{Goethe's} poem »Das Blümlein Wunderschön«).

\emph{Bain} lays great weight on the significance that touch and embrace
have in all forms of the feeling of tenderness, whether this is love of
children and the helpless, or sexual love, or friendship. ``Just as
anger reaches its consummation and its satisfaction in striking someone
to the ground,
% --- p. 305 ---
\opage{305}love is consummated and satisfied in an embrace \ldots\ The
pleasures of love begin and end with sensual touch.'' The strength of
this need finds its explanation in the fact that the sense of touch is
the fundamental sense, the one from which, according to the hypothesis
of evolution, all other senses have developed. Only this fundamental
sensation can afford the impulse of love its highest
satisfaction.\footnote{Emotions and Will. 3 ed. p.~126. --- In this
third edition of his excellent work, the best account of the psychology
of feeling and will yet given, \emph{Bain}, particularly as regards the
explanation of the development of sympathy and of the feeling of
tenderness, essentially adopts Spencer's hypothesis of evolution.} All
the greater is the pain it causes when separation occurs, so that the
deep organic need cannot be satisfied. It then depends on whether an
ideal connection can replace the physical one, whether a spiritual bond
can become as strong as the bodily one. The transition from the
physical to the ideal connection does not as a rule take place except
through resignation. But this resignation can vanish altogether through
the deeper and richer connection with the object which can take the
place of the immediate and momentary connection. Where the object of
feeling is great and manifold, sense-observation is even impossible.
This is so already in the feeling for a single personality. A
personality is never completely given in a single moment, a single
situation; we have it completely only in the totality of its life, in
its history. When we love a human being, we no doubt imagine him in some
definite situation or other; but this stands only as example or type.
(Cf.\ the theory of individual ideas V B, 9.) The feeling therefore
acquires an ideal character when it is to embrace the personality in
its wholeness and unity, which it strives to do if it has got beyond the
stage at which the object is only a means to the self-satisfaction of
the feeling individual.

The feeling becomes still more ideal when it concerns a great,
comprehensive whole (the family, the state, humanity) or is attached to
what by its essence cannot be imagined as limited (the Godhead, nature).
If a definite idea is to be connected with the feeling here, this can
only happen by
% --- p. 306 ---
\opage{306}way of symbolization. The history of religion shows us how
deeply the need for symbols lies in the essence of feeling. Hence the
need to fix the ideal and infinite in definite forms, so that the
feeling may have a rallying point. On the other hand we also see how the
fixed symbols can hamper and constrict the feeling, which is why the
feeling ever anew bursts them and demands new forms for its satisfaction
(the struggle of mysticism with dogmatics).

The development of the life of thought and fancy is thus a necessary
presupposition for a higher development of sympathy. In order to feel
with others, one must have had experiences of one's own, know from
oneself what pleasure and pain are, and \emph{what} calls forth these
feelings. The range of sympathy is therefore determined by the
experiences of particular individuals, nations and ages. Where
circumstances, as among savage nations, compel hardiness and contempt
for torments, no fellow-feeling with the pain of others develops either.
Love of mankind therefore presupposes a certain degree of civilization.
Like hardiness, asceticism too can hamper sympathy, \emph{which is why
the} principle of universal love of mankind, grounded in original
Christianity, could first properly unfold after the ascetic tendency in
the Church had been pushed back. --- Next after having had one's own
experiences of pleasure and pain, the task is to be able to hold these
fast in memory and to be able to apply them to the understanding of the
state of others. It depends on being accessible to lively impressions
and possessing mobility enough to put oneself in fancy in the place of
others. It has \emph{rightly} been remarked that lack of sympathy often
springs from lack of fancy and liveliness of mind, and not really from
lack of feeling. It becomes especially difficult \emph{to put} oneself
in the place of others when their inner or outer circumstances \emph{are
very} different from ours. Different \emph{language} (\emph{Greek} ---
barbarian), different color of skin
% footnote *) on p. 306
(the Negro slaves)\footnote{It was a world-historical moment when
\emph{Abraham Lincoln}, on his entry into Richmond, took off his hat to
a Negro who blessed him. --- A lady who watched this scene from a
window directly opposite turned away in unspeakable horror! (H.
\emph{Rasmussen}: Abraham Lincoln's Liv og Gerning [Abraham Lincoln's
Life and Work]. Kbhvn. 1882. p.~116).}, different
% --- p. 307 ---
\opage{307}rank and different faith have long offered hard resistance to
the growth of sympathy in the human race. --- Formal logical consistency
itself can here become of great significance. Just as one can say with a
certain right that egoism is unwise, since one often works for oneself
by working for others, so one can --- and surely with greater right ---
say that egoism is illogical when it restricts sympathy: it makes
exceptions that do not accord with the nature of the case. In
historical development there lies an inexorable logic which leads
sympathy to victory not only over individual egoism, but also over
family egoism and over national and confessional egoism. Impartial
knowledge plays into the hands of the widest sympathy; both stop only
at the natural limits. --- Finally, the development of the life of
knowledge also becomes significant for the form in which, and the means
by which, sympathy is satisfied. By giving way to the sympathetic
prompting of the moment one can just as well harm as benefit the object
of sympathy. The task then is to be able to check such momentary
emotion in order to take account of the lasting happiness of the
objects, and this is impossible without the ability for circumspect and
many-sided deliberation. Sympathy, like egoism, can show an aversion to
limitation, a need for full discharge, and can so far be said itself to
contain an egoistic element.

The relation between the intellectual and the emotional element in
sympathy varies infinitely in particular cases. Now it is fancy's
reflection of the nature and fate of the object, now the immediate unity
in which we live and feel with it, that gives the feeling its
peculiarity. Poetic sympathy is characterized by the preponderance of
the first-named element, so that at times even a pure satisfaction of
fancy is sought in busying oneself with the misfortunes and dark sides
of life and depicting them. Writings that give a lively and correct
description of individual and social sufferings can produce a certain
repellent effect, because the element of feeling proper does not show
itself. The intellectual element in sympathy is also the element of
distance; it is what lets the object of sympathy recede somewhat in
order to be apprehended all the more fully. It is therefore significant
only where the foundation of feeling is strong and deep. The emotional
element lies deeper
% --- p. 308 ---
\opage{308}than the intellectual; the latter has essentially the
significance of purifying, expanding and elevating.

\textbf{11.} We now return to the question of the possibility of
disinterested sympathy. It is clear from the foregoing that the word
interest may very well fit sympathy without its thereby becoming hidden
egoism. The race can as little be separated from the individual as the
individual from the race. In the instincts that underlie maternal
feeling and the feeling of sexual love there is precisely this wonderful
thing, that it is impossible to draw the line between what the race
wills and what the individual wills. However ideal a character sympathy
may assume, however exalted and comprehensive that may be to which man
attaches his pleasure, it is nevertheless just as much a part of him as
he is a part of it. The pleasure and pain he feels in it are his own
pleasure and pain; what other could he feel? But in ``disinterested''
love he does not feel it as his own in the sense that devotion to the
object should be only a means to so much the greater self-enjoyment.
Since he does not want to annihilate himself, the ``disinterested'' can
only mean that he immediately shares the pleasure and pain of others and
rests in it without demanding more for himself than precisely to be able
to be animated in this way.

It is impossible to love something or someone without feeling joy and
satisfaction in it. With every strong emotional movement there is
connected a peculiar pleasure, whatever the character of the feeling.
Even in sorrow there is, alongside the bitterness, a depth and a life in
the mood, a strong movement of the powers of the soul, which has
something captivating and alluring about it. All the sluices are opened,
as it were. It is the pleasure or impulse toward weeping and grief of
which Homer speaks. The strong emotion moreover brings with it a series
of organic reflexes, and with this discharge a certain comfort is
connected. It is thus in the spiritual and physical exaltation that the
ground of the attractiveness of
% footnote *) on p. 308 (continues on p. 309)
sorrow must be sought.\footnote{This explanation, which has been given
by W. \emph{Hamilton} and \emph{Bouillier}, seems to me the most
probable one of this remarkable phenomenon. (Cf.\ \emph{Bouillier}: Du
plaisir et de la douleur. Chap. VII.) It is more natural than the
explanation hinted at by \emph{Spencer} (Princ. of Psychol. II. p.~590
f.), according to which the need to immerse oneself in sorrow springs
from a \emph{feeling} of suffering undeservedly, so that sorrow, by an
effect of contrast, lets the consciousness of one's own worth come
vividly forward.}
% --- p. 309 ---
\opage{309}What can thus take place with the feeling of sorrow holds more
or less of all other feelings. Here, to be sure, lies the germ of an
egoistic turn even within the sympathetic feelings. There can be a
hysterical need to have one's feeling set in motion. Connected with this
is the fact that the sympathetic feeling is so often satisfied without
consideration and therefore without real benefit to the object of
sympathy. The mark of unselfish sympathy is in this as in other respects
the possibility of resignation. Pure and strong sympathy must even be
able to deny itself.

Sympathy can be put to yet another test. Even if we are absorbed in the
feeling for the object, we can still hold fast the wish that it should
be precisely we ourselves who work and live for it. Now if circumstances
turn out such that our service is a hindrance to the object's nature
and worth being properly unfolded, or such that others can work for it
better and more fully, then it depends on whether our sympathy is strong
enough for us to be able to step back and resign ourselves to its not
falling to us to further what we love.

Such resignation is possible and is the most glorious proof of the
purity of sympathy. It was so far with full right that \emph{Molinos}
and \emph{Fénelon} in theological form, as \emph{Spinoza} before them in
philosophical form, maintained as the highest a love independent of all
regard for self, of all reward and punishment. The Catholic Church
condemned this doctrine, most likely chiefly because it did not wish to
lose the means of education that regard for reward and punishment
affords. But psychology cannot admit that disinterested love is a
chimera; the chimerical begins only where the demand goes so far that
the life and movement of feeling are to vanish for an absolutely simple,
resting state. Such a state would, as we have often remarked, be one
with the cessation of conscious life itself.

\textbf{12.} Akin to the higher forms of sympathy is \emph{the ethical
feeling}. But only akin; it does not ---
% --- p. 310 ---
\opage{310}as \emph{Adam Smith} and \emph{Herbert Spencer} teach ---
wholly coincide with sympathy. We have already seen that sympathy, left
to itself, can work against itself, and that it must therefore undergo a
refining in order to reach its goal. That an action springs from
sympathy thus does not establish that it is ethical; it depends also on
whether it furthers the progress of life and happiness in the world. Not
sympathy alone, but the union of sympathy and wisdom is what leads to
right actions. The immediate feeling of love becomes, through union with
wisdom, the \emph{feeling of justice}, the concept of justice being taken
in the wide sense that both Greek and Christian ethics have developed,
namely as the ordering and distributing principle which lets every
benefit, every good imparted, flow to the right person, at the right
time and place and in the right measure. --- As sympathy undergoes this
metamorphosis, its intellectual elements are expanded. It now embraces
not only single individuals or circles of individuals, but bears within
it the idea of an organic whole of human personalities, each of which
lives its life out from a peculiar center, and each of which therefore
has a claim to a peculiar form and direction of sympathy. As the view is
thus widened, the individual feels himself only as one among many, as a
single member of a great realm developing through the ages. What his
impulse of self-preservation and his sympathetic impulse prompt him to
is finally subordinated to regard for what the progress of this realm
demands. This demand he perceives within himself as a law; and as this
law comes into greater or lesser contrast with what stirs immediately in
him in an egoistic or sympathetic direction, there arises the feeling of
duty, which is conditioned by man's feeling himself bound by an ideal
law. The feeling of duty need not, as \emph{Kant} thought, contain an
element of displeasure because the lower, sensuous promptings in our
nature are pushed back before the majesty of the ethical law. There can
be a relation, even a contrast, between a higher and a lower in us
without any real feeling of displeasure arising thereby. The feeling of
the truth and glory of the ideal can rouse our activity in such a way
that the hindrances only make us feel our powers all the more
definitely. But of course it is not always so.
% --- p. 311 ---
\opage{311}The hindrances can be so strong that the most painful
contrast and dividedness arises in the mind. Feeling then stands against
feeling, and the one feeling passes judgment on the other. In this
painful feeling of the contrast between the acknowledged ideal and the
imperfect reality \emph{the ethical} feeling of remorse expresses
itself, in which the individual turns in rejection against his own being
and his own actions. Very often remorse is the first form of the feeling
of duty, the birth-pangs of ethical character. Just as in the
development of knowledge an original sanguineness shows itself which
leads to hasty expectations and inferences and thereby to
disappointments, so in the domain of feeling too a blind craving can
\emph{sweep} man along, so that the corrective must be developed
afterwards with pain. The Greeks already had a sharp eye for how
infatuation is followed by remorse.\footnote{Cf.\ the allegory in the
9th book of the Iliad, v.~502--507.} The development of the ethical
feeling makes it possible to forestall remorse (the ethical
disappointment), in that scruple already asserts itself over against the
possible action, not only over against the action performed. In
\emph{conscience} there make themselves known both the judging power of
the ideal over what lies in the past and the ability to apply an ideal
test to future conduct. It is the ethical memory. In it the experiences
of the past fuse with everything that circumspect reflection teaches
into one collected power, which can express itself with the immediacy
and strength of instinct.

Not all remorse belongs to the ethical feeling. We have mentioned in
passing that remorse can be felt already at the standpoint of the mere
impulse of self-preservation (5). This will happen when we have acted
against our dominant selfish interest. Remorse acquires an ethical
character only when we feel ourselves in conflict with the demands of
sympathy; and the more deeply and inwardly we apprehend the ethical law,
the more we feel ourselves bound to it in our inmost being, the more
violently and penetratingly does remorse work in us.

The ethical feeling runs through a whole scale of forms of development.
Historically regarded, it finds its full explanation neither in the
impulse of self-preservation, nor in sympathy, nor in the influence of
knowledge on feeling. It has developed in the shelter
% --- p. 312 ---
\opage{312}of educating powers, authorities. These powers have been
educative partly with, partly against their will. They have often
pursued their own ends and have nevertheless worked toward the
development of ethical feeling by working on human nature through reward
and punishment, checking certain feelings and nourishing others. At the
lowest stage authority appears as overwhelming physical power; here it
instills fear and trembling, thus purely egoistic feelings. When, on the
other hand, at higher stages authority stands as the life-protecting and
life-furthering power, fear is mixed with sympathy and admiration and
thereby passes over into \emph{reverence}. From this there is only a step
to the ethical feeling proper, which presupposes a conviction
independent of external authority.\footnote{I have given a more
detailed investigation of the ethical feeling and its development in my
work »Om Grundlaget for den humane Etik« [On the Foundation of Humane
Ethics] (Kbhvn. 1876) and in two essays in the periodical »Vor Ungdom«
[Our Youth] for 1880.}

\emph{The religious feeling} is historically and psychologically closely
connected with the ethical. It too has at its lowest stage the character
of fear. That it is fear that from the first creates gods is confirmed by
the fact, on which all anthropologists seem agreed, that men worshiped
evil beings before good ones. Belief in gods is at the lowest stages one
with belief in immortality, being belief in the spirits of the dead, and
only those spirits which were considered able to work harm became
objects of worship. Religious worship is here a simple acknowledgment of
power. A higher stage is reached where the extraordinary, the
incomprehensible and the wonderful awaken the religious feeling; this
then acquires the character of admiration and reverence and begins to
become disinterested in its nature. The religious feeling acquires a
real ethical significance only when the great powers on which man feels
himself dependent stand before him as bearers of ethical ends and
values. This stage is reached in the higher national religions. The
religious feeling has its lasting source, unassailable by any
intellectual progress, in the great question of the connection of
ethical endeavors with, and their significance for, the development of
the world as a whole. All egoistic and personal stirrings of hope and
fear
% --- p. 313 ---
\opage{313}it can separate out, under the influence partly of insight
into the fixed, lawful order of nature, partly of the demands of ideal
ethics. But there then always remains the question of the relation
between the ethical ideals and the factual reality of the world. The
fact that everything we know and admire as true, beautiful and good has
developed under the conditions given in nature already gives the idea of
nature a religious character, and contains the motive for the idea of an
ethical world order, according to which the inmost kernel of reality,
the inmost force in the development of the world, is of the same nature
as that which works its way forward in human ideals. But since what is
ideal and valuable in the world of experience always appears as a
\emph{struggling} power, the religious feeling acquires the character of
faith and presentiment, and that idea stands --- theoretically regarded
--- as the last hypothesis or the last postulate, for whose validity no
other pledge can be had than precisely those ideal values whose real
grounding is postulated. Under what form the idea is expressed --- to
what speculations, symbols and dogmas it leads --- it is not the
business of psychology to investigate. Psychology has only to show how
the religious feeling, just as well as the ethical feeling and all other
ideal feelings, is determined in its development partly by the influence
of knowledge, partly by the contrast between egoism and sympathy.

The relation between the ethical and the religious feeling is marked by
the fact that in the former we feel our power of action spurred, in the
latter our dependence. When religiousness and ethics come into conflict,
it is thus the passive and the active pole of our nature that come into
contrast with each other. This conflict can arise both within the
consciousness of the single individual and within the race as a whole,
in that the life of feeling of some individuals leads them more in an
ethical, that of others more in a religious direction. But a definite
and absolute separation between passivity and activity, dependence and
power of action, is as little possible in the domain of feeling as in
that of knowledge. In our receiving we are always active, in our
activity receptive. The very powers we employ in our strongest exertion
of will we nevertheless feel as something given to us. We feel that a
nourishment streams to us from within, without which we can do nothing,
and that all our activity really serves only to help forward and unfold
% --- p. 314 ---
\opage{314}what is laid down in us by a quiet and unconscious growth. The
ethical feeling is religious through the element of devotion and
reverence which is inseparable from it, and the religious feeling is
ethical when it is more than egoistic superstition and sentimental
enthusiasm. It is the great merit of Kant and Fichte to have pointed to
this inner connection. They thereby had a purifying and ennobling effect
on both the religious and the ethical feeling, and have paved the way
for a more perfect harmony between the two.

\textbf{13.} The disinterested feelings presuppose that idea and fancy
are developed enough to hold fast the object of feeling as something
that has its independent value. Only thereby do unselfish love and
ethical and religious feeling come into being. But also apart from their
object and content, idea and fancy acquire significance for feeling. The
very activity of ideation and fancy can become a source of peculiar
feelings.

Knowledge is from the first the servant of impulse. Thoughts are the
scouts of the impulse of self-preservation. Knowledge is valued because
it is a means to power. At this stage no real \emph{intellectual
feeling} arises. Even where it is not external power or external goods
that are sought, but where knowledge is regarded as a means to spiritual
freedom and independence, the feeling awakened by the activity of
knowing is not purely intellectual. The intellectual feeling first comes
into being where the inner relation between the ideas itself becomes the
determining thing, quite apart from what inner or outer consequences
knowledge brings with it for us. The presupposition is that the struggle
for existence does not make its hard and inescapable demands.
Furthermore, such a host of ideas must have formed that they can in the
main arrange themselves according to their own laws, without the
immediate intervention of feelings and impulses. There then arises joy
in agreement, consistency and coherence, and a feeling of displeasure at
disharmony, contradiction and incoherence; and this pleasure or
displeasure is felt not merely because it is our standard of truth that
is upheld or violated, but because there is something immediately
satisfying or paining in the harmony or disharmony itself. The joy in
new facts and discoveries also belongs here. Even if they overturn
hitherto accepted assumptions
% --- p. 315 ---
\opage{315}and thereby produce unrest and \emph{doubt}, they nevertheless
open a view to more distant horizons than had hitherto been suspected.

Just as there are musical and poetic natures, so there are also
intellectual natures. For these, self-contradiction, unclearness and
lack of coherence are just as painful as false notes and mediocre verse
are for the former.

\emph{The aesthetic feeling} is in some of its forms akin to the
intellectual. Pleasure in symmetry and rhythm, in the form of phenomena
generally, finds its explanation in the easy and clear way in which the
observations arrange themselves. The powers of knowledge here work
without hindering resistance, subdue the material to themselves as in
play. Already the higher sense-sensations (colors and tones) awaken, as
we have seen, moods which detach themselves from the general feeling of
life. This is still more the case with combinations and groups of colors
and tones.

The feeling of beauty plays a part already within the animal world,
since colors, tones, scent and rhythmic movement are used as enticements
in \emph{sexual selection}. In man too the feeling of sexual love rouses
fancy to a freer and bolder life and opens the eye to colors and forms.
\emph{Plato} therefore called Eros the impulse toward the beautiful (8).
At this stage beauty is still only a means, just as truth is as long as
knowledge works in the service of the impulse of self-preservation. But
through the psychological process described earlier, what is originally
only a point of attachment for instinct can become an independent end.
This aesthetic development goes in part hand in hand with the general
development of sympathy. The two support each other; the capacity for
unselfish devotion is common to both.

In the desire to adorn oneself (with feathers, pearls and pieces of bone,
or by tattooing) an aesthetic feeling already expresses itself in the
lowest men. The next step is joy in weapons and other implements, apart
from their usefulness. They are worked with taste and adorned with
pictures. Thereby \emph{the means of} \emph{waging the struggle for
life} themselves become sources of the feeling of pleasure. And as it
goes with the implements, so it goes with their use. When the pressing
need is satisfied and one has rested after exertion,
% --- p. 316 ---
\opage{316}there arises an impulse to movement for the sake of movement
itself. The beast of prey plays when it is not tormented by hunger,
fatigue or dangers. The savage performs his war games and finds in the
violent movements an outlet for his regained energy. In play, which
comes forth in the use of ``the luxury of powers,'' \emph{Schiller}
(Ueber die ästhetische Erziehung des Menschen. 27ter Brief) saw the germ
of all art, a thought which \emph{Herbert Spencer} has sought to carry
through.

The aesthetic feeling has thus developed out of the impulses that lead
to the preservation of the individual and of the race. It presupposes a
\emph{surplus of energy} which is not used up in the struggle of life
and can therefore be employed in another way. But with this only the
material is given. The particular way in which it is employed depends on
intellectual development. For impressions to work aesthetically, there
must be a certain articulation of the sense-organs, such that finer
nuances are apprehended and not all sensations are absorbed into the
general feeling of life. A free play of colors, forms, tones and
movements must be able to unfold. But the direct impression is not
enough. For it to work aesthetically, it must not merely assert itself
by its strength, its kind and its form; its effect must also be able to
branch out into consciousness by awakening a wealth of ideas and moods
to clearer or dimmer appearance. In the aesthetic effect, therefore, a
distinction can be made between the immediate, directly working element
and the associations it
% footnote *) on p. 316
calls forth.\footnote{\emph{Fechner}: Vorschule der Aesthetik. Leipzig
1876. Kap.~9--13.} In music \emph{the direct factor} reigns, in poetry
\emph{the associative}; the visual arts stand between the two in this
respect. And yet music has a decidedly intellectual character; what
matters is not merely the strong, elementary impressions produced by
sound and rhythm, which are dominant in the childhood of the art, but
the contemplative enjoyment of the rising and falling, the conflict and
harmony, of tones. The art of tones rests on the ``unconscious
application of original ideas of magnitude and relation, a hidden
measuring and
% footnote **) on p. 316
counting.''\footnote{\emph{Hanslick}: Vom Musikalisch-Schönen. 6te
Aufl. p.~169.} The visual arts approach music the more they work by the
play of colors and the harmony of forms. But
% --- p. 317 ---
\opage{317}here the definite subject, the individual phenomena that are
to be represented, make definite demands. Their figures must be
recognizable and their situations understood. Associations of ideas and
historical memories are thus necessary here for the immediate
apprehension itself. Poetry, finally, works predominantly through the
ideas and feelings it awakens; the direct factor is only a means. It
gives what music cannot, the definite feeling with its full content of
thought and fancy; and it gives what the visual arts cannot, the
historical unfolding of characters and actions. --- In all its various
forms art gives access to the exercise and employment of powers that
were originally taken up by the struggle for existence. There therefore
exists the most intimate interaction between art and real life; the
ideal and the real working of the spiritual powers pass over into each
other, prepare each other mutually.

It accords well with the theory of the origin of art from the struggle
for existence that the sense for the beauty of art precedes the sense
for the beauty of nature. Art stands nearer to man than nature; the
former is his own work, which he cannot disown, while the latter can
long stand as a foreign, hostile or indifferent power. Children and
savages as a rule have no sense for the beauty of nature. Everything
connected with man and his pursuits interests them, but nature only in
so far as it serves the purposes of man. At the primitive, practical
standpoint a beautiful region is the same as a fruitful one, i.e.\ a
region fruitful in corn and grass. Peasants therefore wonder at the
tourists' desire to visit barren heaths, dune country and mountains. A
distinguished American traveler said to an Englishman: ``Your country is
very beautiful; in many parts one can walk whole miles without seeing a
tree outside the hedges.'' The sense for the wild, sublime and romantic
in nature is not much more than a hundred years
% footnote *) on p. 317
old.\footnote{Cf.\ the interesting essay on the sense of nature in
ancient and modern times in the 2nd volume of Friedländer's
»Sittengeschichte Roms«. --- To the objection that landscape painting
already in the 16th and 17th centuries represents mountain scenery with
sympathy and understanding, the author remarks that one cannot infer
from single artists to the whole of their contemporaries.}
\emph{Rousseau}
% --- p. 318 ---
\opage{318}was the one who awakened the sense of nature, and especially
the sense for wild, undisturbed nature, in wide circles. He discovered
the mountain landscape, which hitherto had awakened horror and dread in
most people. He taught men to turn their backs on human life and listen
to the language of nature. Joy in nature stands as the highest stage of
development of the aesthetic feeling and at the same time as one of the
best examples of disinterested sympathy.

Two special aesthetic feelings, the feeling of the sublime and the
feeling of the ludicrous, we shall later go into somewhat more closely,
since they will furnish good examples for illustrating the general
psychological laws of the life of feeling. The present work does not aim
at a description and classification of all feelings, but at an
investigation of the general psychology of feeling. Whoever wishes a
description of the particular feelings will find one in Bain's
»Emotions and Will«, in Nahlowsky's »Gefühlsleben«, in A. Horwicz's
»Psychologische Analysen« and in Sibbern's »Psykologiske Patologi«
[Psychological Pathology].


\section*{C. The Physiology and Biology of Feeling}\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{C. The Physiology and Biology of Feeling}

\textbf{1.} If it is true that it is only by abstraction that we
distinguish between knowledge and feeling, and that every concrete state
of consciousness consists of elements of knowledge and of feeling, there
will be no reason to expect that knowledge and feeling should be bound
up with the activity of different organs of the brain. The matter was
bound to look otherwise as long as one started from the idea of
different parts or faculties in the soul. \emph{Plato} accordingly
placed thought in the head, the feeling of courage and honor in the
breast, and the sensuous impulses in the abdomen. \emph{Aristotle}
referred all feeling of pleasure and displeasure (as well as all
sense-sensation) to the heart, whereas pure rational activity was in his
opinion not bound up with any physical function. In modern times
% footnote *) on p. 318
\emph{Descartes}\footnote{Les passions de l’âme. I, 33.} and his school
(\emph{Willis}, \emph{Malebranche}) referred feeling, with all other
phenomena of consciousness, to the brain, and so on this point, as on so
many others, were forerunners of modern physiology. But the old theory
of the contrast between brain and heart as
% --- p. 319 ---
\opage{319}a parallel to the contrast between understanding and feeling
did not let itself be so easily displaced. It relied on what immediate
experience seemed to teach. Every strong movement of feeling is bound up
with sensations in the breast or abdomen; the heart beats harder,
breathing is checked or intensified, digestion is modified; in short,
emotion reaches deep into the functions of vegetative life. As late as
% footnote *) on p. 319
\emph{Bichat}\footnote{Recherches physiologiques sur la vie et la
mort. Paris. An VIII. p.~71.} says: ``Everything seems to prove that
organic life is the goal in which the passions end and the center from
which they go out.'' The brain is in his opinion the seat of knowledge
and ``is never affected by the passions, whose only seat is the organs
of the inner life'' (namely, now the liver, now the lung, heart, spleen
or stomach). --- \emph{Charles Bell} and \emph{Gall} seem to have been
the first in our century to refer feeling to the brain, like the
understanding and the other phenomena of consciousness. When \emph{Gall}
thought he could point out different parts of the brain as organs for
the different special feelings (self-love, reverence, hope, benevolence,
etc.), this lacked all physiological foundation and all psychological
probability. As we have seen in an earlier section (II, 4), it has not
yet been possible to demonstrate with certainty any localization of
definite functions within the cerebrum, and even those who maintain such
a localization (like H.~\emph{Munk}) state expressly that it is only
sense-perception, not intelligence (the combining activity of ideas),
that is localized. Since the development of feeling is so closely bound
up with knowledge, there will be just as little reason to expect a
special localization for it as for knowledge. In the different forms of
feeling, as in the different forms of knowledge, the same brain cells
can be thought to work together, only in different degrees and in
different combinations. Since, according to what has gone before,
feeling seems to arise and unfold more slowly than knowledge, it must be
assumed that it is represented physiologically by a greater spread of
the nervous process in the mass of the brain.

Yet an elementary feeling can arise even when the higher organs of the
brain are wanting. A rat that has lost its
% --- p. 320 ---
\opage{320}cerebrum, optic thalami and corpora striata starts when one
imitates the cry of a cat, entirely in the same way as it would do in
an uninjured state. The elementary, instinctive feeling of fear (cf.\ B,
2 d) can thus, like the elementary sense-sensation, arise without the
function of the cerebrum. The same perhaps also holds of certain morbid
outbursts of feeling that stand in no connection with mental
% footnote *) on p. 320
influences.\footnote{Cf.\ \emph{Vulpian}: Physiologie du système
nerveux. Paris 1866. p.~549, where such elementary feelings are referred
to the pons Varolii (protubérance annulaire).}

But in any case there is this much justification in the old idea of
the connection of feeling with the vegetative organs, that these do in
reality play an important part in every feeling, even if they are not
its physiological seat. Feeling makes stronger claims on the nerve
centers than knowledge does, and the tension produced finds vent by
being propagated to more or fewer parts of the rest of the organism.
While the preponderance of the elements of knowledge shows itself in
this, that as much energy as possible is concentrated in the brain, so
that the rest of the organism is kept as quiet and passive \emph{as
possible}, the state determined by feeling has precisely a tendency to
spread. From the brain the movement propagates itself to the heart,
which in strong emotion may even stop altogether, so that death ensues.
Strong and sudden fright, anger, grief or joy can in this way have a
\emph{fatal} effect. If the movement is less strong, the heart, after a
short stoppage, begins to beat faster than before, and thereby sends a
more vigorous stream of blood up to the brain, which in this way
receives the recoil of its own movement. In warm-blooded animals this
reaction of heart upon brain is stronger than in cold-blooded ones, and
in higher animals than in lower. Man notices this reaction in the course
of a couple of seconds. --- Not only the heart, but other internal
organs too are affected by the discharge of the strong movement in the
brain. The more violent beating of the heart itself in fright and
anxiety is explained by some from the fact that the arteries suddenly
contract and thereby offer greater resistance to the stream of blood,
so that the heart works harder.
% --- p. 321 ---
\opage{321}The sudden pallor in fright proves at any rate that such a
contraction takes place. In other cases the muscles of the vessels
relax, so that a more abundant stream of blood is sent out (blushing).
Emotion can also affect the lachrymal glands (grief), the intestines
(fear), the liver (anger), the respiratory organs (fright), etc. Emotion
can further bring about a heightening or a loss of the irritability of
the motor centers (tetanus, paralysis, St.~Vitus's dance); but can
conversely also sometimes lead to
% footnote *) on p. 321
recovery.\footnote{\emph{Claude Bernard}: Étude sur la physiologie
du cœur. (Revue des deux mondes. 1865. Reprinted in ``La science
expérimentale''). --- \emph{Wundt}: Physiol. Psychol. II. p.~330. ---
\emph{Ranke}: Physiologie des Menschen. 3. Aufl. p.~399. --- C. Lange:
Rygmarvens Patologi [The Pathology of the Spinal Cord]. p.~255. 391 ff.
--- \emph{Darwin}: Expression of Emotions. passim.} --- We disregard
here the effect that feeling has on the voluntary movements, since that
is most naturally referred to the psychology of the will.

The reason why it is first and foremost the centers of the vegetative
processes that are affected by the strong tension produced in the state
of feeling must be sought in the fact that these centers (which are
situated in the medulla oblongata) are in constant activity as long as
life runs its course. They therefore possess the greatest degree of
susceptibility to impressions (the greatest irritability). Hence they
respond quickly and easily to every influence conveyed to them as a
consequence of the state of other centers.\footnote{Cf.\ A.
\emph{Freusberg}: Ueber die Erregung und Hemmung der Thätigkeit der
nervösen Centralorgane. (Pflüger’s Archiv für Physiol. 1875). p.~185.
--- See also \emph{Spencer}: Mindre Afhandlinger [Minor Essays]. Danish transl. p.~98 f.}

The movement of feeling thus reinforces itself by passing through a
circuit. Some have at times wished, especially from a spiritualistic
standpoint, to distinguish sharply between feeling proper and the
reaction from the internal organs. This latter moment certainly belongs
also to the general feeling of life, and, as remarked earlier (A, 3 a),
the state of those vegetative organs can call forth a feeling of a
character similar to that awakened by external experiences and mental
influences. But although the two stages in the development of a feeling
are separated by a small interval of time, there is
% --- p. 322 ---
\opage{322}nevertheless no reason to draw a sharp boundary between them
psychologically. They at once fuse together, and only both together make
up the feeling in its full peculiarity. On the other hand, it may be
useful in medical, pedagogical and moral respects to keep the two stages
apart. In different individuals they appear with different strength. In
some the discharge to the internal organs is relatively weak, even in
strong emotion; in others it is disproportionately strong. Often it
serves to relieve and lighten, and not to reinforce, what goes on in the
brain.

\textbf{2.} Still greater difficulties than the physiological
localization of feeling are presented by the question of the nature of
the organic process to which feeling corresponds. Since the nature of the
nervous process is unknown, nothing more than a hypothesis can be
reached here. Nevertheless it is of interest to bring up this question,
since light is thereby thrown on the significance of feeling in the
economy of life.

\emph{Aristotle} conceived the feeling of pleasure as bound up with
every natural and normal activity of life, and this conception is still
the most common and the most probable.\footnote{In \emph{Léon Dumont}:
Théorie scientifique de la sensibilité. Paris 1875. p.~26--63, a survey
of the different theories is to be found.} In feeling we have the
innermost state of the conscious individual, as it is determined by the
influences it receives and the activity it itself exerts. It is then
natural to see in the contrast between pleasure and displeasure --- the
fundamental contrast within the world of feeling --- an expression of
the contrast between advance or decline in the life-process itself. As
a main rule, then, it could be laid down that pleasure indicates
heightened activity of life, a higher and freer employment of energy.
The normal functioning of the various organs, brain and nervous system
as well as muscles and vegetative organs, is accompanied by the feeling
of pleasure. If, on the other hand, greater demands are made than an
organ can satisfy, or if, conversely, an organ does not get sufficient
employment for its energy, displeasure or pain is felt. Since all
function is bound up with the release of potential energy, the
dissolution of the stored-up organic capital,
% --- p. 323 ---
\opage{323}one and the same degree of activity may at different times be
bound up with pleasure and with pain, all according to the energy that
is at one's disposal.

A somewhat divergent conception has been put forward in the most recent
times by \emph{Fechner}. Since pleasure and displeasure are not only
quantitatively but qualitatively different from each other, they must,
according to Fechner, correspond to processes that differ not only in
degree but in form. He therefore assumes that pleasure rests on inner
agreement or harmony, displeasure on a disharmonious relation between
the moments of the physical process to which the life of consciousness
is bound.\footnote{Vorschule der Æsthetik. I. p.~12. 79. --- II.
p.~266.} --- It is, as has been said, impossible to decide anything on
this. Fechner's theory leads in essentials to the same result as the one
founded by Aristotle, inasmuch as it must be presupposed that the
harmony in question signifies an advance, the disharmony a decline, in
the organic respect. If disharmony gains complete preponderance, life
dissolves, just as when it is overwhelmed by impressions, overstrains
its powers, or is kept out of all activity. Pleasure thus stands in any
case as the expression of heightened life, pain as the expression of
decline and as a warning of death.

It is not meant, of course, that in pleasure and displeasure a
reflection or comparison asserts itself as to how far things are going
forward or backward with us. Such reflection is in any case impossible
in the simplest forms of feeling. Originally pleasure and displeasure
announce themselves of their own accord, and only then, provided that
knowledge is sufficiently developed, can one brood over their
significance.

\textbf{3.} The theory stated is confirmed by the fact that impressions
that cause displeasure and pain are as a rule also harmful. Of pain from
blows and wounds, where the organism is downright mutilated, this is
self-evident. Likewise with fatigue and with overwhelming
sense-impressions. Bitter substances have a tendency to dissolve organic
tissue; the agreeableness of sweet taste finds its explanation in the
presence of sugar in most of the vegetable constituents of human
food.\footnote{\emph{Grant Allen}: Physiological Æsthetics. London 1877.
p.~69 ff.}

% --- p. 324 ---
\opage{324}On the other hand it might seem a serious objection to the
Aristotelian theory that pleasure is sometimes bound up with what is
harmful, displeasure with what is beneficial. This objection leads to a
more precise statement of the theory. It is only the \emph{partial} and
\emph{momentary} influence of the impression or activity that expresses
itself in the feeling of pleasure or displeasure. A palatable poison
brings about a momentary advance in one part of our organic life. Later,
when it spreads in the organism, it unfortunately displays other
properties, by which it threatens life. But the feeling of pleasure in
the taste was not therefore a deception; a thermometer does not show us
the degree of heat that will be present in a few hours, but the one that
is here already.\footnote{Cf.\ \emph{Lotze}: Medicinische Psychologie.
p.~237 ff.}

The general rule of pleasure as a sign of advance, displeasure as a sign
of decline, is hereby essentially restricted. We are sent from
\emph{Aristippus} to \emph{Epicurus}: it is no longer by the pleasure or
displeasure of the moment that life can be judged, but by the duration
and final victory of pleasure. But it must still be required of the
theory that it be able to explain why the pleasure or displeasure of the
moment is no sure criterion. It is no satisfactory answer to say with
Lotze that ``the purposiveness (teleology) of nature extends, not
necessarily, but only in some fortunate cases, to wholly unaccustomed
events or morbidly perverted states.'' The question is: \emph{why} then
does purposiveness not extend further? It cannot, after all, be from a
mere whim. And with this there is then naturally connected the question
why purposiveness is present at all, so far as it is.

This is one of the points where the hypothesis of evolution brings in a
clear light where before there was utter
darkness.\footnote{Cf.\ \emph{Spencer}: Principles of Psychology. Part
II. Chap. 9.} We do not understand feeling as long as we keep it
completely isolated from the will; it has also appeared in what has gone
before that feeling and impulse cannot be separated. The feeling of
pleasure leads to a striving to hold fast and appropriate what awakens
pleasure; displeasure and pain lead to withdrawing from and defending
oneself against what is their cause. This rule holds both at the
standpoint of instinct and at that of conscious will. The difference
% --- p. 325 ---
\opage{325}concerns only the nature and cause of the pleasure and the
pain. Now if a being were so organized that it felt pleasure in
everything that was harmful to it and displeasure in everything that was
beneficial to it, it would not be able to live. Natural selection itself
therefore leads to a certain harmony between the feelings and the
conditions of life. But that this harmony is not complete follows of
itself. To wholly foreign or very rare conditions the organization
cannot be \emph{adapted}. Pleasure in what is harmful is thus a sign of
an imperfection in development, which will perhaps gradually be
remedied. Such imperfections will, by the nature of the case, occur
especially, on the one hand, with a sudden change in the conditions of
life, on the other, with the transition to very complex and many-sided
conditions. Both apply to man. His life of feeling is not yet adapted to
the tasks and demands of social life. Civilization is barely a few
thousand years old where it is oldest, and before it there lie perhaps
myriads of years in which animal and barbarous impulses held sway. It is
then no wonder that pleasure and pain cannot without further ado count
as sure guides, and that it has even been possible to maintain the
exact opposite of the main rule stated and to regard pleasure as a
danger and misfortune, pain as beneficial. The single individual already
often has the experience that pain has a ``secret formative power''; in
education pain is regarded as a good in so far as it warns and checks.
In the development of the race, too, pain stands as the warning voice,
though it is only the ascetic cast of thought that ascribes value to it
in and for itself. Just as feeling is brought, through the struggle for
existence, into a certain harmony with the conditions of life, so too it
is the struggle for existence that teaches that we cannot rely on the
feeling of the moment, but must seek a higher standard.

The general rule that pleasure indicates the advance of life,
displeasure the decline of life, fits the higher, ideal feelings just as
well as the lower ones. In sympathy and in the ethico-religious feelings
the individual feels himself as part of a greater whole, as member of an
organism whose pulse-beat he senses within himself. He does not separate
his cause from that of the more
% --- p. 326 ---
\opage{326}comprehensive organism. What furthers its life thereby
furthers his too.

\section*{D. The Validity of the Law of Relation for the Feelings}\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{D. The Validity of the Law of Relation for the Feelings}

\textbf{1.} If the distinction we make between knowledge and feeling is
correct, there must be certain laws that belong only to the one kind of
psychical elements, not to the other. This too has proved to be so,
inasmuch as within the domain of the feelings we find nothing
corresponding to the laws of the association of ideas. A feeling can
enter into connection with one or more ideas; but a direct association
of feelings there hardly is. --- On the other hand, both knowledge and
feeling are elements in every one of the states of the life of
consciousness; we must then expect that there are laws common to both
kinds of elements, because they spring from the general nature of the
life of consciousness. This too we find confirmed, inasmuch as the law
of relation proves itself a fundamental psychological law by holding
also in the domain of the feelings. Here it even comes forward in a more
striking way.

At about the same time as \emph{Hobbes} had stated the law of relation
as a psychological principle (see II, 5), \emph{Spinoza} demonstrated
its validity as regards feeling. We feel, as he expresses it, pleasure
in an advance in perfection or energy, displeasure in a decline; but it
is not perfection in and for itself that awakens pleasure; for if man
were born with it, he would feel no pleasure in it. Likewise with
imperfection; only he who knows perfection is saddened by it. This is,
as one sees, Fechner's law (IV A, 4) applied to feeling. As his
predecessors in this domain \emph{Fechner} himself cites
\emph{Bernouilli} and Laplace. These mathematicians taught that the
value of a thing cannot be determined purely objectively, but becomes
different according to the relation of things to the interests of each
single individual. A distinction must be made between \textit{fortune
physique} and \textit{fortune morale}. In physical fortune one
disregards the circumstances of the individual to whose lot it falls. A
sum of 1000 kroner signifies an equally great physical fortune for all.
But for him who already owns a million kroner, the
% --- p. 327 ---
\opage{327}mental fortune (i.e.\ the fortune that is really felt) from
that increase becomes small in relation to what it is for him who owns
nothing, or who is even in a debt that drives him to despair.
Psychologically regarded it is only this ``moral fortune'' that we have
to do with; that a fortune is merely ``physical'' can signify nothing
else than that no real feeling of pleasure is awakened.
% TR: "fortune physique" / "fortune morale" (Bernoulli, Laplace);
% Høffding's "Lykke" is rendered "fortune" throughout this passage.

\textbf{2.} Just as we distinguish between a color in and for itself
and its particular shades, which are in part determined by its relation
to other color effects, so pleasure and pain too stand before us as
fixed forms, although it is only through their contrast to each other
that they are what they are. Every individual has, in the fundamental
mood that governs his life, a practical standard, a level, above which
his feelings rise only in single moments, and below which they fall
only by exception. When the movements of feeling rise high above this
``standard of life'' or sink deep beneath it, there arise the so-called
emotions or \emph{affects}.

A feeling comes forth in its full strength only when it stands in
complete contrast to another feeling. That we do not always notice the
part contrast plays in our feelings --- a part that is far greater than
in sensations and ideas, because the life of feeling is through and
through stamped by the great contrast between pleasure and displeasure
--- is surely to be explained by the fact that we as a rule turn toward
the new feeling, which gets its strength from the contrast with the
vanishing feeling. Over the victor we forget the vanquished. Precisely
because preceding pain gives joy greater life, the former is easily
left altogether out of
% footnote *) on p. 327
account\footnote{Cf.\ John XVI, 21: ``A woman when she is in travail
hath sorrow, because her hour is come: but as soon as she is delivered
of the child, she remembereth no more the anguish, for joy that a man is
born into the world.''}; this also happens where it is not pain proper,
but only a lesser degree of satisfaction, that forms the background of
the new movement of feeling.

Just as contrasting colors not only set each other off but also easily
pass over into each other, so a feeling also often prepares the way for
its opposite. The transition from
% --- p. 328 ---
\opage{328}a strong feeling to its opposite takes place more easily than
the transition from indifference to strong feeling. In the first case
the source has, so to speak, been opened\footnote{Cf.\ the first series
of observations in Ludv. Feilberg's book ``Om størst Udbytte af
Sjæleevner'' [On the Greatest Yield from the Powers of the Mind].}, and
it is only a matter of the stream being led in another direction; in the
latter case the living force must first be released. Often a feeling
can be developed only by roundabout ways; thus piety and obedience
toward authority as a rule precede the ethical feeling proper in its
purity and independence. Even the great contrasts of feeling (pleasure
--- pain, love --- hatred, hope --- fear, reverence --- contempt)
prepare the way for each other. Satiety with the one member of the
contrast begets a disposition to experience the other, especially at a
stage of development where man immediately follows the promptings of the
moment, or in strong nervous exaltation. (Cf.\ the famous
``Himmelhoch jauchzend --- zum Tode betrübt''). Thus there often comes,
in the course of development of a mental illness, a point where extreme
feelings of misfortune, disappointment and being misunderstood turn over
into excessive joy in imagined loftiness and
% footnote **) on p. 328
glory.\footnote{Examples in \emph{Ideler}: Biographien
Geisteskranker. Berlin 1841.} That ``les extrêmes se touchent'' is seen
nowhere better than in the domain of the life of feeling, where the
sharpest and most important contrasts are at home. There are natures
that do not get peace of mind until passion has raged itself out. The
voice of conscience first becomes audible in many as a contrast to a
violent stirring of wild impulses; temptation must thus, strange as it
sounds, become very great for it to be possible to overcome it. Often
conscience first awakens when a crime has been committed, and then drives
to immediate self-denunciation.\footnote{\emph{Bischoff}: Merkwürdige
Criminalrechtsfälle. 2ter Bd. Hannover 1835. p.~43 f.}

The physiological basis for this peculiarity of the feelings must be
sought in the conditions of life of the nervous system. The energy of
the nerve organs is limited; when it is exhausted by lasting influence,
they demand either rest or influence of another kind. That is why pains
are intermittent; even when the cause continues to act, there still
comes a point where the capacity to suffer is for the time being
exhausted, and there then comes a
% --- p. 329 ---
\opage{329}period of rest in which strength is gathered for new
% footnote *) on p. 329
suffering.\footnote{Ch.~\emph{Richet}: Recherches expérimentales et
cliniques sur la sensibilité. Paris 1877. p.~303--307.} Mental suffering
and joy, too, express themselves in this rhythmical fashion; strong
outbursts are followed by calmer moods, which can then again pass over
into momentary affects. \emph{Herbert Spencer} has drawn attention to
the fact that the expressions of the movements of feeling in dance,
poetry and music have a rhythmical
% footnote **) on p. 329
character.\footnote{First Principles. Part. II. Chap. 10: The rhythm
of motion. \S~86. --- When hysterical patients alternately laugh and
weep with violence, and when little children likewise pass suddenly
from the one state to the other, this is, as \emph{Darwin} (Expression
of Emotions. p.~208) remarks, explicable by the great similarity between
convulsive weeping and convulsive laughter.}

Those feelings of pleasure and displeasure are the most violent that are
bound up with the most intermittent organic functions. The feelings
bound up with the preservation of the individual and of the race can
have the greatest degree of violence, because the deep-lying organic
conditions of life on which they rest are subject to a natural rhythm.
The special sense-functions (sight and hearing especially) go on more
continuously and are therefore not subject to such great contrasts.
(Cf.\ also A, 3 e). --- For the rest, it must be remembered that a
feeling can be very strong without being violent (IV, 7 a).

As finite beings we have only a limited capacity for pleasure as well
as for pain. The profound idea of the ancients of the envy of the gods
has a real basis here. Happiness leads by its own excess to
unhappiness, when it consumes the potential energy of our nature. But
in virtue of the same law of contrast we must set over against that idea
the idea of the compassion of the gods, since pain too consumes itself.

This alternating play, which heightens the feelings of pleasure but
also threatens their continuance, and indeed seems able to dissolve the
life of the spirit into conflicting forces, has often been regarded as
an imperfection, and the human spirit has, in contrast to it, formed an
image of an ideal state in which the full feeling of blessedness is not
broken by contrasts or changes. Even \emph{Spinoza}, who, as long as he
speaks as a
% --- p. 330 ---
\opage{330}psychologist, shows so full and correct an understanding of
the law of relation, passes over at the end of his \textit{Ethics} into
a mystical speculation that abolishes all contrast, all change and
transition. But it has never been possible to give this ideal state a
definite positive content. Such a thought and its influence is
psychologically intelligible only through its contrast with the
sufferings of the present life. Its very genesis thus confirms the law
of relation.

\textbf{3.} It has been mentioned earlier (VI A, 2 and B, 2 d) that
feeling arises more slowly than knowledge. It is only with very strong
and sudden impressions that this difference in time falls away. But the
state that arises in such moments is characterized by an indissoluble
fusion of elements of knowledge and of feeling. Such simple ``shocks''
as the elementary sensations from which, according to a hypothesis
stated earlier (V A, 2), all sense-sensations arise, can with equal
right be counted as feeling and as knowledge. Something similar holds at
a higher stage of all wonder and surprise, of every impression of the
new and extraordinary. As a consequence of the strong contrast with the
preceding state or with the rest of the content of our consciousness,
our own new state produced by the influence plays at least as great a
part as the content of the new observation or idea. This is, in the
psychological respect, what is peculiar to all spiritual awakening.
Owing to the fresh current of feeling, the new knowledge stands with an
extraordinary clearness and in a particularly alluring light. That is
why the ancients taught that wonder was the beginning of wisdom. But
also only the beginning. For from this border phenomenon different roads
may be taken. Either the element of feeling gains the upper hand to
such a degree that unrest and enthusiasm make clear appropriation and
deeper penetration impossible, or else it acts as an inner animating
force and leads to many-sided and steady work in the service of the new
idea.

In some cases wonder passes over into fear, disappointment and contempt,
or into love and reverence, all according to the nature of what has
awakened the wonder. Wonder thus stands as an introduction to very
different psychological processes, and it was to that extent a correct
thought that lay at the bottom of it
% --- p. 331 ---
\opage{331}when \emph{Descartes} and \emph{Malebranche}, in their
presentation of the feelings, let wonder open the series as a
presupposition of each of them. Yet it is more natural, with Bain, to
form a separate class of feelings, containing wonder and other
``feelings of relation'' (emotions of relativity), where the moment of
relation and contrast that asserts itself in all our states of
consciousness determines not only the strength and quality of the
feeling, but its whole nature and content.

\textbf{4.} It is a necessary consequence of the law of relation that
frequent repetition weakens the freshness and strength of feeling. The
background against which it originally unfolded so vividly and
energetically necessarily becomes more and more indistinct; a
distribution of light and shadow sets in, so that the contrast gradually
falls away. This process is only a form of the general adaptation that
belongs to all life. Under all conditions the living strives to come
into equilibrium with its conditions. For feeling it often has the
effect that enthusiasm is succeeded by indifference and slackness and at
last stands as an inexplicable memory. From this the pessimists fetch
one of their most dangerous arguments. It is true that for sorrow and
pain as well as for joy and inspiration adaptation has a damping effect;
but it is not always that we therefore praise ``the healing hand of
time''. (Cf.\ B, 11).

S.~\emph{Kierkegaard} has taken this psychological law as his starting
point for showing the boundary between the aesthetic and the ethical
conduct of life. Every awakening and enthusiasm is of an aesthetic kind;
we are in an attitude of enjoyment as we are seized by the strong
influence. The ego lets itself be carried away by the waves of feeling
that break forth involuntarily. But in daily work, under the depressing
and damping influence of repetition, it must be shown whether the
feeling possesses any other strength than that momentary flaring up.
That is why the possibility of repetition stands for S.~Kierkegaard as
the fundamental ethical
% footnote *) on p. 331 (continues on p. 332)
problem.\footnote{``He who will only hope is cowardly; he who will only
recollect is voluptuous; but he who wills repetition is a man\ldots.
When one has sailed round existence, it shall be seen whether one has
the courage to understand that life is a repetition, and the desire to
rejoice in it.'' --- Gjentagelsen. Et Forsøg i den experimenterende
Psykologi. Af Constantin Constantius [Repetition. An Essay in
Experimenting Psychology. By Constantin Constantius]. Kbhvn. 1843. p.~5.
--- From another side the problem of repetition comes forward later in
S.~Kierkegaard, namely in his polemic against official Christianity as
``an established order that has forgotten its origin''. (See
``Indøvelse i Kristendom'' [Practice in Christianity], the newspaper
articles, and ``Øjeblikket'' [The Moment]).}

% --- p. 332 ---
\opage{332}But while Kierkegaard poses the problem in a way that betrays
his brilliant psychological eye, it is striking to see with what haste
he turns his back on psychology. The problem, according to his
assertion, cannot be solved with the help of psychology. ``Repetition''
--- he says (p.~92) --- ``is a transcendence'', which can hardly be
understood otherwise than that it lies beyond the naturally human. He
has overlooked that there is a psychological law of nature on which the
ethical demand can rely --- as indeed ethics, if it is not to hover in
the air or be a constant appeal to the supernatural, must build on what
is psychologically possible.

For it is only in so far as feeling is regarded as a purely passive
state that it is dulled by repetition and habit. Active movements and
skills, on the other hand, are perfected by repetition; here habit
becomes practice. To the active side of our nature belongs not only the
capacity for muscular movement, but also observation and thinking,
attention and will. Feeling is damped by repetition precisely because
the practiced movement can gradually take place
unconsciously.\footnote{Already \emph{Hume} (Treatise II, 3, 1) and
\emph{Bichat} (La vie et la mort. p.~47--56) have insisted on the
different effect of repetition on knowledge and activity on the one
hand, and on feeling on the other. --- Of later writers \emph{Fries}
(Neue Kritik der Vernunft. \S~36) treats this matter in a very able
way.} To that extent, then, a decisive contrast would here be
established between the conditions of the feelings and those of the
ideas. But now the section immediately preceding (B) has taught us how
the development of knowledge benefits feeling. The purely elementary
feelings, i.e.\ those called forth by single, definite sensuous
impressions, cannot gain by repetition (any more than the simplest,
immediate sense-sensations can). The ideal feelings, on the other hand,
i.e.\ those bound up with and determined by a greater or smaller circle
of ideas, can not only preserve their strength (if not their violence),
% --- p. 333 ---
\opage{333}but also gain by repetition. The same sum of energy that is
released in the moment of awakening can also be released later, only
distributed, so to speak, into several streams, no longer in a
concentrated state. Feeling then gains in manifoldness and inwardness
under repetition what it loses in freshness. In the course of adaptation
the object of feeling discloses its nature and presents itself under
different points of view, and on the other hand the different elements
in the individual's own nature are brought into interaction with the
object. Within the fundamental relation, then, a manifold of changing
relations may be found. Feeling is thereby spread over an ever greater
domain of life and can be fed from many more sources than at first. So
it goes, e.g.\ in our relation to a person with whom we live. The inner
growth that the feeling has undergone often first becomes clear when the
relation is put to the test; there may then again take place a
conversion from the distributed to the concentrated form, by which it
appears that the capital invested has borne interest.

From Goethe's ``Briefe aus der Schweiz'' a fine example is given here
to illustrate the influence of repetition on feeling. --- The subject is
the sublime impressions of a mountain journey in Switzerland. --- ``A
young man whom we took with us from Basel said that he did not have the
same feeling as the first time, and gave the credit to novelty. I would,
however, say to this: when we see such an object for the first time, the
unaccustomed soul first expands, and this brings about a painful joy, an
overflowing fullness, which moves the soul and draws voluptuous tears
from us. Through this operation the soul becomes greater in itself
without knowing it, and is no longer able to have that first feeling.
Man believes he has lost, but he has gained: what he loses in delight,
he gains in inner growth.''

Much, then, is inevitably lost that cannot be regained. It is with the
budding feeling as with the first breath of the newborn, by which the
lung is first distended in such a way that it is never afterwards quite
emptied; no later breath, then, can be like the first. To that extent
repetition is impossible. But pessimism \emph{wrongly} regards this as a
mere loss; seen from another side it is a great
% --- p. 334 ---
\opage{334}gain. It depends on each individual's view of life from which
side he chooses to see the matter.

\textbf{5.} This contrast between the influence of repetition and
adaptation on the passive and on the active side of feeling leads us to
bring up the contrast that the older psychology (especially after
Kant's excellent presentation in ``Anthropologie'' \S~71) set up between
\emph{affect} and passion. By affect or emotion is then understood a
sudden surging up of feeling, which for a while overwhelms the mind and
checks the free and natural connection of the elements of knowledge.
Passion, on the other hand, is the movement of feeling that has become
nature, that is ingrained by habit. In passion the element of impulse in
feeling comes especially to the fore, so that it stands at the
transition between feeling and will (cf.\ IV, 7 d and VI B, 2 c). What
affect is in the single moment, with violent, expansive movement,
passion is in the depths of the mind as a saved-up sum of force that
lies ready for use. That is why passion does not exclude sober
deliberation; on the contrary, it finds its expression in a thought that
governs the whole circle of ideas. ``Affect'', says \emph{Kant}, ``acts
like water that breaks through a dam, passion like a stream that digs
itself ever deeper into its bed. Affect is like an intoxication that can
be slept off, passion like a madness that broods over a thought that
strikes ever deeper root.''

Feeling thus begins as affect and --- if it finds constant nourishment
--- passes over into passion. Anger and grief are affects, vindictiveness
and melancholy are passions. The deepest and most central current in a
man's being is his ruling passion, which is conditioned and prepared by
his innate nature, and fed, developed and clarified by all movements of
feeling and experiences. It can grow both by satisfaction and by
disappointment. Just as the affects pave the way for passion, so passion
finds vent in violent outbursts and thus leads to affect; but it can
also find its satisfaction in a well-considered and calm fashion.

By the way in which a passion can be satisfied, it can awaken affects of
another kind than itself. Thus when love of the fatherland in the moment
of danger awakens
% --- p. 335 ---
\opage{335}warlike courage and the lust for battle. Or when, in carrying
out a plan of murder that springs from cold self-interest, cannibal
impulses are awakened, so that the murderer maltreats his victim in a
way that is not necessary for his purpose.\footnote{Cf.\ \emph{Anselm
von Feuerbach}: Aktenmässige Darstellung merkwürdiger Verbrechen.
Giessen 1828. I. p.~93: ``It is therefore not to be regarded as a lying
invention and an empty evasion when murderers in their confessions tell
how, in the execution, they were seized by rage or savagery, were
deprived of all presence of mind and carried away by an irresistible
power, so that they no longer knew what they willed or did\ldots.
Everything that happens in the execution of the crime cannot therefore
be derived from the main motive.''}

The word passion is taken in this connection in a narrower sense than
in ordinary usage, which does not make this definite distinction
between affect and passion. At the same time we here keep every moral
valuation away from the concept of affect and passion. There are good
passions or affects as well as bad ones; but both kinds have the same
general psychological characteristics, just as nourishing and poisonous
plants can belong to the same family. Everything that is to be able to
assert itself as a power in us must express itself as affect and
passion. Pure reason is powerless over against the actual life of the
soul, where it is always feelings that struggle with one another. It is
therefore psychologically incorrect when people so often speak of the
struggle of reason with the passions. Directly, such a struggle cannot
take place at all. A thought can displace a feeling only by awakening
another feeling that is able to push the former aside. Among modern
psychologists \emph{Spinoza} and Hume especially have insisted on this,
whereas it is overlooked by Kant (in
% footnote **) on p. 335
his systematic writings)\footnote{Kant's psychology, as is well known,
does not quite agree with his ethics. Thus he says in his Anthropology
(\S~70): ``Die durch die Vernunft des Subjects \emph{schwer oder} gar
\emph{nicht} bezwingliche Neigung ist Leidenschaft''. But according to
Kant's moral principle there is an absolute imperative and an
\emph{unconditional capacity} to obey reason.} and still more by his
followers. \emph{Greek} psychology was even inclined to regard the
matter from the entirely opposite side. When it is said that passion and
reason struggle with each other, it ought, according
% --- p. 336 ---
\opage{336}to \emph{Plato} (Protagoras. Heise's transl. p.~196 ff.),
properly to be said that thoughts struggle with thoughts: passion is at
bottom a false knowledge, which is displaced by the true. This
consistent elimination of the element of feeling forms an illuminating
contrast to the conception that results from many-sided observation.

Repetition thus has a different influence on affect and on passion; the
former is weakened, the latter nourished. And this is to be derived from
the fact that passion is active in its nature and more closely bound up
with definite and distinct ideas than affect is.

\textbf{6.} The law of relation gives each single feeling its fixed,
definite form and makes it possible for the one feeling to pave the way
for, and become of significance for, the other. But just as a
superficial conception of the law of relation in the intellectual domain
draws the conclusion that everything is equally true or untrue, so in
the domain of feeling the conclusion has been drawn that all feeling of
pleasure is an illusion, because it exists only through its contrast
with the feeling of displeasure. On this assertion \emph{Schopenhauer}
has built his pessimistic philosophy. At the bottom of all consciousness
there lies, according to his theory, a blind but unbridled will or
impulse, which clings to life and brings conscious beings to preserve
their existence and propagate it further. Everything that lives and
feels wills first and foremost to live. This ``will to live'' is not
motivated by life's being a good, by its causing pleasure and joy; the
relation is the reverse, that we fancy that life brings happiness
because we are constantly led by an obscure power to cling to
% footnote *) on p. 336
it.\footnote{Schopenhauer's forerunner, the Italian poet
\emph{Leopardi}, expresses the same thought: ``All men want to live;
therefore they must find life beautiful and pleasant; they want to find
it beautiful and are therefore angry with the disturber of the peace who
allows himself to think otherwise.'' (Opuscules et Pensées. Trad. de
l’italien par A. Dapples. Paris 1880. p.~114). The theory that all
pleasure rests on the relief of a want was held in antiquity in part by
\emph{Epicurus}, in modern times especially by Italian thinkers
(\emph{Cardanus}, \emph{Verri}), from whom \emph{Kant} took it over
(Anthropol.\ \S~57), without, however, drawing the consequences that
\emph{Schopenhauer} later drew.} So-called pleasure arises through the
satisfaction of a want; the want, bound up with displeasure, is the
positive thing; pleasure is
% --- p. 337 ---
\opage{337}on the other hand something negative, and signifies only the
