cessation of the want. Life's greatest goods, health, youth and freedom,
we therefore do not prize as long as we possess them; only when they
have vanished does their value dawn on us.

Schopenhauer's theory conflicts entirely with the biological
significance of feeling. If there really existed such an unquenchable
ardor, cooling only in single moments to flare up the more strongly
afterwards, it would soon consume itself. Pain proves to be throughout
bound up with what harms life or threatens its existence. Therefore pain
and displeasure cannot be the first, the positive thing, which is
checked and broken by experiences in the struggle of life. If we want to
call the tendency to movement that is present already before the
awakening of consciousness impulse or will, then in itself it cannot
lead to displeasure or pain. These can arise only when this impulse
meets a greater resistance than it can overcome. To the normal
performance of the organic functions there is bound a happy fundamental
mood, a feeling of lightness and freedom, to which we certainly only
seldom direct our attention directly, and which as a rule we notice only
when it succeeds or is succeeded by indisposition. But health, youth and
freedom are goods precisely in that they make possible the full
development and employment of our powers. A morbid feeling of life soon
paralyzes all our faculties; mental illness as a rule begins with
disturbance and morbidity in the feeling of life. Its significance and
positive value rests not on its pushing itself into the foreground, but
on what it carries and conditions.

The Schopenhauerian theory fits best the feelings that are immediately
bound up with the preservation of the individual and the species. The
theory might seem to fit best the very earliest period of life; at
least an unbiased observer has also come to the result that in the first
half-year of human life the feelings of displeasure are the
preponderant ones, and that the feelings of pleasure in this period
arise predominantly through the removal of states of displeasure, not
through the production of positive states of
% footnote *) on p. 337
pleasure.\footnote{\emph{Preyer}: Die Seele des Kindes. p.~91. 93.}
But on the other hand Schopenhauer teaches precisely that the feeling of
displeasure
% --- p. 338 ---
\opage{338}at existing first really awakens when consciousness has
developed. If that were so, we should expect the first period to be
filled with the feeling of pleasure, and not predominantly with
displeasure.

Schopenhauer denies the whole psychological process by which the
development of knowledge also benefits feeling. He is, it is true,
inconsistent enough to assume ideal and disinterested feelings (joy in
art and science, sympathy, especially compassion); but he cannot explain
the difference between them and the feelings bound up with purely
physical impulses. In spite of all his thoughts and ideas, man is still
just as brutal as the animal, strives after the same thing as it ---
only the animal reaches its goal far more easily and
% footnote *) on p. 338
with less torment and pain.\footnote{``Am \emph{Ende und im Realen}
handelt es sich nur um \emph{dieselben Dinge}, die auch das Thier
erlangt, und zwar mit unvergleichlich geringerem Aufwande von Affekten
und Quaalen.'' Parerga und Paralipomena. Berlin 1851. II. p.~260.} ---
In this respect Schopenhauer stands as a decided opponent of the
hypothesis of evolution, not quite without justification over against
optimistic exaggerations of ``wie wir's so herrlich weit gebracht''.
That the brutal instincts of self-preservation (Schiller's ``Hunger und
Liebe'') still in great part govern life cannot be denied; but the
hypothesis of evolution can also very well admit this without giving up
its main teaching. (Cf.\ C, 3). It is one thing that a development
really takes place; another, how much has already been accomplished at
the stage at which we now find ourselves. It would be unthinkable if the
development of ideas and thoughts, which is after all admitted by all,
should have had no influence whatever on the life of feeling. It is in
any case a significant advance that those impulses are now compelled to
prove their justification by being attached to more or less ideal and
universal ends (in the family, the state, etc.).

It is a misunderstanding of the law of relation to assume that pleasure
always needs pain as its background. It comes forward most strikingly
when it succeeds pain; but it can quite well have a weaker feeling of
pleasure as its background, and this holds especially wherever the
impulse of self-preservation does not act immediately. The struggle for
a higher and nobler human existence therefore aims at pushing back the
immediate
% --- p. 339 ---
\opage{339}instinct of self-preservation and opening as wide an access
as possible to the goods that are more than the relief of the bare want
to live.

\textbf{7.} It is in itself a meaningless usage to call a pleasure or
displeasure negative. Every feeling as such is a real, and hence
positive, state. Even ``illusory'' or ``imagined'' joy is real joy. The
hypochondriac really feels displeasure and will not let it be argued
away. What is meant by expressions like positive and negative, true and
untrue, can in this connection only be the reality or non-reality of
the object of the feeling. One cannot criticize the feelings without
criticizing their causes and their objects.

Just as people have spoken of positive and negative feelings of
pleasure, so it has also been held that there is a zero point that
signifies indifference, unconcern, a point, that is, where neither
pleasure nor displeasure is felt. It is a big question whether such
neutral states really exist. A purely theoretical consideration might
certainly lead to the assumption that on the line leading from the
highest pleasure to the strongest pain there must be a midpoint lying
equally far from both extremes. But this theoretical midpoint cannot be
the expression of an actual state of consciousness. If we come to it
from the side of pain, it will look like pleasure; from the side of
pleasure, on the other hand, it will appear as displeasure --- both so
long as no adaptation has taken place. This is a simple consequence of
the law of relation.

Immediate experience is difficult to interpret here, because the law of
relation itself brings it about that we declare certain states
indifferent when we look back on them in very agitated moments. Yet
those psychologists seem to be right who assert that sharp and exact
observation will always discover fine quiverings of pleasure or
displeasure in the apparently neutral states. Even to the most abstract
processes of thought there are bound faint moods of liking or disliking.
Bain cites wonder as an example of neutral states or excitations of
feeling (neutral excitements). As we have seen, it is a border
phenomenon, where elements of knowledge and of feeling often seem to be
in equilibrium. But as an awakening
% --- p. 340 ---
\opage{340}and movement of spiritual powers it is surely to be regarded
as a feeling of pleasure, like all free and fresh activity. When wonder
stands as the introduction to fear, grief, scorn or indignation, on the
other hand, it is a feeling of displeasure. Wonder does not belong to
the violent feelings that let the object fall entirely into the shadow
of the movement of the mind itself; but it is not therefore neutral.

We can very well conceive an unbroken series of transitions from
pleasure to pain without there needing to be any neutral point in the
series. One finds by experiment that when, e.g., one holds one's hand on
a body whose degree of heat increases, faint feelings of displeasure
arise at a certain point beside the pleasant sensation of warmth; they
gradually grow and at last entirely displace the pleasant
% footnote *) on p. 340
feeling.\footnote{\emph{Horwicz}: Psychologische Analysen. II, 2.
Magdeburg 1878. p.~26.}

The background of our life of feeling at any time, the general feeling
of life, has under normal conditions the character of pleasure, although
as a rule we do not become conscious of it, since, as just remarked, we
use our health, freedom and youthful strength to give ourselves up to
activities that awaken stronger and more definite feelings. Only in the
period of convalescence, or when attention is for some reason or other
drawn to our condition, does the feeling of pleasure bound up with
health become really conscious to us. Of \emph{Kant}, who rejoiced
greatly in his health, which was so good a basis for his energetic
mental work, one of his biographers says: ``His health was not merely a
complete absence of all pain; it was a real sensation and a true
% footnote **) on p. 340
enjoyment of the highest well-being.''\footnote{\emph{Wasianski}: Kant
in seinen letzten Lebensjahren. Königsberg 1804. p.~32.} --- It is
surely a correct remark of \emph{Horwicz} that real indifference would
be sensed as unpleasant apathy; the normal mood of the feeling of life
is a faint feeling of pleasure, which lies midway between emptiness and
dissatisfaction on the one side, and particular good form or elation on
the other
% footnote ***) on p. 340
side.\footnote{\emph{Horwicz}: Zur Lehre von den körperlichen
Gemeingefühlen. (Vierteljahrsschrift für wissenschaftl. Philosophie.
1880). p.~303.}

% --- p. 341 ---
\opage{341}At the bottom of the assumption of neutral states there lies
not only an overlooking of the weaker degrees of pleasure and
displeasure, but also a confusion of the state of the mind as a whole
with the effect that single ideas and experiences have. Many impressions
and ideas come and go without awakening any noticeable feeling or having
any distinct influence on our general state of feeling. But this general
state itself is at every moment determined by the predominance either of
the feeling of pleasure or of the feeling of displeasure.

\textbf{8.} As an example of feelings whose character is determined by
the law of relation, we shall here go somewhat more closely into the
feelings of the sublime and the ludicrous. The consideration of these
feelings will at the same time be able to serve to show how a feeling
is developed to higher forms by displeasure passing over into pleasure,
egoism into sympathy, and what significance the development of knowledge
has for feeling.

\emph{The feeling of the sublime} is in its simplest forms akin partly
to wonder, partly to fear. Sublime is that which comes before us with
such a fullness of impressions that the ordinary measure of
intuitability is far exceeded. We try to take it in with our intuition,
but are unable to, or able only with difficulty. So act high, steep
mountains, the desert, the surface of the sea, the starry sky. The idea
of infinite time, too, gives the impression of the sublime, as when it
is said that every thousand years a bird comes and whets its beak on an
enormous mountain, and that when the mountain has in this way been worn
away, only the first second of eternity has passed. But even before
immense extension in space and time, overwhelming force acts. The
feeling of the sublime surely first asserts itself as a consequence of
the idea of extraordinary and superior human power. The savage has this
feeling over against a mighty warrior, whose strength and skill in
battle make his own vanish for his imagination, just as we feel our
existence as a nothing in relation to infinite space and infinite time.
The sublime in extension in time and space (what \emph{Kant} called the
mathematically sublime) no doubt acts not only by setting our faculty of
intuition in more than ordinary activity, but also and especially by
giving the impression of an immense
% --- p. 342 ---
\opage{342}power that acts in the enormous mass of parts of space and
time. As remarked in an earlier connection, interest in the human
expresses itself before the feeling for nature (B, 13). The feeling of
superior power as sublime arises within the human
world\footnote{Cf.\ \emph{Grant Allen}: ``The Origin of the Sublime.''
(Mind 1878).} and spreads from there out over nature, the powers of
nature being more or less distinctly conceived in analogy with human
power. In the roaring of the winds and the sea, in the chasing clouds
and the mightily towering masses of the mountains, the spirits of the
dead and the gods of the tribe reveal themselves.

The feeling of superior power will not always be disinterested wonder
or admiration. In the struggle of all against all the mighty warrior
may just as well turn his weapons against his own tribesmen as against
enemies. He protects his countrymen, it is true, but demands their
submission in return. Of a similar kind, at the lowest stage, is the
relation to the gods. Since evil beings are worshipped and honored
before good ones, the feeling toward the divine must bear predominantly
the stamp of fear. Only where the deity stands essentially as the
protecting and gracious power does fear become reverence (see B, 12).
There is sublimity not only in the Jehovah of the Old Testament, who
brings forth a world by his word and gives his people the stern law on
the mountain top amid lightning and thunder, but also in the teaching of
Buddhism and Christianity about the infinity of the divine compassion
and love, before which human sin and suffering vanish like dew before
the sun.

The displeasure bound up with the feeling of the sublime in its
primitive forms disappears with higher development, partly because the
intellectual elements of the feeling become richer and nobler, partly
because the object of the feeling is embraced with sympathy. There is,
as was stressed earlier, an element of sympathy in reverence that
separates it from fear. It is therefore hardly correct when some
psychologists regard an element of displeasure as abiding in the feeling
of the sublime, whether one then holds,
% footnote **) on p. 342
with \emph{Edmund Burke},\footnote{Philosophical Inquiry into the
origin of our ideas of the sublime and beautiful. Part. II. Sect. 1--2.}
that fear or
% --- p. 343 ---
\opage{343}terror more or less definitely asserts itself before the
sublime, or, more idealistically, with
% footnote *) on p. 343
\emph{Kant},\footnote{Kritik der Urtheilskraft. \S~27.} finds a moment
of displeasure in our lower, sensuous nature being pressed down in order
that our supersensible being may assert itself the more clearly. The
moment of displeasure falls away when the feeling of the sublime takes
on purer and higher forms. At the same time the egoistic moment
contained in fear also disappears.

The contrast that is the presupposition for the feeling of the sublime
to arise need not, then, have any painful character. We can even
recognize our smallness with joy, when our soul is at the same time
enlarged and filled by the great. We sacrifice life in order to gain
life.

Here, as everywhere (V A, 6; --- B, 8 a; --- C, 7 b. --- VI B, 2 e), the
successive precedes the simultaneous. The feeling of our smallness and
the feeling of the greatness and loftiness of the object will for some
time first alternate rhythmically with each other, before they fuse
into the feeling of the sublime
% footnote **) on p. 343
proper.\footnote{Cf.\ on such fusion VI B, 2 e.} It is especially at
this more restless stage of the development of the feeling that a
moment of displeasure will be able to assert itself. The painful feeling
at the overwhelming belongs at this stage.

\textbf{9.} While the feeling of the sublime presupposes a certain
spiritual development, the feeling of \emph{the ludicrous} is possible
at the lower stages of the development of the life of consciousness, as
soon as definite ideas can assert themselves. Indeed, even before such a
stage is reached, we meet the physiological expression of this feeling;
laughter appears before anything ludicrous can yet assert itself for
consciousness.

\textbf{a.} Laughter can arise from purely physical causes, so that it
is then not at all the expression of an emotion. Severe cold can call
forth not only shivering but also laughter. The ancients mention a
convulsive laughter that was called forth by eating a herb growing in
Sardinia (hence ``sardonic laughter''). Laughter is often bound up with
% footnote ***) on p. 343 (continues on p. 344)
hysterical convulsive fits.\footnote{\emph{Laycock}: On the reflex
functions of the brain (British and Foreign Medical Review. 1845. 19de
Bd. p.~306) mentions a case of reflex laughter as the consequence of an
epilepsy brought on by a brain tumor.} Gladiators who were wounded in
the
% --- p. 344 ---
\opage{344}diaphragm died laughing. \emph{Ludvig Vives} (de anima lib.
III) tells of himself that at the first mouthfuls he took after a
rather long fast he could not hold back his laughter. Here belongs also
laughter from tickling; already the child in its 8th week laughs when
one tickles the soles of its feet.

These phenomena must most nearly be counted under reflex movement. Only
as regards the last observation cited can there be \emph{doubt}, since
the child, according to \emph{Preyer}, laughs only when it is in a
\emph{pleasant mood}. Such a mood is in itself enough to call forth
smiles and laughter. Already in the very first days a faint raising of
the corners of the mouth can be observed in the satisfied and sleeping
child (the counterpart of the lowering of them that expresses
displeasure and is often the prelude to crying). Real smiling, however,
is first observed in the fourth week, in connection with beaming eyes
and a general, indescribable expression of well-being, together with
some bleating sounds, recurring in jerks, which evidently also express
well-being. Laughter is only an intensification of the smile and of
these sounds, and is originally, like them, only the expression of a
well-being grounded immediately in the general
sensation.\footnote{\emph{Darwin}: Expression of Emotions, p.~211 f.
--- \emph{Preyer}: Die Seele des Kindes. p.~141. 184--186.}

Like the infant, the idiot too, who, as \emph{Eschricht} has said, is
an infant in the body of an older child or of an adult, gives
expression to his well-being by smiles and laughter. Many idiots go
about with a constant smile on their faces and frequently break out in
laughter, especially when they get food, are caressed or hear music. In
most of them this laughter cannot be called forth by definite
ideas.\footnote{Cf.\ \emph{Eschricht}: Om Muligheden af at helbrede
Idioter [On the Possibility of Curing Idiots]. Kbhvn. 1854. p.~76.}

% footnote ***) on p. 344
\emph{Ewald Hecker}\footnote{Die Physiologie und Psychologie des
Lachens und des Komischen. Berlin 1873.} has attempted a physiological
explanation of laughter from tickling. Experiments seem to prove that
tickling has as its consequence a reflex influence on the sympathetic
nerves, which shows itself in a rhythmical narrowing of the blood
vessels
% --- p. 345 ---
\opage{345}corresponding to the rhythmically interrupted touch. From
this it then follows that the blood supply of the brain is likewise
rhythmically checked; and laughter now stands as a purposive reflex
movement, the chest being compressed by the strong expirations, so that
the outflow of blood from the brain is checked. The movements of
expiration are interrupted and rhythmical, like the touches of
tickling.


This explanation, however, does not cover all the phenomena of
laughter, especially not those where laughter appears as the expression
of immediate joy and well-being. While the jerkiness of expiration in
laughter finds a fine explanation in Hecker's theory, it is not clear
why an immediate feeling of well-being, which contains no contrast or
rhythm, should be expressed precisely in this convulsive way. ---
Besides, it must be remarked that already in laughter from tickling a
central or psychical influence can assert itself. As a rule we do not
laugh when we are prepared to be tickled, and therefore we cannot tickle
ourselves either. There must, then, be something unexpected and sudden
in the impression, and when the first touch has taken place, an obscure
expectation must be awakened that it will continue (with the same
strength), an expectation that is disappointed, since the touch ceases
the next moment, only to begin again at once. It will appear that
herein lies an analogy between laughter from tickling and the laughter
called forth by the idea of something ludicrous.

Laughter has often been regarded as something peculiar to man, so that
it could serve as a means of defining man (\textit{animal risibile}).
If one requires for laughter the idea of something ludicrous, this
would also be correct. But apes too smile and laugh, not only when they
are tickled or get especially good food, but also when they see again
someone they are fond of, or are reconciled again with their
% footnote *) on p. 345
keeper.\footnote{\emph{Darwin}: Expression of Emotions. p.~132--135.}
Besides, \emph{Ludvig Vives} (de anima lib. III) already remarked that
what we express by laughter can be expressed in other ways by the
animals (e.g.\ by wagging the tail).

\textbf{b.} The feeling of pleasure, which is the simplest psychical
cause of laughter, will by the nature of the case very often, and at a
primitive stage almost exclusively, be called forth by impressions
% --- p. 346 ---
\opage{346}that satisfy the impulse of self-preservation and appeal to
the feeling of self. Life is first and foremost a struggle to survive.

A strong and suddenly awakened feeling of self easily breaks out in
laughter. In idiots at a somewhat higher stage than the one just
mentioned, personal vanity is the most frequent cause of outbursts of
laughter. Two conditions now come together here: the sudden and
unexpected, and the strong feeling of self. This kind of laughter we
shall meet especially in characteristic forms where a hard and doubtful
struggle has been waged and is suddenly crowned with victory, so that
what threatened life now all at once lies powerless and harmless. Then
arises the gloating in which the Homeric heroes break out over their
vanquished enemies. What finds vent here is not only a feeling of rescue
and deliverance. The image of the adversary in his full strength and
dangerousness is suddenly succeeded by the image of the same adversary
crushed and clipped in his great plans. The urge to dwell on the
nothingness and impotence of the vanquished enemy, in contrast to his
former bearing, often expressed itself in \emph{barbarous mockery} of
prisoners or corpses. In more or less brutal forms this kind of feeling
of the ludicrous constantly finds occasion to arise, as long as life,
in the human world too, is a struggle for existence. \emph{Thomas
Hobbes} therefore seized an essential point of view when he explained
laughter as a suddenly awakened feeling of superiority (sudden glory.
Human Nature IX, 13). Hobbes, with his doctrine of the state of nature
as a war of all against all, was naturally bound to be led to lay the
main weight on the feeling of self \emph{or of power}. The one-sidedness
of his conception was that he did not inquire whether the feeling of
power that expresses itself in laughter does not undergo essential
changes and take on a different character according to the nature of
that on which it is grounded. But wherever there is struggle, in the
service of whatever cause, there may be in those struggling an
\emph{element of scorn} in the way in which they regard their
adversaries' plans and efforts. Even in religious poetry and religious
polemic we find this form of the feeling of the ludicrous again. Thus it
says in the 2nd Psalm: ``The kings of the earth rise up, and the rulers
have taken counsel together against the Lord and his Anointed. ---
`Let us break their bands asunder and cast their
% --- p. 347 ---
\opage{347}chains from us!' --- He that dwelleth in heaven laugheth; the
Lord mocketh them.'' And when \emph{Pascal} defends himself against the
Jesuits' complaints that he had used laughter as a weapon in his
``Provincial Letters'', he says that just as there are two things in
religious truth, a divine beauty and a divine majesty, so there are also
two things in error: the impiety that makes it terrible, and the
impudence that makes it ludicrous; therefore the saints feel both hatred
and contempt toward error and show their \emph{zeal}, not only by
forcefully repelling the wickedness of the impious, but also in refuting
their error with laughter, following in this God's own example.
(Lettres écrites à un Provincial. XI.).

The very fact that laughter can be used as a weapon shows that it has
power as its presupposition. One can laugh at another only because he
is the weaker. It is naturally not always real superiority that finds
vent in this way. Precisely \emph{embarrassment} and \emph{shyness} can
express themselves in smiles and forced laughter. Indeed, the
consciousness of complete impotence, when one nevertheless will not
bow, breaks out in the same way. In \emph{Paludan}-Müller's ``Lucifers
Fald'' [The Fall of Lucifer], Lucifer laughs for the first time when
``with trembling beyond compare --- yet with \emph{defiance} that bends
not'' he takes possession of his kingdom.

That no one likes to be the one laughed at is naturally explained by
Hobbes's theory. Only few would have taken the matter as humorously as
Socrates, who at the performance of the ``Clouds'' stood up so that the
original and the caricature could be compared. The mere fact that a
thing has a side from which it can be made ludicrous is enough to show
that it does not represent absolute power. Precisely for that reason,
on the other hand, it is so tempting to find something ludicrous
wherever claim is made to authority and complete recognition. Everything
sublime, all authority and dignity, has in laughter its dangerous enemy.
Laughter here is not so much the expression of superior power as of
liberation. Even what under ordinary conditions would not seem ludicrous
to us becomes so when it has a situation of enforced seriousness as its
background. Boys in a school lesson can find the most insignificant
thing ludicrous. \emph{Merely} the fact that something can happen
without the will of the ruling authority
% --- p. 348 ---
\opage{348}is enough to awaken a \emph{consciousness of freedom}.
Authorities that are on the point of losing their dominion naturally
become the object of the feeling of the ludicrous. The appearance of
comic poetry (Aristophanes, Molière, Holberg) therefore always marks a
turning point, where the consciousness of freedom breaks forth.

\textbf{c.} Scorn, however, is not a necessary element in the feeling of
the ludicrous. This feeling undergoes an essential change when it has
sympathy as its basis. When an inward bond unites the one who laughs
with the object of the laughter, a new and peculiar feeling arises. This
is already the case when we laugh at what we ourselves have said or done
at an earlier time; we give ourselves up, though we must necessarily
have another understanding of our absurd conduct than one who merely
sees it from outside or as an adversary. To laugh at someone for whom
one feels sympathy is like laughing at oneself. Here there is a
doubleness in the feeling: one sees the worth of the object in the midst
of its smallness. In one and the same moment one applies a double
standard. So when one laughs at the child's helplessness or groundless
fear, or at its naivety. There is here a subterranean connection between
the one who laughs and the object of the laughter. The sting is taken
out of the laughter. The feeling of the ludicrous on the basis of
sympathy is called humor. This can be more than a passing mood. It can
develop into being the fundamental mood of a view of life which indeed
has an open eye for the finite, painful, meaningless and disharmonious
in the world and sets it in sharp contrast to the great and significant,
but which, in its heartfelt fellow-feeling with all that lives and in
its firm faith in the powers that rule in nature and history, has got
beyond all bitterness. The humorous conception of life has reconciled
itself to the experience that even the great and lofty has its
limitation, its finite side, and its laughter at the small and finite
does not forget that this can be the form of a valuable content.

Comic poetry rests not only on the consciousness of freedom, but also on
a more or less distinctly apparent humorous conception of life. Only
thereby does it become different from the lampoon and satire, where the
egoistic, or at any rate the antipathetic, moment rules, in that
% --- p. 349 ---
\opage{349}a single definite person or a single cause is struck at. Only
on the basis of humor does art become really free. The power on which
the humorist and the comic poet rely is no longer their own ego in
contrast to other egos; it is truth and reason, whose power they
maintain through the exposure of the low and meaningless. The feeling of
power is thus not egoistic here; what is seen in its nothingness is only
that in the object of the laughter which conflicts with the true and
right. After one has laughed at a person, one therefore often feels the
urge to declare that he is
% footnote *) on p. 349
at bottom a decent fellow after all.\footnote{\emph{Rousseau} reproaches
Molière (in his ``Lettre à d’Alembert'') for having presented a
respectable person as ludicrous in the ``Misanthrope''. \emph{Lessing}
on that occasion drew attention to the difference between laughing and
laughing at (lachen --- verlachen). (Hamburgische Dramaturgie Nr.
28--29). --- Already \emph{Aristotle} distinguishes between the lampoon
and comedy. (Poët. c. 4).}

\textbf{d.} Whether the basis is antipathetic or sympathetic, it is
common to everything ludicrous that something powerless \emph{suddenly}
appears in its nothingness through its \emph{contrast} with a superior
power. The ludicrous presupposes that for a moment we have let
ourselves be duped, bewildered, caught in an illusion or held in the
tension of an expectation, and that the whole thing then all at
% footnote **) on p. 349
once dissolves into nothing.\footnote{The conception to which we here
adhere has been stated by \emph{Kant} (Kritik der Urtheilskraft. \S~53
Anm.), Zeising (Æsthetiche Forschungen. Frankfurt 1855. p.~282--290) and
\emph{Spencer}: Latterens Fysiologi [The Physiology of Laughter].
(Mindre Afhandlinger. Danish transl. Kbhvn. 1877).} The psychical
element in tickling is, as already remarked, the expectation that is
awakened but at once disappointed. The child laughs at every sudden
movement, especially when it is repeated in jerks, as when one quickly
squats down in front of it, then gets up, and repeats this sinking and
rising. Sudden deliverance from painful or dangerous situations acts in
the same way. All wit rests on the expectation being strained by a knot
being tied or the solution of a riddle being announced. He who prepares
his witticism robs it of its effect. The Horatian verse: ``The mountains
are in labor: a ludicrous mouse will be born'' can serve as a motto for
everything ludicrous. --- In a farce that was played some years ago at
the ``Bouffes Parisiens'' there was a character who right
% --- p. 350 ---
\opage{350}from the beginning of the piece sat quite still at one side
of the foreground, wholly untouched by the action. Now and then one
wondered to oneself what he was sitting there for. At last someone
addresses him, but is interrupted by another: ``Don't talk to him; he is
deaf!'' Now, then, one had got the explanation of his passivity. But in
the same moment the character adds with a very melancholy expression:
``and dumb!'' This awakened
% footnote *) on p. 350
a storm of laughter.\footnote{As far as I remember, the farce was a
parody of a diplomatic congress on the occasion of the Greek affairs,
and Greece had access to the congress --- without daring to take part
in the negotiations.} --- To the Emperor Charles V is attributed the
remark about his relation to Francis I of France (with whom he was
constantly at war, especially over Milan): ``My brother Francis and I
are in complete agreement; we both want Milan!'' That in which the
agreement is to consist is precisely what causes the strife; an
expectation is awakened of seeing the relation between the two
contending princes from a new side; but this expectation is dissolved
the next moment.

The ludicrous rests on an \emph{effect of contrast}. Two thoughts or two
impressions, each of which awakens its own feeling, and of which the
latter tears down what the former builds up, suddenly meet each other.
No closer logical grounding of the effect of this can be given, any more
than one can ground logically why two complementary colors set each
other off. When the ludicrous has often been explained (among us
especially by S.~\emph{Kierkegaard} in ``Uvidenskabelig Efterskrift''
[Unscientific Postscript] p.~394 ff.) as resting on a contradiction,
this is too abstract and at the same time too narrow a conception. Even
if one can often formulate the clash by which the feeling of the
ludicrous arises as a logical contradiction (e.g.\ being dumb and deaf
--- and yet answering oneself that one is), the real process proper is
not thereby hit upon, which consists essentially in a contrast of
feeling. A certain intellectual development is naturally necessary in
order to apprehend the ludicrous, just as it is a presupposition for the
contrast effect of colors that the eye can apprehend each single color
by itself. The feeling of the ludicrous is in this respect akin to
surprise and wonder, which also rest
% --- p. 351 ---
\opage{351}on opposition and contrast (cf.\ 3). That the moment of
feeling is the essential thing in these phenomena can be seen from the
fact that they do not bear repetition and habit. While knowledge (e.g.\
insight into logical contradictions) is practiced and strengthened by
repetition, feeling is dulled (cf.\ 4). The ludicrous does not bear too
frequent repetition.

Even where we laugh at the logically contradictory, the feeling of
power (the antipathetic or the sympathetic) plays a part. For we have
our own reason as the firm ground from which judgment is passed, and we
become more or less clearly conscious of it as we laugh at the
meaningless. Even if we do not celebrate our own victory in laughter, we
celebrate the victory of reason.

\textbf{e.} The feeling of the ludicrous thus rests, like the feeling of
the sublime, on a contrast. But the two feelings also stand, besides, in
a relation of contrast to each other. They build on one and the same
fundamental relation, the relation between greatness and smallness, seen
from different sides. It was probably this that \emph{Socrates} was
thinking of when (at the end of the banquet \emph{Plato} has depicted
in the ``Symposium'') he insisted that it was the business of the same
man to write comedy and tragedy. This proposition is confirmed already
by the old Greek tragedians, who wrote satyr plays alongside tragedies;
but it first stands before us in its full truth through Shakspere's
great example. It has been aptly said that Shakspere's humor was a part
of his fidelity to the world. For man's real position is this, that he
must assert his power outwardly, abolish and annihilate resistance,
while he himself must feel his smallness over against the great powers
in nature and history. He has fought his way to full victory over
himself and over the world who neither gloats nor fears. Much laughter,
says \emph{Hobbes} already, is a sign of a small mind, which feels its
strength only in comparison with those who stand beneath it, while it is
the sign of a great mind to help others and to compare oneself with the
greatest (Leviathan. cap. 6). In humor we feel ourselves at once great
and small, and it is sympathy that makes laughter humorous, just as it
makes fear into reverence.

% --- p. 352 ---
\opage{352}
\section*{E. The Influence of Feeling on Knowledge}\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{E. The Influence of Feeling on Knowledge}

In an earlier section (B) it has been shown how the connection of ideas
becomes useful to the feelings and makes their development possible.
But deeper than this influence of the life of ideas on feeling lies the
influence of feeling on the life of ideas. The original connection of
the element of feeling and the element of knowledge is always the
beginning of the whole higher psychological development; but during
this --- that is, during the psychological process depicted in section
B --- the element of feeling by no means behaves wholly passively.

\textbf{1.} In considering the development of feeling by means of
knowledge, we presupposed that there was nothing that hindered the
connection of ideas. But feeling itself can act as a \emph{hindrance}.
When the feeling $\alpha$ has grown inwardly together with the idea a,
it will be able to hinder the connection, in itself natural, between a
and a$_{2}$ a$_{3}$\ldots\ and still more between a and b; that is to
say: we cannot complete the train of thought consistently, because the
feeling will not extend beyond its original object. Here the inertia of
feeling is at work, which thus becomes the source of many
inconsistencies in history and in daily life. When the Greeks could not
extend love of humanity to the barbarians, this did not come from purely
intellectual narrowness (though the limited historical experience
contributed here); but full ethical consistency was checked by national
feeling. Christianity overthrew these barriers, not by intellectual
superiority, but by the deep movement of feeling that it awakened.
Within Christianity, intolerance has set up new barriers and checked the
consistent unfolding of the religion of love. The result to which
logical thinking seems able to lead in a single moment thus takes long
periods in history: a revolution in the life of feeling is the result
of the experiences of life of great spans of time. Therefore historical
criticism may use logical contradiction as a criterion only with
caution; historical development would be impossible if inconsistencies
were not possible. Historical research has to seek out the real
feelings that have acted to hold back and check in the particular cases.
The measure of the courage and energy of thought can be taken from
% --- p. 353 ---
\opage{353}the barriers of feeling in spite of which the new thoughts
have worked their way forward.

But once the step has been taken, feeling is the faithful preserver of
what has been won. Here its inertia benefits knowledge. By b fusing
through a with $\alpha$, it strikes deep roots in the soul. Knowledge
gains in firmness and certainty, and it is in reality the property of
the personality only when it has in this way its root in feeling, in
the immediate state of the individual.

When a certain idea or a certain circle of ideas is carried by strong
interest or passion, its position with regard to other ideas is
changed. It becomes a stronger center of association than it otherwise
would be. In all experiences regard is had only to what in one way or
another concerns the idea that is carried and reinforced by the
interest. All other elements in the world do not exist for
consciousness. Feeling here brings about a \emph{selection of quality}.
All ideas that do not harmonize with the ruling feeling are displaced,
just as those living beings perish that are unable to adapt themselves
to the given conditions. Lotze has particularly stressed the fusion of
ideas with the feeling of life given at the same time. If the feeling
of life is altered, the way to the ideas bound up with it is also
barred to us; even when new experiences again call forth single ones of
them, the common bond is lacking. ``In this way it could'', Lotze
continues, ``be explained why things we experience during severe
illnesses, or before their outbreak, while the feeling of life was
already altered, are wholly forgotten when we get well, and why the
series of ideas in a paroxysm of fever are not remembered in the free
interval, but are continued in the next attack, when the sick feeling
of life has returned.''\footnote{Drei Bücher der Metaphysik. p.~600.}

\textbf{2.} Some psychologists have ascribed to the single ideas an
impulse of self-preservation, by which they seek to assert themselves in
consciousness with a certain strength and to displace one another from
it. The psychology of knowledge has proved the incorrectness of this
assumption (V B, 6). \emph{The strength of the single idea} rests in
the first place on its relation
% --- p. 354 ---
\opage{354}to the other ideas in consciousness. The idea that can rely
on the greatest number of experiences and memories will have the
greatest possibility of becoming the ruling one. But besides the mutual
relation of the ideas, their relation to the feelings is also of
importance. In strong tension or deep and lasting interest, even ideas
that hang together with very comprehensive and often repeated
experiences can be pushed entirely aside. The fetish-worshipper lays
greater weight on the few cases in which he can believe he has been
helped by his holy stone than on the many where this belief is wholly
impossible. He who loves a person inwardly does not see the unlovely
features in him. Very beautifully and movingly such a relation is
depicted by \emph{Prevost} in his ``Manon Lescaut''. Love makes blind
--- but only because it makes extraordinarily seeing on a single point.

In the biographies of criminals countless examples are found of the
blinding power of feeling and passion. The violent covetousness for an
object --- be it a royal crown or a silver watch --- overwhelms thought,
or rather concentrates all thoughts on the object and on the means of
obtaining it. In ``Macbeth'' \emph{Shakspere} has shown in masterly
fashion how the idea of the criminal act can govern the mind to such a
degree that it stands as the only reality:

\begin{verse}
This thought,\\
whose murder yet is but a dream, so shakes\\
my whole being that all my vital power\\
perishes in surmise, and \emph{nothing} is\\
except that \emph{which is not}.\\
\hspace*{\fill}(Act I. Sc. 3).
\end{verse}
% TR: Høffding quotes Macbeth in Danish translation; rendered here line
% for line from the Danish. Shakespeare: "My thought, whose murder yet is
% but fantastical, / Shakes so my single state of man that function / Is
% smother'd in surmise, and nothing is / But what is not."
\noindent From this comes the often incredibly foolish way in which
crimes are carried out. ``In most criminals'', says the famous jurist
\emph{Anselm} v.
% footnote *) on p. 354
\emph{Feuerbach},\footnote{Aktenmässige Darstellung merkwürdiger
Verbrechen. II. p.~342.} ``it can quite definitely be shown that and how
the criminal's understanding has been dazzled, darkened, taken captive
by desire through the magic power of overmastering incitements, and
restricted in the free use of its activity, and how precisely this
% --- p. 355 ---
\opage{355}restriction has been a main contributing cause of his
committing his deed.'' Such crimes come about through a drowning out
not only of the demands of conscience but also of those of prudence.

In the particular cases it is impossible to know beforehand whether the
impulse of feeling or the connection of ideas will be the stronger. It
comes down to a trial of strength. But other conditions can make the
matter still more complicated. Precisely a very strong impulse of
feeling can, by an effect of contrast, call forth a vivid idea of the
moral law and of the interest we are about to violate; on such an
effect of contrast rest some of the most drastic utterances of
conscience. In other cases the ordinary association of ideas can still
call forth images that are able to check the stream of the ruling
feeling. When Lady Macbeth would lay hands on the old king, she was held
back by his resembling her father. (``I had done it, had he not
resembled my father as he slept.'' Act II, Sc. 2). A little more
passion --- and the law of similarity could not have operated!

We stand here at the boundary between feeling and will. For blindness
and the absolute dominion of passionate feeling seldom arise through the
purely involuntary play of feelings and ideas; man can, strange as it
sounds, \emph{will} to deceive himself, can for the sake of his passion
push sober deliberation back. When the satisfaction of passion meets
resistance in a man's mind, he can set his understanding and his fancy
in motion to find reasons to deaden the inner voice. The inner
contradiction is not to be endured and must be removed in one way or
another. Man therefore comes forward over against himself as a
sophistical rhetorician. In all passion something of such sophistry can
be shown.\footnote{Cf.\ \emph{Anselm v. Feuerbach}. II. p.~331 f.} In a
milder form the same thing shows itself in the way in which favorite
opinions are held fast, painted out and carried through, very often with
desperate reasons.

\textbf{3.} It is originally a practical interest that sets ideas in
motion. The task of primitive knowledge is to discover the means of
satisfying impulse. Only
% --- p. 356 ---
\opage{356}gradually does an impulse to knowledge for knowledge's own
sake develop (see V D, 4. --- VI B, 13). And even the attempts at
thought called forth by contemplative enthusiasm emancipate themselves
only slowly and incompletely from the dominion of practical feeling, and
therefore as a rule bear a teleological stamp. The resignation by which
feeling holds itself back in order to let thought go its own ways and
follow its own laws is the work of hard struggles in the history of the
individual and of the whole human race. Man wants to know nature as it
is; --- but he also wants his own ends to be the ends of nature. All
causal laws are for him in the last instance means to the realization
of the highest good. This tendency appears at the crudest stage of
fetishism as well as in the highly developed world of thought of the
idealist philosopher. The content and value of the feelings and ideas
are extremely different at the different stages; but the psychological
relation between feeling and knowledge is the same from the lowest to
the highest stage.

The concept of necessity is originally practical: thought seeks the
conditions without which the valuable end cannot be realized. Thought
thereby acquires from the first a negative character over against
feeling. Feeling is sanguine and impatient; only reluctantly does it
give thought room. --- One easily sees that this impatience is only
another side of the slowness and inertia mentioned earlier; impatience
proceeds from inertia. When \emph{Tertullian} conceived original sin as
impatience, \emph{Fichte} as sloth, they were not so different in their
thinking as the wording might suggest.

The definite, unavoidable connection between means and ends, defying
even the highest passion --- that if one wills a, one must also will b
--- is man's first collision with necessity. When such experiences are
repeated, thought at last draws the conclusion that such a necessary
connection is an essential part of all given reality. Now it can become
an independent task, the object of an immediate interest, to know these
necessary laws and, forgetting oneself, to become absorbed in the great
connection of the phenomena. Then the relation between
% --- p. 357 ---
\opage{357}cause and effect takes the place of that between means and
end. The history of the sciences shows us a progressive transition from
teleology to mechanism --- under the constant protest and resistance of
feeling. Even if science had explained the whole universe according to
its laws, it would still not be able to forbid feeling to subordinate
the whole system of causes and effects to a highest teleology,
inconceivable to us. The last questions in the domain of views of life,
the questions of the value and significance of reality and of life ---
these questions, to which thought has never been able to give a
conclusive answer, are decided in the last instance at the dictation of
the feelings. This comes forward with particular clarity in our day in
the great significance that the contrast between the optimistic and the
pessimistic view of life has acquired. Here in the end it is always our
own innermost nature and our personal experience of life that turns the
scale.

\textbf{4.} Feeling acts on ideas not only by holding back and
narrowing, preserving and selecting, but also by \emph{altering}. Just
as one and the same landscape looks different according to the light
that falls on it, so the same things and events appear to us quite
different according to our different moods. Here the feeling of life
with its fluctuations plays an especially important part.
\emph{Lichtenberg} says: ``I have very distinctly noticed that I often
have one opinion when I am lying down and another when I am standing,
especially when I have eaten little and am weary.'' Feeling does not
change at once with ideas, but spreads out over the new ideas, even if
these stand in no connection with those that have awakened the feeling.
This ``dominion of a feeling that surges on'' (to use Lotze's apt
expression) is sometimes striking and unintelligible to ourselves,
especially when the feeling is due to inner, organic states; but for the
most part it exerts its influence on the new content of ideas without
our noticing it. We owe much to this influence; for by it a heightening
of mental life at one point can also further other mental activities.
Thus, e.g., music, wine and brisk bodily movement can help the activity
of thought along. Here belong a great many of the examples in Feilberg's
book ``Om størst Udbytte af Sjælsevner''. Among other things
% --- p. 358 ---
\opage{358}he rightly stresses how new impressions and situations can
produce a movement in the whole of consciousness, quite apart from the
content of what is new. ``New situations bring the soul into what the
chemists call \textit{status nascendi}, the state in which substances
find themselves at the moment of their production. Just as chemistry
teaches that substances in this state possess a particularly great
tendency to enter into new combinations, so it is also in the soul. The
as it were floating state, freed from something old, contains
heightened chances for the beginning of new states (value of
possibility)'' (p.~30).

This phenomenon might be called \emph{the expansion of feeling}. Every
strong feeling strives for sole dominion in the soul and gives its color
to
% footnote *) on p. 358
mental activities.\footnote{This law was already pointed out by
\emph{Hume} (Treatise I, 3, 8) and plays an important part in his theory
of knowledge. Later \emph{Beneke} developed it in an interesting way
(Psychologische Skizzen. I. p.~362 ff.) --- Cf.\ also \emph{Spencer}:
Princ. of Psychol. \S~260--261. --- A fine example is found in Goethe's
``Erster Epistel'' (Ernst und wichtig scheint mir die Frage; doch trifft
sie mich eben in vergnüglicher Stimmung. --- --- Und dem heitern
erscheint die Welt auch heiter). --- Cf.\ also Mme de \emph{Staël}'s
remark (Corinne I, 1): ``Quand on souffre, on se persuade aisément que
l’on est coupable, et les violents chagrins portent le trouble jusque
dans la conscience.''} It is the same law according to which feeling
procures itself outward expression. There is, so to speak, both a
mental and a bodily mimicry: the former is the influence of feeling on
ideas, the latter its effect on the muscular movements. --- We describe
in what immediately follows some particular forms of this law.

\textbf{a)} \emph{The anticipating effect of feeling}. In a strong
tension of mind one is inclined to take impressions that are expected
as given before they really occur. When, e.g., we are impatiently
awaiting a carriage, we believe every moment that we hear a rumbling.
--- The experiments on physiological time give good examples of this.
When, e.g., a certain impression is to be signaled, attention can be so
strained that one signals another impression instead of the expected
one, not from confusion, but because any impression whatever, under the
strong tension, leads to the action one was on the point of performing.
When one follows with the eye the movement of a pointer
% --- p. 359 ---
\opage{359}on a circular dial, and at each revolution a stroke of a bell
takes place without one's knowing beforehand at what moment it will
sound, in most cases the stroke of the bell is combined with a position
of the pointer that lies before the real sound, so that the latter is
apparently heard before it really occurs. How much earlier it is heard
differs in different individuals and depends on ``the
% footnote *) on p. 359
personal equation''.\footnote{\emph{Wundt}: Physiol. Psychologie, II.
p.~226. 265.} --- A large part of the so-called mesmeric and
spiritualist phenomena can be explained by the strained expectation that
the experimenter calls forth in the persons he operates
% footnote **) on p. 359
on.\footnote{A detailed investigation of the influence of ``expectant
attention'' in such phenomena is found in Carpenter's Mental physiology.
p.~279 ff. 618 ff.} --- In the domain of thought, too, feeling can act
anticipatingly, in that it decides the matter by its postulates instead
of following the lengthy and sober course of the development of
thought. Contemplative enthusiasm sets up its demands for unity and
harmony in the view of the world and often believes it has won a result
by the mere setting up. A great example is Plato's doctrine of ideas
and its after-effects. It is the peculiarity of great geniuses to act
anticipatorily, led by a deep feeling; but critical revision must not
be omitted if admiration for the profundity of genius is not to lead to
dogmatism. Besides, only experience can decide whether the great ideas
really are anticipations.

\textbf{b)} \emph{The realizing effect of feeling}. An idea can, under
strong movement of feeling, be transformed into an apparently real
observation. Images of memory and fancy differ only in degree from real
observations, and a strong feeling can efface this difference of
degree. When the memory is bound up with a feeling akin to the original
feeling, the difference between ideality and reality is at once
diminished. Illusions and hallucinations arise especially in very
exalted or morbid states of feeling. The critical distinction between
idea and reality becomes altogether impossible the more man lets
himself be led by his immediate feeling.

% --- p. 360 ---
\opage{360}\textbf{c)} \emph{The idealizing effect of feeling}. It lies
in the nature of feeling not to ask about differences, conditions and
limits. It has an absolute character and finds vent in superlatives
(always, never, only, etc.). Closer determination, the fixing of
conditions and limits, is the task of knowledge. But now, since feeling
constantly presses on thought and is never quite satisfied with the
given, it drives thought ever further, at last beyond the limits of
knowledge. This pressure feeling exerts through the concentration on a
single thought mentioned earlier, which is thereby loosened from its
connection with and limitation by other thoughts. The practical and
ethical interest acts especially in this way. Feeling thereby leads to
the formation of an ideal world, in which the imperfections and
sufferings of the given world are removed. The idealizing speculation
that stems from this source has its great significance. The great
philosophical systems stand as great thought experiments, as acute
workings-out of a series of the fundamental thoughts of the human
spirit. The struggle of views of life that has led to these bold
attempts will itself in turn reap manifold benefit from them. In any
case criticism will never be able to stop up the source of idealization.
Feeling is bent back into itself by criticism, and resignation will
perhaps temporarily check its flight. But the idealizing impulse can be
satisfied without any dogmatizing. In the ethical and social relations
of human life, in artistic and scientific work, there is employment and
outlet enough for the ideal impulses. Feeling will then lead to
increasing what is valuable in the world, and with it the very basis of
an ideal faith (cf.\ VI B, 12).

\textbf{d)} The \emph{spurring and awakening influence} of feeling. By
its very obscurity and inexplicability feeling exerts a great influence
on knowledge. The single stones in the building of knowledge can as a
rule easily be pointed out. But feeling has its source in the original
instincts, and we know only a small part of its course. The quiet power
of the conditions of life, whose effect first shows itself after long
periods of time, means more than the single, clearly apparent and
conscious experiences. But in whatever way feeling may have arisen, it
will not only spread out and govern everything;
% --- p. 361 ---
\opage{361}it will also itself be explained and justified. This need of
explanation hangs together with man's impulse of self-preservation. He
experiences in pleasure and pain the action of the world on his
life-process; they are signs that he must interpret and whose causes he
must track down, if his life is to endure and advance. At higher stages
of development too, the individual finds his innermost being expressed
in the feelings and therefore seeks to prove their justification. But
feeling cannot justify itself. In every man there is a mystical vein;
there will always be something in his immediate moods that he can
express to other men only very imperfectly. If he appeals directly to
his feeling, all discussion stops. Feeling is in itself no source of
knowledge. The struggle of views of life must be waged with clear
thoughts. Feeling plays an important part here as releasing and
helping forward; it asks, or leads to asking, but does not itself give
the answer. --- While feeling, as purely individual and inexpressible,
isolates individuals, it brings them together through the need of
explanation and justification. Only in union can they find the full
explanation. Thus feeling acts to found communities, calls forth
congregations, parties, schools and scientific societies.

Illuminating examples of the need of feeling for explanation and
justification can be taken from the effect of music and from the
general course of development of mental illnesses.

Feeling exerts an attractive force not only on ideas that are of the
same kind as its original cause, but also on other ideas that call
forth similar feelings. Feeling can thereby become an intermediate link
between ideas of different kinds. A man born blind, to whom one tried to
describe the color scarlet, cried out: ``it must be something like the
blast of a trumpet!'' It is the kindred effect on feeling that lets the
idea of a shrill sound arise when the impression of a dazzling and
strong color is described. (Cf.\ VI A, 3 e).
% footnote *) on p. 361
\emph{Wundt}\footnote{Physiol. Psychologie, I. p.~486. --- Cf.\
\emph{Nahlowsky}: Das Gefühlsleben. p.~142 ff.} has called this
phenomenon \emph{the analogy of sensations}. It is in virtue of it that
music
% --- p. 362 ---
\opage{362}calls forth images and ideas from other domains of
% footnote *) on p. 362
sense. \emph{Hanslick},\footnote{Vom Musikalisch-Schönen. 6te Aufl.
p.~48.} who argues against the conception that music acts directly on
feeling, maintains that the analogy concerns not feeling but
sense-sensation itself. Without analogy between the sensations there
would surely be no analogy between the corresponding feelings. But that
feeling itself plays a decisive part cannot be denied when one compares
the feeling one has in light and free breathing, at the breaking forth
of light after darkness, and at the sound of pure and clear tones after
dissonance and confused noise. The feeling awakened by music
involuntarily procures itself --- though perhaps most in those who are
not specially and technically trained --- a more or less distinct
symbolic expression by means of the analogous sensations. Events and
experiences from inner and outer nature serve for a more concrete
painting-out of the general
% footnote **) on p. 362
mood.\footnote{Cf.\ what \emph{Goldschmidt} tells of himself (Livs
Erindringer og Resultater [Memories and Results of a Life]. I. p.~47):
``From the earliest I can remember, song has not acted on me with an
invitation to sing along, but has transformed itself into images''.}
Musicians warn against giving oneself up to such reveries, lest one be
lost to the specific effect of music; but it is no doubt impossible to
avoid them entirely. Music owes its great power over men precisely to
the circumstance that the moods it awakens can, by countless hidden
threads, touch all our experiences of life and branch out toward all
sides of our being.

According to a conception developed especially by \emph{Guislain}, the
beginning of all mental illness consists in a morbid disturbance of the
life of feeling. The intellectual disturbances, as well as the abnormal,
now violent, now bizarre, now convulsive determinations of will, are
then to be only consequences of a primary disturbance in sensibility.
``For me'', says \emph{Guislain}, ``mental derangement is in most cases a
pain in the heart (une douleur du sens affectif).''\footnote{Neue Lehre
von den Geistesstörungen. Nach dem Französ. von Kanstatt. Nürnberg 1838.
p.~4. --- Cf.\ \emph{Griesinger}: Die Pathologie und Therapie der
psychischen Krankheiten. 2. Aufl. p.~65. 214. --- \emph{Prichard}: Om
Sindssygdomme [On Mental Illnesses]. Translated by Selmer. Kbhvn. 1842.
p.~14 ff. --- See also above V B, 5 (the cases described by
Krishaber).} A \textit{stadium melancholicum} forms in
% --- p. 363 ---
\opage{363}most cases the first period in the history of the mental
illness. The intelligence is for the time being untouched.\footnote{Thus
a patient said of his state before the actual outbreak of the illness:
``Es war mir damals recht bang um’s Herz, aber noch klar im Kopf.''
(\emph{Krafft}-\emph{Ebing}: Die Melancholie. Eine klinische Studie.
Erlangen 1874. p.~57).} But the sick man involuntarily searches for the
cause of his painful feelings. Only with great self-control can he hold
fast to the conviction that the only cause lies in his own morbid
state. Soon he forms an explanation for himself. He accuses those around
him or other innocent persons (especially when they have something
mysterious in their conduct, like the secret police, the Freemasons, the
Jesuits). He believes himself persecuted, misjudged and molested. These
ideas fix themselves in the sick man's consciousness especially when
they are favored by illusions and hallucinations.

These false ideas are an attempt to explain the new, altered and
abnormal feelings. The individual has been torn by his
morbid-passionate mood out of normal harmony with his surroundings;
feeling is now determined wholly from within and is no longer the
subjective announcement of man's position in the world. In a healthy
state, too, objectively unmotivated feelings can arise; but they then
easily find their correctives; what is morbid consists precisely in
their not being able to be corrected and governed. The altered feeling
now becomes the basis of a new conception of the world, in which the
sick man absorbs himself more and more and so constantly feeds his
despair.

But often, in the midst of the greatest despair, a sudden turn from
darkness to light can occur (see D, 2). By a certain impulse of
self-preservation the mind finds in an imagined world a substitute for
what was lost. ``Such a state of mind'', says
\emph{Ideler}\footnote{Biographien Geisteskranker. p.~18.} of a woman
who had gone mad from unhappy love, ``can, when some composure again
becomes possible, have only a twofold outcome: either the soul sinks
into the darkest melancholy, when the certainty of its loss presses it
% --- p. 364 ---
\opage{364}to the ground; or, if it does not lack the power of
resistance, it forces upon itself a delusion that promises it the
fulfillment of its warmest wishes\ldots\ The whole striving of the
madman then goes toward developing that imagining ever further in
accordance with the need of the heart\ldots\ and removing with
sophistical dialectic all contradictions into which it has come
\emph{over against} the real world.''


%% ============================================================
%%  VII. THE PSYCHOLOGY OF THE WILL (pp. 365--412)
%% ============================================================
% --- p. 365 ---
\chapter[The Psychology of the Will]{The Psychology of the Will\opage{365}}
\label{ch:will}

\section*{A. The Primitiveness of the Will}\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{A. The Primitiveness of the Will}

\textbf{1.} Just as Eros in Greek mythology appears both as one of the
oldest and as one of the youngest of the gods, so in psychology, according
to the point of view, the will can be presented as the most primitive and
as the most composite and derived of psychical manifestations. If we will
admit will only where there is a conscious choice between possibilities,
the will presupposes a higher development of knowledge and feeling and
stands as something derived in relation to these, so that it cannot be
given with the first awakening of consciousness. But all willing
presupposes a content, which must be given in order to be chosen. This
content must be developed and assert itself in and for consciousness. This
holds not least of the movements that are the condition of all outward
action, and it has therefore rightly been held that a germ of the will is
found in the capacity for independent movement, which is present even
before the awakening of consciousness. In order to understand the nature
of the will it will then be necessary to go back to this original germ and
follow the course of development from unconscious movement to conscious
but involuntary movement, and from this again to movement chosen with
consciousness.

\textbf{2.} Even the simplest organisms possess the capacity to set
themselves in motion without awaiting influence from outside. The source
of movement lies in the individual organism itself. Such an
``\emph{automatic}'' or ``spontaneous'' movement is of course not without a
cause. It is called forth by internal
% --- p. 366 ---
\opage{366}changes, through which accumulated potential energy is released.
Of this kind are the movements that an amoeba or a hydra constantly
performs, and which even seem to be peculiar to all organic cells, those
too that are elements in higher organisms (the white blood corpuscles, for
example). The amoeba has got its name (``change-animal'') from its
constant movements, motivated from within, and from this, in turn, such
constant change of form has been called amoeboid
movement.\footnote{\emph{Waldeyer}: Om Organismernes simpleste
Livsytringer [On the Simplest Vital Manifestations of Organisms].
(Tidsskrift for popul. Fremstillinger af Naturvidenskaben. 1878).
p.~245. 249. --- M. \emph{Forster}: Text Book of Physiology. p.~2. 74.}
The internal changes that release the potential energy must in turn
proceed from the activity of nutrition, which is the fundamental organic
process. Spontaneous movement in living beings is possible only because
life itself is a constant process, a taking up and working over of
elements.

But this in turn implies that spontaneity, the capacity for
self-movement, means only independence of the external influences of the
\emph{moment}, not total and constant independence of them. Life rests on
a definite relation of interaction between the organism and its
surroundings, and it soon ceases when this relation is entirely
abolished. An absolute spontaneity would be a sucking of one's paw, which
can keep life going only for a short while.
% TR: "Sugen paa Lappen" (sucking the paw, as the hibernating bear was
% believed to do): living on one's own stored reserves.

Spontaneity is therefore bound up with irritability, the capacity to
answer external influences in a peculiar way, i.e.\ by a movement of
another strength, and perhaps also of another kind, than the impression.
Only through this capacity does independence benefit the organism, since
adaptation to circumstances becomes possible. The ultimate ground of
irritability, too, must be sought in the organic process, in particular
in the great decomposability of organic matter. ``A truth,'' says
\emph{Pflüger},\footnote{Ueber die physiologische Verbrennung in den
lebendigen Organismen. (Pflüger’s Archiv für Physiol. 10ter Bd. 1875).
p.~311.} ``which meets all biologists at every step, is the altogether
astonishing decomposability of all living matter\ldots.\ This
decomposability is the cause of irritability. Is it not in truth
vanishingly small vis vivas which, acting
% --- p. 367 ---
\opage{367}in a ray of light, call forth the mightiest effects in the
retina and in the brain? Is not the gentle vibration produced by the
point of a needle passing over an exposed muscle sufficient to occasion
an instantaneous contraction, with simultaneous formation of carbonic
acid and lactic acid? How altogether minimal are the vis vivas of the
nerves, by whose help they are able to heighten the processes, and hence
also the chemistry, in the organs in the mightiest way?''
% TR: "Opløselighed" presumably renders Pflüger's "Zersetzbarkeit"
% (decomposability); "levende Kræfter" = lebendige Kräfte, vis viva.

\textbf{3.} In higher organisms too, endowed with a central nervous
system, it is justified to distinguish between spontaneity and
irritability. It does not seem possible to explain all the movements of
such an organism as reflex movements. The potential energy stored up in
the central ganglia can be discharged without any influence through
centripetal nerves. This holds in the greatest degree of the centers of
respiration and circulation situated in the medulla oblongata. An
immediate influence of the altered state of the blood (through the
accumulation of carbonic acid) takes the place of influence from
outside. The blood acts as a stimulant at the same time as it acts as
nourishment. Likewise the condition of the blood can immediately affect
higher centers, whereby automatic ideas (dream-images, hallucinations)
and movements arise.

% footnote *) on p. 367
\emph{Alexander Bain}\footnote{The Senses and the Intellect. Book I.
Chap 1. --- Emotions and Will. II. Chap. 1.} has maintained that the first
movement is always of this kind, and that this kind of movement plays a
part in every form of movement. The energy stored up by the process of
nutrition itself seeks discharge, and finds it along the motor nerve-paths
that are originally laid down. The organism sets itself in motion without
waiting for impulses from outside. Bain, who in this carries further what
Joh.\ \emph{Müller} had indicated, names as supports for this view the
first movements of the fetus, --- awakening that is not due to impressions
from outside, --- the strong impulse to movement in young animals and
children, --- the greater liveliness and delight in movement in men and
animals in the morning and after good nourishment, --- and finally the
peculiar energy that some individuals possess, what might be called the
``temperament of will'' (volitional
% --- p. 368 ---
\opage{368}constitution). Movement thus precedes sense-perception and
originally sets in independently of external influences. It is more
intimately and inseparably bound up with our nature than
sense-perception is. A man is capable of extraordinary activity
independently of what he sees, hears and thinks; his sense-perception
and thinking have importance as determining the direction of the
movement, but do not call it forth in the first place. --- \emph{Fichte}
already taught that what is original in us is an urge to action; this
urge is given prior to the consciousness of the actual world and cannot
be derived from it.

In most recent times \emph{Preyer} has joined Bain's view. --- ``How
does the \emph{first} embryonic movement come about? That it cannot be
caused by passive contact I have learned from a close observation of the
chick in the egg, which, as I found, moves from the fifth day of
incubation on. Here the trunk moves first, then also the limbs and head,
without the least change in the surroundings, and long before reflex
irritability is present at all. The cause of these remarkable primitive
movements of the body of unborn animals must therefore lie in themselves,
and cannot be derived from a reaction of the superficial parts upon the
central ones\ldots.\ The cerebrum has no part whatever in this, since
brainless monsters and headless animal embryos move their limbs in the
same way. In the formation of the motor ganglia in the spinal cord and
cervical cord a certain quantity of potential energy must be stored up,
which already through the flow of blood or of lymph, or even through the
rapidly advancing formation of tissue, is uncommonly easily
% footnote *) on p. 368
converted into kinetic energy.''\footnote{Die Seele des Kindes. p.~127
f.}
% TR: the print reads "fra den femte Dags Bevægelse af" (literally "from
% the fifth day's movement on"), apparently garbled; rendered "from the
% fifth day of incubation on".  Worth checking against Preyer.

The independence of sense-impressions that these automatic movements
express cannot, of course, be complete. Just as important as it is that
the activities most significant for the maintenance of life can be
initiated without waiting for external impulses, just so important is it,
on the other hand, that an adaptation to external circumstances can take
place. Just as important as it is that the organism from the first comes
forward actively over against its surroundings, just so important is it
that it also lets its activity be determined by
% --- p. 369 ---
\opage{369}the nature of its environment. Even before the awakening of
consciousness such adaptation and determination can take place by means
of reflex actions. (Cf.\ II, 4 b). In these it is not the immediate inner
state that arouses the movement, but an impression from the surroundings
or from a part of the organism. By a purely mechanical route more or
less purposive movements are thereby released in answer to impressions.
There has been dispute as to whether such purposive reflex movements are
connected with consciousness or not; but even if a dawning consciousness
accompanies them (as probably already in the fetal state), they are not
the fruit of conscious deliberation. Elementary feelings and sensations
may perhaps be present; but they are subjected to no further working
over. What is characteristic of reflex action is precisely the immediate
transition from impression to movement. Reflex action takes place just
as involuntarily as spontaneous movement.

The simplest reflex movement would be one released by a single
impression. As soon as several impressions coincide, their effect will
depend on whether the movements that each of them strives to release
harmonize or not. If the impressions tend to release different and
incompatible movements, it depends on which of them is the strongest;
this is no doubt somewhat weakened by the resistance, but nevertheless
determines the result. A frog deprived of its cerebrum croaks when the
skin of its back is gently stroked; but when at the same time its hind
foot is strongly irritated, the croak fails to come. The most important
inhibitions of reflexes proceed from the cerebrum. In the first period of
the child's life, when this organ does not yet intervene, no reflexes are
therefore inhibited.

\textbf{4.} When the cerebrum takes part in determining the movement, a
\emph{spontaneity} of a \emph{higher order} than that elementary one
appears. Through its wealth of cells this organ can both subject the
impressions propagated to it to a thorough working over and initiate
active movements independently of the impressions of the moment. In the
movements that arise here, consciousness undoubtedly asserts itself.

In so far as a boundary can be drawn between reflex movement and
instinct, it must be marked by instinct's being more composite, more
active and more conscious than reflex
% --- p. 370 ---
\opage{370}movement. Instinctive movements appear as a combined system of
means for attaining an end that lies beyond the momentary horizon of the
individual, and whose realization it perhaps does not even live to see
itself. It is not an instantaneous discharge, as in elementary
spontaneity and simple reflex movement, but an application of various
forces toward a more or less distant end. Impressions are necessary to
call forth the instinctive action, but this is nevertheless determined
far more by the tendencies to movement laid down in the organization
than by the impression. The impression acts only as the opening of a
valve. Hence the animal is easily deceived, as when insects, deceived by
the smell, lay their eggs in the carrion-flower. The impulse is so strong
that the impression is subjected to no control. When the instinct is not
immediately satisfied, it takes on the character of a drive and appears
as an obscure feeling, which is attached to certain indistinct ideas and
turns into displeasure when the movement is inhibited (cf.\ VI B, 2 c).

It is not clear whether instinct is bound to the cerebrum. \emph{Flourens}
already showed that removal of the cerebrum abolished instincts such as
the nutritive drive and the sexual drive. Goltz reports of several of his
dogs that, after larger parts of the cerebral cortex had been removed,
they no longer showed, as before, any aversion to eating dog's flesh. But
on the other hand, instincts manifest themselves clearly even in beings
in whom the cerebrum plays no part (the nutritive instinct of newborn
children). It is therefore probable that instinctive movements can also
proceed from the midbrain (the corpora striata and optic
thalami).\footnote{Vulpian: Physiol. du syst. nerv. p.~692 f.}

On the other hand, \emph{will proper} is bound to the cerebrum. Will
proper is psychologically characterized by ideas of the purpose of the
action and of the ways that lead to its realization, and by a lively
feeling of the value of the purpose. The phenomena of will are therefore
so intimately bound up with knowledge and feeling that only by renewing
the errors of phrenology could one ascribe to them an independent center.
On this the most recent investigators, too, are agreed (see II, 4d). It is
otherwise with the
% --- p. 371 ---
\opage{371}question of the initiation of movements. If those investigators
are right who assume motor centers within the cerebrum, the transition
from organs of consciousness to organs of movement takes place already
here, since those centers are necessary intermediate links for the
conduction of the impulses of will to the motor
nerve-paths.\footnote{\emph{Wundt}. I. p.~156 f.} If, on the other hand,
those are right who with Goltz deny the right to assume centers of
movement in the cerebrum, that transition first takes place through the
connecting paths between the cerebrum and the more deeply lying
% footnote **) on p. 371
parts of the brain.\footnote{\emph{Goltz} in Pflügers Archiv. 26de Bd.
1881. p.~36--37. --- Munk: Ueber die Functionen der Grosshirnsrinde.
p.~52.}

\textbf{5.} The prehistory of the will necessarily had to lead us beyond
psychology, in accordance with the general law that the unconscious
precedes the conscious. But on the other hand we understand this
prehistory rightly only when we know the manifestations of will from our
own consciousness. The task now is to show the psychological connection
of the will with the other elements of consciousness.
