Psychologically we speak of will wherever we become conscious of an
activity and do not behave in a purely receptive way. But according to
what the psychology of knowledge and of feeling has taught us, we never
behave in a purely receptive way. In all sense-perception and thinking,
and in all feeling, a self-activity stirs. Only if it were possible to
conceive consciousness as a series of sensations with passive
after-effects would we arrive at an absolute passivity. But an absolutely
passive sensation stands as an abstraction, which is only approximately
realized in certain abnormal states. Since it has turned out to be
precisely the fundamental peculiarity of consciousness that all its
individual elements and states are united in a synthetic activity, the
very existence of consciousness may be said to be an activity of will.

The stronger the individual sensations and ideas are, each taken by
itself, the more this activity of will recedes. Exclusive sensations tend
to call forth hypnotic states. \emph{Bonnet} rightly said that a being
which throughout its whole life had only one single sensation, and had it
only in one single degree, would
% --- p. 372 ---
\opage{372}have no will at all. We may add that it would have no
consciousness either (see II, 5). Only at the moment when the sensation
itself set in would an elementary activity of will be aroused, as the
attention turned to the impression. No impression occurs without laying
claim to attention and calling forth a greater or lesser activity, which
helps to apprehend it as clearly and distinctly as possible. Along with
the sensation we also become more or less conscious of this
\emph{involuntary attention}; in any case it helps to give the momentary
state its character. This appears with particular energy when the simple
sensation already calls forth a strong feeling, such as astonishment or
fright.

The alternation of sensations brings a somewhat higher form of activity
of will to light. When a new sensation turns up, it will be more or less
welcome according to the relation in which it stands to its
predecessors. If it stands in glaring contrast to them, or if for other
reasons it arouses displeasure, the mind will strive to push it back and
turn away from it. It is otherwise if (as with complementary colors) it
brings a beneficial variety, or for other reasons carries with it an
element of pleasure. These elements of pleasure and displeasure are of
course most often so weak that we do not become clearly conscious of
them, and yet in each individual case they determine the way in which
things look to us, since they guide an involuntary selection among the
impressions that present themselves to us. Just as the plant moves toward
the light, so our observations move toward what arouses pleasure and
interest and away from the opposite.

The transition from involuntary to \emph{voluntary attention} and
selection takes place as memory and free imagination develop (cf.\ V B).
The choice among the impressions presenting themselves can now be
determined by earlier experiences. From being a mere drive, attention
becomes a process of selection guided by a conscious purpose. We set
ourselves, e.g.\ to follow certain tones or a certain theme through a
piece of music, or to observe the timbre of a single instrument. The
investigations into physiological time have shown how great an influence
strained attention can
% --- p. 373 ---
\opage{373}have on the speed of observation, indeed that it can even
anticipate observation (cf.\ VI E, 4 a). The difference between
involuntary and voluntary attention lies here in this, that in voluntary
attention the strain, the directing of the powers of the mind in a
certain direction, is present prior to the impression, while in
involuntary attention this strain is first aroused by the impression
itself. We see to a great extent what we will to see, and on the whole
we can see only when we will to see. The original sanguine tendency in
human nature anticipates experience and only gradually, often
reluctantly, lets itself be taught by it. Fortunately experience is able
to get us to open our eyes, compels us to will to see. But activity of
will is always the presupposition.

Voluntary attention can also be directed to mere ideas, images of
memory or of fantasy. The endeavor to call up and hold fast such images is
connected with a sensation of effort similar to that felt when we try to
observe an obscure object. Yet this sensation seems to be differently
localized. \emph{Fechner} has pointed out --- what everyone's own
observation will confirm --- that while in voluntary observation of
external phenomena the strain is directed toward the sense organ that
plays the chief part, in the activity of memory or fantasy the strain
withdraws entirely from the external sense organs and appears as a strain
and contraction of the scalp and a pressure, going from outside inward,
on the whole
% footnote *) on p. 373
skull.\footnote{Elemente der Psychophysik. II. p.~475. 491. --- The
expression of a person absorbed in his thoughts is especially
characterized by the indefinite direction of the eyes; their lines of
sight often even become divergent, because of the relaxation of certain
eye-muscles, in consequence of the strain having moved to other organs.
The head is commonly lowered, likewise because of a muscular relaxation.
\emph{Darwin}: Expr.\ of Emot. p.~229.} The muscular strain that asserts
itself here is, however, not always present, and in any case does not
constitute the whole physiological process connected with attention.
Possibly impulses go back from the centers to which the voluntary
concentration of consciousness is bound to the centers of
sense-perception (just as they otherwise go to the centers of movement),
whereby the
% --- p. 374 ---
% footnote *) on p. 374
\opage{374}activity of the latter can be strengthened.\footnote{\emph{Wundt}
I. p.~218 f.} This would be the physiological form of the psychological
fact that an idea stands out more clearly before us through our setting
ourselves the task of imagining it. Some individuals can even call forth
hallucinations in themselves at will. Finally, \emph{Carpenter} has
sought to ground the assumption that in attention, as in all activity of
will, there takes place a flow of blood to the organ whose function is
strengthened. In the endeavor to hold fast an idea or a train of thought
there thus takes place an increased flow of blood to certain parts of the
cerebrum (ideational hyperaemia). With this agrees the influence that
every emotion has on the circulation of the blood. In every act of
willing an emotion stirs.\footnote{Mental physiology. p.~382 f.}

An activity of will asserts itself not least in the preservation of the
coherence among our ideas and in all thinking. It is the presupposition
of the arrangement of the elements of consciousness not coming to be
governed by purely accidental associations of ideas. Just as the waking
state is distinguished from the sleeping one by the stronger ``latent
innervation'' that keeps the body from approaching a posture determined
by mere gravity, so waking consciousness differs from dream-consciousness
in that all thoughts are more or less consciously directed toward one
end. In its most primitive form this end is orientation in the
surroundings, in order to find means for preserving existence. But even
at the highest stages of spiritual development a purpose, and a feeling
aroused by it, governs the course of thought. The more such a spiritual
center of gravity is lacking, the more incoherent the life of
consciousness becomes, until at last it dissolves into momentarily
shifting ideas.

In both involuntary and voluntary attention, feeling and will work
immediately together. Several of the phenomena belonging here could
therefore already be mentioned under the psychology of feeling (cf.\ VI
B, 2 c.\ 7--9.\ 11. --- D, 4--6. --- E, 2). The unity of psychical life
comes clearly to light here when one considers the importance of
attention for
% --- p. 375 ---
\opage{375}sense-perception and thinking, the intimate connection of
feeling and will, and the connection of feeling and idea, which lies
deeper than all association of ideas (V B, 8 c. --- VI E).

\textbf{6 a.} Before we follow the development of the will further in its
interaction with the life of knowledge and of feeling, we must see how
the will gradually gains power over bodily movement. This is the first
important course of exercises the will has to run through, so important
that \emph{ideas of movement} have even often been regarded as an
essential element in all conscious will. Apparently all action is
directed outward; but it nevertheless always presupposes an inner action,
a determination of the ideas by the thought of the purpose. The thought
of the purpose is thus the most important element, and it is only a
special, if frequent, case when it is ideas of movement that are
attracted and determined by the thought of the purpose.

The presupposition for the formation of ideas of movement is that
movements have been performed. As we have seen, the organism does not
wait for impulses from outside to set itself in motion; it is itself a
little world, which can produce impulses in its own interior. These
spontaneous movements, together with the reflex movements, are the
material that underlies the ideas of movement. By an involuntary urge we
are thus led to have the experiences that are necessary for the
development of voluntary movement.

Just as the psychology of knowledge begins with sense-sensation, so the
psychology of the will ends with the impulse to movement. What precedes
sense-sensation and what follows the impulse to movement we come to know
only by roundabout ways. In the simplest voluntary movements
sense-sensation and impulse to movement immediately coincide. The state
of consciousness present in involuntary movements consists, as already
remarked, in a feeling of pleasure or displeasure with indefinite
sense-sensations and a certain restlessness called forth by the awakening
urge to movement, a restlessness that rises to pain when the movement is
hindered. But through the sensations of movement (cf.\ V A, 7) and
through observation of our own movements by the help of sight and touch,
memory-images of the movements performed are formed. Between these
% --- p. 376 ---
\opage{376}memory-images and the motivating state of consciousness a
natural connection is formed. If the movement has had unfortunate,
pain-causing consequences, its memory-image will be connected with
displeasure, which can hinder the repetition of the movement when the
same state of consciousness sets in again. On the other hand, there will
be a tendency to repeat movements whose performance and consequences have
been connected with pleasure.

What makes possible the will's mastery over bodily movements is thus,
first, the connection between the feeling of pleasure or displeasure and
a certain impulse to movement, and second, the more precise determination
of this impulse to movement by memory-images of movement (ideas of
movement). If these two conditions were not possible, all outward
voluntary movement would be impossible. But on them too rests the whole
development of outward voluntary action from its lowest stage to the
highest.

When attention has once been directed to an idea of movement, as we have
prepared ourselves to perform the movement, the performance takes place
easily and quickly. This preparation or inner readiness, by which the
movement is as it were adopted or fixed in consciousness, as we become
completely one with the idea of movement and the corresponding feeling,
--- cannot be described more closely. It is the fundamental element in
the consciousness of a willed movement and can be known only by immediate
self-observation, in the same way as the inner process by which we call
up and hold fast an idea or a train of thought. Physiologically regarded,
it no doubt rests on a similar process.

The investigations into physiological time have shown that when one has
once prepared oneself to perform a certain movement as soon as a definite
signal is given, the movement sets in involuntarily, without its release
taking any time, and without any new impulse of will being needed. On the
other hand it takes some time if one wants to cancel that state of
preparation again, or as it were to annul the first impulse of will; and
if the signal occurs before this has succeeded, the movement can be
released without or against our will. It happens, e.g., that a signal
awaited with strained attention fails to come, and that the experimenter
turns round impatiently to see whether
% --- p. 377 ---
\opage{377}the apparatus is not out of order; now the signal sounds, and
with it follows the willed movement, although one had already given
% footnote *) on p. 377
it up.\footnote{Sigm.\ \emph{Exner}: Experimentelle Untersuchungen der
einfachsten psychischen Processe. (Pflügers Archiv. 1873). p.~616 f.
(Also in Hermann’s Handbuch. II, 2. p.~286 f.).} By preparing oneself to
perform the movement on the supposition that a certain impression will
come, one has thus brought oneself into a state analogous to that in
which the animal guided by an instinct finds itself. The willed action is
released just like the instinctive one; the difference between them lies
in what goes before.

\textbf{b.} Nature paves the way for our will. But nature gives us both
too much and too little. The original spontaneous movement is strong; but
it must be guided in a definite direction and adapted as regards degree
and form in order to serve our purposes. In involuntary movements several
muscles are set in activity at once. Here the task is then sometimes to
dissolve these accompanying movements and to form others in their place.
Under the influence of the laws of voluntary movement mentioned, thus
under the discipline of pleasure and pain, a process of selection takes
place, which leads partly to \emph{isolation}, partly to
\emph{combination} of movements.

Thus the vocal organs are moved at first involuntarily, as the child
gives vent to its displeasure or its well-being. But of the sounds so
produced, those are later held fast especially which prove to bring
agreeable effects. This is the child's first language; only after it
comes the period in which it tries to imitate what it hears. It goes
similarly with many other bodily movements; they are first produced
involuntarily and at random, and then they are held fast and repeated, or
inhibited and displaced. At first no reflex is inhibited; but discipline
pushes back more and more of them (as when the child is accustomed to
cleanliness). The infant, in whom the cerebrum does not yet actively
intervene, lacks a central apparatus of inhibition, which is the
condition of self-control. The development of voluntary movement
presupposes a certain development of ideas, and hence also of the brain.

% --- p. 378 ---
\opage{378}The child performs many movements quite instinctively as soon
as it has strength enough for them. Sucking, biting, smacking, chewing and
licking are, according to \emph{Preyer}, just as instinctive as the
chick's pecking at grains and insects. The same holds for the most part
also of sitting, standing, crawling, walking and running. Imitation plays
a subordinate part here too; it has most importance as encouragement.
Even a child who had never seen anyone crawl or walk would perform these
movements as soon as it had strength
% footnote *) on p. 378
enough.\footnote{\emph{Preyer}: Die Seele des Kindes. p.~146--176.}

Voluntary movement proper sets in when these movements are called forth
with a definite intention and guided toward a definite end. The first and
most important examples of this are the grasping movements, where the
wish to appropriate an object makes the hand move toward it and close
around it. The limit of how far the isolation and combination of
movements can go lies in the original organization. There are
accompanying movements that cannot be dissolved, and independent
movements that cannot be combined. --- How far practice and adaptation
can go is seen from the fact that the grown-together Siamese twins had
brought their movements into agreement to such a degree that they walked,
ran and jumped as necessity required, without making any arrangement,
just as if they formed only one single individual.

\textbf{c.} This process, by which the will becomes master of the body in
such a way that the individual can come forward with energy and
compactness over against its surroundings, takes place more slowly in man
than in the animal. Kittens go through the course of education necessary
for them in less than a month, while children need almost two years for
theirs. This difference shows the importance of the innate foundation.
This importance appears just as clearly when we compare normal and
idiot children. The latter are not able, without special and laborious
guidance, to learn the use of their organs of sense and movement.

``Even in the lesser degrees the idiot is easily recognized by the lack
of a real gaze and of a firm bearing. The
% --- p. 379 ---
\opage{379}lack of gaze shows itself in some idiots as a dead staring, in
others as a wild rolling of the eyes; the lack of a firm bearing in some
in the form of a persistent immobility and torpor, with a certain
inclination to monotonous swinging or circular movements of single parts
of the body, in others in the form of an everlasting restlessness and
purposeless working of the arms and legs.''\footnote{\emph{Eschricht}: Om
Muligheden af at helbrede og opdrage Idioter [On the Possibility of
Curing and Educating Idiots]. p.~7.} Education here too therefore
consists in a selection, carried out partly by strengthening some
movements, partly by inhibiting and neutralizing others. Sluggishness is
to be counteracted, and the restless and disordered play of the muscles
tamed. In education one then commonly starts from the given foundation.
Thus an idiot girl who ceaselessly rocked her body and swung her arms or
legs was got to wind yarn, whereby the restless movement was guided to a
definite end, which, through the pleasure it gave, could afterward itself
motivate the movement. The excessively strong movement was sought to be
inhibited by having the patient led up and down in the garden until
fatigue compelled her to keep entirely
% footnote **) on p. 379 (continues on p. 381)
still.\footnote{Op.\ cit. p.~66.}

\section*{B. The Will and the Other Elements of Consciousness}\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{B. The Will and the Other Elements of Consciousness}

\textbf{1.} The higher development of the will, \emph{conditioned by the
development of knowledge and feeling}.

\textbf{a.} We cannot, as has often been remarked in the foregoing,
conceive of any consciousness that is entirely dissolved into single,
momentary sensations. A certain degree of memory, and with it also a
certain duality, a weaker or stronger contrast between two currents, must
always be assumed to be present. But the relation between the two
currents can vary without end. We have already seen that the contrast
between sense-perception and thinking runs through a whole scale of
degrees, and that the same was the case with the contrast between
physical and ideal feelings. In the domain of the will we meet a related
contrast: between drive and willing proper.

% --- p. 380 ---
\opage{380}\emph{Drive} had already to be described within the psychology
of feeling (VI B, 2 c), and we have likewise mentioned it in the inquiry
into the primitiveness of the will; instinct, involuntary attention and
the urge to movement are different manifestations of drive, when the word
drive is taken in the widest sense.

Already in drive consciousness is freed from the impression of the
moment. A striving beyond the momentary state announces itself; the
equilibrium is abolished. Drive is psychologically conditioned by a more
or less clear idea of what can heighten the pleasure of the moment or
diminish its pain becoming connected with the momentary feeling and
sensation. Drive presupposes a contrast between the actual and something
possible or future. By this drive differs from reflex movement and
instinct (A, 4), where the impression is perhaps sensed, but where an idea
of what ought now to follow does not assert itself. In drive proper there
always lies a more or less conscious demand. --- In a wider sense of the
word, drive comprises all tendencies to movement connected with feeling
and sensation. It would be best if in psychology we could avoid using the
word drive otherwise than in the narrowest sense, of something different
from reflex movement and instinct. But usage does not allow us to; we
cannot, e.g., avoid speaking in psychology too of a drive of
self-preservation, a drive to movement, etc., as a short and
comprehensive designation for tendencies to movement that are partly
unconscious, partly half conscious. And to this is added, as will have
become clear from the foregoing, that experience shows us manifold
transitions between the different stages and kinds of movement. These
can to that extent be presented as different forms of development of
drive, which culminate in \emph{desire}, where the purpose of the movement
is an object of distinct consciousness. --- Drive proper and desire are
distinguished from mere instinct in that they contain an idea of the end,
but not always of the means, while instinct leads to the application of
means for furthering an unconscious end. Instinct is a manifestation of
drive in so far as the actions and movements that lead to the end become
objects of idea and interest.

It is a fateful turning point when a definite idea is connected with the
feeling of pleasure and displeasure and thereby
% --- p. 381 ---
\opage{381}becomes the expression of the object of drive. (Cf.\ VI B,
2a--b). The movement is thereby guided in a definite direction and will
not be able to be altered without a definite exertion of force in another
direction. As soon as drive is aroused, the equilibrium is abolished, and
it then depends on whether one can master the movement. --- It is not
those who know, nor yet the ignorant, who strive after knowledge; and
whether this striving is aroused depends on whether ignorance is felt with
displeasure and with the idea of something that is better than ignorance.
--- It is at this stage that revolutions arise in the inner and the outer
world. The greatest degree of suffering inhibits and strikes to the
ground; only when so much relief and progress has been attained that the
idea of a better state can assert itself --- however it may stand with
the means of attaining it --- only then does the movement break out. It
is neither the free nor the unfree who make revolution, but the half
free. Hence, as \emph{Tocqueville} has remarked, the most dangerous moment
for a bad government is when it begins to reform itself. The slightest
arbitrary acts on the part of Louis XVI seemed harder to bear than the
whole of Louis XIV's
% footnote *) on p. 381
despotism.\footnote{L’ancien régime et la révolution. Livre II. chap.
1. --- Livre III. chap. 4.} The most recent history of Russia offers
entirely corresponding examples.

\textbf{b.} If the life of ideas is somewhat more developed, thoughts of
purposes can arise whose attainment would be connected with pleasure, but
which nevertheless do not immediately set drive in motion. Such purposes,
and the feelings determined by them, correspond to the free memory-images
in the domain of knowledge: just as these need not be called forth by the
momentary sensations, so those need not lead at once to action. It is by
this that wish differs from drive. To have wishes is, from a purely
practical point of view, a luxury. Wish is contemplative in relation to
drive. But on the other hand wish can be the first form of drive. What at
first stands as a distant possibility, filling the mind with pleasure at
the mere thought, can, when by being a constant thought it passes more
into flesh and blood, arouse a drive.

% --- p. 382 ---
\opage{382}But the same higher intellectual development that makes wish
possible will also call forth the consciousness of the importance of
drive's not being immediately followed by action, but of there being an
\emph{interval} between thought and execution, so that the ideas that are
naturally connected with the thought of the purpose can come forward and
gain influence on the action. (Cf.\ IV, 4--6). Such an interval can arise
simply through the action's being hindered and experience teaching that
it was good that it was not carried out; its importance can also be
impressed upon us by our growing wise through harm, namely by seeing what
hasty action leads to; and it can finally be brought about by the idea of
the purpose standing in such close connection with other ideas (e.g.\ the
idea of the necessary means) that these at once turn up and thereby
inhibit the impulse to movement. In each case --- whether it is the
consequences of the action or the means to its execution that assert
themselves --- it is the laws of the association of ideas that are at
work. Jeppe would dearly like to drink for one more shilling; but his
back reminds him of the consequences. ``My belly says, you shall; my
back, you shall not.'' It is the association of ideas and the feelings
aroused by it that come forward as inhibiting over against the drive or
wish of the moment.
% TR: Jeppe is the drunken peasant of Holberg's comedy "Jeppe paa
% Bjerget" (1722), hesitating before the innkeeper's door (Act I).

Here there takes place, as regards the will, the same turn as when
feeling passes over from physical to ideal feeling. The action is
determined by more comprehensive considerations than drive and wish
permit. Naturally these more remote considerations too must first assert
themselves in the form of drive or wish; but the decisive thing is that a
single incitement does not become immediately and solely determining. The
process that is thereby begun, of which Jeppe's struggle with himself
before the innkeeper's door is a simple example, can develop into higher
forms the more comprehensive the associations of ideas become. Here the
% footnote *) on p. 382
strength of memory,\footnote{``Our purpose is bound to our memory'' ---
or, as it says more energetically in the English text (Hamlet Act III.
Sc.~2): »Purpose is but \emph{the slave} to memory«. --- Cf.\
\emph{Spinoza}: Eth.\ III. Schol: ``We can do nothing by the decision of
the mind unless we hold it fast in memory.'' (Nihil ex mentis decreto
agere possumus, nisi ejus recordemur).} the life of fantasy and
% --- p. 383 ---
\opage{383}the clarity of thought become of immediate importance for the
development of the will. The more firmly and clearly the thought of more
comprehensive purposes than those of the moment, or the thought of the
difficulty or the calamitous character of the action demanded by drive
and wish, stands out before consciousness, the sooner will the momentary
incitement be inhibited and the will be determined by more remote or
higher considerations. Even if the wish stirs, it now becomes a ``pious''
wish, as the consciousness of the impossibility or impropriety of
realizing it asserts itself. There is then here a trial of strength
between the logic of drive and the higher logic. Drive, according to its
nature, goes straight at its object and is able to rake up sophisms
against other considerations. (Cf.\ VI E, 2). Jeppe asks himself: ``Is
not my belly more than my back? I think so,'' --- and consoles himself at
last that Jacob the Shoemaker will give him credit, which he knows very
well he will not. The more the wish fixes itself, the more difficult it
is for other thoughts and feelings to inhibit its unfolding. The wish
then becomes a resolve. Psychologically it depends only on the strength,
not on the value, of the forces that determine the action. The momentary
incitement can be pernicious; but it can also be ethically justified and
yet succumb, as when enthusiasm is displaced by egoistic and prosaic
considerations. It then depends on what thoughts and memories the idea
connected with the drive arouses, and what strength of feeling these
command in relation to the immediate incitement.

\textbf{c.} When the momentary incitements rule, there is really no inner
center, no ego in consciousness. But the more memory, and the ruling
feelings determined by it, assert themselves, the more the man's whole
nature, and not merely a single side of it that momentarily
predominates, gains influence on the action. A man's real ego has its
expression in the thoughts and feelings that have taken the deepest root
in him in the course of life. (Cf.\ V B, 5). And only when the action has
been determined by this firm kernel can the man be said to have willed
his action, to have determined himself. The will is in so far determined
by the ruling passion, as the expression of the result that
% --- p. 384 ---
\opage{384}the man's development has deposited up to the moment of action.
Now it is the elements of knowledge, now the elements of feeling, that
predominate, so that one can distinguish between a will of thought and a
will of feeling. We must remember here that it is not always the feeling
most violent at the moment that is in reality the strongest, i.e.\ has
the deepest root in the individual's nature. All the more important is
it that the moment should not become solely determining; hence, again,
the formation of the interval mentioned is so important. It is filled by
deliberation, the inner debate in consciousness, in which all the ideas
and feelings psychologically possible for the individual can have a say.
Through this debate the mere resolve becomes \emph{decision}, and a
choice is made among the possibilities at hand. The die is cast.

This is the highest form of willing. It is predominantly determined from
within, not by the single sensation. Drive knows only one possibility,
one motive; will proper unfolds itself through the interaction or
struggle of several motives and possibilities. It is often determined by
something that lies far beyond the moment, indeed beyond the whole
actuality of the individual, but which nevertheless asserts itself in the
individual's consciousness, is represented within it. The psychological
understanding of the voluntary action depends on whether it is possible
to trace the individual's course of development and follow it step by
step to the moment of action, each single transition being explained by
the general psychological laws. This is of course an ideal; but it states
the principle and the presupposition of all psychological inquiry and of
all practical dealings among men.

\textbf{2.} \emph{The reaction of the will} upon \emph{knowledge and
feeling}.

Such a reaction takes place at all stages of the will's development. We
are thereby led into a web of psychological processes that it is
impossible to unravel and survey. We must be glad to find certain guiding
points of view, and, referring to earlier sections as regards the
elementary phenomena, we shall here chiefly bring out how the more highly
developed will can act in a determining and guiding way upon knowledge
and feeling.

% --- p. 385 ---
\opage{385}\textbf{a.} \emph{The reaction of the will upon knowledge}.
Just as with outward movement, nature also helps us to get going in the
activity of ideas and thought before we ourselves can consciously
intervene. The will is nowhere creative, but altering and selecting. And
this altering and choosing is in turn determined by the will's own
nature, so that Shakspere's word is confirmed, that ``over that art which you
say adds to nature, is an art that nature makes.'' ---
We cite a few single examples of this.

1) It is, as shown earlier, only through manifold disappointments that
we work our way forward to the apprehension of what is real. Prejudices,
passions and unclear observations easily lead us astray. And besides,
involuntary hallucinations and illusions can sometimes arise, with which,
when the kernel of the mind is otherwise sound, a hard struggle is
fought. One such sufferer struggled for 27 years with hallucinations that
tempted him to lay violent hands on other people; even his best friends
suspected nothing before the day came when he declared himself overcome
and sought out a lunatic asylum. His physician says: ``When he told us
the torments that had assailed his soul in this long series of years, we
could not but wonder \emph{that} he had not succumbed much earlier. Seven
years have now passed since his apparent cure; he has been able to attain
a high position, but the inner struggle has not
ceased.''\footnote{\emph{Brierre de Boismont}: Des hallucinations. 3 éd.
p.~525.} --- Conversely, some persons can call forth hallucinations in
themselves at will, whereby it then often goes with them as with Goethe's
``Zauberlehrling,'' in that the phantoms gain power over them and cannot
be driven away.\footnote{Op.\ cit. p.~27. 39. 462 f.}
% TR: the emphasized word in the physician's sentence is "over" in the
% print ("undre os over, at"); the emphasis is put on "that".

2) The course of memories and ideas is subject to definite laws. When we
call forth certain ideas at will, this happens only by virtue of these
same laws, just as it is only by virtue of the laws of external nature
that we can alter it and subject it to our purposes. The presupposition
of the will's intervention in the course of ideas is that a seeking, an
interest, asserts itself. The idea that has aroused this interest now
becomes a specially accented and energetic
% --- p. 386 ---
\opage{386}center of association (cf.\ VI E, 1), which is able to loosen
given associations of ideas and to form new ones. Isolation and
combination are the chief forms of the will's influence on the life of
ideas, as on outward movement. --- In the spiritual domain it is certain
that ``he that seeketh shall find''; for the very fact that there is
seeking marks a great spiritual advance, a concentration that will always
exert its influence, however great or small the result may turn out to
be. --- This seeking arises when, of two connected ideas a and b, one has
vanished or is unknown to us, but we notice an empty place; attention is
then concentrated on a, whereby all connections of similarity and of
contiguity with a are favored. Hereby an impulse is given which ---
often, however, only through unconscious after-effects --- can lead to the
goal. --- The will acts here, in Goldschmidt's apt expression,
\emph{boringly}.\footnote{\emph{Goldschmidt}: Erindringer [Memoirs]
p.~183 f.}
% TR: "borende": boring, as an auger bores; so "Boren" below and the
% will's "bore" in b, 3).

Such boring and voluntary intervention may be necessary, but does not
always lead to as valuable a goal as the involuntary promptings.
\emph{Willed} thoughts and fantasies have as a rule a more abstract and
pale character than those that turn up ``of themselves.'' Thinking goes
best when ``it thinks'' in us. It is of the greatest importance for
spiritual health and the right conduct of life that there be a harmonious
relation between the involuntary unfolding of the life of thought and
voluntary intervention. The harmonious relation is no doubt different for
each single individual.
% TR: the print has "\emph{Ville} de Tanker", evidently for "Villede
% Tanker" (willed thoughts) divided wrongly.

3) In moral respects it is of great importance that the thoughts and
ideas that underlie the conduct of life be brought forward as objects of
\emph{repeated} contemplation and deliberation.\footnote{Cf.\ on this
\emph{James Sully}: On some elements of moral self-culture. (In his book:
Sensation and Intuition. London 1874).} They thereby gain firmer ground
in consciousness, so that they are recalled more easily under the
changing circumstances of life and are displaced with more difficulty by
the impressions and affects of the moment. They gain at the same time in
clarity and coherence and thereby become better fitted to guide action.
Finally, only thereby can an anticipatory adaptation to more difficult
and more intricate circumstances of life take place.

% --- p. 387 ---
\opage{387}4) Besides the will's being able thus in single cases to gain
direct or indirect influence on the course of ideas, \emph{the
development of the life of will as a whole} acts back upon the life of
thought, strengthening and clarifying it. A firm decision, carried
through with determination and without wavering, cleanses the whole
spiritual atmosphere and drives away the mists that hinder the clarity of
thought. It takes courage to think; one must be not only free of
prejudice, but also free of fear. Consistency in thought and firmness in
character are related. And only through firm willing does real
self-consciousness become possible. What manifests itself in the unity
and coherence of memory and in the immediate feeling of self is completed
by the act of will, the strongest psychical experience proceeding from
within that we can have. Only in our self-activity do we become rightly
conscious of ourselves.

\textbf{b.} \emph{The reaction of the will upon feeling}. It might seem
to be so peculiar to feeling to arise and develop involuntarily that no
intervention of the will could be thought of here. The will's activity is
indeed even more indirect here than in the course of ideas, and what it
can accomplish here is surely also more limited and conditioned. But it
is nevertheless of great theoretical and practical interest to see what
ways stand open here.

1) Even if we cannot prevent a feeling from arising in us, we can
perhaps prevent it from spreading by \emph{inhibiting} the \emph{organic
movement} connected with the feeling, through which the feeling
strengthens itself. The art of self-control consists chiefly in this,
since it cannot immediately strike the feeling at its first stage. Help
in this is given by the presence of other people, which brings us ``to
pull ourselves together.'' Adam \emph{Smith}\footnote{Theory of Moral
Sentiments. Part.\ III. Sect.~3.} already stressed the importance of the
way in which the involuntary outburst and expression of feeling is
inhibited by the thought of other persons, really or ideally present.
Often this inhibiting perhaps goes too far and leads to a false shame or
to blasé indifference. But in any case here is a way by which the very
germ of the feeling can be struck, for it is weakened by being frequently
hindered in its organic spreading. On the other hand,
% --- p. 388 ---
\opage{388}the fact that the feeling is dammed up can also make it bore
itself so much the deeper into the man's nature. Which consequence
follows in the single cases will depend on the man's character; in the
long run, however, the inhibition of outburst and expression will surely
always have a reacting influence.

Conversely, a feeling can be aroused by one's beginning by putting
oneself in the posture corresponding to the feeling, by producing the
corresponding looks and movements. Savages excite themselves for battle
by violent dances. Participation in the external ceremonies can, in
Pascal's opinion, form the introduction to real conversion. One is
certainly in a different mood when one clenches one's hands than when one
folds them, --- when one stretches out one's arms than when one sets them
against one's breast. A characteristic contrast appears especially
between the mood under muscular tension and under muscular relaxation.
--- It is by this route that persons in a hypnotic state can be brought
by the experimenter into various moods.\footnote{\emph{Carpenter}: Mental
physiology. p.~602--605. --- \emph{Preyer}: Die Entdeckung des
Hypnotismus. p.~36--41. 85.} \emph{Campanella} maintained that by
imitating others' looks and movements one could also clearly enter into
their feelings. A stranger who came in to him found him writing a letter
with the same expression on his face as the man to whom he
% footnote **) on p. 388
was writing used to have.\footnote{Vita Campanellæ. Autore E. S.
Cypriano. Amstelod. 1722. p.~48. --- Cf.\ on this also \emph{Burke}: On
the sublime and beautiful. Part.\ IV. Sect.~4. --- \emph{Fechner}:
Vorschule der Æsthetik. I. p.~156 f.}

2) By \emph{alteration of external circumstances of life} the arising of
many feelings can be prevented, or they can at any rate be cut off from
nourishment. The power of customs and institutions rests on the influence
of the circumstances of life, and political reforms are indirectly
reforms of the life of feeling. Even our daily habits and surroundings
are often of great importance here. By bringing ourselves under certain
definite conditions we can further or inhibit the arising of certain
feelings. Much that our will cannot directly bring about is attained when
we bind ourselves in such a way that it is afterward not in our power to
release ourselves. There is a spiritual as well as a bodily hygiene.

% --- p. 389 ---
\opage{389}3) If the feeling cannot be altered in these ways, it may be
possible to succeed by directing attention to another \emph{end}. But when
this is to happen through one's own inner endeavor, it presupposes that
the momentary feeling does not lay claim to the whole of consciousness.
The will cannot ``bore'' without definite starting points. The first
condition is thus that there be seeking, that a ``hunger and thirst'' be
present. When the man is wholly absorbed in the present state, ``laughs
and is full'' (Luke VI, 25), no ground can be laid for any new feeling.
Yet the individual can sometimes feel the need of a change in his life of
feeling, but be unable without help to work toward it. Thus
\emph{Lichtenberg} wished for himself ``the first differential of a
push'' in order to get the better of his hypochondria.

The feelings for which a ground can be laid by exertion of the will all
belong, of course, to the ideal feelings. The endeavor of will develops
the life of feeling by the help of the laws of the association of ideas,
which make it possible that, once one new idea can be held fast although
it stands in conflict with the mighty feeling, several can soon follow
after. The feelings that are most easily remembered, and that suffer least
by repetition, are also those that can most easily be called forth by way
of inner endeavor. Ideas, by their greater mobility, become means in the
service of the will. What I make my constant thought will gradually also
determine my feeling. Naturally it holds of such feelings, as of ideas
produced at will, that they hardly get the life of those that arise
involuntarily.

It is the intervals between the strong emotions that must be used to gain
mastery over the life of feeling. Education must here necessarily precede
self-education, and even when we take the matter into our own hands, we
often need a helping hand (``differentials of push'') so as not to sink
back.

4) A clear insight into the causes of the feeling acts back upon it,
clarifying and purifying it. \emph{The endeavor to understand} the feeling
that masters me will therefore be able to set me freer over against it.
Feeling as a rule has an indefiniteness that forms part of its power, and
that can vanish before
% --- p. 390 ---
\opage{390}clear knowledge, as ghosts before the light of day. It is a
common experience that the conviction of the inevitability of grief
softens it. Knowledge of causes acts most of all on feelings such as
hypochondria, which nourish illusions and suspicion. \emph{Kant} became
master of his hypochondria, ``which in earlier years bordered on
weariness of life,'' through the knowledge that it was due to his flat
and narrow chest.
% footnote *) on p. 390
``The oppression,'' he says,\footnote{Von der Macht des Gemüths durch
den blossen Vorsatz seiner krankhaften Gefühle Meister zu sein.
(Kehrbach’s edition p.~26).} ``has remained; for its cause lies in my
bodily build; but I have become master of its influence on my thoughts
and actions.'' \emph{Lichtenberg}, who has been called
% footnote **) on p. 390
``the Columbus of hypochondria,''\footnote{\emph{Feuchtersleben}: Zur
Diätetik der Seele. 10te Aufl. p.~169.} relates that when, during his
nervous illness, he stopped his ears with his fingers, he felt better,
since he now regarded the morbid buzzing as something artificial.

When knowledge leads to the conviction of a fixed order of things, into
which we with all our wishes and sorrows are woven in such a way that its
laws are the laws of our life, the way is paved for resignation. There
are those who, instead of feeling day by day ``the slings and arrows of
outrageous fortune,'' choose with \emph{Spinoza} and \emph{Goethe} to
resign once for all. ``These convince themselves of the eternal, the
necessary and the lawful, and seek to form for themselves such concepts
as are irrefutable, indeed as are not abolished but confirmed by the
contemplation
% footnote ***) on p. 390
of the transitory.''\footnote{Aus meinem Leben. 16tes Buch. --- Cf.\
``Spinoza’s Liv og Lære'' [Spinoza's Life and Teaching]. p.~139 f.}

\textbf{c.} Through its influence on knowledge and feeling \emph{the will
acts back upon itself}. Ideas and feelings are the motives of the will,
and it is thus possible, according to what has now been developed, that
our motives themselves can become objects for the will. In this sense one
can will one's own will. One can do so also in the sense that one can set
oneself the task of training in oneself the capacity to make decisions, to
put an end to the inner debate and deliberation. Finally, \emph{to will}
can also mean that one wills to maintain and carry through one's decision
and not let it be overturned by moods turning up
% --- p. 391 ---
\opage{391}later. This finds application especially when the chosen end
requires the application of a whole series of means, a multiplicity of
single actions. One wills a, and therefore wills also b, c, d\ldots

The will is never either something concluded or something absolutely
beginning. It is impossible to point out any point where receptivity,
passivity, gives way entirely to activity, or conversely. No
psychological dissecting knife, however sharp and surely handled, will be
able to hit a line that separates the drawing power of fantasy and feeling
from voluntary surrender. There can be an infinitely varying relation
between the two sides, but at no moment does either of them fall away
entirely. When it says in Goethe's ``Fischer'': »Halb zog sie ihn, halb
sank er hin«, the doubling repeats itself within the second member; for
to \emph{sink} is to \emph{let} oneself \emph{sink}. Here, then, there is
no first and no last, but an infinite relation of interaction between
% footnote *) on p. 391
acting and suffering.\footnote{Cf.\ V A, 8; B, 11. --- VI B, 12; E, 2.}

\textbf{3.} \emph{Relations of opposition between the will and the other
elements of consciousness}.

The higher development of the life of will is possible only through the
influence of knowledge and feeling. But during the transition from lower
to higher forms of will, stoppages and paralyses can set in as a
consequence of a disharmonious relation between the different elements of
the life of consciousness. In the main the general law of inhibition is
at work here, in that sensations and ideas that cannot fuse with the given
motive of action tend to cow and displace it.

The very ``will to live'' can be cowed in this way. With increasing
civilization, emancipation and enlightenment the frequency of suicide
increases; it is greater in Protestant than in Catholic countries, in the
towns than in the
% footnote **) on p. 391
country.\footnote{\emph{Legoyt}: Le suicide ancien et moderne. Paris
1881. p.~293--315.} Reflection weighs the value of life, which instinct
does not engage in. The more possibilities, wishes and needs assert
themselves, the more difficult it can become to get a favorable result
from the calculation. Over against possibility actuality pales, and the
reflective, contemplative life can therefore lead to weariness of life,
% --- p. 392 ---
\opage{392}as was already the case in the monasteries of the Middle Ages,
where spiritual slackness and enervation (acedia, animi remissio, mentis
enervatio) were counted among the cardinal sins. In more recent times this
feeling seems to be even more frequent. Naturally suicide cannot always be
derived from lack of will; but it is certain that it is most often a
capitulation before a resistance that one regards as insuperable. The
energy of the will is measured by the resistance.

When children are to learn to walk, self-confidence is an essential
support. An action proceeds more easily when one has a strong assurance
that it will succeed. All reflection and \emph{doubt} paralyzes, and in
any case begins a period in which energy is dissolved and divided. The
child can perhaps walk briskly when no one speaks to it, but totters and
falls when various impressions divide its attention. The will must
according to its nature always be limited. Its object is one definite
thing, and thoughts and feelings attached to other things must always act
in a more or less paralyzing way. Hence a higher moral faith, which opens
wider spheres and horizons, often brings at first a decline in energy, so
that there comes to be an inverse relation between extent and strength.
If only strength is taken into account, instinct and authority evidently
stand as the foremost forces determining the will. With immediate
certainty the way is here traced out; the future almost loses the
character of possibility. There are, as Turginjew says somewhere, no
stronger powers in us than precisely those feelings that we ourselves
know only as dimly surmised secrets. Enlightenment and reflection always
contain, to that extent, a danger. The question is whether the will of
instinct can become a will of reason with the same energy, just as within
the life of feeling the task is to develop the ideal feelings to the same
degree of strength as the physical ones possess. In the opinion of many
the Negroes have gone backward since their emancipation, and much
distrust of the consequences of political freedom is explained by the
same cause. All spiritual development takes time.

Shakspere's Hamlet stands as the famous type of such a stage of
transition. The poet has presented in him his own and his age's life of
thought and feeling, and yet has set him in an age in which purgatory is
believed in, and in which blood-revenge is a duty.
% --- p. 393 ---
\opage{393}Even if this should be only the consequence of the frame and
main features of the old legend having been preserved, although the
character of the chief person is altered, Hamlet, as he appears to us in
the tragedy, gives the impression of a life of thought and feeling that
does not fit the task set him. Saxo's Amleth has no scruples, although he
behaves circumspectly enough. Each of the steps in his intricate plan he
takes with certainty, guided by the drive of self-preservation and by the
instinct of revenge. Hamlet has Amleth's cleverness and wit, but not his
ruthlessness, although he is placed far more favorably for being able to
act, since, as he says himself, he has both ``cause, and will, and
strength, and means'' to do it. What is only hinted at in Orestes (in
\emph{Aeschylus}, and, characteristically enough, somewhat more strongly
in \emph{Euripides}), a \emph{doubt} and wavering in the decision, fills
out the whole of Hamlet's character. Lack of energy and of the capacity
for decision can hardly be ascribed to him, since on several occasions he
shows presence of mind enough; but there is a dividedness in his being,
an inclination to sink away into reflections and feelings, which are no
doubt aroused by his circumstances and his task, but which lead far away
from these and consume a part of the energy that in Amleth and Orestes
goes immediately to the benefit of the action. It is what he himself
expresses, that ``conscience [i.e.\ reflection] does make cowards of us
all,'' and ``the native hue of resolution is sicklied o'er with the pale
cast of thought.'' Hamlet is rightly regarded as the representative of
the man of the modern age, in so far as it is peculiar to this age that
reflection has awakened and turns, searching and testing, against instinct
and authority. The problem is whether a new certainty can be won in place
of the one lost.
% TR: Shakespeare, quoted by Høffding in Danish (Foersom's version), is
% given in his own words here and elsewhere (not in the Hamlet footnote
% on p. 382, which contrasts the Danish with the English); "Bevidstheden" is the
% Danish rendering of "conscience".

It is originally in the very interest of will and action that an interval
be established between the emergence of the motive and the decision.
Consciousness in general arises, of course, only when the impression does
not immediately release movement (see IV). In the interval the motives
are to contend with one another, so that the innermost being of the soul
may become determining for the action. But this play of possibilities can
exert an alluring or alarming power over the mind, so that it loses itself
in it without coming to decision and action. The way to madness lies here.
``As long as passion preserves its
% --- p. 394 ---
\opage{394}original energetic character, it only rarely leads to insanity,
because it summons understanding and will precisely to the highest
exertion of force, whereby both mutually keep each other in the path of
sobriety. Only passive passion, which dissolves into empty longing, vain
craving, foolish hoping or cowardly despondency, only that is the root of
insanity.''\footnote{\emph{Ideler}: Biographien Geisteskranker. p.~156.}

The task is therefore to return from the world of possibilities to what
is necessary according to circumstances. This limitation and narrowing
demands a resignation. S.~\emph{Kierkegaard} has aptly said that what is
lacking in such cases is really ``power to obey, to bow under what is
necessary in one's self, what must be called one's
% footnote **) on p. 394
limit.''\footnote{Sygdommen til Døden [The Sickness unto Death]. Kbhvn.
1849. p.~32.} To will is to bind oneself to something entirely definite.
Deliberation must therefore, however many winding ways it strikes into,
lead back at last to a simple starting point immediately given in our own
nature. A concentration must set in after the expansion. \emph{Aristotle}
already says: ``If one is always to deliberate, one is led into infinity.
Everyone stops inquiring how he is to act \emph{when he has led the
principle back} to \emph{himself}.''\footnote{Eth.\ Nicom. III, 5. (ed
Bekker p.~1113 a). --- This concentration, or return to an active
starting point, can take place with a jerk. Thus \emph{Plutarch} has
Caesar tear himself out of deliberation in \emph{passion}, to give himself
up to the future and cross the Rubicon (μετὰ θυμοῦ τινος ὥσπερ ἀφεὶς
ἑαυτὸν ἐκ τοῦ λογισμοῦ πρὸς τὸ μέλλον. Cæsar. cap.~22).} If the will is
not sick, such a principle or beginning (ἀρχή) must be able to be found.
The misfortune of the doubter and the dreamer is that he cannot find his
way back to himself. If he cannot himself remedy this, he must call on
the help of others, so that he may undergo a new education that can give
the motives their natural power over his mind.
% footnote †) on p. 394 (continues on p. 395)
\footnote{The use of opium produces lack of initiative, paralyzes the
capacity to begin and to attempt. De \emph{Quincey}: En Opiumsæders
Bekendelser [Confessions of an Opium-Eater]. p.~157: ``The opium-eater by
no means loses his moral consciousness, his higher longing and desire; he
wishes and longs as vehemently as ever before to be able to accomplish
what he regards as possible or feels to be the demand of duty; but his
apprehension of what is possible infinitely exceeds his capacity not only
to carry out, but even to make an attempt.''}

% --- p. 395 ---
\opage{395}The art of life consists on this point in bringing about the
right relation between expansion and concentration, freedom and
boundness.

\textbf{4.} \emph{The consciousness of the will}.

\textbf{a.} It turned out to be the distinguishing mark of will proper, in
contrast to instinct, that we know \emph{what we will}, become conscious
of the purpose and content of the will. On the other hand, we have not
yet raised the question of how we know that we \emph{will}, or what it
really is that stirs in us when we will something.

In the case of the phenomena of knowledge and feeling there was no reason
to raise a corresponding question. Sensations, ideas and feelings are
clearly prominent elements of consciousness. But it is not so easy to
point out the elements of will immediately.\footnote{As regards the
physiological side of the matter, see V A, 7 and VII A, 4.} What we
experience in our consciousness when we will can, on closer
investigation, be traced back to elements of knowledge and feeling. In
drive there is given a feeling of pleasure or displeasure with a certain
restlessness, brought about by dawning sensations of movement. By these
latter drive differs from mere feeling. In decision, the typical
expression of will proper, there is given the thought of a purpose and of
the means that are to be applied to reach it, the feeling of pleasure at
the thought of the realization of the purpose, and more or less lively
sensations of strain and collectedness. Neither in drive nor in decision,
then, do elements appear to our inner experience that do not occur
elsewhere too. What is peculiar to these phenomena is the definite
concentration or pointedness that arises when we regard the possible
action as ours. We adopt or anticipate the action, regard as an
accomplished act what, outwardly regarded, still stands only as a
possibility. In contrast to the inner action that the decision expresses,
the many changing wishes and fantasies stand only as possibilities.

% --- p. 396 ---
\opage{396}\textbf{b.} But here, then, the problem of reality turns up in
the domain of inner experience. What criterion can be given that there
has really been \emph{willing}, that an inner action has taken place? How
do we here distinguish possibility (wish and fantasy) from actuality?

Concentration differs only in degree from expansion. In every lively wish
a certain concentration also takes place. Wishes are related to decision
as the nebula to the articulated star system. But here it is not so easy
to make sure how far the formation has advanced. If the outer action does
not follow upon the inner, how can I then be certain that I have really
willed? Here it appears how much we lack a psychical dynamometer. --- The
decision stands as the conclusion of an inner debate. But what certainty
is there that the debate will not be taken up anew, so that the decision
perhaps ``is sicklied o'er with the pale cast of thought''? And if that
happens, was that decision then more than a wish?

In practice we rely here on our immediate feeling. We believe that we can
immediately notice in ourselves that a determination has taken place. We
feel a peculiar active expectation, feel ourselves set for a certain
movement. People whose calling compels them to make firm and unalterable
determinations (such as military men and judges) acquire more certainty
in this respect than others, though often, to be sure, only within the
circle of their professional activity. --- Full certainty can never be
had. We can here arrive only at a practical faith in ourselves, supported
by our self-experience, our acquaintance with our character. In any case
it is an inference that we draw, not an immediate fact, as a sensation, a
thought or a feeling is.

Although in practice sharp boundaries are drawn between wishes and
decisions, it is on the other hand also admitted that it is exceedingly
difficult to know oneself. Socrates confessed of himself that he did not
know whether it was a flaming and composite being that dwelt in him, or
whether it was a milder and nobler nature. Nowhere do confusion and
illusion lie so near as in the question of possibility or actuality in
the domain of inner nature. The
% --- p. 397 ---
\opage{397}anticipating influence of feeling (VI E, 4 a) manifests itself
nowhere more easily and more dangerously than here, where external control
is lacking. Many regard themselves as great heroes of will because they
have wallowed in ``great decisions,'' although these have never received
the tangible and prosaic form of outer action. Here, therefore, a constant
criticism must be exercised, of the same kind as that exercised over
against facts in the domain of external nature (see V D). The
consciousness of the will, our inner actuality, is just as little
immediate as the consciousness of external actuality. And just as little
there as here can our knowledge reach a complete conclusion. New depths
and new riddles always show themselves.
% TR: the print has "den ydre Vilkaarlighed" (external arbitrariness);
% its errata correct this to "Virkelighed" (actuality), followed here.

\textbf{c.} The ethical view agrees entirely with the psychological one
here, in that it pronounces its judgment on thoughts and wishes as well as
on decisions and actions. But on the other hand the ethical view likewise
rightly starts from the premise that our innermost being lies in the will.
The difficulty of finding the will clearly and distinctly in a single
phenomenon arises precisely from the fact that the will does not begin at
any single point, but from the beginning of the life of consciousness
stirs in every association of ideas and every emotion (cf.\ A, 5). What
is concentrated in the decision is an energy that is applied in less
intensive form in all knowledge and feeling. The psychology of the will
therefore really embraces the whole domain of the life of consciousness
(cf.\ IV, 7 e). The phenomena specially so called phenomena of will mark
only the high points of a process that extends through the whole life of
consciousness.

\textbf{5.} \emph{The will and unconscious psychical life}.

\textbf{a.} It holds of the will even more than of the other forms of
psychical life that it is not fully understood as long as we keep to the
clear daylight of consciousness. Even if our decisions and actions are
determined by motives that spring from our own innermost nature, it is not
said that these motives always stand out clearly before our consciousness.
In such cases we know well enough that we will, and \emph{what} we will,
but not distinctly \emph{why} we will it.

Here as everywhere (see III) there is a whole scale of transitions from
the conscious to the unconscious. At every moment there is no doubt one
thought, one mood, at the
% --- p. 398 ---
\opage{398}\emph{center of consciousness}, from which the other thoughts
and feelings asserting themselves at the moment shade off into the
unconscious. What is determined by the constant thought and springs from
the ruling feeling is what both we ourselves and others understand best.
But what at the moment, or at most moments, occupies the center of
consciousness need not be what is in reality most deeply grounded in our
nature. \emph{The center of individuality} does not always coincide with
the center of consciousness. Especially in weak and little-developed
individuals the center of consciousness shifts, even if certain thoughts
and feelings are those that most frequently rule. When it then comes to
action, it is no wonder if something happens that surprises both the
onlookers and the agent himself. Something can turn up that has never
before been forward in the foreground of consciousness, and which the
individual therefore does not rightly own to.

Nature gives us from the first a push forward, in that we already find
ourselves in activity when consciousness awakens. Only little by little
can consciousness gain influence on activity (inner and outward), and this
influence never becomes complete. The spontaneous drive to movement, the
reflex movements, and the half-conscious movements accompanied by obscure
feeling always preserve a certain independence, even after conscious
thought has officially taken the chair. They play a part, varying in the
single cases, in all conscious willing, and sometimes they break out in
open revolt. Here belong, e.g., the obscure incitements, which everyone
no doubt knows, to smash objects, to interrupt a serious speech, or to do
similar unreasonable and unmotivated things. This phenomenon has been
called the ``antilogy of the
% footnote *) on p. 398
will.''\footnote{\emph{Spitta}: Die Willensbestimmungen und ihr
Verhältniss zu den impulsiven Handlungen. Tübingen 1881.} Against the
rational will there rises up a drive inexplicable to ourselves, which is
nevertheless as a rule overcome, but in many cases with great effort. Such
phenomena establish the correctness of the proposition that we first come
to know our character from our actions. Since our nature or our character
is more comprehensive than the small part clearly lit up by
consciousness, and since our actions too can never fully and
% --- p. 399 ---
\opage{399}many-sidedly reveal our nature to ourselves and others, there
always remains a possibility of new experiences.

As long as the unconscious tendencies to activity work in the same
direction as the conscious thoughts and feelings, they are not noticed.
Their strength fuses with that of the conscious motives, to which we give
the honor or the shame for the whole action. We feel free and unhindered
in our activity. Only when they work against the end of conscious
endeavor do we become aware that there is something in us of which we are
not, or at any rate not yet, fully masters. The inner dividedness, the
feeling of a resistance, is at the same time a feeling of unfreedom.

Such a feeling of unfreedom often marks the transition to a higher stage
of the life of will. It is the condition for a judgment being passed on
our earlier willing. For as long as our will works with undivided energy,
there is no room for any valuation or judging, but we work our way
forward in a straight line. The interval spoken of earlier here shows its
great importance. It can bring stoppage, also wavering and disharmony in
the mind, but it is necessary for a higher development. It can lead to a
complete condemnation and rejection of the earlier direction of will. And
when in memory we go back to such times, when conflicting forces moved in
us, we often take the part of the vanquished. In regret and repentance we
acknowledge that it was the better, the justified part of us that
succumbed. Just as deliberation can lead to losing oneself in infinite
reflection, so repentance can lead to morbid brooding over what cannot be
undone. But in repentance there stirs, on the other hand, a drive to
higher development, a drive that, like all drive, is aroused by the
feeling of the contrast between ideal and actuality. The self-rejection
and self-contempt to which repentance can lead would be unbearable if
repentance itself were not precisely a sign that nobler forces dwell in
us. What wounds, then, also heals. That self-rejection has its great
psychological and ethical importance in being a sign that we are working
our way out of the formerly ruling direction of will, just as it is a
sign of recovery from mental illness when one acknowledges that one has
been insane.

% --- p. 400 ---
\opage{400}\textbf{b.} If one keeps purely empirically to what asserts
itself in and for consciousness before and during the action, one cannot
establish the validity of the law of causality in the domain of the will,
or of psychical life in general. The law of causality has here, as
everywhere, only the significance of a presupposition, a postulate, with
which we approach every domain of our inquiry. Where we understand
anything at all, it is by finding a causal connection, and when we want to
understand the life of will, we must therefore also assume that the law
of causality can find application here as in other domains. This
assumption is in itself justified and cannot be refuted. For even where we
cannot, not even hypothetically, find any explanation, it is nevertheless
most natural to assume that the causes lie so deep, or that the
circumstances under which they act are so intricate, that we are unable
to see through them. That is the conclusion we draw in every other domain
of inquiry when explanation fails us. \emph{Determinism}, which assumes the
validity of the law of causality for the life of the soul and of the will
as well, therefore need not come forward in dogmatic form.
\emph{Indeterminism}, on the other hand, proceeds dogmatically, in that, in
good scholastic fashion, it disguises its ignorance by deriving
inexplicable actions from a cause-free will presupposed for this purpose.
This concept is a psychological counterpart to the ``vital force,'' now
pretty well abandoned, in physiology. That we cannot find the cause does
not prove that there is no cause, and proves still less that there exists
a cause-free force. Observation certainly cannot settle the dispute, since
from the indeterminist side it can always be maintained that it rests on
an illusion when one believes one experiences that the victorious motive
was also the strongest beforehand. But determinism is nevertheless more
favorably placed with respect to experience and observation than
indeterminism; for there is a possibility that it can be experienced and
known that there are causes; but it is meaningless to speak of an
experience that there is \emph{no} cause.

Precisely the more highly developed the will is, the clearer an
understanding of it can be won. The whole-cast, energetic characters we
can understand, because every utterance and action is determined by the
thoughts and feelings that give the character
% --- p. 401 ---
% footnote *) on p. 401
\opage{401}its stamp.\footnote{Conversely, it is also precisely
especially pronounced and energetic characters that have embraced
determinism. Thus the Stoics were determinists, while the Epicureans held
to freedom from causes; energetic and practical Calvinism is
deterministic, in contrast to soft, more passive Lutheranism; the English
philosophical school, with its practical tendencies, is deterministic, in
contrast to the speculative German school.} Where psychological
understanding fails, we stand as a rule before what is restlessly
fermenting, wild and uncontrolled --- thus precisely the opposite of
self-determination and true will. In the incoherent ideas of the insane
and of idiots (``Ideenjagd'') the cause-free would sooner have to be
sought, if one wanted to find it within the psychological domain.

What we call motives in the psychology of the will is, by the nature of
the case, not anything different from ourselves, from our ego or our
nature. Usage, to be sure, lets a motive ``act upon us,'' as if it were
an external power that pushed against us. Or one uses the image of the
scale-pan, where the weight is put on from outside. The motives act in us
and are themselves determined by our nature, partly by our earlier willing
and acting. The voluntary and the involuntary, the conscious and the
unconscious stand in an infinite interaction. Even the actions that are
not the fruit of an express choice spring from our being, and, if we will
not entangle ourselves in illusions (illusions which the theory of ``the
cause-free will'' can easily encourage), we must ascribe them to
ourselves. And on the other hand deterministic psychology impresses upon
us that every change in our character is possible only through the
influence of definite causes. The task, then, is not to will in general,
but to will something entirely definite. How much we can accomplish we can
never know beforehand. Our self-knowledge is always limited, and we cannot
know how much energy still rests unused in our nature. The experimental way
is the only one that can be applied here. Psychological determinism is
experimental and thereby differs from fatalism.\footnote{The fatalistic
proposition that what is to happen will happen, whatever we do, is a
self-contradictory superstition, which ought not constantly to be set up
as an argument against determinism. Indeterminism can in any case be hit
by an entirely similar sophism. ``For,'' one might say, ``if I have a
cause-free will, then I need only sit quite still and wait for it to
stir; and if it will not stir, what can I do about it?''}
% --- p. 402 ---
\opage{402}The conviction that in a single case we have used all our
powers can only be a practical faith, a postulate.

According to indeterminism all ``free'' actions must arise without
connection with the rest of the individual's nature. Consistent
indeterminism cannot help itself with phrases such as ``relative
independence'' or such (in this connection) self-contradictory
expressions. When it asserts that certain phenomena have no cause, it
must absolutely and ruthlessly declare the psychological connection
broken. But that is a denial of the principle of psychology. Such actions
can be summed up, but they cannot be links in an inner development. We
would then get the individual's psychical life dissolved into a series or
sum of members, a conception that we have constantly combated in the
foregoing. The life of consciousness would be dissolved into atoms, into
moments that had nothing to do with one
% footnote *) on p. 402
another.\footnote{Cf., e.g., J. G. \emph{Fichte}: Sittenlehre §~10.
``In a doctrine of nature every member can be explained. In a series of
determinations of freedom no member can be explained, but each is a first
and absolute one. Every free decision is itself substantial; it is what
it is absolutely through itself.'' The consequence of this must be a
psychological atomism. The decision is then no longer the conclusion of a
preceding process, but stands in an entirely indifferent relation to what
goes before: so it turns up in a blind and accidental way! --- On closer
study of Fichte's profound work one will, however, see that he is too
much of a psychologist to be in earnest with this doctrine.} Our
investigations have, on the contrary, at every point established it as
the peculiarity of the life of consciousness that past, present and future
do not stand strange and isolated over against one another. Everything
belonging to psychical life stands in the most intimate interaction. And,
as we have seen, purpose is ``the slave to memory.'' If memory has its
psychological laws, purpose and decision must also have theirs. One cannot
take the single act of will out of the whole course of development of the
life of will, or the will as such out of the intimate connection in which
it stands with the other sides of psychical life.

Further, indeterminism tears the individual loose from its natural
foundation. Every willing being has certain
% --- p. 403 ---
\opage{403}presuppositions, with which it begins and on which it always
builds. When the individual comes into possession of itself, it finds
itself at an entirely definite point; this point it must take as something
given. The task here for the individual is then to reconcile himself with
his nature, so that he sets up his tasks with constant regard to the
starting point given him. He must put up with the fact that there is
something that has taken the initiative in him before he himself could; at
any rate he could not take the initiative to his own existence. The
resignation demanded here by the nature of things can take on a religious
character, in that it appears as trust and confidence in the world-law
that has governed the coming into being of the individual with this
definite natural foundation. (Cf.\ VI B, 12).

The general reasons that lead to the assumption of the identity
hypothesis concerning the relation between soul and body (II, 8) also
speak decidedly against the assumption of a cause-free willing: such a
willing would come into irreconcilable conflict with the law of the
conservation of energy.

Finally, in so far as indeterminism relies on ethical grounds, the debate
about the ``freedom'' of the will does not concern psychology. It becomes
ethics' own affair whether it will erect its building on dogmatic ideas
instead of on the guiding thoughts of the science of experience. It must,
however, be remarked that the dogma of a cause-free will will prove on
closer investigation to be a presupposition that ethics itself can do
without, indeed even a
% footnote *) on p. 403
questionable one.\footnote{See the last chapter of my book ``Om
Grundlaget for den humane Etik'' [On the Foundation of Humane Ethics].}

Psychology must, according to its principle, start from the premise that
every spiritual phenomenon, and hence also every decision and action, has
its cause. It maintains that we ourselves are the cause of our actions, and
seeks only to find in the single cases what this ``we ourselves'' is like,
and what elements within our individuality they are that can make the
action understandable. It seeks to describe the different stages of
development that the active side of our nature can run through, and the
transition from the one to the other. For the highest stage within this
course of development there has been used, from the time of Socrates
% --- p. 404 ---
\opage{404}on, the expression inner or spiritual freedom, in a sense
that must surely be kept apart from the cause-freedom of indeterminism.
Freedom in the Socratic sense of the word stands in contrast not to
necessity, but to chance, blindness and spiritual bondage. It is that
concentration and independence in the will which brings it about that a
man in his whole conduct is in harmony with his innermost conviction, with
his deepest feeling and his clearest knowledge. It is in this sense of the
word that it is said: ``Ye shall know the truth, and the truth shall make
you free.'' Freedom is thus for psychology a result, which is reached,
and can only be reached, by definite ways.

Just as it is the business of psychology to explain spiritual phenomena,
so it is the business of ethics to \emph{evaluate} them. Evaluation and
explanation need by no means meet each other as hindrances (cf.\ I, 9).
When my ethical feeling is sufficiently alive, I condemn my evil thought
or my evil action, however well I understand how they came about under the
given circumstances. I condemn them even the more strongly the better I
understand them: for the more closely must they hang together with my
nature, and the more concerned about myself must I become.

\section*{C. The Individual Character}\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{C. The Individual Character}

\textbf{1.} All life of consciousness is individual. Memory and thinking,
pleasure and pain, drive and decision all presuppose a common inner
center. It is the task of psychology to present the elements, forms and
laws common to all life of consciousness. But these appear in reality in
infinitely many different combinations and nuances. The general, abstract
individuality of which psychology speaks is only a schema, which in every
given case is filled out in a different way. This multiplicity general
psychology cannot exhaust; that is the business of immediate experience of
life, of poetry and of history. Psychology has only to point to certain
typical differences, which are conditioned by the relation among the
various psychical elements and forms of activity.

In the first place it will make a characteristic difference whether it is
the elements of knowledge, of feeling or of will that predominate in the
individual. Next, within
% --- p. 405 ---
