\opage{405}each single kind of ruling elements, there can again be a single
direction that predominates. Thus within the domain of knowledge
sense-perception and thinking stand as opposites; within sense-perception
the different senses (cf., e.g., the difference between the painterly and
the musical gift); and within thinking the associations by similarity and
by contiguity. Within the domain of feeling the contrast between physical
and ideal feelings is of great importance; next, the contrast between
pleasure and displeasure, egoism and sympathy. As regards, finally, the
will, there are individuals who are predominantly guided by drive and
instinct, while others work their way laboriously forward through a
series of decisions. There are some in whom the will appears especially
as inhibiting, others in whom the positively selecting and potentiating
activity of will is most prominent. --- And to all these differences are
added, in each single domain, differences in strength, speed and extent.

From of old, psychology has laid most weight on the original dispositions
of feeling that determine the fundamental tone of the spiritual life,
whatever direction it may otherwise take. Both \emph{talent} and
character are determined by \emph{temperament}, just as feeling occupies a
central position over against knowledge and will. Temperament is
conditioned by the organic constitution and shows itself immediately in
the feeling of life, the fundamental mood that, apart from definite
external experiences, rules the mind. In temperament the vegetative life
of man, so to speak, becomes determining for his spiritual life. As a
background given from the first, it conditions the way in which all
experiences are taken up by him, and therefore also the way in which he
acts back upon his surroundings.

Already in antiquity \emph{Galen} set up the doctrine of the four
temperaments, corresponding to the four elements of Empedocles and the
four organic humors of Hippocrates. The physiology on which the doctrine
of temperaments relied has long since been abandoned. For the most part,
irritability is now taken as the basis, following
\emph{Haller}.\footnote{Cf.\ H. \emph{Hertz}: Temperamentslærens Historie
[History of the Doctrine of Temperaments]. Kbhvn. 1856.} The differences
of temperament thus rest on the different strength, speed and liveliness
with which
% --- p. 406 ---
\opage{406}influences from outside are received and answered. \emph{Kant}
thus distinguished between temperaments of feeling and of activity: the
temperaments of feeling are the light-blooded (sanguine) and the
heavy-blooded (melancholic), the temperaments of will the warm-blooded
(choleric) and the cold-blooded (phlegmatic). In most recent times
\emph{Wundt} has taken the relation between the strength of emotion and
its speed as the basis, and has thereby come back by another way to the
old fourfold division: choleric (strong and quick), melancholic (strong
and slow), sanguine (weak and quick) and phlegmatic (weak and
% footnote *) on p. 406
slow).\footnote{\emph{Kant}: Anthropologie. 2te Aufl. p.~255 ff. ---
\emph{Wundt}: Physiol. Psychol. II. p.~347 f. --- \emph{Sibbern} comes
closest to Kant. Psykologisk Patologi [Psychological Pathology] §~55.}

However essential a point of view has been grasped here, irritability or
the capacity for reaction being the fundamental property of everything
living, there is nevertheless something that the old fourfold division
does not properly bring out, namely the disposition toward the one or the
other of the great contrasts of the life of feeling; and yet the whole
color and direction of the life of moods is conditioned by this. To the
four old temperaments one might therefore add the bright and the dark
temperament. And this contrast is more fundamental than the one
underlying the first four temperaments, since it has its root in the very
fundamental conditions of the existence of the individual organism.
Physiologically regarded, the contrast between the bright and the dark
temperament points to the influence of the vegetative functions on the
brain, while the first four temperaments must be traced back to the
greater or lesser ease with which the nervous central organs are set in
activity by external
% footnote **) on p. 406
influences.\footnote{Cf.\ \emph{James Sully}: Pessimism. A history and a
criticism. London 1877. p.~405--414.}

\textbf{2.} The origin of the individual character points back to the
origin of the individual organism. In another connection (VI B, 7) we have
had occasion to mention how early the germ arises from which a new
organism develops. When fertilization has taken place and growth begins,
the result is determined at every point by the relation between growth and
differentiation determined from within, on the one side, and the
\emph{mechanical conditions} under which the
% --- p. 407 ---
\opage{407}development takes place, on the other. In the single cases it
is extraordinarily difficult to say whether the formation of a form is
conditioned by inner processes or by the influence of the ``mechanical
factors.'' Malformations often arise from an originally healthy germinal
rudiment, which becomes diseased
% footnote *) on p. 407
during development.\footnote{\emph{Kölliker}: Entwickelungsgeschichte
des Menschen. 2te Ausg. p.~385 ff. --- Panum: Bidrag til Kundskab om
Misfostrenes fysiologiske Betydning [Contribution to the Knowledge of the
Physiological Significance of Monstrosities]. Kbhvn. 1877.
(Universitetsprogram). p.~70 ff.} --- After birth too, physical conditions
(nourishment, climate, etc.) intervene determiningly. Bodily stunting,
e.g., leads also to spiritual stunting, and statistics establishes the
influence of external circumstances on men's actions. But for each single
individual the power of the physical conditions is nevertheless always
conditioned by the inner conditions with which the individual meets them.
Individuality is thus here always presupposed.

The same holds as regards \emph{social} causes. Imitation, education,
relations of authority play an extraordinarily great part in the spiritual
development of every individual. Even \emph{Fichte}, who so one-sidedly
and energetically maintained the inner originality of personality, its
capacity to determine itself, cannot explain the development from lower
to higher stages without presupposing influence from outside, even if
this is to consist only in the release of the spring. In spiritual growth
it is still more difficult to keep the inner and the outer influences
apart than it was in bodily growth. While some regard individuality as
given from the first, so that what is lived through, experienced and
learned gets only an entirely subordinate importance, others have
sometimes (thus \emph{Helvetius}, and in our days \emph{Stuart Mill})
derived all difference in mental powers from different education. Against
this speaks the experience that education acts most on intermediate
natures. The stubbornness of racial characters in spite of uniform
education likewise shows that an original foundation constantly asserts
itself.

A more deeply lying point of view is given us by \emph{heredity}. Organic
individuals arise through propagation. The germs of the new organisms
develop from earlier organisms, and since
% --- p. 408 ---
\opage{408}it now turns out that they inherit the constitution of these
in a greater or lesser degree, it seems to be a natural assumption that
the origin of individuality and its properties are due to the stock from
which it springs. The properties that cannot be explained by physical and
social causes will perhaps find their explanation when one goes back to
the earlier generation. What stands as inexplicable in the individual can
become explicable in the race. There is no individual trait for which one
will not be able to get an explanation by investigating the history of
the race. Such an investigation becomes difficult and intricate because
heredity ramifies without end, and because several generations can be
skipped
% footnote *) on p. 408
(atavism).\footnote{The expression ``atavism'' was first used by
\emph{Duchesne} of plants. \emph{Proper Lucas}: Traité philos. et
physiol. de l’hérédité naturelle. Paris 1847--50. II. p.~43, ---
\emph{Aristotle} already knew this phenomenon. Hist.\ anim. VII, 6. (ed.\
Bekker p.~585 b). De gener.\ anim. I, 18 (p.~722 a).} What has once been
deposited in the human organization is not easily rooted out again.
Through this interaction between a fixed type (which, even where it is
broken, can turn up again by atavism), --- properties implanted by
crossing, --- and properties acquired by adaptation to new circumstances
of life and experiences, by practice and suffering, --- a prospect opens
of an infinity of different combinations or syntheses.
% TR: "Proper Lucas" as printed, for Prosper Lucas.

Heredity in the race has often been compared with memory in the single
man. But just as memory does not preserve everything, and therefore does
not explain everything in a man's life either, so heredity too is no more
than a tendency in nature to preserve what has been acquired. How far this
tendency goes, and how far it is able to overcome new circumstances and
experiences, is a question that must be taken up anew in each single
case. Empiricism, which derives the content and peculiarity of
consciousness from individual experiences of life, therefore always keeps
a justified place open. There are in the race, as in individual
consciousness, two currents or tendencies (cf.\ V B, 2), which can stand
in the most various relations to each other. A special class of
individual differences rests precisely
% --- p. 409 ---
\opage{409}on whether the inherited, originally given constitution or what
is experienced in the course of life plays the greater part in the
formation of character. The sanguine and choleric temperaments thus let
external influences exert a greater influence than the melancholic and
phlegmatic.

Without going here more closely into the theories of heredity, we shall
merely stress a couple of general results.\footnote{Cf.\ Decandolle: Sur
la part d’influence de l’hérédité, de la variabilité et de la selection
dans le développement de l’espèce humaine. (In the book: Histoire des
sciences et des savants depuis deux siècles. Genève, Bâle, Lyon. 1873.
p.~308--402). --- Ribot: L’hérédité psychologique. 2e éd. Paris 1882.}
--- 1) The more deeply something is taken up into the organization, the
sooner it is inherited. What has just been acquired is in unstable
equilibrium and is easily cancelled by crossing influences. --- 2)
Physical properties are inherited more easily than spiritual ones, simple
talents more easily than those that rest on the cooperation of several
mental powers. Instincts are inherited soonest; next come the powers of
sense; dispositions of feeling are inherited sooner than intellectual
capacities. --- 3) Only elementary forms and dispositions are inherited.
What is innate thus has a greater or lesser indefiniteness, and it depends
on individual experiences in what degree and in what direction it is
developed.

\textbf{3.} In the physical, social and inherited circumstances we have
the elements from which the building of individuality is from the first
erected. Our experience shows us no other domains where we could seek the
stones for this building. Only by a purely mystical expedient would one
escape the interminable investigations that are thus necessary in the
case of every individuality. Individuality has sometimes been ascribed an
absolute originality, regarded as an ``Urposition'' or an eternal monad.
If one takes such a presupposition as basis, the problem of the origin of
individuality naturally falls away entirely. But such a presupposition is
arbitrary and unscientific. It can certainly be said that there is in
nature a law of individuality, in so far as development in all domains
leads to the formation of individual wholes at different stages (see
above p.~102). But it is precisely the task of inquiry to find the
elements
% --- p. 410 ---
\opage{410}of which these wholes are formed, and the laws according to
which they arise. Inquiry can and must admit that over against the
individual it does not succeed in dotting the i, that here, as in all
synthesis (cf.\ V C, 8), there is an x that escapes it --- that
individuality therefore stands as an irrational number, which can only be
approximately determined. But it is of decisive importance here, as in all
domains, to keep empirical inquiry free of mystical and speculative
interventions.

From a purely psychological standpoint we must go a step further. Even if
the individual organism, which in spite of its wholeness and relative
closedness is yet a republic of cells, were explained as a synthesis of
elements, and its origin could be understood according to the law of the
conservation of energy, the individual consciousness, the formation of a
peculiar center for memory, acting and suffering, would not thereby be
explained. That such an inner center can come into being is the greatest
problem, the fundamental problem in all our knowledge. Each single trait,
each single property, we can perhaps explain to ourselves by the help of
the power of heredity and the influence of experiences; but the inner
unity to which all single elements are referred, and by which
individuality is a \emph{psychical} individuality, stands as the constant
riddle. It is impossible for us, as we have remarked in an earlier
connection, to carry through an analogy to the conservation of energy in
the spiritual domain. Psychical individuality is one of the actual
boundary points of science.

\textbf{4.} Not only individual properties and peculiarities of family or
race, but also the forms and characteristic marks valid for the whole
human race, for all human consciousness, have in most recent times been
sought to be explained by way of heredity. Even before Darwin's hypothesis
of the origin of species came forward, \emph{Herbert Spencer} (in the
first edition of ``Principles of Psychology,'' 1855) set up the theory
that the fundamental forms and powers of consciousness have developed
through the adaptation of the primeval generations to the conditions under
which their life was led. The forms of thought and feeling that are
typical of the human race are therefore a priori for the single
individual: i.e.\ they cannot be fully explained
% --- p. 411 ---
\opage{411}by its experiences, but these, conversely, are conditioned by an
original foundation. On the other hand, those forms find their explanation
when one goes back to the whole race and the infinite series of
experiences it must have had in the course of its development. What is à
priori for the individual is thus a posteriori for the race.

This hypothesis is an attempt to lead the debate between idealism and
realism into a new path and to settle it by giving each its due. Idealism
is right as regards the single individual, while realism holds as regards
the whole race. The earlier treatment of the problem (cf.\ the dispute
between \emph{Locke} and \emph{Leibnitz}, \emph{Hume} and Kant) really
took account only of individual consciousness. Spencer has given up one
of realism's most advanced positions, in order the more energetically to
hold a more withdrawn position, a position that the narrower and purely
empirical standpoint of the older realism prevented it from taking up.

This attempt at a solution nevertheless arouses misgivings of a twofold
kind.

In the first place, race is a collective concept. At any given time the
race consists of a certain number of individuals. It is these individuals
who struggle for existence, exercise their powers, and in adaptation to
the conditions acquire an organization that can be inherited by the next
generation. But the individuals --- however far back we go --- always
come with an organization, thus with certain forms and powers that they
have not themselves acquired, thus with something a priori for them. At
every stage of the great process of development there is a given
foundation that determines the effect of all experiences. It must
therefore hold for the race as for the individual that the outer always
presupposes the inner, that what is acquired is conditioned by what is
originally laid down. This is a fundamental relation that always repeats
itself. We here enter upon an infinite series; thought descries no limit,
but has its footing in the ruling law.

Spencer's theory recalls Plato's mystical doctrine of knowledge as a
recollection from pre-existence.\footnote{Cf.\ my treatise on Plato's
psychology in Tidsskrift for Filologi 1876, p.~209 ff.} There is
% --- p. 412 ---
\opage{412}indeed the great difference that, while Plato explains what in
our consciousness cannot be derived from our experiences as acquired in a
spiritual pre-existence, where the souls in the company of the gods beheld
the eternal ideas, according to Spencer the foundation on which our
spiritual life is built has been brought about by the labors, sufferings
and struggles of millions of generations. But Plato and Spencer both
overlook that what is at issue is a question of principle, and that if
existence presupposes an a priori, such an a priori is also presupposed
for every pre-existence, whether we think of it in a realistic or in a
mystical way. An absolutely first or last we do not reach, according to
the nature of our knowledge. The fundamental relation between an inner
and an outer, which is the general law of life, and therefore also of
psychical life, is a special example of the law of relation, like the
relation between subject and object.

In the second place, a definite distinction must be made between the
psychological and the epistemological point of view. Psychologically
regarded, the hypothesis of development is a great advance. It opens for
us a wide horizon, a prospect of explanation which was hitherto closed. It
is, psychologically as physiologically, an entirely justified principle,
and will surely also prove more and more to be a fruitful one, that what
is inexplicable in the individual can become explicable in the race. But
epistemologically the matter stands otherwise. The ultimate principles to
which the analysis of our knowledge leads us are also the ultimate
presuppositions that we can reach. All explanations, proofs and
hypotheses --- thus also the hypothesis of development itself --- rest on
them. How comprehensive this logical foundation of all our knowledge is,
it is the business of the theory of knowledge, not of psychology, to
investigate. Psychology is a special discipline, which itself presupposes
the general principles of our knowledge and cannot explain their validity.
The unassailable standpoint of idealism is given in the necessity of
thought that underlies every realistic hypothesis.

\end{document}
